How to read this deck

Every card answers something the final study guide actually asks, plus a few marked high-yield cards the decks stress.

One card per study guide item

Why it mattersThe final study guide names eighteen topics and, inside each, a specific list of things to know. Each card answers one of those named items. Cards tagged high-yield · beyond the listed asks cover something the decks stress about that topic.

The panels are colour-coded

Why it mattersWhy the fact behaves as it does.
Exam anchorThe pearl, the trap, the number to memorise.
GapHonestly absent from the decks and the book — go look it up.
CautionSources disagree; the panel says which one to answer.

Every card has a source line

Why it mattersThe grey line at the foot of each card names the deck and slide (or speaker note), or the Hess chapter and section. Where the book adds to a deck, the text says Hess adds. Where no deck covers an item, the card says so first and answers from Hess.

01

Weaning Parameters and Extubation Assessment

16 items · Sensorium · CXR · CBC · MIP · VC · RSBI · VE · the rest of the extubation assessment

1The readiness screen: what must be true before any weaning parameter is measured2Sensorium (level of consciousness)3CXR (chest radiograph)4CBC (Hb/Hct, WBC)5MIP / NIF (maximum inspiratory pressure / negative inspiratory force)6VC (vital capacity)7RSBI (rapid shallow breathing index): formula, cutoff, worked example8V̇E (minute ventilation), spontaneous VT and RR9ABG and oxygenation criteria (PaO₂/FiO₂, PEEP, FiO₂, pH, shunt)10Hemodynamic stability11Pulmonary mechanics: static compliance, VD/VT, airway resistance12Spontaneous breathing trial (SBT)13Secretions, cough and airway protection14Cuff-leak test and post-extubation stridor15All weaning/extubation thresholds on one page16Why patients fail weaning, and what to fix first

1The readiness screen: what must be true before any weaning parameter is measured

Weaning is "a process of gradually reducing mechanical ventilatory support until a patient is able to assume sustainable spontaneous breathing". The goal is to transfer the WOB from the ventilator back to the patient safely. The deck's big principle is "We don't wean the ventilator, we wean the patient." The best predictor of weaning success is improvement of the underlying disease process. Before a weaning attempt, three groups of conditions should return to normal: patient/pathophysiologic, cardiac/circulatory, and dietary/acid-base/electrolyte. No single value determines weaning readiness. The deck groups the criteria into four concepts:

  1. Clinical readiness: is the patient recovering?
  2. Ventilatory readiness: RSBI, RR, VT, V̇E.
  3. Oxygenation readiness: P/F ratio, FiO₂, PEEP.
  4. Pulmonary reserve: VC, MIP/NIF, compliance, VD/VT.

The formal screen. RCP 203 (Lssn 2b slide 45; Ch 11 slide 90) lists when a ventilated patient "should undergo a weaning assessment":

  • evidence for "some reversal" of the underlying cause
  • adequate oxygenation (P/F ≥ 150–200, PEEP ≤ 5–8 cm H₂O, FiO₂ ≤ 0.4–0.5)
  • pH ≥ 7.25
  • hemodynamic stability (no myocardial ischemia or significant hypotension)
  • the capability to initiate an inspiratory effort

Hess adds: Box 20-7 gives the same content as four numbered criteria (oxygenation and pH are combined into one). It also warns that no single criterion is better at predicting extubation readiness than a spontaneous breathing trial (SBT), and that over-reliance on weaning parameters may prolong ventilation. RCP 203 adds that RR and RSBI are the best indicators of whether a patient can tolerate spontaneous breathing. MIP/NIF and VC are obtained less often. They are used mainly to monitor neuromuscular patients.

Definitions you will be tested on:

  • Weaning success: no ventilatory support for at least 48 hours after extubation. Oxygen, bronchodilators, PSV or CPAP may still be used.
  • Weaning in progress: the patient is extubated but still on NIV.
  • Weaning failure: a failed SBT, or reintubation within 48 hours of extubation.
  • Hess adds: extubation failure (the need to reintubate) occurs in 5% to 15% of patients.
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 9 (notes), 10, 17 (notes), 18 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 45 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 90 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 42 · Hess Ch 20 — Assessing Readiness for Liberation, Box 20-7 · Hess Ch 17 — Extubation

2Sensorium (level of consciousness)

Sensorium is the RCP 203 deck's other word for level of consciousness (LOC). You assess it quickly by asking the patient the time of day, where they are, and who they are. An alert patient is "oriented × 3" (time, place, person). Common causes of disorientation are:

  • neurologic injury
  • sedation and analgesics (especially opioids)
  • severe hypoxemia or hypercapnia

The deck states that only alert patients can fully cooperate and take part in their own care. The Glasgow Coma Scale is called "the most objective assessment of consciousness". Scores run from 3 (deep coma) to 15 (fully alert): 13–15 mild, 9–12 moderate, ≤ 8 severe (coma). The slide notes "GCS ≤ 8 = Intubate! [Consider need for airway protection]". Watch the wording elsewhere: the RCP 105 Critical Issues slide text prints mild as 13–14 and severe as 3–8 ("< 8 = Intubate"). Its own notes give mild 13–15 and "GCS ≤ 8". Hess Ch 41 grades severe as less than 9, which is the same as ≤ 8 for whole-number scores. Use 13–15 / 9–12 / ≤ 8.

Why it mattersWhy it matters in weaning.
  • VC and MIP are effort-dependent. A heavily sedated, confused or non-command-following patient may only look weak.
  • Diminished mental status is a sign of SBT failure. RCP 203 (Lssn 3a slide 60; Ch 11 slide 93) says to discontinue an SBT if there is a change in mental status (somnolence, coma, agitation, anxiety).
  • After neuromuscular blockade, the deck checks recovery with three bedside tests: head lift for 5 seconds, tongue protrusion for 5 seconds, and hand grip.
  • Sedation choice. Dexmedetomidine (Precedex) gives sedation without respiratory depression. It may be used as a continuous infusion before, during and after extubation.

Hess adds:

  • Box 42-5 (burn patients): "Sensorium: The patient must be awake and alert enough to protect the airway." Balancing pain medication against the need for an alert sensorium is a challenge. Combining SBTs with periodic sedation discontinuation helps assess mental status.
  • Ch 20: the ability to follow commands is desirable before extubation but not essential in patients who can otherwise protect the airway. Daily spontaneous awakening trials (SATs) coupled with SBTs shorten mechanical ventilation.
  • Ch 17: patients unable to complete four simple tasks (open eyes, follow with eyes, grasp hand, stick out tongue) are more than four times as likely to fail extubation.
Gap · still not covereda numeric GCS (or other LOC score) threshold for extubation. The sources give GCS ≤ 8 only as an intubation/airway-protection trigger; the RCP 202 Trauma deck says only to protect the airway when the GCS is low (slide 3 notes). Look in Hess Ch 41/Ch 17.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slides 6, 8 · RCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slide 20 (and notes) · Hess Ch 41 — Deficits (GCS) · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 6, 16 (notes), 21, 50 (notes), 71 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 60 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 93 · RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 3 (notes) · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5 · Hess Ch 20 — Causes of a Failed Spontaneous Breathing Trial; Sedation · Hess Ch 17 — Extubation

3CXR (chest radiograph)

No source in the vault gives a CXR criterion or cutoff for weaning or extubation. What the sources do give:

  • What a CXR evaluates. It looks at the thoracic structure: the lungs, pleural space, inserted catheters, and radiopaque lines and tubes. PA or AP films are most common.
  • What it can show in the weaning deck's terms. The deck lists the problems that decrease compliance and cause weaning failure:
    • Sudden: tension pneumothorax, pleural effusion, and migration of the ETT into a main-stem bronchus.
    • Gradual: pneumonia, ARDS, atelectasis.
    • The deck's memory trick is "Sudden = Search for the problem. Gradual = Treat the disease."
  • Specific CXR findings in the decks:
    • Pneumonia: increased density from alveolar consolidation or atelectasis, and air bronchograms.
    • VAP: new or progressive infiltrates on chest radiograph.
    • Inhalation injury: the initial CXR may be normal.
  • ETT position (RCP 203 Ch 9, slide 34): the only two ways to confirm proper tube placement above the carina are CXR or fiberoptic laryngoscopy. On the CXR, the ETT tip should be about 4–6 cm above the carina, usually between T2 and T4 (ribs 4 & 5). Hess Ch 7 also says 4 to 6 cm, but Hess Ch 17 says 3 to 7 cm (adults, neck neutral). Use 4–6 cm (the deck).

Hess adds:

  • Ch 17: ETT placement is verified by chest radiograph. The tube is secured at the 21-cm mark (women) / 23-cm mark (men) at the teeth until chest x-ray confirmation.
  • Ch 44 (myasthenia gravis): the presence of atelectasis was a risk factor for extubation failure and for reintubation.
Gap · still not coveredany CXR finding stated as a weaning or extubation requirement (e.g., "resolving infiltrates" or "no new infiltrate before SBT"). Look in Hess Ch 20 and Ch 7 or an instructor weaning handout.
SourceRCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 20 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 29 · RCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slide 27 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 44 · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 28 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 34 · Hess Ch 7 — Evaluation of Tubes and Catheters · Hess Ch 17 — Endotracheal intubation · Hess Ch 44 — Myasthenia Gravis

4CBC (Hb/Hct, WBC)

No source gives a CBC cutoff for weaning. The decks give the normals and the reason the CBC matters.

CBC value Normal (RCP 103 Clinical Data) Weaning relevance
Hemoglobin (Hb) 12–16 g/dL Anemia → ↓ oxygen-carrying capacity "even when oxygenation appears normal"
Hematocrit (Hct) 40–50% same as Hb
RBC 4–6 million/mm³ (men ~5, women ~4) same as Hb
Total WBC 5,000–10,000 /mm³ Infection and inflammation indicator

The weaning deck says a patient who is "septic, anemic, malnourished, or [has] severe electrolyte abnormalities" is much more likely to fail a weaning attempt, even with excellent ventilator settings. The Chang text in the notes lists inadequate oxygen delivery (low O₂ content or cardiac output) as a contributor to respiratory muscle weakness. For infection, two decks give the WBC patterns:

  • Pneumonia: ↑ WBC (bacterial), ↓ WBC (viral).
  • VAP: leukocytosis or leukopenia, together with fever, purulent secretions and a new CXR infiltrate.

The one deck Hb number for ventilated patients (RCP 105 Management of MV, "Strategies to Improve Oxygenation"): maintain a normal Hb level and correct anemia (hemoglobin < 10 g/100 mL). Anemia (Hb < 10 g/100 mL) should be reported along with blood gas results. This is an oxygenation-management threshold, not a stated weaning criterion.

Hess adds: fever and infection increase both O₂ consumption and CO₂ production. That raises the ventilatory requirement and can cause failure to liberate.

Gap · still not covereda Hb/Hct or WBC value stated as a weaning/extubation criterion. The decks give only Hb < 10 g/dL as an anemia-correction threshold in ventilated patients. Hess's only Hb transfusion thresholds are in Ch 23 (the ECMO chapter): 7–8 g/dL for ECMO, with 7–7.5 g/dL cited from general critical-illness studies. None is tied to weaning. Look in Hess Ch 20 / Ch 5 or an instructor handout.
SourceRCP 103 Clinical Data, slides 49, 50 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 9 (notes), 30 (notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 14 (and notes) · RCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slide 27 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 44 · Hess Ch 20 — Causes of a Failed Spontaneous Breathing Trial · Hess Ch 23 — Blood Transfusion

5MIP / NIF (maximum inspiratory pressure / negative inspiratory force)

MIP (aka NIF) measures inspiratory muscle strength, especially the diaphragm. The Chang text defines it as the negative pressure the patient can generate in 20 sec when inspiring against an occluded measuring device (negative pressure manometer). It is effort-dependent. Maximal effort may not occur until the ventilator has been off for a short period (20 to 30 sec).

Reading the sign. More negative = stronger. When books write "≥ −20 to −30", they mean the magnitude: the patient must generate at least 20–30 cm H₂O of negative pressure (−20, −30, −40 …).

Hess adds:

  • Measurement (Ch 20): an aneroid manometer on the ETT or trach. The airway is occluded for 20 to 25 s with a unidirectional (one-way) valve, and the patient forcibly inhales after maximum exhalation. Hess's figure caption states that with this one-way-valve system, MIP is measured at FRC.
  • Caveat: with high airway resistance or low compliance, even −20 cm H₂O might not be adequate.
  • Ch 39: the diaphragm is the last muscle paralyzed and the first to recover from neuromuscular blockade.
MIP / NIF value What it means Source
−20 to −30 cm H₂O (in 20 sec) or more negative Weaning readiness Weaning deck slide 16
At least −30 cm H₂O Weaning "will likely be successful"; studies averaged −37 (weaned) vs −30 (not weaned) Weaning deck slide 15 (notes, Chang)
Minimum −20 cm H₂O Needed on vent patients to be successfully extubated RCP 203 Ch 3 slide 19 (notes)
More negative than −20 cm H₂O Adequate inspiratory muscle strength Hess Ch 20
Less than −20 cm H₂O Muscle-strength criterion in burn patients Hess Box 42-5
−80 to −100 Normal Weaning deck slide 15; Neuro slide 21
−60 to −100 Normal Neuro slide 43
Normal NIF beyond −60; −40 COPD; −20 myasthenia gravis Normals by population (written "> −60" in RCP 203 and "< −60" in the Burn deck; both mean "at least 60 cm H₂O of force") RCP 203 Ch 3 slide 19 (notes); Burn slide 37 (notes)
Weaker than −30 Impending acute ventilatory failure (neuromuscular disease, an intubation indicator) Neuro slides 21, 43
Caution · sources differUse on this exam: the weaning deck's −20 to −30 cm H₂O range. −20 is the floor every source agrees on.
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 15 (notes), 16 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 19 (notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 37 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slides 21, 43 · Hess Ch 20 — Respiratory Muscles · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5 · Hess Ch 39 — Muscle Strength

6VC (vital capacity)

VC is the maximum exhalation following a maximum inspiration. It includes IRV, VT and ERV. It reflects pulmonary reserve: lung volume plus respiratory muscle strength.

Normals:

  • Weaning deck: ~50 mL/kg (4.8 L, based on IBW) = IRV 3.1 L + VT 0.5 L + ERV 1.2 L.
  • RCP 203: adult VC 3 to 5 L.

Weaning threshold: the weaning deck says VC > 10 mL/kg (Chang: the "minimal VC … consistent with successful weaning" is 10 mL/kg).

Other numbers you may see:

  • RCP 203 notes give a "critical minimum" of about 1 liter (15 mL/kg).
  • Hess Ch 20 lists 15 mL/kg among the classic predictors.
  • Hess Ch 44 (myasthenia gravis) uses FVC > 10 mL/kg as one sign that SBTs can begin.
  • Not a weaning number: in neuromuscular disease, FVC < 20 mL/kg signals impending ventilatory failure. The Neuro deck gives normal FVC as 60–70 mL/kg (slide 43) and 65–75 (slide 21).
Caution · sources differUse on this exam: > 10 mL/kg (weaning deck).

How to measure it on a vent patient. VC requires cooperation and is effort-dependent. Poor effort gives a falsely low value, and it is not possible in deeply sedated or paralyzed patients. If the patient has been on full support, let them breathe spontaneously for 3 min first. Then:

  1. Suction if needed.
  2. Disconnect the ventilator.
  3. Attach a respirometer (Wright spirometer) to the ETT or trach.
  4. Have the patient take a deep breath and exhale fully. Read the VC.
  5. Reconnect promptly.

RCP 203 adds that the bedside VC is a slow VC (SVC), done at least three times, with the best result recorded, and only in alert and cooperative patients. The weaning deck's neuromuscular-blockade slide adds that the patient needs enough VC to take an effective breath and produce a good cough after extubation.

SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 11 (notes), 15, 16 (notes), 50 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 18 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 40 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slides 21, 43 · Hess Ch 20 — Assessing Readiness for Liberation · Hess Ch 44 — Myasthenia Gravis

7RSBI (rapid shallow breathing index): formula, cutoff, worked example

Formula: RSBI = f ÷ VT (in liters), expressed in breaths/min/L. The deck's memory trick is "Rate ÷ Size." Rapid shallow breathing increases dead-space ventilation, because anatomic dead space is unchanged while VT falls.

  • Slow, deep breaths = low RSBI = good.
  • Fast, small breaths = high RSBI = bad (more likely to fail).

Cutoff:

  • The deck slide (Chang) says < 100 breaths/min/L for weaning readiness. The Chang text calls 100 "rounded from 105".
  • The notes add "< 105 in many protocols". They also give a tip: know Chang's < 100; for the NBRC you will commonly see < 105.
  • Hess Ch 20: RSBI < 105 predicts successful liberation, and > 105 may predict failure.

How it is measured (Chang):

  1. Take the patient off the ventilator and let them breathe spontaneously for 3 min (or until the pattern is stable).
  2. Measure minute expired volume (V̇E) and spontaneous f.
  3. Average VT = V̇E ÷ f.
  4. RSBI = f ÷ VT (L).

Accuracy relies on a stable breathing pattern and use of minimum PS.

Worked example 1 (RCP 203 slide): the patient breathes 38/min with an average VT of 300 mL. Convert to liters: 0.3 L. 38 ÷ 0.3 = 127. That is above 100/105, so it predicts weaning failure.

Worked example 2 (same formula): measured V̇E = 10 L/min at f = 25/min.

  • VT = 10 ÷ 25 = 0.4 L.
  • RSBI = 25 ÷ 0.4 = 62.5.
  • That is < 100, so the patient is a good weaning candidate.
  • Trap: if you divide by mL (25 ÷ 400 = 0.06), the answer is meaningless. The deck says "in L, not mL."
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 11, 12 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 15, 16 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 42 · Hess Ch 20 — Assessing Readiness for Liberation

8V̇E (minute ventilation), spontaneous VT and RR

Normals (weaning deck):

  • V̇E 6–10 L/min
  • spontaneous VT 5–10 mL/kg
  • spontaneous f 12–20
  • PaCO₂ 35–45 mm Hg

V̇E as a weaning parameter. The Chang text (Monitoring deck) says excessive WOB, meaning minute ventilation in excess of 10 L/min, is often associated with poor outcomes when weaning. A high V̇E means a high ventilatory demand. The Chang notes say: the higher the VD/VT, the greater the minute volume demand. At the bedside you measure VT, f and V̇E with a respirometer on a one-way valve over 1 minute, and VT = V̇E ÷ f.

Spontaneous VT. The minimal spontaneous VT consistent with successful weaning is 5 mL/kg. The NHLBI ARDS protocol in the RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slide 94) accepts ≥ 4 mL/kg PBW during its SBT; use 5 mL/kg unless the question is about the ARDS protocol (conflict, see conflicts file). In 11 studies, spontaneous VT averaged 368 mL in weaned vs 277 mL in non-weaned patients.

Hess adds: Ch 20 lists the classic predictors as:

  • spontaneous VT (5 mL/kg)
  • RR (30 breaths/min)
  • minute ventilation (12 L/min)
  • VC (15 mL/kg)
  • PImax (−20 cm H₂O)

The book prints these values without < or > signs. It also says high minute ventilation is a common cause of high respiratory-muscle load. Box 42-5 (burn) uses an unassisted VT of 6 to 10 mL/kg.

Caution · sources differUse on this exam: the deck's 10 L/min as the V̇E ceiling. Recognize Hess's 12 L/min.
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 11 (notes), 16 (notes), 17 (notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 30 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 15 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 94 · Hess Ch 20 — Respiratory Muscles; Assessing Readiness for Liberation · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5

9ABG and oxygenation criteria (PaO₂/FiO₂, PEEP, FiO₂, pH, shunt)

The deck's question is: "Does this patient still need a lot of oxygen and PEEP to stay oxygenated?" If yes, the patient is not ready. The example given is FiO₂ 100% and PEEP 15 cm H₂O, which is "usually not ready" even if the patient is comfortable.

Weaning deck slide and Chang criteria:

  • PaO₂ > 60 mm Hg (or SaO₂ > 90%) on FiO₂ ≤ 0.40
  • P/F ≥ 150 mm Hg
  • shunt (QS/QT) < 20%
  • P(A−a)O₂ < 350 mm Hg on FiO₂ 100%

P/F categories on the same slides: > 380 normal; < 300 ALI/mild ARDS (> 300 acceptable for most patients); < 200 ARDS. Elsewhere the decks add < 150 = proning.

Shunt categories: ≤ 10% normal, 10–20% mild, 20–30% significant, > 30% critical/severe. A shunt > 20% should be corrected before any weaning attempt.

A–a gradient normals: 5–15 mm Hg on room air; 25–65 mm Hg on 100%.

RCP 203 screen: P/F ≥ 150–200, PEEP ≤ 5–8 cm H₂O, FiO₂ ≤ 0.4–0.5, pH ≥ 7.25.

During an SBT (RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 59; Ch 11 slide 92):

  • SpO₂ ≥ 85–90% or PaO₂ ≥ 50–60 torr
  • pH ≥ 7.30
  • rise in PaCO₂ ≤ 10 torr

After neuromuscular blockade, before extubation (weaning deck): PaO₂ > 80 mm Hg and PaCO₂ < 45 mm Hg. COPD patients are compared to their baseline.

Don't reduce support for a low PaCO₂. Hyperventilation (↓ PaCO₂) during weaning may be due to hypoxia, pain, anxiety or inappropriate settings. Reducing support then further increases the WOB.

Hess adds:

  • Box 20-7: P/F > 150 to 200; FiO₂ ≤ 0.4 to 0.5; pH > 7.25. PEEP is printed in the book as "PEEP = 8 cm H₂O".
  • Box 42-5 (burn): P/F > 200 mm Hg.
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 13 (notes), 14 (notes), 26, 50 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 45 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 59 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 90, 92 · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 37 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Box 20-7 · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5

10Hemodynamic stability

Before weaning:

  • The P/F criterion requires that the patient "also have acceptable vital signs and hemodynamic status".
  • Cardiac/circulatory problems should be corrected before a weaning attempt.
  • RCP 203 defines stability as no myocardial ischemia or significant hypotension.

During the SBT (RCP 203):

  • HR < 120–140/min, with a change < 20% from baseline
  • SBP < 180–200 and > 90 mm Hg, with a change < 20%
  • no vasopressors required

Clinical signs of SBT failure (Chang) include tachycardia, hypertension, hypotension and arrhythmias.

Hess adds:

  • Box 20-7: hemodynamic stability = absence of active myocardial ischemia and absence of clinically significant hypotension, i.e., no vasopressors or only low-dose vasopressors.
  • Ch 20 stopping criteria for an SBT:
    • HR > 140/min or a sustained increase > 20%
    • bradycardia (sustained decrease > 20%)
    • SBP > 180 or < 90 mm Hg
  • Cardiac dysfunction can delay liberation until it is managed.
  • CPAP trap: a patient with marginal left-ventricular function may tolerate a CPAP trial, because positive pressure supports the failing heart, and then develop congestive heart failure when extubated.
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 6, 9, 14 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 45 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 59 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 90, 92 · Hess Ch 20 — Box 20-7; Approaches to Liberation; Recognition of a Failed Spontaneous Breathing Trial

11Pulmonary mechanics: static compliance, VD/VT, airway resistance

These three measurements do not depend on patient cooperation or effort. Low compliance, high airway resistance and a high VD/VT all increase the workload of breathing.

Parameter Formula (per deck) Weaning value
Static compliance (CST) VT ÷ (Pplat − PEEP) > 30 mL/cm H₂O (weaning deck). RCP 203: Crs < 25–30 is linked with weaning difficulties; "normal" intubated adult Crs is 40–80.
VD/VT VD/VT = (PaCO₂ − PĒCO₂) ÷ PaCO₂ (Hess Ch 50, Enghoff modification of the Bohr equation). The deck notes state the same equation in words but are cut off: "PaCO₂ minus the mean partial pressure of the carbon dioxide in the exhaled…" < 60% while intubated. Hess Ch 20: < 0.6.
Airway resistance (Raw) (PIP − Pplat) ÷ flow (L/sec) No critical weaning value established. Normal is 0.6–2.4 cm H₂O/L/sec, and higher on the vent because of the ETT and circuit.

Hess adds: Box 42-5 (burn) requires respiratory system compliance of at least 50 mL/cm H₂O.

Caution · sources differUse on this exam: the deck's > 30.

Hess adds (VD/VT worked example, Ch 50): PaCO₂ 40 mm Hg and PĒCO₂ (mixed exhaled PCO₂) 27 mm Hg → VD/VT = (40 − 27) ÷ 40 = 13 ÷ 40 = 0.325 (32.5%). The normal VD/VT is approximately 0.3. The deck adds the concept: the higher the VD/VT, the greater the minute volume demand.

SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 17 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 52 · Hess Ch 20 — Assessing Readiness for Liberation · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5 · Hess Ch 50 — Physiologic Mechanisms of Hypercapnia

12Spontaneous breathing trial (SBT)

The SBT is the best bedside test of whether the patient, not the ventilator, can sustain breathing. It is the "major diagnostic test" for extubation.

How it is done:

  • It can be done on the ventilator or with a Briggs adaptor (T-tube).
  • It lasts up to 30 min. Patients who fail do so within the first 20 to 30 minutes, so an extended (e.g., 120-min) trial is not needed.
  • Methods, with similar success:
    • T-piece (patient does all the work)
    • low PS 5–8 cm H₂O to offset the ETT
    • CPAP ≈ 5 cm H₂O
    • automatic tube compensation (ATC)
  • SIMV is not recommended as a stand-alone weaning mode.
  • RCP 203 Ch 11 (slides 90, 91): daily SBTs are the quickest route off the ventilator. Its protocols are T-tube, CPAP, PS, or CPAP plus PS, and "no one approach appears to be better than others" (the same message as Chang). CPAP helps triggering in patients with auto-PEEP; PS offsets the WOB of the artificial airway. Start with a 2–5 minute supervised screening phase; if tolerated, continue the SBT at least 30 but no more than 120 minutes (conflict with Chang's 30 min, see conflicts file).

Pass criteria:

  • Chang: normal respiratory pattern (no rapid shallow breathing), adequate gas exchange, hemodynamic stability. Only 13% of patients who passed an SBT and were extubated required reintubation.
  • RCP 203: pass after at least 30 minutes, with RR ≤ 30–35/min and an RR change < 50%, plus the gas-exchange and hemodynamic limits in items 9–10. Tolerating the SBT for its full duration (slide 93; 30–120 min per slide 91) means you can consider extubation.

Failure signs:

  • Chang: agitation/anxiety, diminished mental status, diaphoresis, cyanosis, ↑ WOB. Also tachypnea, use of accessory muscles, paradoxical abdominal movement.
  • RCP 203 also lists thoracoabdominal paradox.

If the patient fails: stop, return to full support and rest, find and correct the cause, and repeat the SBT every 24 hours.

NHLBI "ARDS Protocol" weaning (RCP 203 Ch 11, slide 94):

  • Ready for an SBT: FiO₂ ≤ 0.40 with PEEP ≤ 8, or FiO₂ ≤ 0.50 with PEEP ≤ 5 cm H₂O; PEEP and FiO₂ ≤ the previous day's; acceptable spontaneous efforts; SBP ≥ 90 mm Hg without vasopressors; no neuromuscular blockade.
  • Trial: up to 120 minutes on a T-piece, trach collar, or CPAP ≤ 5 cm H₂O with PS < 5 cm H₂O.
  • Tolerance: SpO₂ ≥ 90% and/or PaO₂ ≥ 60 torr, spontaneous VT ≥ 4 mL/kg PBW, RR ≤ 35/min, pH ≥ 7.30, and no distress (distress = two or more of HR > 120% of baseline, marked accessory-muscle use, abdominal paradox, diaphoresis, marked dyspnea).
  • Tolerated ≥ 30 minutes → consider extubation; if not tolerated, resume the pre-weaning settings.

Hess adds:

  • Tolerating SBTs lasting 30 to 120 minutes should prompt consideration of permanent ventilator discontinuation. Removing the airway is a separate decision based on airway patency and the ability to protect the airway (CPG 20-1).
  • The 2017 guideline suggests the initial SBT use 5–8 cm H₂O of inspiratory pressure augmentation rather than T-piece or CPAP (for patients ventilated more than 24 hours).
  • Conflict: the deck says the SBT methods have similar success; Hess's 2017 CPG suggests starting with PS 5–8 cm H₂O. For this exam, use the deck's "similar success" and recognize the CPG preference.
  • It recommends extubation to preventive NIV for high-risk patients who pass the SBT.
  • Hess's stopping criteria: RR > 35 for ≥ 5 min, SpO₂ < 90%, plus the HR/BP limits in item 10, and agitation, diaphoresis or anxiety.
  • SAT before SBT: do not do a SAT in patients with:
    • active seizures
    • alcohol withdrawal
    • escalating sedative doses
    • neuromuscular blockers
    • myocardial ischemia in the previous 24 h
    • increased ICP
  • If the SAT fails, restart sedatives at half the previous dose.
  • "The poorest outcomes … have been reported with SIMV."
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 19 (notes), 20, 21, 26 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 44 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 59, 60 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 90, 91, 92, 93, 94 · Hess Ch 20 — CPG 20-1, CPG 20-2, Approaches to Liberation, Recognition of a Failed SBT, Sedation, Key Points

13Secretions, cough and airway protection

Passing the SBT answers "Can the patient breathe?" Extubation also needs "Can the patient protect the airway?"

What the decks say:

  • Extubation is the removal of an ETT. Adequacy of airway clearance and ability to protect the airway must be assessed before extubation.
  • RCP 203 Ch 9 (slide 62): removal of a tracheal tube should be "considered only" in patients who:
    1. can maintain adequate oxygenation and ventilation without ventilatory support (to be demonstrated via an SBT)
    2. are at minimal risk for upper airway obstruction
    3. have adequate airway protection and are at minimal risk for aspiration
    4. can adequately clear pulmonary secretions on their own
  • Reasons for reintubation within 48 h include hypoventilation and hypercapnia, respiratory acidosis, rapid shallow breathing, excessive secretions, and respiratory muscle disuse atrophy.
  • Patients are often reintubated because they cannot protect their airway or clear secretions, even if their ABGs are initially acceptable.
  • Cough strength: the Neuro deck uses MEP ("think cough"). Normal is 60–100 cm H₂O. < 40 cm H₂O reflects an ineffective cough and poor secretion clearance.

Hess adds:

  • Ch 17 readiness criteria. A patient is ready to be extubated when the original reason for intubation is corrected and the patient has:
    • the ability to protect the lower airway from aspiration
    • the ability to clear secretions (cough strength)
    • acceptable nature of secretions (quantity and character)
    • the ability to prevent upper-airway obstruction
  • Cough peak flow ≤ 60 L/min → nearly five times as likely to fail extubation.
  • Secretions > 2.5 mL/h → three times as likely to fail.
  • All three risk factors (weak cough, secretions, unable to do the four simple tasks) → failure rate 100%. With none of them it is 3%.
  • Ch 20: suctioning more often than every 2 hours should preclude extubation.
  • Ch 44 (myasthenia gravis): SBTs can begin when PImax < −20, PEmax > 40 cm H₂O and FVC > 10 mL/kg. Bulbar weakness and the inability to handle upper-airway secretions are not measured by standard weaning parameters.
SourceRCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 64 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 7 · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 62 · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 43 · Hess Ch 17 — Extubation · Hess Ch 20 — Causes of a Failed SBT · Hess Ch 44 — Myasthenia Gravis

14Cuff-leak test and post-extubation stridor

No deck in the vault covers the cuff-leak test; the answer below is from Hess Ch 17, 20 and 42. The deck points are the steroid line from RCP 105 Airway Management and the post-extubation steps from RCP 203 Ch 9 (end of this item).

Why it mattersWhy the test exists. In some patients the upper airway swells during intubation. Before extubating them, assess the leak around the ETT during positive-pressure ventilation with the cuff deflated:
  • Qualitatively: deflate the cuff and listen for a leak through the upper airway.
  • Quantitatively: measure the leak volume or the percentage of VT that leaks.

Limits and risks.

  • The test's value is controversial because false positives and false negatives are common.
  • It increases aspiration risk, because secretions above the cuff can fall into the lower airway when the cuff is down.

Who should get it. Reserve it for patients at high risk of post-extubation stridor:

  • traumatic intubation
  • intubation > 6 days
  • large ETT
  • female sex
  • reintubation after unplanned extubation

Burn patients (Box 42-5). Upper-airway edema must be resolved enough that an air leak is audible around the ETT (cuff deflated) at a moderate inflating pressure of 20 to 30 cm H₂O.

If the test is failed. The 2017 ATS/ACCP guideline suggests systemic steroids at least 4 hours before extubation. A repeat cuff-leak test is not required. The deck says the same: prophylactic corticosteroids decrease the risk of post-extubation stridor and reintubation. Complications during and immediately after extubation include sore throat, stridor, hoarseness, odynophagia, vocal cord immobility, aspiration and cough.

After extubation (RCP 203 Ch 9, slide 63): assess breath sounds, work of breathing, and vital signs. If stridor develops, give cool, humidified O₂ via aerosol mask and recommend aerosolized racemic epinephrine. Recommend the patient be NPO (except for sips of water) for 24 hours. The most serious complication of extubation is laryngospasm.

Gap · still not covereda numeric cutoff for the quantitative cuff leak (leak volume in mL or % of VT). Hess describes measuring it but gives no number. Look in Hess Ch 17 refs 39–40 or an instructor handout.
SourceHess Ch 17 — Extubation; Box 17-2 · Hess Ch 20 — CPG 20-2 · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5 · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 64 · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 63

16Why patients fail weaning, and what to fix first high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Weaning failure is related to three things: an increase in airflow resistance, a decrease in compliance, and respiratory muscle fatigue.

↑ Resistance. Causes are a small ETT, secretions in the ETT, kinking or biting of the ETT, condensation in the tubing, and bronchospasm. Bronchospasm is patient-related; the others are mechanical. The Chang text adds:

  • An ETT raises WOB by 54% to 240% in normal subjects.
  • An 8-mm ETT has a cross-section of 50 mm², compared with the adult glottis at 66 mm².
  • Use an ETT size 8 or larger when appropriate.

↓ Compliance.

  • Sudden: tension pneumothorax, pleural effusion, main-stem migration of the ETT.
  • Gradual: pneumonia, ARDS, atelectasis.

Muscle fatigue.

  • WOB depends on transpulmonary pressure and VT: ↓ compliance or ↑ resistance → ↑ PTP → ↑ WOB.
  • Prolonged full support causes diaphragm disuse atrophy. It is retrained with short T-tube trials and PS.
  • Other contributors:
    • inadequate O₂ delivery
    • insufficient nutrition
    • electrolyte imbalance, especially hypokalemia, hypophosphatemia, hypocalcemia and hypomagnesemia
  • Undernutrition depletes diaphragm glycogen and protein and contributes to inability to wean.
  • Overfeeding raises O₂ consumption, CO₂ production and RQ, which can induce respiratory distress during weaning.

Hess adds:

  • Fatigue signs are tachypnea, abnormal respiratory movements (respiratory alternans, abdominal paradox) and ↑ PaCO₂.
  • A rest period of 24 hours may be needed after fatigue.
  • Muscle atrophy can develop in as little as 24 hours of total rest on controlled ventilation.
  • Auto-PEEP may delay liberation in COPD, because it increases the pleural pressure needed to initiate inhalation.
SourceRCP 105 Weaning & Extubation / Pharmacotherapy (Chang Ch 13, 16), slides 27, 28 (notes), 29, 30 (notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 58, 59 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Respiratory Muscles; Causes of a Failed Spontaneous Breathing Trial
02

Therapeutic Hypothermia (TH)

9 items · Hyperglycemia, hypoglycemia, hypokalemia · cold diuresis · GCS · apoptosis · excitatory neurotransmitters · cell injury · autoregulation · ABG changes with temperature · advantages and disadvantages

1What Therapeutic Hypothermia is: target temperature, who gets it, how and for how long2Hyperglycemia, Hypoglycemia and Hypokalemia in the cooled, brain-injured patient3Cold Diuresis4Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)5Cell Injury, Excitatory Neurotransmitters and Apoptosis6Autoregulation (cerebral) and CPP7ABG changes based on temperature (T°)8Advantages and Disadvantages of Therapeutic Hypothermia9Ventilating the post-arrest / brain-injured patient: PaCO₂, PaO₂ and ICP

1What Therapeutic Hypothermia is: target temperature, who gets it, how and for how long

The instructor's TH PowerPoint is not in the vault. The deck answer below comes from RCP 105 Critical Issues (HIE/TBI) and RCP 105 Monitoring in MV, with Hess Ch 18 filling in the rest. Therapeutic hypothermia (TH) is cooling the patient on purpose to protect the brain. On the slide, TH runs at 32 to 34 °C, and it "helps to improve the neurological outcomes of post-arrest patients." The instructor's notes add that current practice usually calls it Targeted Temperature Management (TTM), which generally keeps the temperature between 32 °C and 36 °C. Cooling selected post-cardiac-arrest patients to about 32–36 °C reduces brain metabolism and helps improve neurologic outcomes. TH appears on the list of treatments for hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy (HIE), together with oxygenation, ventilation and perfusion, seizure control and hyperbaric oxygen therapy (HBOT). The instructor's notes add that routine HBOT for HIE is still being researched and is not standard therapy for most HIE cases; HBOT is most useful when dysfunctional hemoglobins are increased, such as in CO poisoning. The notes say that hypothermia with cooling blankets has been used to reduce oxygen consumption and to minimize the effects of cerebral hypoxia.

HIE is a condition caused by severe cerebral hypoxia that damages the cells and neurons of the brain and spinal cord. The deck names three causes:

  • inadequate ventilation and oxygenation, e.g. respiratory arrest or CO poisoning
  • inadequate perfusion, e.g. cardiac arrest or shock
  • ↓ cerebral perfusion pressure (CPP), from ↓ MAP or ↑ ICP

The Monitoring deck lists three settings under TH:

  • post-cardiac arrest, because hypothermia reduces the inflammatory and other responses of the brain after blood flow is restored
  • head trauma, where it is induced to decrease the basal metabolic rate
  • coronary artery bypass surgery, where excessive cooling of the phrenic nerves may paralyze the hemidiaphragms (see item 8)

After ROSC, the RT helps by maintaining oxygenation and appropriate ventilation and supporting TTM, while avoiding hypotension and hypoxemia.

Hess adds: in the studies, patients were cooled to 32 °C to 36 °C for at least 24 hours after resuscitation from ventricular fibrillation, pulseless electrical activity or asystole. All comatose adults with ROSC after cardiac arrest should have TTM; comatose means lacking meaningful response to verbal commands. Hess's post–cardiac arrest care algorithm asks the same question: if the patient does not follow commands after ROSC, initiate targeted temperature management. TH is likely beneficial after traumatic brain injury in adults, but cannot be recommended in children. Internal cooling (cold saline, endovascular cooling catheters) reaches the target temperature faster and gives better outcomes. Commercial devices use pads with channels of circulating water. One Key Point says TH should be considered after resuscitation from VF, PEA or asystole. For reference, Hess gives normal body temperature as 37 °C (98.6 °F) and defines hypothermia as a core temperature of 95 °F (35 °C) or less.

Caution · sources differWhich number to use: if the question says "TH", give the slide's 32–34 °C. If it says "TTM", give 32–36 °C, where the deck notes and Hess agree.
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slides 3, 9 (and notes), 11 (and notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 11 (and notes) · Hess Ch 18 — Post–Cardiac Arrest Care; Algorithm 18-8 (description); Key Points · Hess Ch 1 — Vital Signs · Hess Glossary — hypothermia

2Hyperglycemia, Hypoglycemia and Hypokalemia in the cooled, brain-injured patient

Why it mattersWhy glucose matters. The brain makes up about 2% of body weight but uses 15% of the body's energy. It holds only a small amount of glycogen (in the astrocytes) and cannot use fatty acids, so it depends on a constant supply of oxygen and glucose from the blood. The deck notes say that a lack of glucose (e.g. hypoglycemia, starvation) can severely affect the normal functions of the brain.

Hyperglycemia is a named complication of TH. Hess: "An increased number of patients develop pneumonia and/or sepsis and hyperglycemia with hypothermia after therapeutic hypothermia."

Glucose numbers. The deck gives normal glucose as 70–110 mg/dL:

  • ↑ = hyperglycemia, linked to diabetes mellitus and treated with insulin
  • ↓ = hypoglycemia, treated by giving glucose

Hess adds (Ch 11, about ICU nutritional support, not TH specifically): tight glucose control carries a higher risk of hypoglycemia, so the recommendation is to keep blood glucose at 140–180 mg/dL and to treat < 70 mg/dL as hypoglycemia. Hess Ch 5 adds that insulin-secreting tumors or exogenous insulin overdoses lead to hypoglycemia, and that severe liver injury can also produce it; if severe, that hypoglycemia can bring about coma and death.

Hypokalemia. No source in the vault ties hypokalemia to TH (see the gaps note at the end of this item). What the sources do say:

Hypokalemia (↓ K⁺) What the sources say
Normal K⁺ 3.8–5.0 mEq/L (RCP 103 lab slide) · "3 to 5 mEq/L", described as a "narrow normal range" (RCP 105 Mgmt of MV) · 3.5–5.5 mmol/L (Hess Ch 5)
Effects Flattened T wave on EKG (RCP 103) · PVCs and respiratory muscle weakness (RCP 203) · Hess adds: muscular weakness, malaise, fatigue and myalgias; severe depletion → paralysis, rhabdomyolysis, and life-threatening arrhythmias (U waves, QT prolongation, T-wave changes)
Causes Metabolic alkalosis, fluid loss (RCP 103) · excessive K⁺ loss (trauma, severe infection, vomiting, non-potassium-sparing diuretics) and inadequate intake (prolonged IV infusion without K⁺) (RCP 105) · Hess adds: intracellular shift caused by insulin, β₂ agonists, and respiratory and metabolic alkalosis (Box 5-4)
Treatment Oral (safer) or slow IV potassium chloride (KCl), because hypochloremia often coexists with it · during IV KCl, UOP should be > 40 to 50 mL/hour · never give undiluted KCl (it can cause arrhythmias and cardiac arrest) · < 40 mEq in one hour or < 200 mEq in 24 hours · IV drip concentration < 40 mEq/L (RCP 105)

Which K⁺ normal to use: the three sources disagree. Use the RCP 103 lab-values slide (3.8–5.0 mEq/L), since it is the dedicated normal-values slide. Recognize 3.5–5.5 as Hess's range.

Gap · still not coveredhow TH or rewarming changes potassium (any "hypokalemia during cooling" or "rebound on rewarming" mechanism), and any TH-specific glucose target — the TH PowerPoint (not in vault); Hess Ch 18 names only hyperglycemia as a TH complication.
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slide 4 (and notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slides 51, 53 · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 53–55 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 11 · Hess Ch 18 — Post–Cardiac Arrest Care · Hess Ch 11 — glucose control · Hess Ch 5 — Potassium; Box 5-4; Glucose

3Cold Diuresis

None of the sources (no deck, Hess, or the midterm guide) uses the term "cold diuresis". None of them describes increased urine output caused by cooling either. Every use of "diuresis" in the vault is unrelated to cooling: diuretic therapy (e.g. furosemide in pulmonary edema or CHF), osmotic diuresis from hyperglycemia, mannitol, contraction alkalosis from diuresis, and postobstructive diuresis. None of these is connected to hypothermia. This term must come from the instructor's TH PowerPoint.

Gap · still not covereddefinition, mechanism and clinical consequences of cold diuresis — the TH PowerPoint (not in vault). Searched: "cold diuresis", "cold-induced", "diuresis", "diures" + cold/urine/renal in all decks, Hess and the midterm guide.
Sourcenone for cold diuresis (searched all decks and Hess) · other uses of "diuresis": Hess Ch 4 — Metabolic Alkalosis (contraction alkalosis); Hess Ch 5 — Box 5-8

4Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)

The GCS is a standardized way to measure the level of consciousness (LOC). It adds three components: Eyes (E) up to 4 + Verbal (V) up to 5 + Motor (M) up to 6. The maximum is 15 and the minimum is 3. The lower the score, the worse the neurologic injury. The instructor stresses that the motor response is the best predictor of neurologic outcome. The deck's (RCP 105 Critical Issues) worked example: eyes open to pain (E2) + incomprehensible sounds (V2) + withdraws from pain (M4) = GCS 8.

Eye opening Verbal response Motor response
Spontaneous 4 Oriented 5 Obeys commands 6
To voice 3 Confused 4 Localizes 5
To pain 2 Inappropriate words 3 Withdraws 4
None 1 Incomprehensible words 2 Flexes (decorticate) 3
None 1 Extends (decerebrate) 2
None 1

(Table from Hess Ch 1, Table 1-4. The deck's own chart is an image with no text.)

Severity bands:

Band GCS Where the sources differ
Mild 13–15 the 105 slide text says 13–14
Moderate 9–12 all agree
Severe 3–8 all agree; Hess says "less than 9"

GCS ≤ 8 means intubate (consider the need for airway protection). Patients at 8 or less often cannot protect the airway, cough effectively, handle secretions, or limit aspiration risk. The instructor calls this "a guideline, not an absolute law." In intubated patients the verbal score cannot be tested, so the GCS ranges from 2T to 10T, where "T" means intubated. A patient who is heavily sedated or chemically paralyzed may have a low GCS without severe brain injury, which matters in sedated, cooled patients. Hess adds: Hess Ch 1 says a score of 3 "suggests brain death." Hess's TTM criterion is "comatose (i.e., lacking meaningful response to verbal commands)."

Caution · sources differWhich to use: the RCP 105 slide text says "Mild 13 to 14" and "< 8 = Intubate", but its own notes and the RCP 203 deck say mild 13–15 and GCS ≤ 8 = intubate. Hess Ch 41 agrees on the bands (it calls 13–15 "minor" and severe "less than 9") but gives no intubation cutoff. Use 13–15 and ≤ 8.
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slides 20 (and notes), 21 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 8 · Hess Ch 1 — Level of Consciousness, Table 1-4 · Hess Ch 41 — Deficits · Hess Ch 18 — Post–Cardiac Arrest Care

5Cell Injury, Excitatory Neurotransmitters and Apoptosis

Cell injury: the timeline. When cerebral circulation stops, neuronal oxygen stores are used up within about 20 seconds and the patient loses consciousness. Within 5 minutes of complete cerebral anoxia, brain glucose and ATP are lost and energy failure follows.

The deck's mechanism of cerebral cellular injury: no oxygen → no ATP (energy failure) → neurons stop functioning → calcium enters the cells → cells become damaged → the neuron dies. The instructor's one-line version is "Energy failure → Calcium overload → Neuronal death."

The notes give the full chain:

  1. The first event after energy depletion is failure of the Na⁺/K⁺ pumps, which depolarizes the neuronal membrane.
  2. Depolarization makes neurons release excessive glutamate into the synaptic cleft.
  3. Some glutamate receptors are nonselective cation channels, so excess Ca²⁺ floods into the neurons. This activates catabolic (destructive) enzymes and nitric oxide synthase (↑ nitric oxide).
  4. Passive influx of Cl⁻ (and Na⁺) causes osmotic edema and rapid cell death.

Excitatory neurotransmitters. Glutamate is the brain's primary excitatory neurotransmitter. During hypoxia, too much glutamate is released, which lets too much calcium into neurons and speeds up cell death. The instructor says this is "the only concept worth mentioning" and that you do not need to memorize the whole pathway. Hess adds (Ch 44, in its section on ALS, not hypoxia): "Glutamate is the principal excitatory brain neurotransmitter." There, decreased uptake of glutamate may overstimulate the glutamate receptors → ↑ intracellular calcium → proteolytic enzymes → cell membrane injury (glutamate-induced neurotoxicity of motor neurons).

Apoptosis means programmed cell death (Hess Glossary). The RCP 103 ARDS deck adds that apoptosis is "a normal process in the body". It becomes harmful when it destroys cells that are needed; in ARDS, those are the surfactant-producing type II cells.

How TH fits in. The sources say only that TH reduces brain metabolism, lowers oxygen utilization and consumption, and reduces inflammatory and other responses after blood flow returns (see item 1). They do not say that TH blocks glutamate release, calcium entry or apoptosis. Do not add that link from memory.

Gap · still not coveredapoptosis in the brain after hypoxia-ischemia, and how TH affects excitatory neurotransmitter release or apoptosis — the TH PowerPoint (not in vault).
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slides 4 (notes), 5 (and notes), 9 (notes), 11 (notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 11 · RCP 103 Diffuse Alveolar Disease — ARDS, slide 5 (notes) · Hess Ch 44 — Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (glutamate) · Hess Glossary — apoptosis

6Autoregulation (cerebral) and CPP

Cerebral autoregulation is the brain's ability to keep its blood flow stable despite changes in blood pressure. It works by constricting and dilating the cerebral blood vessels, adjusting cerebrovascular resistance to keep flow constant. Under normal conditions, the brain regulates its own blood flow regardless of the systemic blood pressure and cerebral vascular resistance. Autoregulation may be lost in severe hypotension or head trauma (TBI). Once it fails, blood flow depends completely on the patient's BP and ICP, so keeping an adequate CPP becomes essential.

The instructor's example question: "Why doesn't everyone with a BP of 90/60 develop brain ischemia?" Answer: because autoregulation normally keeps cerebral blood flow constant.

Cerebral perfusion pressure:

  • Formula: CPP = MAP − ICP
  • Normal CPP: 70 to 80 mm Hg (deck), with a critical threshold of ≥ 70 mm Hg (Monitoring deck)
  • Mortality rises about 20% for every 10 mm Hg drop in CPP. In severe head injury, keeping CPP above 70 mm Hg cut mortality by 35%.
  • Normal ICP: 8 to 12 mm Hg; the critical value is 20 mm Hg.

Worked example (deck): if MAP stays at 90 and ICP rises from 10 to 30, CPP falls from 80 to 60 (90 − 10 = 80 → 90 − 30 = 60).

Trauma deck (RCP 202 Lssn 7b, slide 23): the goal of medical and surgical maneuvers is to maintain CPP > 60 mm Hg, by lowering ICP, raising MAP, or both; in the injured brain, autoregulation is decreased, so the brain is more susceptible to swings in MAP.

Hess adds:

  • Normally, auto-regulation "has a dramatic ability to maintain a constant flow over a wide range of systemic pressures." Ch 41 also says it is decreased in the injured brain, as the Trauma deck does.
  • Hess agrees with the Trauma deck: CPP goal > 60 mm Hg (Ch 41, Ch 53).
  • Ch 53 gives normal ICP < 10 mm Hg, MAP about 90 mm Hg and normal CPP > 80 mm Hg.
Caution · sources differWhich to use: the number depends on the deck behind the question. For therapeutic hypothermia / HIE (the RCP 105 Critical Issues deck), use CPP 70–80 (≥ 70) and ICP 8–12 (critical 20). For a trauma or head-injury question, use the Trauma deck's CPP > 60 mm Hg (Hess agrees).
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slides 8 (and notes), 10 (notes), 14 (and notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 52, 53 · RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 23 · Hess Ch 41 — Intracranial Physiology · Hess Ch 53 — Intracranial Pressures and Cerebral Perfusion Pressure

7ABG changes based on temperature (T°)

The analyzer always runs at 37 °C. The blood gas analyzer's electrodes are kept at 37 °C, so every ABG is measured at 37 °C, whatever the patient's temperature. The physics: gas solubility is inversely proportional to temperature.

  • Colder blood → ↑ solubility → ↓ partial pressures and ↑ pH
  • Warmer blood → ↓ solubility → ↑ partial pressures and ↓ pH

So for a hypothermic patient, the values actually present in the patient are lower PaO₂, lower PaCO₂ and higher pH (it "looks alkalotic") than the 37 °C printout. Put the other way, the 37 °C printout reads higher than the patient's actual PaO₂ and PaCO₂ (Monitoring deck). The deck's table:

Measured at 37 °C (normothermia) Corrected to 30 °C (hypothermia) Corrected to 40 °C (hyperthermia)
pH 7.405 7.508 (↑, alkalotic shift) 7.362 (↓, acidotic shift)
PCO₂ 43.0 mm Hg 30.6 mm Hg (↓) 49.7 mm Hg (↑)
PO₂ 94.2 mm Hg 61.6 mm Hg (↓) 112.8 mm Hg (↑)

The slide's own rule: Cold → gas pressure ↓ → PaO₂ ↓, PaCO₂ ↓, pH ↑; Hot → gas pressure ↑ → PaO₂ ↑, PaCO₂ ↑, pH ↓. "Don't memorize the numbers. Understand the direction." The notes add that temperature correction estimates what those partial pressures would be at the patient's actual temperature.

Correction formulas (deck notes):

  • PO₂ = measured PO₂ − 5 × (37 − T)
  • PCO₂ = measured PCO₂ − 2 × (37 − T)
  • pH = measured pH + 0.015 × (37 − T)

Hess gives the same idea as percentages: PO₂ changes about 7% per °C, PCO₂ about 4% per °C, and pH 0.0146 unit per °C. HCO₃⁻, O₂ content, O₂ capacity and saturation do not change with temperature.

Worked example (Hess Stop and Think). A patient cooled to 33 °C after cardiac arrest has these values at 37 °C: pH 7.40, PCO₂ 40, PO₂ 90. Hess gives the temperature-adjusted values as pH 7.46, PCO₂ 32, PO₂ 68. The deck formulas give nearly the same answer with 37 − 33 = 4:

  • PCO₂ = 40 − 2×4 = 32
  • pH = 7.40 + 0.015×4 = 7.46
  • PO₂ = 90 − 5×4 = 70, close to Hess's 68

Should you correct? The sources disagree.

  • RCP 105 Monitoring deck: "corrections to the patient's core temperature should be done."
  • RCP 203 deck (slide 8): takes no stance on correcting; it says always document the patient's FiO₂, O₂ device, mode of ventilation and related assessments at the time of sampling, e.g. the patient's temperature ("ex: pt temp").
  • Hess Ch 4: the 37 °C pH and PCO₂ reliably reflect in vivo acid–base status, and routine ABGs may be reported at 37 °C without correction. Its Key Point says "Temperature correction of blood gases is unnecessary." Temperature-adjusted values may be more appropriate only rarely, e.g. severe hypothermia when calculating the A–a gradient. If corrected values are reported, they must be labeled, with the 37 °C values also reported.
  • Alpha-stat = manage pH and PaCO₂ so the 37 °C values stay normal regardless of body temperature. pH-stat = keep the actual pH at 7.4 regardless of temperature. Hess says it is unclear whether outcomes differ between the two.
Caution · sources differWhich to use: for the instructor's exam, know the deck's direction-of-change table and formulas. If asked whether to temperature-correct a cooled patient's ABG, answer from the decks: correct to the patient's core temperature (RCP 105 Monitoring) and document the temperature at sampling (RCP 203 slide 8). Recognize Hess's position (correction is "unnecessary"; report the 37 °C values and label any corrected values) for a Hess-worded question.

Other temperature effects on gas exchange:

  • A decrease in temperature shifts the oxyhemoglobin curve to the left: ↑ Hb affinity for O₂, lower P50 (normal P50 ≈ 27 mm Hg) (Hess Ch 50).
  • Fever shifts it to the right (Monitoring deck; Hess).
  • Hypothermia lowers PETCO₂ because CO₂ production drops (Hess Table 2-2).

Caution: two places in the RCP 203 slide 9 notes (the sentence saying the reported values will be "LOWER than the actual values" and "Hypothermic patients look 'alkalotic' with lower measured PaO₂/PaCO₂") say the reported values are lower. That is backwards compared with its own table and with the 105 deck. Trust the numbers in the table.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8 Quality Control, slides 8, 9 (and notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 10 (notes), 11 (notes) · Hess Ch 4 — Temperature Adjustment; Regulation of pH; Stop and Think; Key Points · Hess Ch 50 — oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve · Hess Ch 2 — Table 2-2

8Advantages and Disadvantages of Therapeutic Hypothermia

Advantages Disadvantages / complications / RT cautions
Improves neurologic outcomes of post-cardiac-arrest patients (deck; Hess: 32–36 °C for ≥ 24 h after VF, PEA or asystole) If the temperature drops below the desired range → coagulopathy and arrhythmias (Hess)
Lowers oxygen utilization / oxygen consumption and minimizes the effects of cerebral hypoxia (deck notes) More pneumonia and/or sepsis (Hess)
Reduces brain metabolism (deck notes); decreases basal metabolic rate in head trauma (Monitoring deck notes) Hyperglycemia (Hess)
Reduces inflammatory and other responses of the brain after blood flow is restored (Monitoring deck) Needs continuous temperature monitoring, and the ventilator humidifier must be set to the patient's target core temperature (Hess)
Likely beneficial after TBI in adults (Hess) Cannot be recommended in children with TBI (Hess)
Internal cooling (cold saline, endovascular catheters) reaches target faster with better outcomes (Hess) ABGs must be interpreted with temperature in mind: the 37 °C values read higher than the actual PaO₂/PaCO₂ (Monitoring deck); document the patient's temperature at sampling (RCP 203)
Do not use an HME in a hypothermic patient, because it decreases the exhaled humidity available (RCP 203 Equipment)
PtcO₂ accuracy is affected by hypothermia (Monitoring deck)
Hypothermia is a listed cause of bradycardia (< 60/min) during MV (Monitoring deck)
During CABG surgery, excessive cooling of the phrenic nerves can paralyze the hemidiaphragms (Monitoring deck, under its "Therapeutic hypothermia" bullet)

Hess adds (not about TH itself, but about cold patients):

  • Unintended hypothermia increases infection risk; the link is less clear when hypothermia is induced (Ch 27).
  • In postoperative hypothermia, vasoconstriction can reduce tissue perfusion → metabolic acidosis. Shivering increases O₂ consumption and CO₂ production, raising the risk of myocardial ischemia and hypercapnic ventilatory failure (Ch 39).
Gap · still not coveredthe TH PowerPoint's own advantages/disadvantages list (e.g. rewarming problems, cold diuresis, electrolyte shifts, shivering control during TH) — TH PowerPoint (not in vault).
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slides 9 (and notes), 11 (notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 3, 11 (and notes), 50 · RCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8 Quality Control, slide 8 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 47 · Hess Ch 18 — Post–Cardiac Arrest Care · Hess Ch 27 — temperature · Hess Ch 39 — Patient Temperature

9Ventilating the post-arrest / brain-injured patient: PaCO₂, PaO₂ and ICP high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Hess (post-arrest): normal blood gas values should be kept during the post-resuscitation period. Hyperventilation, increased airway pressure and intrinsic PEEP can cause increased cerebral vasoconstriction, increased ICP, increased cerebral ischemia and ischemic brain injury. Post-arrest care also means avoiding excessive ventilation and hyperoxia.

Deck (TBI respiratory management):

  • Sedate the patient to prevent sudden ICP spikes from coughing or patient–ventilator dyssynchrony.
  • Avoid endotracheal suctioning if possible and suction less often. This does not mean never: suction when secretions obstruct the airway.
  • Oxygenation: keep PaO₂ > 60 mm Hg and SaO₂ > 90%.

Deck on PaCO₂:

  • The slide says PaCO₂ may be titrated as low as 26 mm Hg during the first 24 hours. Going below 26 mm Hg (3.5 kPa) gives no added benefit on ICP.
  • The instructor's notes describe current practice: keep PaCO₂ normal (35–45 mm Hg). Routine hyperventilation is no longer recommended because too much vasoconstriction can worsen brain ischemia.
  • Temporary hyperventilation to about 30–35 mm Hg is allowed only as an emergency measure for critically raised ICP or impending herniation.

Mechanism: ↑ PaCO₂ → cerebral vasodilation → ↑ cerebral blood flow and ↑ ICP. ↓ PaCO₂ → vasoconstriction → ↓ ICP but ↓ blood flow.

Hess adds (Ch 41, Ch 53):

  • Hyperventilating to PaCO₂ < 25 mm Hg lowers ICP quickly, but prophylactic hyperventilation is not recommended. Use it only as a temporizing salvage measure.
  • The brain re-equilibrates, and pH normalizes within 4 to 6 hours of starting hyperventilation.
  • Target PaO₂ in trauma patients is 80 to 100 mm Hg.
  • A single episode of SBP < 90 mm Hg is associated with doubled mortality.
  • Hess's post–cardiac arrest care algorithm (Ch 18): avoid excessive ventilation; start at 10 breaths/min and titrate to a PETCO₂ of 35–40 mm Hg; titrate FiO₂ to the minimum needed to achieve an SpO₂ of 94%; treat hypotension (SBP < 90 mm Hg).
Caution · sources differWhich to use: for "what PaCO₂ do you target?", answer normal, 35–45 mm Hg (the instructor's notes on current practice). Use 30–35 only for emergency ICP control. Know the slide's 26 mm Hg floor as the lowest acceptable value.
SourceRCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slide 22 (and notes) · Hess Ch 18 — Post–Cardiac Arrest Care; Algorithm 18-8 (description) · Hess Ch 41 — Hypoxia; Hyperventilation; Hypotension · Hess Ch 53 — Intracranial Pressures and Cerebral Perfusion Pressure
03

Communication

4 items · Closed & open-ended questions · 5 levels of communication · social, personal & intimate space (inches and feet)

1Closed-ended vs Open-ended Questions2The 5 Levels of Communication3Social, Personal, and Intimate Space (Proxemics): the inches and feet4The 3 V's of Communication

1Closed-ended vs Open-ended Questions

Closed-ended questions usually produce a short, specific answer, often yes/no or a single piece of information. The slide says they "give limited insight" but deliver specific information. The notes add that they are extremely useful when you need specific information quickly, especially with a patient in respiratory distress. Examples: "Are you short of breath?", "Do you use oxygen at home?", "How many liters do you use?" Open-ended questions are thought-provoking and give broader information. They let the patient tell their story and often uncover information you wouldn't have thought to ask about: "Tell me about your breathing today," "What happens when you become short of breath?" The one-line contrast on the slide is closed-ended = specific information; open-ended = broader information, and both are appropriate; the RT needs to know when to use each. The interview strategy the deck teaches is start broad, then narrow down: "Tell me about your cough" → "When did it start?" → "Is it productive?" → "What color is the sputum?" This is an open-ended question followed by focused closed-ended questions. The RCP 103 Clinical Data deck says the same: use open-ended questions to start the interview, and use closed questions to clarify details or when the patient cannot speak fully. The full questioning-technique list on the slide is closed-ended, open-ended, clarification, leading questions (generally AVOID), compound questions, facilitation, respectfully identify inconsistencies, silence (pause!), and support and reassurance. Two to avoid: a leading question "suggests the answer you expect or want" ("You're feeling better after that treatment, aren't you?"). A compound question asks multiple questions at once, so the patient's "Yes" leaves you asking yes to what? The deck's summary sequence is: start with an open-ended question → use closed-ended questions to focus → clarify anything ambiguous → avoid questions that lead or confuse → use silence and facilitation to listen → summarize/confirm.

Hess adds: closed-ended questions "yield a limited number of possible answers" and "in general they have limited value during the initial patient interview or assessment." Open-ended questions "are considered the most valuable form of questioning during the initial patient assessment", yield "the broadest amount of information," and "almost always begin with the words 'what,' 'why,' or 'how.'" Leading questions "should not be asked because they can produce useless, unreliable, or inaccurate responses," and compound questions "generally result in a response to the last part of the question only." Hess's list names confrontation in the slot where the deck says "respectfully identify inconsistencies". It describes the same skill: honestly pointing out a mismatch, e.g. "You said your breathing was fine, yet I noticed your respiratory rate was quite high."

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1a — Communication & Collaboration, slides 5 (notes), 33 (and notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 8 · Hess Ch 30 — Questioning Techniques; Respiratory Recap "Questioning Strategies and Techniques"

2The 5 Levels of Communication

The deck lists the levels from 5 (most superficial) down to 1 (deepest):

Level Name What it is (deck notes) Example (deck notes)
5 Cliché conversation Basic social interaction with little meaningful information; often how rapport begins "How are you today?" "Fine."
4 Reporting facts Exchanging objective information; where a lot of routine healthcare communication occurs "I've been coughing for three days." "I'm on 2 liters of oxygen at home."
3 Personal ideas or judgments The patient shares what they think or believe; explore it instead of immediately correcting it "I don't think these breathing treatments are helping me."
2 Feelings and emotions The patient trusts you enough to tell you how they actually feel "I'm scared I'm going to end up on the ventilator again."
1 Peak communication "Highest level; intimate; reserved for select few", i.e. intimate personal relationships, not the RT–patient relationship —

The exam point on the slide: the RT should be able to move beyond superficial communication (Levels 4–5) and establish enough trust to reach meaningful therapeutic communication, often Levels 2–3. The notes call Levels 2–3 the "sweet spot" (the therapeutic zone). Level 1 is not the goal: it belongs primarily in close personal relationships, and professional boundaries matter. Professional boundary: the patient may communicate at Level 2; the RT doesn't necessarily reciprocate at Level 2 (patient self-disclosure → RT therapeutic response). The slide's takeaway is to develop and maintain relationships built on "honesty, trust, and respect," and the chain the notes stress is Rapport → Trust → Cooperation. The instructor also notes the table's source dates from 1990, so the five levels are a teaching model, not a rigid contemporary healthcare standard. The slide cites Table 60–1 (the deck does not name the book or edition the table comes from). The current Hess (4th ed.) Ch 30 does not reproduce a five-levels table, so the deck is the only source for this ask.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1a — Communication & Collaboration, slides 11 (and notes), 12 (notes), 13 (and notes)

3Social, Personal, and Intimate Space (Proxemics): the inches and feet

Proxemics is the use and interpretation of space, i.e. how physical distance affects interpersonal communication. It is one of the nonverbal cues, alongside kinesics (body motion), vocal characteristics/paralinguistics, and touch. The deck gives four zones. Its kinesics/proxemics note prints them in inches and feet: Intimate 1″ to 18″ (1.5′); Personal 1.5′ to 4′; Social 4 to 12′; Public 12′ to 25′. The slide-19 notes give the same zones in feet, written as intimate 0–1.5 ft, public 12+ ft.

Zone Distance in inches Distance in feet Used for (deck notes)
Intimate space 1″ to 18″ 0 to 1.5 ft (18″ = 1.5′) Normally reserved for very close relationships; the RT enters it constantly (suctioning, auscultating, placing a BiPAP mask, intubating/assisting with an airway, performing an ABG, trach care)
Personal space 18″ to 48″ (converted) 1.5 to 4 ft Comfortable distance for one-on-one conversation with a patient: interviewing or educating
Social space 48″ to 144″ (converted) 4 to 12 ft Groups, coworkers, meetings, or less personal conversations
Public space 144″ to 300″ (converted) 12 to 25 ft (also written 12+ ft) Lectures and presentations

Conversion note: the deck prints the intimate zone in both units (1″ to 18″ = 1.5′). The other inch values are the deck's feet × 12 (1.5 ft = 18″, 4 ft = 48″, 12 ft = 144″, 25 ft = 300″); the deck itself does not print them. Know the feet boundaries 1.5 / 4 / 12 / 25 cold. The instructor's own qualification: don't memorize 1.5 ft, 4 ft, 12 ft and 25 ft as rigid boundaries. They are conceptual zones, not physiological measurements, and they vary with culture, relationship, situation, environment, individual preference, and patient condition. The RT skill is what happens before you enter intimate space: explain and ask, e.g. "I'm going to listen to your lungs now. Is that okay?" The slide quote: "we're given permission to enter a space that patients normally reserve for people very close to them. Don't take that privilege for granted." Hess Ch 30 defines proxemics and lists "uncomfortable distance or spacing between people" among the environmental factors. However, it gives no distances: its Stop and Think tells the student to "perform a literature search, and identify three or four distance zones." The deck is the only source with numbers.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1a — Communication & Collaboration, slides 18 (notes), 19 (and notes), 22 (and notes) · Hess Ch 30 — Nonverbal Cues; Environmental Factors; Stop and Think (distance zones)

4The 3 V's of Communication high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Verbal = 7% (the words we use), Vocal = 38% (tone, volume, pitch, rate), Visual = 55% (facial expressions, gestures, posture, body language). Therefore 93% of communication is nonverbal. When verbal and nonverbal messages conflict, people may rely more heavily on vocal and visual cues, particularly when interpreting feelings and attitudes, so Verbal + Vocal + Visual should be congruent. The instructor's caveat in the notes: the percentages trace to Albert Mehrabian's 1960s research on communicating feelings and attitudes when verbal and nonverbal signals were inconsistent. Mehrabian himself states that the formula does not apply to communication generally; "Change the PEEP to 10 cmH₂O" obviously carries far more than 7% of its meaning in the words. Memorize 7 / 38 / 55 for the exam, knowing it is an overgeneralization. Hess adds: body language and tone of voice "often represent more than 90% of the message" and represent the "how" rather than the "what."

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1a — Communication & Collaboration, slide 27 (and notes) · Hess Ch 30 — Skills of the Receiver

Burn and Inhalation Injury

7 items · Dynamic and static lung compliance ranges · carbon monoxide (CO) levels

1Static and Dynamic Lung Compliance Ranges2Static vs Dynamic Compliance: the difference, the formulas, a worked example3Carbon Monoxide (CO) Levels: COHb percentages4How CO Is Measured: CO-oximetry, not pulse oximetry5COHb Half-Life and Treatment of CO Poisoning6Why Compliance Falls in Burn and Inhalation Injury7CO vs Hydrogen Cyanide

1Static and Dynamic Lung Compliance Ranges

The Burn deck's numbers (memorize these for this exam). On its pulmonary-mechanics slide (slide 37, notes) the deck lists "Lung Compliance (how easily the lungs expand)" = 50–100 mL/cm H₂O and "Static Compliance" = 70–100 mL/cm H₂O. Read the labels exactly: the deck calls the 50–100 figure "lung compliance", not "dynamic compliance". No source in the vault (no deck and not Hess) gives a normal range for dynamic compliance. The 50–100 number is identical to Hess's figure for acceptable respiratory-system compliance (CRS) in mechanically ventilated patients, which Hess calculates with plateau pressure. That makes it a static measurement. Do not memorize "dynamic = 50–100" as a sourced fact.

Exam-strategy note (an inference, not a source statement): the study guide asks for "dynamic and static ranges" from this deck, and slide 37 shows exactly two ranges. If a question forces you to pick a "dynamic" number, 50–100 is the only one on the slide that is not labeled static. This is at least consistent with physiology: Cdyn includes resistive pressure and so reads lower than Cstat (item 2), and the 50–100 band starts lower than the static 70–100. Still, no source labels it dynamic.

Every compliance range the sources give, so a distractor cannot surprise you:

Source What it is called Range
Burn deck, slide 37 (notes) Lung compliance ("how easily the lungs expand") 50–100 mL/cm H₂O
Burn deck, slide 37 (notes) Static compliance 70–100 mL/cm H₂O
RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 7 Adult static compliance 60–100 mL/cm H₂O; "normal compliance is around 80"
RCP 105 Waveforms, slide 26 (notes) Normal static compliance, intubated adult "typically around 60–100"
RCP 203 Lssn 3a, slide 52 "Normal" Crs (aka CStat), intubated adults 40–80; Crs < 25–30 → ↑ WOB and potential weaning difficulty
Hess Ch 51 Acceptable CRS in mechanically ventilated patients 50–100 mL/cm H₂O
Hess Ch 51 Normal lung (CL), chest wall (CCW), respiratory system (CRS) 200, 200, 100 mL/cm H₂O

RCP 105 Weaning, slide 17 Static compliance for weaning readiness > 30 mL/cm H₂O
Any source Dynamic compliance No range given anywhere
Caution · sources differWhich to use: on this RCP 202 exam use the Burn deck's 50–100 ("lung compliance") and 70–100 ("static"). The deck is your instructor's source for this ask. Note that Hess's "lung compliance" (CL) is a different, larger number (200), because Hess separates the lung from the chest wall.
Gap · still not covereda normal range for dynamic compliance — searched every deck (Burn, 105 Principles/Waveforms/Monitoring/Management, 103 ARDS, 203) and all of Hess (Ch 51 especially); none gives one.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 37 (notes) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 7 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis, slide 26 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 52 · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation, slide 17 · Hess Ch 51 — Compliance; Respiratory System Compliance · Hess Ch 42 — Box 42-5

2Static vs Dynamic Compliance: the difference, the formulas, a worked example

Compliance = ΔV ÷ ΔP: how much volume you get for a given pressure. The Principles of MV slide gives the rule that separates the two: use PIP for dynamic compliance and PPLAT for static compliance. Static compliance (Crs, aka CStat) is a pressure measurement with "no" airflow (an inspiratory pause/plateau). Its formula is printed in the decks and in Hess: Cstat = VT ÷ (Pplat − PEEP). Dynamic compliance (Cdyn) uses the peak pressure, measured while gas is still flowing. Applying the deck's rule (PIP in place of Pplat) gives Cdyn = VT ÷ (PIP − PEEP). No source prints this second formula word-for-word; it is the deck rule applied to the static formula.

Why it mattersWhy Cdyn reads lower than Cstat. The RCP 203 deck breaks PIP − PEEP into two parts: resistive pressure (PIP − Pplat) plus elastic pressure (Pplat − PEEP). A PIP-based compliance therefore has the airway-resistance pressure in its denominator. That makes it lower than the Pplat-based static value whenever flow and resistance are present, and the gap grows as resistance rises. The RCP 203 deck shows the two patterns:
  • ↑ Raw (Crs unchanged) during VC: ↑ PIP, ↑ PIP − Pplat, no change in Pplat, ↓ Cdyn, ↑ Crs − Cdyn.
  • ↓ Crs (Raw unchanged): ↑ PIP and ↑ Pplat, no change in PIP − Pplat, ↓ Cdyn, and Crs − Cdyn unchanged (both decrease proportionately).

The two values become equal only when flow is zero at end-inspiration (e.g. a true descending-ramp flow pattern), where PIP = PAlv = Pplat. In that case the Waveforms deck says static compliance may be calculated using PIP.

Worked example (Hess Ch 51's patient, Figure 51-19): VT 320 mL, Pplat 40, PEEP 26 cm H₂O → CRS (static) = 320 ÷ (40 − 26) = 320 ÷ 14 ≈ 23 mL/cm H₂O. Hess calls this decreased, well below the acceptable 50–100. The same patient's PIP is 43 cm H₂O. Applying the deck's PIP rule: Cdyn = 320 ÷ (43 − 26) = 320 ÷ 17 ≈ 19 mL/cm H₂O, lower than the static 23 because of the 3 cm H₂O of resistive pressure (43 − 40). (The Cdyn arithmetic is ours, using Hess's numbers and the deck's rule.)

SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 7 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis, slide 25 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 37 (notes), 39/41 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 54 · Hess Ch 51 — Respiratory System Compliance; Airways Resistance

3Carbon Monoxide (CO) Levels: COHb percentages

The Burn deck gives no COHb percentage bands. The RCP 203 decks do, in two slightly different versions:

COHb level Meaning Source
1–3% WNL (within normal limits) RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 17 (notes); Lssn 3b slide 26 (notes)
5–10% Smokers same
10–15% Heavy smokers same
> 20% CO poisoning same
< 1.5% Normal CO level RCP 203 Lssn 1 slide 17
3–6% "Normal" for smokers, inner-city dwellers, those near industrial areas same
≥ 3–4% (nonsmokers) / ≥ 10% (smokers) Outside the expected physiological range with smoke inhalation/CO poisoning same
< 5% Treatment end point: mild–moderate CO poisoning gets 100% O₂ (non-rebreather) until COHb is less than 5% RCP 105 Nontraditional Settings, slide 22 (and notes)

The two 203 decks differ on the normal value (< 1.5% vs 1–3%) and on smokers (3–6% vs 5–10%). The > 20% = CO poisoning cutoff appears in both Lssn 3a and 3b. The instructor's notes add that the decision to use HBO is not based on the COHb level alone: symptoms, neurologic findings, cardiac involvement, pregnancy and the overall picture count. The symptoms of severe CO poisoning are unconsciousness, convulsions, and neurological impairment.

Hess adds: HbCO produces an acute physiologic anemia. An HbCO of 50% is physiologically similar to an isovolemic hemodilution to 50% of baseline hemoglobin, and unconsciousness routinely occurs at this level.
