That shows other mechanisms are involved: CO binding to the mitochondrial cytochrome system (blocking O₂ utilization) is likely more toxic than CO binding to hemoglobin. 5% to 25% of patients with serious exposure develop delayed major neurologic sequelae. Hess's Case 1 (unconscious smoke-inhalation patient, HbCO 38% by CO-oximetry, depressed consciousness) was treated with HBO.

Mechanism (Burn deck): CO binds hemoglobin about 200–250× more strongly than O₂ → carboxyhemoglobin (COHb) → less Hb available to carry O₂ → tissue hypoxia. It also causes a leftward shift of the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve, so the remaining O₂ is held more tightly and released less readily. (RCP 203 Lssn 1 gives the bond as about 210 times stronger than O₂.)

Gap · still not covereda symptom-by-percentage COHb table (headache/nausea/confusion bands) — not in any vault deck or in Hess Ch 42, 14, 2 or 50; only the 203 decks' level bands above and Hess's 50% statement exist.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 17 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 26 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1, slide 17 (and notes) · RCP 105 Nontraditional Settings, slide 22 (and notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 39 (notes) · Hess Ch 42 — Carbon Monoxide Exposure; Case 1

4How CO Is Measured: CO-oximetry, not pulse oximetry

Measure carboxyhemoglobin with CO-oximetry (the deck lists "ABG for CO-oximetry" among the tools for diagnosing inhalation injury). The rule: smoke exposure + normal PaO₂/SpO₂ does NOT rule out CO poisoning → check COHb with CO-oximetry. PaO₂ can be NORMAL because PaO₂ measures dissolved O₂ in plasma, not O₂ on hemoglobin. A conventional pulse oximeter can read misleadingly normal/high because it cannot reliably distinguish oxyhemoglobin from carboxyhemoglobin. The RCP 105 Monitoring deck says dyshemoglobins (e.g., carboxyhemoglobin) give false high readings (SpO₂ > actual SaO₂), and CO-oximetry measures the individual hemoglobin components (O₂Hb, COHb). Bedside correction (RCP 103 Sleep Apnea): subtract COHb from the SpO₂. A pulse-ox reading of 90% with 7% COHb is a true saturation of 83%. A quick screen (RCP 203 Lssn 3b): exhaled CO in ppm ÷ 6 ≈ HbCO% (e.g. 48 ppm ≈ 8%), used for moderate CO poisoning in conscious, cooperative patients.

Hess adds: two-wavelength pulse oximetry does not measure HbCO. The oximeter overestimates HbO₂ saturation by an amount roughly equal to the HbCO level, displaying a high SpO₂ despite significant HbCO. Newer multiple-wavelength pulse oximeters give a noninvasive SpCO, but its accuracy is ±6%, so it should not be used for triage or patient management. A low SpCO never rules out CO poisoning and must be confirmed by blood HbCO. A CO-poisoned patient breathing 100% O₂ can have a PaO₂ above 400 mm Hg despite a low CO-oximetry saturation. HbCO measured close to the time of exposure is more informative, because O₂ given before ED arrival lowers the hospital value.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slides 28 (and notes), 39 (and notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV, slide 40 · RCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slide 16 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 26 · Hess Ch 42 — Carbon Monoxide Exposure · Hess Ch 2 — Limitations; Pulse Oximetry to Measure Hemoglobin, Carboxyhemoglobin, and Methemoglobin

5COHb Half-Life and Treatment of CO Poisoning

Breathing COHb half-life: Burn deck (slide 39 notes) COHb half-life: Hess Ch 42
Room air ~4–5 hours about 5 hours (21% O₂, ambient pressure)
100% O₂ ~60–90 minutes about 74 minutes (range 26–148 minutes)
HBO "can reduce it further, often to ~20–30 minutes" less than 30 minutes (100% O₂ at 3 atm)

Use the deck's figures on this exam. They agree with Hess's in substance, but Hess's single 100% O₂ value is 74 min, not a 60–90 range.

Treatment. Administer 100% oxygen immediately; it accelerates displacement/elimination of CO from hemoglobin. If spontaneously breathing, use a non-rebreather with a tight seal and enough flow to keep the reservoir inflated. If the patient cannot protect the airway or ventilate, intubate and ventilate at FiO₂ 1.0. Consider HBOT for severe CO poisoning, especially with significant neurologic depression or cardiac involvement. HBOT can also be used later for compromised skin grafts/flaps. A typical HBO protocol (deck and Hess): 3 atm for the first treatment, then 2 or 3 atm for subsequent treatments, for 90 minutes, with two 10-minute air breaks to reduce oxygen-toxicity seizures. No HBO chamber? You do not need one to start lifesaving treatment: 100% O₂ immediately → COHb by CO-oximetry → assess severity → decide on transfer for HBOT. Don't delay O₂ while deciding about hyperbarics. Hess adds: for CO poisoning, use a nonrebreathing mask at 15 L/min irrespective of SpO₂ and PaO₂; 100% O₂ is the treatment of choice; give 100% O₂ until a safe HbCO level is reached. Hess's recap says to consider HBO particularly with neurologic depression or delayed neurologic sequelae.

Hess adds: HBO for CO remains controversial. Unstable patients are poor candidates for a monoplace chamber (access is severely limited). Wheezing/air trapping (pneumothorax risk) and high fever (seizure risk) are relative contraindications. MbCO dissociates more slowly than HbCO, which can cause a rebound of HbCO several hours after normobaric O₂.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slides 39 (and notes), 40 (notes), 47 · Hess Ch 42 — Carbon Monoxide Exposure; Respiratory Recap "Carboxyhemoglobinemia" · Hess Ch 14 — Clinical Practice Guideline 14-1; Rationale for Supplemental Oxygen

6Why Compliance Falls in Burn and Inhalation Injury high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Inhalation injury → inflammation → ↑ alveolar-capillary (pulmonary capillary) permeability → pulmonary edema → ↓ compliance and impaired oxygenation, an ARDS-like picture. On CXR it looks like noncardiogenic pulmonary edema. On exam, tachypnea may result from hypoxemia, ↓ compliance, and pain/anxiety. The chest wall is the second problem: a circumferential chest burn gives rigid eschar + underlying edema → restricted chest expansion → ↓ ventilation, so a chest escharotomy may be required. Hess adds: chest wall burns decrease CCW, and until thoracic escharotomies are done, high airway pressures may be necessary to overcome the decreased compliance of the chest wall. Ventilator plan per the deck: VT ≈ 6 mL/kg PBW, Pplat ≤ 30 cm H₂O, appropriate PEEP, avoid VILI. The same slide-37 note also lists NIF/MIP < −60 cm H₂O normal (< −40 COPD, < −20 myasthenia gravis) and P/F > 380 normal, < 300 mild ARDS or ALI, < 200 ARDS, < 150 proning.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slides 9 (notes), 22, 24, 29 (notes), 32 (notes), 37 (notes) · Hess Ch 42 — Respiratory Failure (ventilator management); Box 42-4 · Hess Ch 51 — Chest Wall Compliance

7CO vs Hydrogen Cyanide high-yield · beyond the listed asks

CO poisoning: hemoglobin can't effectively transport/deliver O₂. Hydrogen cyanide (HCN) is produced by combustion of nitrogen-containing compounds in a low-oxygen atmosphere. It binds the cytochrome oxidase system and inhibits cellular metabolism → tissue and systemic acidosis: cells can't effectively USE O₂. Treatment (deck): 100% O₂ plus hydroxocobalamin (Cyanokit) for suspected (severe) cyanide poisoning. Hydroxocobalamin binds cyanide to form cyanocobalamin (vitamin B12), which is then eliminated.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 45 (and notes) · Hess Ch 42 — Hydrogen Cyanide Poisoning
05

ECMO

5 items · Access points · inclusion criteria · the difference between VV and VA

1Access Points: where the cannulas go2Inclusion Criteria (= Indications)3Difference Between VV and VA ECMO4The Two Knobs: blood flow vs sweep gas5Where the Deck and Hess Disagree: the traps

1Access Points: where the cannulas go

ECMO is done by inserting a large-bore cannula into a central vein, draining blood from the patient, pumping it through an artificial gas exchanger (oxygenator), and returning the oxygenated blood to the patient, either to the venous (VV) or arterial (VA) system. The deck's figure (Hess Figure 23-3) shows three basic cannulation strategies, and Hess's text adds the other sites:

Configuration Drainage (blood out) Return (blood back) Notes
VV, dual-site (Figure 23-3 A) Femoral vein Right internal jugular vein (into the right atrium), or the contralateral femoral vein Drainage and return cannulas in different vessels
VV, single-site: dual-lumen catheter (Figure 23-3 C) Superior and inferior vena cavae Right atrium Catheter usually in the right internal jugular vein; can also go in the left subclavian vein
VA in adolescents and adults (Figure 23-3 B) Femoral vein Femoral artery (the most common arterial access) Axillary or subclavian arteries might also be used
VA in neonates and younger children — Carotid artery (typically) Used for cardiac support, or because the internal jugular vein is too small for a double-lumen venous catheter
Open-chest cannulation — — Several configurations, depending on the patient's pathology and surgical history

Single-site (dual-lumen) VV, pros and cons (Hess): it allows single-site cannulation, reduces vessel trauma and infection risk, and potentially allows increased wakefulness and mobility. It is ideally placed under fluoroscopy or transesophageal echocardiography. However, it cannot achieve the higher blood flow required in some patients with sepsis and severe hypoxemia, is more susceptible to flow interruptions, and is more expensive. It is therefore reserved for less severe hypoxemia with higher likelihood of mobility, such as a bridge to lung transplant. Why the access matters: adult VV blood flow commonly ranges 2 to 6 L/min, and the flow achievable is limited by cannula size and location. The cannulation plan must anticipate the patient's needs. Recirculation, a common access-related problem, is oxygenated blood going back through the circuit rather than to the patient. Its causes are improper cannula positioning, hypovolemia, excess flows, or decreased cardiac output. Signs: venous drainage blood brighter red, closer in color to the return tubing, or ↑ SvO₂ on continuous monitoring. It is significant when SpO₂ decreases. It can often be fixed by decreasing ECMO flow and confirming cannula position. The deck's memory trick (slide 12 notes): don't memorize every cannula site; follow the RETURN cannula. Returns to a vein → VV → lungs; returns to an artery → VA → heart + lungs.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slides 12 (and notes), 17 (notes) · Hess Ch 23 — ECMO Basics; Figure 23-3; Monitoring

2Inclusion Criteria (= Indications)

Word play from the study guide: "Inclusion criteria = Indication."

The gate: ECMO is reserved for patients with a reversible process, or, in end-stage lung disease, for patients who can be successfully bridged to lung transplant. The deck's Key Point (slide 28): ECMO may be indicated in selected patients with severe, potentially reversible respiratory failure that cannot be adequately managed with conventional lung-protective strategies. It has traditionally been used as rescue therapy for respiratory failure refractory to conventional therapy. Hess adds (Ch 38, ARDS): ECMO should be reserved for patients with refractory hypoxemia and/or respiratory acidosis despite optimal medical management.

Indications for VV ECMO for respiratory failure (deck slide 14 = Hess Respiratory Recap): severe ARDS (the most common indication), status asthmaticus, pulmonary hemorrhage, pulmonary embolism, persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn, congenital diaphragmatic hernia, meconium aspiration syndrome, air leak syndromes, and end-stage lung disease as a bridge to transplant. The deck adds two with an asterisk: severe PNA/ARDS (notes: "Pneumonia can progress to severe ARDS") and smoke inhalation. Hess's list has neither pneumonia nor smoke inhalation. The slide's takeaway: VV ECMO = severe respiratory failure + adequate cardiac function; the notes stress these are potential indications, not automatic reasons to start ECMO. Hess's text also names airway compression due to mediastinal masses and congenital airway anomalies. Many of these non-ARDS patients have the best outcomes, because they may not have the multiorgan dysfunction common in ARDS.

When to start: the numbers (Hess unless marked):

Criterion Threshold
Predicted mortality Deck (slide 13): consider ECMO when predicted mortality is ≥ 50%; it is more strongly considered when predicted mortality approaches 80% despite optimal conventional therapy. The notes: "Don't get hung up on the exact percentages"; consider ECMO early enough to help, not after prolonged injurious ventilation and multiorgan failure. ELSO (Hess): initiate once predicted mortality reaches 50% to 80%, which Hess calls "arbitrary" (see item 5). Both: patients in the 50–80% range typically suffer from multisystem organ failure (MOF) and may be less likely to benefit
Oxygenation index = (P̄aw × FiO₂ × 100) ÷ PaO₂ ELSO: initiate in neonates at OI 20 to 40; one study: OI ≥ 33 = risk of lung injury outweighs risk of ECMO (neonates); similar values used in children and adults. RCP 105: OI 0–10 normal; higher OI (e.g., 25–40) may indicate the possibility of ECMO
Murray Lung Injury Score Uses PaO₂/FiO₂, CXR infiltrates, lung compliance, PEEP; scored 0 to 4 (4 = most severe ARDS). CESAR enrolled LIS > 3
PaO₂/FiO₂ (EOLIA trial) P/F < 80 for 6 hours or < 50 for 3 hours
PaO₂/FiO₂ (Hess Ch 43, ventilating the septic patient with ARDS) P/F < 100 → consider extracorporeal life support (P/F < 150 → consider prone position and neuromuscular blockade)
Respiratory acidosis (CESAR and EOLIA) pH < 7.20–7.25. Refractory respiratory acidosis with hemodynamic compromise → consider ECMO irrespective of the degree of hypoxemia
Lung-protective strategies failing If low VT, early neuromuscular blockade, prone positioning, and minimizing driving pressures do not meet clinical goals → consider ECMO

The instructor's framing (slide 15 notes): "There is no single ventilator setting or single ABG value that automatically triggers ECMO," and "the optimal timing of initiation of ECMO remains unclear." The ECMO conversation should happen when conventional ventilation is failing: refractory hypoxemia, severe hypercapnia/acidosis, increasing FiO₂ requirements, increasing pressures, proning/rescue strategies not working, and approaching unsafe ventilator settings.

Exclusion side (Hess): mechanical ventilation at high settings (FiO₂ > 0.9, Pplat > 30 cm H₂O) for 7 days or more is a contraindication under current guidelines. Newer data suggest mortality does not rise significantly until at least 10 to 14 days of pre-ECMO ventilation. Absolute contraindications are rare: irreversible coagulopathy, inability to cannulate for anatomic reasons, and futility. Severe neurologic injury and terminal disease with short life expectancy are also contraindications (the deck's slide 15 notes name CNS injury, terminal malignancy, and irreversible multiorgan dysfunction as factors weighed when deciding whether ECMO is appropriate). The RESP Score (from the ELSO registry) predicts survival. Predictors of ECMO mortality (both sources): increasing age, multiorgan dysfunction, and prolonged mechanical ventilation prior to ECMO.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slides 4, 13 (and notes), 14 (and notes), 15 (notes), 28 · RCP 105 Management of MV, slide 66 (notes) · Hess Ch 23 — Indications and Contraindications; Respiratory Recap "Indications for ECMO for Respiratory Failure"; Key Points · Hess Ch 38 — Extracorporeal Membrane Oxygenation · Hess Ch 43 — Implications for Respiratory Care

3Difference Between VV and VA ECMO

The one structural difference: where the oxygenated blood is returned. VV returns it to a vein; VA returns it to an artery.

Venovenous (VV) Venoarterial (VA)
Supports ONLY respiratory failure BOTH cardiac and respiratory failure (Hess Key Point: "cardiac and cardiopulmonary failure")
What it provides Oxygenation and CO₂ removal; "requires an adequate cardiac function". Slide checklist: oxygenation ✓, CO₂ removal ✓, cardiac support ✗ Oxygenation, CO₂ removal, AND systemic perfusion (notes: "adequate perfusion"). Slide checklist: oxygenation ✓, CO₂ removal ✓, circulatory support ✓
Blood path Drawn from a vein, oxygenated, returned to the venous system (near the heart) and mixed with the patient's poorly oxygenated blood; the patient's own heart then distributes it Drawn from a vein, oxygenated, returned to an artery, bypassing the heart and lungs
Typical access Femoral vein → right IJ (or contralateral femoral vein); or a dual-lumen catheter in the right IJ Femoral vein → femoral artery (adults); carotid artery (neonates/young children)
Role in respiratory failure Preferred method, "both for simplicity and for its more favorable safety profile" Salvage technique for adult respiratory failure, because of coronary, brain, and upper-body ischemia, ↑ stroke risk, and ↑ vascular complications
Limb / vessel complications Much less limb-ischemia risk, but cannula thrombosis: catheter-associated DVT in as many as 85% on post-decannulation ultrasound Limb ischemia in 17% (5% need amputation); partly prevented with a distal perfusion catheter
Stroke In VV ECMO for H1N1, intracranial hemorrhage ~9%, contributing to 43% of all deaths (H1N1 cohort only; in VV ECMO studies for ARDS not limited to H1N1, the overall stroke rate did not differ from patients who did not receive ECMO) Ischemic stroke more common in VA, contributing to ~9% of all deaths
Reported survival (deck slide 22 notes; the notes call these numbers "useful historically", see item 5) 50% to 70% for severe respiratory failure (e.g., ARDS) treated with VV ECMO 30% to 50% for severe cardiac failure treated with VA ECMO

Memory line from the deck's Key Points: "VV ECMO provides support for Respiratory Failure / Lung Support. VA ECMO provides support for Cardiac and Respiratory Failure." The deck's phrase VV "requires an adequate cardiac function" tells you that if the heart cannot pump, VV is not enough. The slide 6 notes explain why: VV ECMO does NOT pump oxygenated blood into the arterial circulation, so the patient's own heart still has to provide the cardiac output. That is why VA can support a patient with cardiogenic shock or cardiopulmonary failure, whereas VV cannot. The slide also defines ECLS (extracorporeal life support) as the broader umbrella term: ECMO is one form of ECLS, and ECLS can also include temporary mechanical circulatory support devices that don't necessarily provide gas exchange.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slides 6 (and notes), 8, 12, 22 (notes), 28 · Hess Ch 23 — Introduction; ECMO Basics; Complications (Neurologic Injury; Vascular Injury and Thrombosis); Key Points

4The Two Knobs: blood flow vs sweep gas high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Blood flow and sweep gas are the main variables used to achieve goal oxygenation and CO₂ removal. Oxygenation follows blood flow: as a greater fraction of cardiac output passes through the circuit, more oxygenated blood returns. In a hypoxemic patient, increasing blood flow typically improves oxygenation. CO₂ removal follows sweep gas: CO₂ leaves by passive diffusion across the gradient from blood into the sweep gas. Blood leaving the oxygenator is typically fully saturated even at sweep flows of 1 to 2 L/min, so increasing sweep flow does little for oxygenation but increases CO₂ clearance. Hess gives sweep flow a range of 0.1 to 10 L/min. The deck adds: sweep gas is 100% O₂ or carbogen (95% O₂ / 5% CO₂) (slide 8 text; the slide 8 notes' background says it is "typically a blend of medical-grade oxygen and air, but in some cases, pure oxygen is used"; for a slide-worded question use the slide text); the FiO₂ of the sweep gas sets the O₂ concentration delivered to the membrane oxygenator and contributes to the diffusion gradient for O₂; and sweep gas flow is independently adjusted based primarily on the patient's CO₂ and acid-base status: ↑ sweep flow → ↑ CO₂ removal → ↓ PaCO₂, and ↓ sweep flow → ↑ PaCO₂. The deck's one-line rule (slide 8 notes; Key Points): oxygenation → think BLOOD FLOW + FiO₂; CO₂ removal → think SWEEP FLOW. The deck's "resting" ventilator settings on ECMO (slide 20) are VT < 6 mL/kg, FiO₂ < 60%, RR < 10, PIP < 35, P̄aw < 25, Pplat < 30, PEEP < 15 cm H₂O (between 10 and 15); slide 11 gives a shorter list (FiO₂ < 60%, RR < 10, PIP < 35, P̄aw < 25, PEEP < 15), where P̄aw is mean airway pressure, not plateau pressure. The notes say not to memorize these as "one mandatory ECMO recipe"; learn the pattern: VT < 6 mL/kg PBW, ↓ FiO₂, ↓ RR, ↓ PIP/Pplat, ↓ driving pressure, and enough PEEP to maintain recruitment. RR can be < 10 because the sweep gas is now removing much of the CO₂. Optimal PEEP during ECMO remains uncertain and should be individualized (slide 20). Hess: VT ≤ 6 mL/kg PBW, driving pressure ≤ 15, Pplat ≤ 30 (and potentially lower), PEEP 10–15 cm H₂O.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slides 8 (and notes), 11 (and notes), 16, 20 (and notes), 28 · Hess Ch 23 — ECMO Basics; ECMO Management (Mechanical Ventilation)

5Where the Deck and Hess Disagree: the traps high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Topic Deck says Hess says Use
"54%" "Severe ARDS survival, when managed with ECMO, > 54%" (slide 22) Adults with severe ARDS managed in an ICU survive at approximately 54%; ECMO survival in the trials was 56% (CESAR) and 60% (EOLIA) Answer as the slide does: severe ARDS survival on ECMO is > 54%. That fits Hess: its ~54% is survival of adults with severe ARDS managed in an ICU (conventional care; Hess reports the ECMO results separately), and the ECMO trial arms did better (56% CESAR, 60% EOLIA). Do not quote 54% itself as the ECMO survival figure. Cumulative survival for all respiratory ECMO patients is 61% (both)
57% → 72% "ECMO has significantly increased from 57% in 1993 to 72% in 2007" (slide 22) Survival for children without comorbidities rose from 57% (1993) to 72% (2007) Quote the numbers, but they apply to children without comorbidities
Predicted-mortality trigger "Consider ECMO when predicted mortality is ≥ 50%; ECMO is more strongly considered when predicted mortality approaches 80% despite optimal conventional therapy" (slide 13); notes: "Don't get hung up on the exact percentages" ELSO recommends initiation "once predicted mortality reaches 50% to 80%"; Hess calls this arbitrary Same 50%–80% band. For a slide-worded question: consider at ≥ 50%, more strongly as it approaches 80%. The instructor stresses timing: early enough to help, before prolonged injurious ventilation and MOF. Both add that patients in this range often have MOF and may be less likely to benefit
Growth years 360% from 2009 to 2017 (slide 7) 360% from 2008 to 2017 360% in both. The instructor's slide says 2009–2017, which a slide-worded question will use; Hess says 2008–2017
Indication list Adds severe PNA/ARDS and smoke inhalation (asterisked) Neither pneumonia nor smoke inhalation is on Hess's Respiratory Recap list Instructor's slide: know them as indications

Other outcome numbers both sources share: median ECMO duration in ARDS 9 to 15 days, near-fatal asthma survival 84%, and bleeding is the most common complication. The four complications are bleeding, neurologic injury, infection, vascular injury and thrombosis. The instructor's hedge on the survival numbers (slide 22 notes): they come from the textbook's cited datasets and are useful historically, but ECMO outcomes change over time and differ by diagnosis, age, center, patient selection, and severity. Look at the trend, not the percentages: survival is generally better when the underlying condition is highly reversible and the patient has fewer comorbidities or additional organ failures.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slides 7, 13 (and notes), 14, 22 (and notes), 23, 28 · Hess Ch 23 — History of ECMO Use; Indications and Contraindications; ECMO Outcomes; Complications; Key Points
06

Renal System

5 items · Urinary output (UOP) · diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) · the kidney’s role in acid-base balance

1Urinary Output (UOP): Normal Values, Oliguria, Anuria, Polyuria2Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA): What It Is, Why the Kidney Matters, and Treatment3DKA on the ABG: Anion Gap, Compensation and Kussmaul Breathing4The Kidney's Function in Acid–Base Balance5Renal Failure and the Ventilated Patient: What the RT Sees

1Urinary Output (UOP): Normal Values, Oliguria, Anuria, Polyuria

The daily anchor (Renal A&P deck and Hess Ch 53, same wording): the kidneys filter nearly 200 L of blood every day, and the majority is reabsorbed, so normal urine output is only 1 to 2 L/day. Urine is approximately 95% water. It flows kidney → ureter → urinary bladder → urethra, and leaves the body by micturition (urination). The pharmacology Diuretics deck explains why so little comes out. About 20% of total blood flow through the nephron (~130 mL/min) is filtered at the glomerulus, 99% of that filtrate is reabsorbed in the tubules, and < 1% is excreted as urine. The kidneys get ~22% of cardiac output (1.1 L/min).

The hourly clinical ranges (Diuretics deck, slide 13 notes): Normal 30–60 mL/hr · Oliguria < 30 mL/hr · Anuria < 50 mL/day · Polyuria > 60 mL/hr. The slide text itself writes oliguria as "< 30-60 mL/hr"; the notes' summary pins it at < 30 mL/hr. "Oliguria or Anuria = signs of Renal Failure." The difference between the two is the volume of urine produced in a 24-hour period. Anuria is the more severe medical emergency: it means nearly complete kidney failure (AKI), dehydration, or a total obstruction. The deck sums it up as "Low urine output = BAD sign." Its notes link low UOP to shock, hypotension and poor cardiac output (CHF), which can lead to ↓ UOP, acute kidney injury (AKI), and fluid retention that worsens lung status. Polyuria shows up when filtrate is not reabsorbed (Renal deck, Stop and Think): the result is dehydration, electrolyte imbalance, polyuria, low blood pressure, acid–base disturbances and nutrient loss. In DKA, polyuria comes from osmotic diuresis (see item 2).

Why it mattersWhy ventilated patients make less urine: positive pressure ventilation causes ↓ cardiac output and renal perfusion, ↑ antidiuretic hormone (ADH) and ↓ atrial natriuretic factor (ANF). The result is reduced urine output and fluid retention. Hess Ch 20 lists the same renal complications (reduced urine output; ↑ ADH and ↓ atrial natriuretic peptide). The Monitoring deck adds that oliguria indicates fluid deficiency from decreased renal perfusion, decreased fluid intake, or decreased cardiac output.

Hess adds: good urine production is a marker of end organ perfusion as well as renal function. The most important tests of renal function are the quantity of urine produced and its characteristics (pH, specific gravity, microscopic analysis, culture) (Ch 5). The book's Normal Values page lists urine output: 1 mL/kg/hr. A rapid response team may be called for urine output less than 50 mL over 4 hours (Ch 28, Table 28-5).

The numbers differ by source. Know which deck says what:

Source Normal Oliguria Anuria Polyuria
RCP 202 Renal deck (= Hess Ch 53) 1–2 L/day — — —
RCP 104 Diuretics deck (also the CNS-drugs deck) 30–60 mL/hr < 30 mL/hr (Diuretics slide 13 notes); the slide text of both decks reads "< 30–60 mL/hr" < 50 mL/day > 60 mL/hr
RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9) 50–60 mL/hr < 20 mL/hr, < 400 mL/24 hr, < 160 mL/8 hr — —
RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12) — < 20 mL/hr (or < 400 mL/24 hr or < 160 mL/8 hr) < 100 mL/day —
RCP 105 Principles of MV > 400 mL/24 hr or > 160 mL/8 hr — — —
RCP 103 Clinical Data ~40–60 mL/hr — — —
RCP 202 Burn deck (resuscitation goal) ~0.5 mL/kg/hr, monitored hourly — — —
Hess Normal Values page 1 mL/kg/hr — — —
Exam anchorFor this exam, use the 1–2 L/day figure and the Diuretics-deck ranges (30–60 / < 30 / < 50 mL/day / > 60). The final's renal ask cites "Pharmacology {Chptr 5}", which is the CNS/PNS chapter and has no urine-output values. The Diuretics deck (Ch 19) is the pharmacology deck that gives the full set of hourly ranges, and the CNS-drugs deck (Ch 20) repeats its slide text. If a choice reads "< 20 mL/hr or < 400 mL/day", that is the RCP 105 (Chang) definition of oliguria. While IV KCl is running, UOP should be > 40 to 50 mL/hour (Management of MV deck).
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slides 14, 16 (notes), 22 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 — Diuretics, slides 6 (and notes), 13 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 20 — Drugs Affecting the CNS, slide 16 (notes) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 24, 25 (and notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 46, 55 · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 39 · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 38 (notes) · Hess Ch 53 — Physiology of the Kidney; Urine · Hess Ch 5 — Serum Chemistries Associated with Renal Function · Hess Ch 20 — Box 20-3 · Hess Ch 28 — Table 28-5 · Hess front matter — Normal Values (Hemodynamics)

2Diabetic Ketoacidosis (DKA): What It Is, Why the Kidney Matters, and Treatment

Definition (Hess glossary): DKA is "a buildup of ketones in the blood caused by the breakdown of stored fats for energy; a potentially life-threatening complication in patients with diabetes mellitus." Mechanism (RCP 103 ABG deck, DKA case notes): in type 1 diabetes, the insulin-producing cells in the pancreas are destroyed, which leaves little or no insulin. Without insulin, glucose cannot enter cells and builds up in the blood (hyperglycemia). Excess blood glucose causes osmotic diuresis → dehydration, so the patient is thirsty (polydipsia) and urinates frequently (polyuria). With no glucose available to cells, the body breaks down fat for energy, and this produces ketone bodies, which are acidic. The result is metabolic acidosis. Hess Ch 5 states the same chain: islet cell dysfunction (type 1 diabetes) impairs insulin production → hyperglycemia → if severe, DKA. Severe hyperglycemia can also cause a hyperosmolar state with coma.

Why it mattersWhy it belongs in the renal section: the respiratory system can get rid of carbonic acid (H₂CO₃) by eliminating CO₂, but it cannot excrete the other acids made by cellular metabolism. The renal system eliminates these other acids: phosphoric, uric, lactic, and ketone acids. Ketone acids are fixed acids, i.e. acids the lungs cannot remove.

The deck's classic case (RCP 103 ABG deck): a 17-year-old in a semi-coma, with polydipsia, polyuria, weakness and rapid weight loss. Her respirations were deep and fast (Kussmaul), 24 breaths/min, with HR 105 and BP 115/70. Labs: urine ketones 4.0 mmol/L (normal < 0.6) and glucose 650 mg/dL (normal 70–110). Treatment: immediate infusion of normal saline and IV insulin, transitioned to subcutaneous insulin within 48 hours, and discharged on a daily insulin regimen. The deck's bottom line: "DKA is life-threatening and requires immediate fluid resuscitation and insulin therapy." The Management of MV deck gives the same treatment line, DKA → insulin therapy, fluids, electrolytes. In its general notes on treating metabolic acidosis (not specific to DKA), the deck adds that bicarbonate therapy may be considered if pH < 7.1 and the cause is reversible or dialysis is delayed. Bicarbonate is controversial for routine use because it can cause paradoxical CNS acidosis and volume overload. The ABG deck explains why the cause dictates the treatment: lactic acids → oxygen therapy, ketone acids → insulin (insulin restores glucose metabolism and stops ketone production). On a urinalysis, glucose/ketones point to diabetes and possible metabolic acidosis, and may support a diagnosis like DKA (Clinical Data deck).

Drug link: among diuretics, thiazides have the strongest diabetogenic activity (they raise serum glucose), and some cases of DKA have been reported (Diuretics deck).

Hess adds: peripheral venous blood gases might be useful to evaluate the patient for DKA (Ch 4). Ch 5 gives general potassium rules (stated in general, not specifically for DKA): acidosis and reduced insulin raise serum K⁺, while insulin shifts K⁺ into the cells (listed as a cause of hypokalemia, Box 5-4). Box 5-8 lists DKA among the causes of hypomagnesemia.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slide 5 · RCP 103 ABG Assessments, slides 36 (and notes), 37 (notes), 45 (and notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 27 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 58 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 — Diuretics, slide 31 · Hess glossary — diabetic ketoacidosis · Hess Ch 5 — Glucose; Potassium, Box 5-4; Box 5-8 · Hess Ch 4 — Venous Blood Gases
Gap · still not coveredformal DKA diagnostic cutoffs (a glucose threshold, a serum-ketone or β-hydroxybutyrate level, a DKA-specific bicarbonate/pH/anion-gap cutoff) and an insulin/fluid protocol (rates, doses, potassium replacement thresholds). The sources give only the case values and "fluids + insulin + electrolytes". Look in an endocrine/critical-care text; Hess cites Fayfman et al., "Management of hyperglycemic crises" (Ch 5 reference 32) but does not reproduce it.

3DKA on the ABG: Anion Gap, Compensation and Kussmaul Breathing

The ABG (same RCP 103 case, room air): pH 6.98 · PaCO₂ 24 mm Hg · HCO₃⁻ 6 mEq/L · PaO₂ 98 mm Hg · SaO₂ 97% → "Severe metabolic acidosis due to diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) with partial respiratory compensation." The very low PaCO₂ is the respiratory compensation via hyperventilation (Kussmaul respirations). The pH stays critically low, so the compensation is only partial: it cannot normalize pH because the metabolic derangement is extreme. The PaO₂ of 98 sits inside Hess's normal range (80–100 mm Hg on room air), so DKA here is an acid–base problem, not an oxygenation problem. Hess Ch 4 Case 2 has the same pattern: a 55-year-old woman with known diabetes, in a coma with gasping, deep respirations, has pH 7.25, PaCO₂ 20 mm Hg, PaO₂ 90 mm Hg, HCO₃⁻ 10 mmol/L. Hess reads this as "partially compensated metabolic acidosis without hypoxemia", with a high anion gap "most likely due to ketoacidosis, consistent with diabetic coma."

Anion gap (AG) — DKA is a HIGH-gap acidosis. AG = [Na⁺] − (Cl⁻ + HCO₃⁻), and the normal range is 9 to 14 mEq/L (RCP 103 ABG deck). An anion gap above 14 mEq/L usually indicates metabolic acidosis, meaning there is an excess of fixed acids. The acids release H⁺, bicarbonate is used up buffering them, and their unmeasured anions (ketones, lactate) widen the gap. Increased AG → accumulation of fixed acids, e.g. lactic acidosis, ketoacidosis. Normal AG → loss of HCO₃⁻, e.g. severe diarrhea: Cl⁻ rises to replace the lost HCO₃⁻, which is why this is called hyperchloremic metabolic acidosis. Hess Table 4-7 lists "Ketoacidosis: diabetic, alcoholic, starvation" first under increased gap. Hess adds: ketones are among the unmeasured anions (Box 5-6, "diabetic and alcoholic ketoacidosis"), and the AG is also used to follow the response to treatment, e.g. during the treatment of DKA (Ch 4). Two cautions follow.

  • Renal failure is placed inconsistently. ABG deck slides 46–47 list renal disease/renal failure as normal-gap examples. Slide 48, the Monitoring deck and Hess Table 4-7 all put renal failure in the increased-gap group. Hess's normal-gap list has renal tubular acidosis instead.
  • The "normal" AG varies by source:
    • 103 ABG deck: 9–14 mEq/L
    • Management of MV deck: 10–14 mEq/L without K⁺ (15–20 with K⁺)
    • Monitoring deck: 8–12 mEq/L without K⁺ (12–16 with K⁺)
    • Hess Ch 4: 12 ± 4 mmol/L
    • Hess Normal Values page: 8–16 mmol/L
    For this exam use 9–14, with > 14 = high-gap metabolic acidosis. That is the deck that teaches AG with the DKA case.

Worked example (two from the sources):

  • Normal values (ABG deck): AG = 140 − (105 + 24) = 140 − 129 = 11 mEq/L → normal.
  • Hess Case 2 (diabetic coma): AG = 140 − (105 + 10) = 25 mmol/L → high anion gap (Hess's normal is 12 ± 4 mmol/L) → increase in unmeasured anions (ketoacidosis).

Kussmaul respirations (Renal deck notes): a deep and labored breathing pattern associated with severe metabolic acidosis, particularly DKA. It is the body's compensatory mechanism: the patient expels more CO₂, which is an acid when dissolved in blood. Its features: deep and rapid breaths, an "air hungry" look with large volumes on each breath, and a regular rhythm without periods of apnea, which is what separates it from Cheyne-Stokes. It can be noisy because the breaths are so deep. It indicates severe acidosis. The Clinical Data deck's breathing-pattern table lists Kussmaul's as deep & fast, labored, caused by DKA or severe metabolic acidosis, and its notes add that any severe metabolic acidosis can trigger it. Hess Ch 1: "Kussmaul respirations occur when hyperventilation serves as a compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis, most commonly diabetic ketoacidosis." Conflict to know: Hess Ch 4 describes Kussmaul breathing as "deep and labored breathing with normal or reduced frequency." The Renal deck, the Clinical Data deck and Hess Ch 52 all say rapid ("rapid respiratory rate and a higher than normal tidal volume"). Even Hess Ch 4's own diabetic-coma case (Case 2) describes "a rapid respiratory rate, deep breathing", so "normal or reduced frequency" is the outlier. For this exam, use "deep AND rapid."

How fast the lungs compensate: for every 1 mEq/L fall in HCO₃⁻, PaCO₂ falls by ~1.2 mm Hg. This begins within the first hour and is complete by 12 to 24 hours (ABG deck). Hess adds: the ventilatory response usually starts within minutes, is well advanced at 2 hours, and reaches maximal compensation in 12 to 24 hours. In diabetic acidosis, severe dehydration may increase [Na⁺] and decrease GFR, which increases HCO₃⁻ reabsorption. That concomitant metabolic alkalosis offsets the acidosis to some degree (Ch 4).

Exam anchorOn the ventilator (RT trap): if a ventilated patient's low PaCO₂ is compensation for metabolic acidosis, do not reduce ventilation. "The patient needs to hyperventilate to compensate for acidosis. Correct the underlying cause (DKA, lactic acidosis, etc.)." "Ventilator frequency must not be reduced when hyperventilation is a compensatory mechanism for metabolic acidosis." The reverse also holds: ventilatory support should NOT be increased to correct metabolic acidosis. Metabolic problems are corrected by treating their causes.
SourceRCP 103 ABG Assessments, slides 33 (and notes), 36 (and notes), 38 (notes), 40, 41, 42 (notes), 46, 47, 48 · RCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slide 24 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 25 (and notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 24 (notes), 27, 49 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 27 (notes), 28, 29 · Hess Ch 4 — Anion Gap; Metabolic Acidosis, Table 4-7; Case 2 · Hess Ch 5 — Box 5-6 · Hess Ch 1 — patterns of respiration · Hess Ch 52 — breathing patterns · Hess front matter — Normal Values (Arterial Blood Gases; Blood Chemistry)

4The Kidney's Function in Acid–Base Balance

The one-liner (Diuretics deck notes): "Lungs control CO₂, kidneys control bicarbonate." The Renal deck puts it this way: the kidneys are responsible for regulation of blood volume and its chemical composition, as well as maintaining the correct balance between acids and bases and between water and salts. The renal system can eliminate acids or bases through the urine. The lungs get rid of carbonic acid (H₂CO₃) by eliminating CO₂, but the lungs cannot excrete the other acids of cellular metabolism. The renal system eliminates those (fixed) acids: phosphoric, uric, lactic, and ketone acids. The deck's notes say the kidneys manage pH by "reabsorbing and regenerating bicarbonate from urine: Bicarbonate helps neutralize acids!" The Diuretics deck: the "fundamental function of the Kidney is the control of buffering substances, especially, HCO₃⁻." Hess adds (Ch 53): the chemical buffer system is the body's first line of defense. It reacts within seconds but cannot actually eliminate acids or bases. "Only the renal system can regulate alkaline balance in the blood." When the blood is acidic, the kidney eliminates H⁺ in the urine and retains HCO₃⁻ to raise the pH. When it is alkalotic, the kidney eliminates HCO₃⁻ and retains H⁺ to lower the pH. This happens in the nephrons. Hess Ch 11 gives the split of the metabolic waste load: the kidney removes ~2% (as nonvolatile acids) and the lungs ~98% (as CO₂).

The nephron's three processes (each kidney has ~1 million nephrons, the functional units of the kidney):

Process What it does Acid–base role
Glomerular filtration Passive: hydrostatic pressure forces fluid and solutes through the glomerulus. GFR = the volume of filtrate formed each minute by all glomeruli. Deck: young healthy adults ~90–120 mL/min/1.73 m², average adult about 125 mL/min; Hess: approximately 120 to 125 mL/min Filters HCO₃⁻ along with everything else
Tubular reabsorption "Reclaiming plasma". Begins as soon as filtrate enters the proximal tubules. Nutrients (glucose, amino acids, vitamins) completely reabsorbed; also water, small proteins, Na⁺, K⁺, Mg²⁺, Ca²⁺, Cl⁻ and HCO₃⁻ The proximal tubule is the primary site for reabsorption of HCO₃⁻ (Hess). Hess Ch 4 puts it at 85% to 90% of filtered HCO₃⁻
Tubular secretion "Clearing waste" from the plasma (drugs, metabolites, urea, uric acid). H⁺, K⁺, NH₄⁺ (ammonium), creatinine and certain organic acids move into the filtrate Distal tubules secrete acids. The main urinary buffers are phosphate and ammonia (Hess)

The urine that is excreted contains both secreted and filtered substances. Hess adds the pH triggers: when serum pH falls to the acidic end of its range (< 7.35), tubule cells actively secrete more H⁺ and retain more HCO₃⁻. When it rises to the alkaline end (> 7.45), Cl⁻ is reabsorbed instead of HCO₃⁻, which is eliminated in the urine.

The deck's Stop and Think, "pH 7.31: how will the kidney respond?" (four mechanisms, notes):

  1. ↑ HCO₃⁻ reabsorption, primarily in the proximal tubules, to buffer excess H⁺.
  2. ↑ H⁺ secretion into the tubular lumen, particularly in the distal convoluted tubules and collecting ducts.
  3. Ammonium production: the kidney makes ammonia (NH₃) from glutamine in the proximal tubules. NH₃ binds H⁺ to form NH₄⁺, which is excreted. This lets the kidney excrete acid without making the urine excessively acidic.
  4. Phosphate buffering: HPO₄²⁻ + H⁺ → H₂PO₄⁻, excreted in urine.
Exam anchorTiming, a classic exam point: renal compensation is a "gradual process, taking hours to days" (Renal deck). The RCP 103 ABG deck says "Renal compensation takes 3–5 days" for chronic PaCO₂ changes. Its rule of thumb: for every 10 mm Hg ↑ PaCO₂, HCO₃⁻ ↑ ~3.5–4 mEq/L, and for every 10 mm Hg ↓ PaCO₂, HCO₃⁻ ↓ ~4–5 mEq/L. Hess adds (Ch 4): renal compensation for respiratory acidosis begins as soon as PaCO₂ rises, but full compensation takes several days. The kidneys excrete 70 to 100 mmol/day of fixed acid. The distal tubules normally handle about 70 mmol/day, but their capacity can increase to as much as 700 mmol/day; reaching that maximum takes approximately 5 days. Compare the lungs, which produce rapid changes in pH.

The electrolyte link (Diuretics deck): Na⁺ is reabsorbed with Cl⁻ (electrically neutral, no major pH change) or in exchange for H⁺ or K⁺. Low Cl⁻ (hypochloremia) or low K⁺ (hypokalemia) forces Na⁺ to exchange for H⁺ → loss of H⁺ → METABOLIC ALKALOSIS. Loss of HCO₃⁻ buffer in the urine → METABOLIC ACIDOSIS. Memory trick: "Lose H⁺ = Alkalosis; Lose HCO₃⁻ = Acidosis." Hess agrees (Ch 4): hypokalemia increases H⁺ secretion → ↑ HCO₃⁻ reabsorption, and a low chloride level increases HCO₃⁻ reabsorption along with Na⁺.

Exam anchorTrap in Hess: the Ch 4 Key Points box says "the lungs regulate nonvolatile acid in the form of CO₂; the kidneys regulate volatile acids, also called fixed acids". That is backwards. The same chapter's text says H₂CO₃ is the volatile acid (eliminated by the lungs as CO₂) and "the kidneys can eliminate only nonvolatile acids." Go with the text and the deck: lungs = volatile (CO₂); kidneys = fixed/nonvolatile.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slides 4, 5, 9, 14 (notes), 17 (and notes), 19, 20, 21 (notes), 31 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 — Diuretics, slide 16 (and notes) · RCP 103 ABG Assessments, slide 22 · Hess Ch 53 — Renal Anatomy and Physiology; Glomerular Filtration; Tubular Reabsorption; Tubular Secretion · Hess Ch 4 — Physiology of Acid–Base Balance; The Lungs and Acid–Base Control; The Kidneys and Acid–Base Control; Respiratory Acidosis; Key Points · Hess Ch 11 — Minute Ventilation

5Renal Failure and the Ventilated Patient: What the RT Sees high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Positive pressure ventilation can hurt the kidney. The Principles of MV deck's "Big Picture" is: PPV → ↑ intrathoracic pressure → ↓ venous return → ↓ cardiac output → ↓ renal blood flow → ↓ urine output → renal dysfunction. That deck says the kidneys receive about 25% of circulating blood volume, so a drop in blood volume (hypovolemia) or cardiac output (PPV) can lead to renal insufficiency and eventual renal failure. (The Diuretics deck gives the kidneys' share as ~22% of cardiac output.) The memory line is "Kidney Failure = Urine Output ↓; BUN ↑; Creatinine ↑; and Potassium ↑." Azotemia (Renal deck notes) is abnormally high levels of nitrogen-containing compounds (urea, creatinine, other nitrogen-rich wastes) in the blood. (Lab normals for BUN and creatinine are covered under the Respiratory Drugs topic.)

"The Four R's" of renal failure: Retention of Waste (BUN ↑, Creatinine ↑) · Retention of Fluid (edema, pulmonary edema) · Retention of Potassium (hyperkalemia) · Retention of Drugs (toxicity). The RT connection: "Where does excess fluid eventually end up? (Answer = Lungs)". That brings crackles, hypoxemia and pulmonary edema, and the patient may require CPAP, BiPAP, or mechanical ventilation. Board pearl: hyperkalemia is the most immediately life-threatening electrolyte abnormality in renal failure. Hyperkalemia occurs because a decrease in UOP secondary to renal failure leads to retention of K⁺. And because failing kidneys cannot eliminate acid, acid accumulates → metabolic acidosis. The patient then compensates with tachypnea and Kussmaul respirations. Renal failure is also one of the three major causes of metabolic acidosis (with DKA and lactic acidosis), and its treatment is dialysis if severe.

SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 38, 39, 40 (and notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 19 — Diuretics, slide 6 · RCP 202 Lssn 3 — Renal A&P, slide 23 (notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 27 (notes), 56
07

GI System: Routes of Nutrition Delivery

7 items · Routes of nutrition delivery and how they differ

1The Three Routes (Oral, Enteral, Parenteral) and the Order of Preference2The Enteral Routes and How They Differ3Parenteral Nutrition: When It Is Used, Peripheral vs Central Access, and Its Risks4Enteral vs Parenteral: The Differences, and When to Start5Aspiration Precautions During Tube Feeding (Head of Bed and More)6Overfeeding, CO₂ Production and the Respiratory Quotient7Undernutrition, Refeeding Syndrome and Phosphate: Why Feeding Affects Weaning

1The Three Routes (Oral, Enteral, Parenteral) and the Order of Preference

No deck in the vault covers nutrition routes as a topic; the answer below is from Hess Ch 11, with deck points added where the RCP 104/105/202/203 decks touch on them. Hess Ch 11's Respiratory Recap names exactly three routes of nutrition delivery: oral, enteral, parenteral.

Route What it means Where it sits
Oral The patient eats and drinks "The preferred method of nutrient delivery is the oral route."
Enteral Hess glossary: "Provision of nutrients through the gastrointestinal tract when the patient is unable to chew or swallow." The pharmacology deck defines it this way: "Enteral: Refers literally to the small intestine, intended for absorption anywhere along the gastrointestinal tract. Most common is by Mouth." So in the pharmacology deck's terms oral is enteral. Hess Ch 11's nutrition recap lists oral separately because there "enteral" means feeding-tube delivery Next choice. Hess: "Enteral nutrition should always be considered when a patient has a functioning gastrointestinal (GI) tract."
Parenteral Hess glossary: "Administration of nutrients by a route other than the alimentary canal." The pharmacology deck: "Parenteral: Beside the intestine, any route other than enteral, commonly refers to injection of a drug." Reserved for when the GI tract is not functioning or stimulating the GI or pancreatic systems would worsen the patient's condition (see item 3)
Why it mattersWhy oral often fails in lung disease: people with severe respiratory disease may have trouble eating enough because of dyspnea during food preparation and eating, early satiety, gastroesophageal reflux, bloating from air swallowing, nausea and vomiting. Bronchodilators, anticholinergics, corticosteroids, antibiotics and mucolytics can cause or worsen GI discomfort. Reflux may also come from lung hyperinflation changing the stomach's position or raised abdominal pressure from coughing. The rule: if oral intake still falls short despite high-calorie, high-protein supplements and snacks, enteral delivery by feeding tube should be considered. This should also be the method of choice to feed patients requiring ventilatory support. The Principles of MV deck agrees: ventilated patients often have increased metabolic demands, difficulty eating, and the need for enteral nutrition.

Keeping the oral route working in chronic lung disease (Hess Box 11-1):

  • choose high-calorie, nutrient-dense foods
  • eat small frequent meals rather than fewer large ones
  • drink liquids between meals, not with them
  • add fats for calories and dry milk powder for protein
  • avoid gas-forming foods (cabbage, onions, beans)
  • use medical nutritional products (e.g., Ensure, Boost) if diet alone is inadequate
SourceHess Ch 11 — Nutrition Delivery; Respiratory Recap "Routes of Nutrition Delivery"; Box 11-1 · Hess glossary — enteral nutrition, parenteral nutrition · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 — Principles of Drug Action, slide 4 (notes) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 31 (notes)

2The Enteral Routes and How They Differ

What decides the enteral route (Hess Ch 11): ease and availability of enteral access, the patient's risk of aspiration, tolerance of feedings, clinical condition, and how long feeding is likely to be needed.

Enteral route Placement / description Duration When used / advantages Problems / cautions
Nasogastric (NG) or orogastric (OG) tube Through the nose or mouth into the stomach; easy to place at the bedside Short-term (less than 3 to 4 weeks) Often used for short-term feeding. The tube is also needed for medication administration May be contraindicated in severe reflux, delayed gastric emptying/gastroparesis, or high aspiration risk. Hess adds (Ch 39, postoperative care): an NG tube "can impair the cough reflex and increases the risk of aspiration pneumonia and atelectasis." That chapter advises using NG tubes only when indicated for intestinal decompression
Dobhoff tube (a small-bore NG feeding tube) Small-bore, flexible nasogastric feeding tube, inside diameter typically 4 mm. Inserted over a guidewire (stylet), which is removed after correct placement is confirmed. A weighted end, moved by peristalsis, carries it into the stomach or beyond Not stated separately (it is a nasogastric tube) Smaller and more flexible, so usually more comfortable than other NG tubes Not stated separately
Post-pyloric (small-bowel / "enteric") tube Past the stomach (post-pyloric) into the small intestine: duodenal or jejunal feeding. Optimal placement is past the ligament of Treitz (fourth portion of the duodenum) Short-term tube feeding Patients at high risk of aspiration. Allows uninterrupted duodenal or jejunal feeding in gastric dysmotility and large gastric residual volumes. Feeds run into the small-bowel tube while a larger gastric tube decompresses the stomach and drains gastric secretions. The more distal the feeding, presumably the less likely aspiration —
Percutaneous tubes: gastrostomy (e.g., PEG) or into the small intestine Placed through the skin into the stomach or small intestine by surgical, endoscopic, radiologic or laparoscopic technique. PEG is placed endoscopically in a sedated patient. Insertion takes about 20 minutes. The tube is held in the stomach by a balloon or a retention dome Long-term Suitable for long-term use and can be replaced without another endoscopic procedure. Useful when the patient has difficulty swallowing because of neurologic or anatomic disorders and to avoid the risk of aspiration pneumonia. Generally more comfortable than NG, OG or enteric tubes Requires a placement procedure (PEG: patient sedated, endoscope passed through the mouth into the stomach)

The key differences to state on the exam:

  • Nose/mouth tubes are short-term (< 3–4 weeks); percutaneous tubes are long-term.
  • Gastric tubes feed the stomach; post-pyloric tubes feed the duodenum/jejunum. Post-pyloric tubes are chosen when aspiration risk is high, or when gastric emptying is poor.
  • Comfort runs PEG > NG/OG/enteric, and Dobhoff > other NG tubes.

Hess Ch 11 never uses the word "jejunostomy". It names only tubes placed "through the skin into the stomach or small intestine". A gastrostomy–jejunostomy (G/J) tube does appear in Hess's list of home supplies for a patient ventilated at home (Table 25-5). Hess Ch 46 (lung transplant) also uses a gastrojejunal tube (GJT) for both nutrition and medication administration when severe dysphagia follows surgery, and places one perioperatively in patients at high risk for postoperative dysphagia.

Confirming tube position (RT-relevant): the tip and side hole of a feeding tube should be beyond the gastroesophageal junction. An NG tube can be misplaced into the airway, so radiographic confirmation of feeding tube position is essential before use (Hess Ch 7). The 203 deck makes the same point: "an improperly positioned tube can enter the airway." Hess adds (Ch 2): a colorimetric CO₂ detector can detect accidental tracheal placement of a nasogastric tube; here a color change means incorrect placement in the trachea. With capnography, a PETCO₂ near zero suggests that the gastric tube is not in the trachea.

Formulas: general-purpose enteral formulas are cost-effective and well tolerated, and supply 1 to 1.5 calories per milliliter. A dietician's guidance is needed to choose the right one for each patient.

SourceHess Ch 11 — Nutrition Delivery · Hess Ch 39 — Nutrition · Hess Ch 7 — feeding tube position (Figure 7-19 text) · Hess Ch 2 — colorimetric CO₂ detection; capnometry · Hess Ch 25 — Table 25-5 · Hess Ch 46 — nutritional support after lung transplant · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 59 (notes)

3Parenteral Nutrition: When It Is Used, Peripheral vs Central Access, and Its Risks

When: "Intravenous delivery of substrate may be necessary when the GI tract is not functioning or if stimulation of the gastrointestinal or pancreatic systems would worsen the patient's condition." Hess lists the indications as severe pancreatitis, gastrointestinal fistulas, short bowel syndrome, prolonged ileus, and some cancers. It should not be started if support is expected to last less than 7 days. The Principles of MV deck's list for total parenteral nutrition (TPN) is: GI tract doesn't work, bowel obstruction, severe pancreatitis, major GI surgery, or severe malnutrition.

Peripheral vs central: Hess: "Placement of a central or peripheral venous catheter is required for the infusion of nutrients into the bloodstream; central venous access usually is preferred." The Principles of MV deck defines TPN as nutrition delivered directly into the bloodstream through a central venous catheter, with no GI tract involved. The RCP 203 deck explains the two kinds of line:

  • A peripheral line is a short catheter in a small vein (hand or forearm) for short-term use, giving fluids, electrolytes, or non-irritating medications for a short period.
  • A central line (CVC) is a longer, more flexible tube in a large vein that leads directly to the heart, placed in the jugular, subclavian, arm (PICC) or femoral vein and designed for more long-term use. Its listed uses include "IV nutrition" and medications that would irritate smaller veins.

Risks of bypassing the gut: infusing nutrients into the central circulation leaves the GI tract unstimulated. That can lead to gut atrophy, mucosal compromise, and weakening of the gut barrier, which may increase the risk of bacterial contamination. This is the flip side of Hess's Key Point that enteral delivery is preferred because it carries fewer risks of infection, may have a protective effect on the GI mucosa, and is less costly. On the RT side, dextrose-based TPN (↑ O₂ usage, ↑ CO₂ production) and fat-based TPN (↑ calories, ↓ CO₂ production) behave differently (item 6). Respiratory acidosis during mechanical ventilation has been reported within hours after starting TPN (overfeeding). Hess adds (Ch 5, Box 5-9): parenteral nutrition with carbohydrate overload shifts phosphate into cells and is a cause of hypophosphatemia.

SourceHess Ch 11 — Nutrition Delivery; Key Points · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 47 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slide 30 (notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 59 (notes) · Hess Ch 5 — Box 5-9
Gap · still not coveredthe specific differences between peripheral parenteral nutrition (PPN) and central TPN (allowable dextrose/osmolarity, how long PPN can run), and the named complications of TPN (catheter infection/sepsis, hyperglycemia, liver dysfunction). The sources say only "central or peripheral; central usually preferred", plus gut atrophy, infection risk, CO₂/respiratory acidosis from overfeeding and hypophosphatemia. Look in a nutrition-support text (A.S.P.E.N.).

4Enteral vs Parenteral: The Differences, and When to Start

Enteral (EN) Parenteral (PN)
Uses the gut? Yes; requires a functioning GI tract No; bypasses the GI system
Gut effects Nutrients absorbed via the portal system to the liver, which may increase absorption and enhance immune competence. Nutrients in the gut prevent intestinal atrophy, maintain the absorptive capacity of the GI mucosa, and preserve normal gut flora and gastric pH. They stimulate secretory IgA, which helps prevent bacterial translocation and gut sepsis Gut unstimulated → gut atrophy, mucosal compromise, weakened gut barrier → ↑ risk of bacterial contamination
Safety and cost "Safer than parenteral nutrition and considerably less expensive", though not entirely devoid of risk More risk of infection, more costly (the reverse of Hess's comparison)
Access NG/OG, Dobhoff, post-pyloric, PEG/percutaneous tubes Central (preferred) or peripheral venous catheter
Typical use Method of choice for patients on ventilatory support; always considered when the GI tract works GI tract not functioning, pancreatitis, fistulas, short bowel, prolonged ileus, some cancers; not if < 7 days expected

Hess Key Point: "Enteral delivery of nutrients is preferred to parenteral nutrition because it carries fewer risks of infection, may have a protective effect on the gastrointestinal mucosa, and is less costly." Hess adds (Ch 39): EN improves postoperative morbidity and mortality better than parenteral nutrition, and trauma patients receiving EN for 5 to 7 days had a lower rate of sepsis than those given IV fluids. The Burn deck lists "early enteral nutrition to maintain GI function" as part of burn support. Major burns can impair GI function and gut integrity, and early EN helps maintain the intestinal mucosa and reduces complications.

Critical-care rules (SCCM/A.S.P.E.N., Hess CPG 11-1):

  • Start early enteral nutrition within 24–48 hours in a critically ill patient unable to maintain volitional intake.
  • Use EN over PN.
  • In most critically ill patients it is acceptable to start EN in the stomach.
  • Withhold EN during hemodynamic compromise/instability until the patient is fully resuscitated and/or stable.
  • High-risk/severely malnourished patients are advanced toward the goal over 24–48 hours while monitoring for refeeding syndrome, aiming to provide > 80% of goal energy and protein within 48–72 hours.
  • Protein: 1.2–2.0 g/kg actual body weight per day, possibly higher in burn or multiple trauma.
  • Energy: if indirect calorimetry is unavailable, use a predictive equation or 25–30 kcal/kg/d.
  • Hess's own chapter text gives different figures: 1.0 to 2.0 g/kg of protein for most patients without renal or liver disease (up to 1.5 to 2.5 g/kg/day if catabolism is high) and 25 to 35 kcal/kg for energy. Use the CPG figures, since they are the guideline the chapter presents for critically ill adults.
  • Trophic or full EN are both appropriate in ARDS and in patients expected to need ≥ 72 hours of mechanical ventilation.
  • Minimize NPO orders before tests and procedures.
  • Do not automatically stop EN for diarrhea.

Glucose during nutrition support: keep blood glucose at 140–180 mg/dL, and treat < 70 mg/dL as hypoglycemia. Tight glucose control is not supported because it carries a higher risk of hypoglycemia.

SourceHess Ch 11 — Nutrition Delivery; Nutritional Support in Mechanically Ventilated Patients; CPG 11-1; Key Points; Equations Versus Measurements; Protein Requirements · Hess Ch 39 — Nutrition · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 20 (and notes)

5Aspiration Precautions During Tube Feeding (Head of Bed and More)

Head of bed: Hess CPG 11-1: "In all intubated ICU patients receiving enteral nutrition, the head of the bed should be elevated 30 to 45 degrees." Hess Ch 36's ventilator bundle says to elevate the head of the bed up to 45 degrees, especially during enteral feeding, and Box 36-7 lists "Elevation of the head of the bed to 30–45 degrees." The RCP 105 Procedures deck (VAP prevention) agrees: head of bed 30–45° under "Prevent Aspiration". Its notes say studies disagree on the exact angle, and elevation should be done unless contraindicated (e.g., post neurosurgical, severe hypotension). Its list of VAP-prevention strategies includes "use of oral feeding tube" and aspiration precaution.

Other precautions in the guideline (CPG 11-1):

  • Assess every EN patient for aspiration risk and take proactive steps.
  • Divert feeding lower in the GI tract (post-pyloric) in patients at high risk for aspiration or intolerant of gastric feeding.
  • Switch from bolus to continuous infusion for high-risk or intolerant patients.
  • Consider prokinetic agents (metoclopramide or erythromycin) when aspiration risk is high.
  • Do not use blue food coloring (or any coloring agent), and do not use glucose oxidase strips, as markers of aspiration.
  • Gastric residual volumes should not be used routinely. Where they are still used, avoid holding EN for GRVs < 500 mL without other signs of intolerance.
Why it mattersWhy it matters for the RT: Hess Ch 36 lists enteral feeding among the patient risk factors for VAP. Nasogastric/orogastric tubes are indwelling devices that allow for biofilm formation. Before postural drainage, tube feedings should cease 1 hour before and during therapy. For patients who eat, do the therapy before meals or more than 1 hour after meals (Hess Ch 16).
SourceHess Ch 11 — CPG 11-1 (Monitoring Tolerance and Adequacy of Enteral Nutrition) · Hess Ch 36 — Pathogenesis of VAP; Ventilator-Care Bundles; Box 36-7 · Hess Ch 16 — postural drainage precautions · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 46 (notes), 47 (notes)

6Overfeeding, CO₂ Production and the Respiratory Quotient high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Why it mattersWhy the RT cares: the waste products of metabolism are CO₂ and nonvolatile acids. Overfeeding carbohydrates causes lipogenesis (fat storage) → excessive CO₂ → higher ventilatory needs. Amino acids may increase respiratory drive. "In a patient with limited ventilator reserves, overfeeding may lead to respiratory failure or prolonged mechanical ventilation." Hess Key Point: "Caloric overfeeding should be avoided in patients requiring mechanical ventilation because it may result in hypercapnia and a prolonged ventilatory period." The decks say the same:
  • Overfeeding increases CO₂ production and work of breathing (Principles of MV).
  • Overfeeding may increase work of breathing due to lipogenesis and increased CO₂ production (Management of MV).
  • High caloric enteric nutrition increases O₂ consumption, CO₂ production and RQ, which can induce respiratory distress during weaning in patients with limited pulmonary reserve (Management of MV notes).
  • The COPD chain: carbohydrates ↑ → CO₂ production ↑ → ventilation requirement ↑ → weaning difficulty ↑ (Principles of MV notes).

Respiratory quotient (RQ or R) = VCO₂ / VO₂, the ratio of CO₂ produced to O₂ consumed. A measured R should lie in the physiologic range 0.67–1.3 (Hess). Critically ill patients have an R of approximately 0.90.

Substrate RQ (deck) RQ (Hess Table 11-4) Caloric value, kcal/g (Hess Table 11-4)
Carbohydrate ≈ 1.0 1.0 4.1
Mixed — 0.90 —
Protein ≈ 0.8 0.80 4.1
Fat ≈ 0.7 0.71 9.3
Alcohol — 0.67 7.1

"Higher RQ = More CO₂ produced." That is why excessive carbohydrate (dextrose) feeding is the problem in ventilator patients. The Management of MV deck uses different calorie figures than Hess Table 11-4: each gram of hydrous dextrose gives 3.4 kcal and each gram of fat emulsion gives 9.1 kcal. On that basis, a fat-based diet provides more kcal, produces less CO₂ (less work of breathing), and should contain at least 40% total fat kilocalories. Hess adds: pulmonary-disease formulas give 40% to 55% of calories from fat and less than 40% from carbohydrate, at 1.5 kcal/mL. Hess also notes that if overall calories are not excessive, VCO₂ is more affected by total calories than by the percentage of carbohydrate calories, and that these pricier formulas remain controversial.

Caution · sources differConflict to know: the decks favor fat-based nutrition for ventilated and CO₂-retaining patients ("Why RTs Like Fat-Based Nutrition… Easier Weaning"). The guideline in Hess (CPG 11-1) suggests that specialty high-fat/low-carbohydrate formulations designed to manipulate the RQ and reduce CO₂ production NOT be used in ICU patients with acute respiratory failure. For acute respiratory failure it suggests fluid-restricted, energy-dense formulas instead (especially with volume overload). On this exam, the concept the decks test is carbohydrate ↑ = RQ ↑ = CO₂ ↑, and avoid overfeeding. If a question asks what the current ICU guideline recommends, answer with the CPG.
SourceHess Ch 11 — Minute Ventilation; Indirect Calorimetry; Caloric Equivalent Method, Table 11-4; Nutrition Delivery; CPG 11-1 (Pulmonary Failure); Key Points · Hess glossary — respiratory quotient · Hess Ch 20 — liberation (avoid overfeeding) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 46, 47 (and notes) · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 59, 60 (and notes)

7Undernutrition, Refeeding Syndrome and Phosphate: Why Feeding Affects Weaning high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Undernutrition weakens the pump. In prolonged semistarvation and hypercatabolism, the diaphragm and other respiratory muscles are used as fuel. Protein-calorie malnutrition (PCM) hits fast-twitch fibers hardest. It shows up as a decline in maximum inspiratory and expiratory pressures, vital capacity and voluntary ventilation. Hypophosphatemia impairs expiratory-muscle contractility and endurance. Magnesium deficiency is linked to muscle weakness and may hinder liberation from mechanical ventilation. PCM also impairs cell-mediated immunity and surfactant (Hess Ch 11). It lowers albumin, and low albumin raises the risk of pulmonary edema. The decks agree: the Management of MV deck lists diaphragm glycogen/protein depletion, ↑ infection risk (↓ cell-mediated immunity), and interstitial/pulmonary edema from hypoalbuminemia. The Principles of MV notes warn that a malnourished patient may fail spontaneous breathing trials because "the diaphragm simply lacks the strength to sustain breathing." Hess Ch 11's nutritional-risk flags are weight loss of 10% or more of usual body weight over 6 months and body weight less than 80% of ideal.

Stress raises needs. The hypercatabolic phase of critical illness can last 7 to 10 days, and caloric needs may rise by as much as 100%. Burn patients may require 1.5–2 times, sometimes 2–3 times, the calories of a normal patient (Principles of MV notes).

Refeeding syndrome is "the metabolic and clinical disturbances that occur after the reinstitution of nutrition to patients who are malnourished or starved." It involves severe electrolyte imbalance, vitamin deficiency and hyperglycemia, and can cause arrhythmias, CHF, pulmonary edema, respiratory failure, diaphragm and intercostal weakness, and ↑ VCO₂. Together these prolong mechanical ventilation and make weaning more difficult. Phosphate (Management of MV deck): the deck states hypophosphatemia is present when serum phosphate < 1 mg/dL. Hess Ch 5 gives the adult normal serum PO₄ as 2.7–4.5 mg/dL, so by Hess any value below 2.7 mg/dL is already low. If a question quotes the deck's cutoff, answer < 1 mg/dL; know Hess's normal range. Hypophosphatemia occurs in 30% of ICU patients, 65% to 80% of patients with sepsis, 75% of major trauma, and 21.5% of COPD. It decreases ATP, and severe hypophosphatemia leads to confusion, muscle weakness, CHF, and respiratory failure. CPG 11-1 adds that in acute respiratory failure, serum phosphate should be monitored closely and replaced when needed.

SourceHess Ch 11 — Introduction; Muscle Weakness; Surfactant Production; Immune Function; Hypoalbuminemia; Stress Response to Critical Illness; Refeeding Syndrome; Nutrition Assessment; CPG 11-1 · Hess Ch 5 — Phosphorus · RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 57, 63 · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 46 (notes), 47 (notes)
08

Pulmonary Rehabilitation

7 items · Adequate arterial oxygenation limits · diaphragmatic vs pursed-lip vs thoracic breathing · hyperventilation & hypoventilation

1Adequate Arterial Oxygenation Limits2Diaphragmatic Breathing3Pursed-Lip Breathing4Thoracic Breathing5Hyperventilation vs Hypoventilation6The Five Side by Side (the "know the difference" table)7Exercise Prescription

The breathing-pattern answers lead with the instructor's Pulm Rehab PowerPoint (RCP 202 Lssn 6) and the matching RCP 203 Ch 10 breathing-exercise slides. That deck gives no O₂ numbers and does not cover hyperventilation or hypoventilation, so those answers come from Hess (Ch 24, 50) and the other vault decks (RCP 203, 103, 104).

1Adequate Arterial Oxygenation Limits

The Pulm Rehab deck gives no PaO₂ or SpO₂ number. It names oxygen therapy as a program component that treats hypoxemia when indicated, notes that patients may develop hypoxemia, particularly during exercise, adding to exercise intolerance, and includes oxygen when prescribed in patient education. The number comes from Hess Ch 24, and the RCP 203 deck uses the same pair.

The number: PaO₂ > 55 mm Hg and SpO₂ > 88%. Hess Ch 24 says all exercise training should be performed under conditions of adequate arterial oxygenation (PaO₂ > 55 mm Hg, SpO₂ > 88%). This ensures patient safety and lets the hypoxemic patient exercise longer at a higher intensity, which enhances the benefit. What to do when the patient cannot stay above it:

  • If the initial assessment shows low resting oxygenation or significant desaturation with exertion → give supplemental O₂ to maintain adequate saturation.
  • Severe exercise hypoxemia may require oxygen reservoir systems.
  • When adequate oxygenation cannot be maintained, either reduce the exercise intensity or have the patient stop exercising until oxygenation returns to an adequate level.

Hess also notes that exercise hypoxemia is not a reason to exclude a patient: supplemental O₂ and proper monitoring (e.g., pulse oximetry) let these patients participate fully. A symptom-limited maximal exercise assessment on entry detects exercise desaturation in about one-third of patients. A second O₂ use: patients who desaturate but stay above critical levels (PaO₂ > 60 mm Hg or SpO₂ > 90%) gain little in cardiac function or O₂ delivery from O₂. It may still reduce carotid-body output, reducing dyspnea and allowing training at a higher level.

The deck version of the same decision point (RCP 203, O₂ titration with exercise): evaluation uses SpO₂ 88% or PaO₂ 55 torr as the decision point at peak activity. SpO₂ ≥ 88% (PaO₂ ≥ 55 torr) → the patient does not need additional O₂; terminate the test. ↓ SpO₂ ≥ 2% or SpO₂ < 88% (PaO₂ < 55 torr) → ↑ O₂ by 1 L/min until SpO₂ ≥ 88%. Indications are suspected exercise desaturation (dyspnea on exertion, ↓ DLCO and/or low resting PaO₂) and optimizing O₂ for documented exertional desaturation. There is a caution against testing patients with resting SpO₂ < 85% on room air. Related decks: declines > 4–5% during exercise or sleep are abnormal. The 88% / 55 pair is also the home-O₂ (LTOT) qualifying level in COPD (SaO₂ ≤ 88% or PaO₂ ≤ 55 torr on RA).

Boundary note: Hess writes the rehab limit with ">" (> 88%, > 55). The RCP 203 deck treats exactly 88% / 55 as adequate (≥). The numbers to memorize are the same: 88% and 55 mm Hg.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 6 — Pulmonary Rehab, slides 4 (and notes), 5 (notes), 18 (notes) · Hess Ch 24 — Patient Assessment; Exercise; Patient Selection (exercise hypoxemia) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 42, 66 · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 30 · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 45

2Diaphragmatic Breathing

Deck answer (RCP 202 Lssn 6, slide 12 and notes): breathing retraining = pursed-lip breathing and diaphragmatic breathing, which can help improve breathing efficiency and reduce dyspnea. Diaphragmatic breathing ("belly breathing") encourages more efficient use of the diaphragm and less reliance on accessory muscles and may help some patients control their breathing and reduce dyspnea. How it works: the diaphragm contracts and moves downward, letting the lungs expand more fully, so the abdomen (belly) expands during inhalation and contracts during exhalation. It encourages deeper breaths that reach the lower regions of the lungs, where oxygen exchange is more efficient. It activates the parasympathetic nervous system ("rest and digest"), promoting relaxation by lowering heart rate and blood pressure. What you see: the belly visibly moves outward during inhalation, with minimal chest and shoulder movement. The deck's one-liner: "Deep, efficient, and promotes relaxation by engaging the diaphragm."

How to teach it (RCP 203 Lssn 6b, slides 27–28): diaphragmatic breathing is one of the two inspiratory breathing exercises (the other is lateral costal breathing, item 4). Both are meant to promote effective use of the diaphragm, with less emphasis on the accessory muscles, and improve the efficiency of ventilation by increasing VT and decreasing RR. Steps: semi-Fowler's position, forearms relaxed, knees bent → your hand on the upper abdomen, below the xiphoid → the patient inhales slowly through the nose and "pushes out" against your hand → give progressive resistance to the abdominal movement until end inspiration.

Hess adds: the diaphragm is the major muscle of respiration and provides the primary force for the work of breathing. During inspiration it contracts and flattens; exhalation is elastic recoil of the lungs plus relaxation of the diaphragm. It moves about 1.5 cm in normal tidal breathing and as much as 6 to 10 cm with high work of breathing, and is supplied by the phrenic nerve (C3–C5). Hess's glossary gives the same pairing as the deck: breathing retraining is "a component of pulmonary rehabilitation, consisting of pursed-lip breathing and diaphragmatic breathing." Hess Ch 24 adds paced breathing: all three are "commonly used to reduce shortness of breath and improve gas exchange." Hess's breathing control (= relaxed diaphragmatic breathing) is gentle breathing with the lower chest, upper chest and shoulders relaxed, at normal tidal volume and rate; the patient feels a swelling around the waist on inspiration, which subsides on expiration. It is the default phase between the active maneuvers of the Active Cycle of Breathing (ACB). The RCP 104 deck lists ACB as breathing control (relaxed diaphragmatic breathing) + thoracic expansion control (deep breaths) + forced expiration technique (huff).

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 6 — Pulmonary Rehab, slide 12 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slides 27, 28 · Hess Ch 49 — Respiratory Muscles · Hess Glossary — "breathing retraining" · Hess Ch 24 — Education · Hess Ch 16 — Active Cycle of Breathing; Box 16-2 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slide 55

3Pursed-Lip Breathing

Deck answer (RCP 202 Lssn 6, slide 12 notes): pursed-lip breathing (PLB) is particularly useful in COPD: it prolongs exhalation, helps reduce dynamic airway collapse/air trapping, and can reduce the sensation of dyspnea. Technique: the patient inhales slowly through the nose and exhales gently through lips held as if whistling or blowing out a candle. Timing to memorize: inhale through the nose for about 2 seconds ("smell the roses"), then exhale slowly through pursed lips for about 4 seconds (twice as long as inhalation). Why it helps: the gentle back-pressure from exhaling through pursed lips prevents small airways from collapsing too quickly; slowing exhalation lets more CO₂ leave the lungs; and it gives the patient a sense of control and decreases the work of breathing. Who: commonly taught to patients with COPD or emphysema, but useful for anyone experiencing dyspnea.

Timing conflict between decks: the RCP 203 Ch 10 deck (slide 27 notes) says patients should aim for an expiratory time at least two to three times longer than inspiration. It adds that the back-pressure lessens air trapping and prolongs expiratory time, thereby decreasing the rate of breathing. For this RCP 202 exam use the Pulm Rehab deck: about 2 s in, about 4 s out (exhale twice as long). Both decks put exhalation at about twice inhalation or longer (Rehab: about 2 s in / 4 s out; Ch 10: at least 2–3 times). Hess gives no timing.

Other decks: RCP 104: resistance at the mouth during pursed-lip exhalation transmits back pressure to splint the airways open, preventing compression and premature closure. RCP 103 COPD: emphysema patients (the "Pink Puffer") adopt it to compensate for airway collapse during exhalation; the back-pressure keeps the airways open longer and reduces air trapping. RCP 103 Clinical Data: pursed-lip breathing and the tripod position are compensatory behaviors seen in obstructive lung disease. Hess adds: those with emphysema commonly purse their lips during the expiratory phase to maintain airway patency and better control expiratory flow. By using pursed-lip breathing, patients may be able to maintain adequate oxygenation without necessitating supplemental oxygen. It is also a nonpharmacologic intervention for dyspnea.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 6 — Pulmonary Rehab, slide 12 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slide 27 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slide 59 · RCP 103 COPD PP, slides 6 (and notes), 12 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 26 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Nasal Flaring and Pursed-Lip Breathing · Hess Ch 24 — Education · Hess Ch 47 — dyspnea management

4Thoracic Breathing

Deck answer (RCP 202 Lssn 6, slide 12 notes; repeated almost word for word in RCP 203 Lssn 6b, slide 27 notes, which leaves out the "rest and digest" phrase): thoracic breathing = chest breathing.
