The main difference between diaphragmatic (belly) breathing and thoracic (chest) breathing lies in the muscles engaged and the depth of breathing. In chest breathing the intercostal muscles between the ribs primarily engage. Air enters the upper parts of the lungs, the chest and shoulders rise, the diaphragm is less active, and the belly remains relatively still.

Deck point Thoracic (chest) breathing Diaphragmatic (belly) breathing
Muscles Intercostals primarily; diaphragm less active Diaphragm contracts and moves downward
Depth / efficiency Shallower; less air reaches the lower lungs → faster, less efficient O₂ exchange Deeper; air reaches the lower lungs, where O₂ exchange is more efficient
Nervous system Sympathetic ("fight-or-flight"), associated with stress and anxiety; quicker, shallower breaths Parasympathetic ("rest and digest"); lowers heart rate and blood pressure
What you see Chest and shoulders rise, tension in the upper body; linked with poor posture and increased muscle tension Belly moves outward on inhalation, minimal chest and shoulder movement; relaxed posture
Deck one-liner "Shallow, less efficient, and associated with stress and the use of chest muscles." "Deep, efficient, and promotes relaxation by engaging the diaphragm."

Related terms that are not the same thing:

  • (a) Thoracic expansion exercises. Hess adds: these are the deep-breath part of the ACB (the RCP 104 deck's "thoracic expansion control (deep breaths)"): large breaths with active inspiration (involving both the diaphragm and rib cage musculature) and relaxed expiration. They increase flow through the small airways and collateral ventilation channels to help mobilize secretions, and are limited to three or four deep breaths to avoid fatigue and hyperventilation. They are deep therapeutic breaths, the opposite of the deck's shallow chest breathing.
  • (b) Lateral costal breathing (RCP 203 Lssn 6b, slide 27): an inspiratory exercise similar to diaphragmatic breathing, but with the resistance applied by both hands "cupping" the lower rib edges; the patient "breathes around the waist" and pushes out against your hands. It is a good alternative to the diaphragmatic method, especially for patients who have undergone abdominal surgery.
  • (c) Accessory-muscle breathing. Hess adds: the accessory muscles of inspiration are the scalene, sternocleidomastoid, pectoralis major, trapezius, and external intercostals (the external intercostals lift the ribs). They play a relatively minor role in normal respiration and become prominent during exercise or respiratory distress. Use of accessory muscles implies an increased work of breathing or diaphragm weakness; in severe obstructive disease, both inspiratory and expiratory accessory muscles are often active.
  • (d) Deck example (RCP 103 Neuro, slide 50): after spinal cord injury, loss of intercostal and accessory muscle function leaves ventilation increasingly dependent on the diaphragm. This isolated diaphragmatic breathing gives rapid, shallow breathing and paradoxical abdominal breathing (the chest wall moves inward during inspiration), with reduced rib-cage movement and decreased thoracic compliance.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 6 — Pulmonary Rehab, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10 Airway Clearance & Lung Expansion, slide 27 (and notes) · Hess Ch 16 — Active Cycle of Breathing; Box 16-2 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slide 55 · Hess Ch 49 — Respiratory Muscles · Hess Ch 1 — Use of Accessory Muscles · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 50 (and notes)

5Hyperventilation vs Hypoventilation

The Pulm Rehab deck does not cover hyperventilation or hypoventilation; the answer below is from Hess Ch 50 and the RCP 103 ABG deck.

These are defined by ventilation relative to CO₂ removal, i.e. by the PaCO₂, not by how the breathing looks. PaCO₂ is inversely related to alveolar ventilation. Normal arterial PaCO₂ = 35–45 mm Hg.

  • Hyperventilation (Hess): when minute ventilation increases (hyperventilation), CO₂ removal increases and blood CO₂ levels drop. Removal of too much CO₂ (alveolar hyperventilation) will increase pH, causing an alkalosis. RCP 103 ABG: acute alveolar hyperventilation = PaCO₂ < 35 mm Hg with pH above 7.45 (the notes' definition) → acute respiratory alkalosis. Typical causes are pain, anxiety, hypoxemia, or early stages of lung injury. The most common cause is hypoxemia: when PaO₂ drops to around 60 mm Hg, the peripheral chemoreceptors signal the brain to increase ventilation.
  • Hypoventilation (Hess): when minute ventilation decreases (hypoventilation), less CO₂ is removed and blood CO₂ levels rise. A buildup of CO₂ (due to alveolar hypoventilation) causes a decrease in pH (acidosis). Alveolar hypoventilation is a common mechanism for hypercapnia, caused by ↓ V̇E or ↑ V̇D. RCP 103 ABG: acute ventilatory failure = PaCO₂ > 45 mm Hg with pH < 7.35 → acute respiratory acidosis. It occurs when alveolar ventilation is suddenly inadequate. Causes include narcotic overdose, CNS depression from narcotics or sedatives, severe asthma or COPD exacerbations, neuromuscular weakness, airway obstruction, and acute respiratory fatigue.
Exam anchorThe trap: a patient can be breathing fast and still hypoventilating. In acute ventilatory failure, the deck lists apnea, hypoventilation, or ineffective breathing patterns such as rapid, shallow respirations. Judge by the PaCO₂, not the rate. Rehab tie-in (Hess Ch 24): about one-fourth of rehab patients who do not need O₂ have ventilation limitations, shown by exercise ventilation/maximal ventilatory volume > 0.7 or a rising PaCO₂ during exercise.
SourceHess Ch 50 — Introduction; Ventilation; Physiologic Mechanisms of Hypercapnia · RCP 103 ABG Assessments, slides 4, 6 (notes), 11 (notes), 12, 16 (notes) · Hess Ch 24 — Patient Assessment

6The Five Side by Side (the "know the difference" table)

Pattern What it is Phase / muscles Why it matters
Diaphragmatic Taught breathing-retraining technique (belly breathing; Hess: breathing control) Inspiration by the diaphragm (contracts, moves down): belly moves outward on inhalation, minimal chest/shoulder movement Deep, efficient, promotes relaxation (parasympathetic); less reliance on accessory muscles; reduces dyspnea
Pursed-lip Taught breathing-retraining technique; also seen spontaneously in emphysema Expiration through pursed lips: in through the nose about 2 s, out about 4 s (twice as long) Back-pressure keeps small airways open → less dynamic airway collapse/air trapping, ↓ dyspnea, ↓ work of breathing
Thoracic Chest breathing, the deck's contrast to belly breathing Inspiration mainly by the intercostals: chest and shoulders rise, diaphragm less active, belly still Shallow, less efficient, linked with stress (sympathetic); not the same as Hess's thoracic expansion (3–4 deep breaths)
Hyperventilation Ventilation ↑ relative to CO₂ production n/a (defined by PaCO₂) PaCO₂ < 35 → ↑ pH → respiratory alkalosis; most common cause hypoxemia
Hypoventilation Ventilation ↓ relative to CO₂ production n/a (defined by PaCO₂) PaCO₂ > 45 → ↓ pH → respiratory acidosis; can coexist with rapid, shallow breathing
Sourceas items 2–5 (RCP 202 Lssn 6 — Pulmonary Rehab, slide 12 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 6b — Ch 10, slide 27 (notes); Hess Ch 16, 24, 49, 50, 1; RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9; RCP 103 COPD; RCP 103 ABG Assessments)

7Exercise Prescription high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Deck key points (RCP 202 Lssn 6): pulmonary rehab does NOT significantly improve pulmonary function (PFTs). A COPD patient may still have essentially the same FEV₁, but after rehab they can walk farther and perform more activities. Exercise training generally entails the enhancement of upper and lower extremity function, and exposes the patient to a balance of three types of exercise: stretching and flexibility, strengthening, and endurance. The initial load should be of sufficiently low intensity that the patient can accomplish it without undue discomfort; then start at an intensity the patient can tolerate and progressively build. The Modified Borg Scale rates perceived exertion or dyspnea from 0 = nothing at all to 10 = maximal.

Hess adds the numbers (Hess also says the initial load should be of sufficiently low intensity). Training intensity: push toward a training effect, as much as 70% to 80% of predicted maximal heart rate if possible. Even patients with ventilatory or gas-exchange limitations who cannot reach target heart rates benefit from higher rather than lower exercise levels. Minimum endurance exercise: no less than 20 minutes, 3 times per week. A Modified Borg effort between 4 and 6 means the prescription is adequate. Initial loads after a bicycle GXT: 50% to 80% of the maximum watts for the bicycle and 30% to 40% for the arm ergometer. Advancing the load: if the patient completes the session with Borg < 4, heart rate < 80% predicted maximum, and acceptable O₂ saturation, increase the load for 3 to 5 minutes, then return to the previous workload. Walk tests, especially the 6MWT, are the method of choice to assess exercise capacity before and after rehab. Training benefits are specific to the muscles and tasks trained, so walking is emphasized.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 6 — Pulmonary Rehab, slides 3 (notes), 11 (notes), 12, 18 (and notes) · Hess Ch 24 — Exercise; Patient Assessment
09

Trauma

11 items · Pneumothorax diagnosis and treatment · GCS · MAP vs ICP · MRI vs CT · chest tubes · blunt vs penetrating trauma · + postoperative VTE and PE (Lssn 8b)

1Pneumothorax: what it is and how it is diagnosed2Pneumothorax: treatment, by subtype3Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)4Difference between Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) and Intracranial Pressure (ICP)5Difference between MRI and CT scans6Chest tubes, part 1: indications, size, site and insertion7Chest tubes, part 2: drainage system, troubleshooting, removal and transport8Difference between Blunt Force Trauma and Penetrating Trauma9Primary and secondary surveys, and the other lethal chest injuries10Head-injury priorities: lesions, hypoxia, hypotension and hyperventilation11Postoperative VTE and Pulmonary Embolism

1Pneumothorax: what it is and how it is diagnosed

The Trauma deck calls a pneumothorax ("the presence of air in the pleural space, which can cause the lung to collapse") one of the most common and potentially lethal injuries in chest trauma: about 20% of patients who reach a trauma center alive have one. There are three subtypes: simple (closed), open and tension (treatment is in item 2). The chest-tube deck lists these causes: positive pressure ventilation, a ruptured bleb due to emphysema, a bronchopleural fistula, a leaking subpleural cyst, and chest trauma. Iatrogenic causes are thoracentesis, central vein/pulmonary artery catheterization, and bronchoscopy/transbronchial biopsy.

Bedside diagnosis (Trauma deck). The common signs are chest pain and respiratory distress, with diminished or absent breath sounds on the affected side. Subcutaneous emphysema (crepitus) means air is escaping into the tissues, but it is a sign of an air leak and does not by itself confirm a pneumothorax. The classic findings are hyperresonance and tracheal deviation away from the affected side, but tracheal deviation is a late and unreliable finding and is not required to diagnose tension pneumothorax. A CXR can confirm a pneumothorax in a stable patient. The decks give the exam pattern to memorize:

Finding Pneumothorax Source
Trachea Pushed AWAY from the affected side ("Toward loss / Away from pressure"); a late, unreliable sign RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slides 39, 50 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 7b, slides 8, 9 (notes)
Percussion Hyperresonant (very loud, low pitch) RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slide 44; RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 39
Breath sounds Absent (Hess Table 1-3: decreased or absent) RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slide 50 (notes)
Fremitus / expansion Decreased fremitus; unilateral decreased expansion RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 37
Palpation Crepitus (subcutaneous emphysema); with positive-pressure ventilation, think air leak and assess for pneumothorax RCP 203 Lssn 2a, slide 40
Pulse Pulsus paradoxus (systolic drop >10 mm Hg on inspiration) occurs with tension pneumothorax RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 36

The 203 deck's caveat: tracheal shift generally implies a large or tension pneumothorax, not every pneumothorax. The Trauma deck: hypotension with respiratory distress should raise concern for tension; recognize the combination of respiratory distress, unilateral diminished breath sounds and hemodynamic instability rather than waiting for tracheal deviation. On the ventilator, a pneumothorax raises both PIP and plateau pressure (a compliance problem), and the weaning deck lists tension pneumothorax as a sudden decrease in compliance.

Imaging. The Trauma deck: a CXR confirms the diagnosis in a stable patient, and chest CT finds injuries a conventional CXR misses, including small pneumothoraces (item 5). Tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis in an unstable patient: decompress without waiting for tracheal deviation or a CXR. In infants (RCP 203 deck), transillumination is a rapid bedside screen: normally only a ~1 cm halo forms, a chest that broadly "lights up" suggests a pneumothorax, and a negative test does not rule one out, so recommend an immediate chest x-ray if signs persist. Hess adds: plain radiography is used first but is "notorious for missing" pneumothoraces. On the film (Ch 7), the diagnosis rests on the visceral pleural line, with absent vascular markings and increased lucency; an expiratory film makes the line easier to see, and in a supine patient air gives the deep sulcus sign. Ultrasound is more sensitive than chest radiography (absent lung sliding and B lines); the trauma E-FAST exam detects pneumothorax and hemothorax but is limited by subcutaneous emphysema. CT's "occult" pneumothoraces are of questionable clinical relevance.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 8 (and notes), 9 (notes), 10 (and notes), 11, 18 (notes) · Hess Ch 41 — Pneumothorax · Hess Ch 7 — Pneumothorax and Air Leaks; Ultrasonography; radiographic positions (expiratory) · Hess Glossary (deep sulcus sign) · Hess Ch 1 — Table 1-3 · Hess Ch 20 — Physical Assessment · RCP 105 Chest Tubes (Chang Ch 14), slide 3 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slides 31, 39, 40, 44, 50 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slides 36, 37, 39 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis, slide 9 (notes) · RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation, slide 29

2Pneumothorax: treatment, by subtype

Subtype (Trauma deck) What happens Treatment
Simple (closed) Air collects in the pleural space without progressive pressure buildup or hemodynamic compromise; no external wound (air leaks from a tear in the lung). It primarily compromises breathing Small ones may resolve on their own; larger ones may need a chest tube to remove the air. The RCP 105 chest-tube indication list starts with "Large tension pneumothorax (> 25%)"; despite the word "tension", its notes describe this indication simply as air entering the pleural space and collapsing the lung, with the tube removing that air until the leak seals and the lung re-expands
Open ("sucking chest wound") A chest-wall defect lets air move between the atmosphere and the pleural space with breathing. Hess adds: air is preferentially drawn in if the wound is about two-thirds or more of the trachea's diameter Immediately, a sterile, occlusive dressing (often taped on three sides) so air is not sucked in on inspiration. Definitive: a chest tube and surgical closure of the chest wall. Hess adds: place the tube away from the wound, most advocate 28–32 French in the 4th–5th rib interspace anterior to the midaxillary line on an underwater seal with suction, and some advocate 14 French pigtail catheters
Tension (most lethal) Air enters but is "unable to escape" (one-way valve) and builds up under pressure: lung collapse, the mediastinum may shift toward the opposite side, ↓ venous return → ↓ cardiac output → obstructive shock and possible cardiac arrest. It compromises breathing AND circulation Immediate decompression, without waiting for a CXR, when tension is suspected with hemodynamic instability. Needle decompression is a temporary, lifesaving step; definitive treatment is a chest tube on a drainage system. A 14-gauge catheter is commonly taught (size, length and site follow current protocols), and finger thoracostomy may be preferred in some trauma settings. Sites: 2nd ICS, midclavicular line (traditional) or 4th–5th ICS, anterior to the midaxillary region (often preferred in adult trauma protocols because of lower catheter failure rates). The slide 11 notes (Hess's text, also in Hess Ch 41) add: a 14-gauge, 4.5-cm catheter at the 2nd ICS midclavicular line; about 30% of trauma patients have a chest wall > 5 cm thick, so where the anterior approach is improbable or inadequate, consider the 4th–5th ICS at the anterior axillary line

The Trauma deck's ladder: small, stable → observation and oxygen; moderate → needle aspiration; large or symptomatic → chest tube; tension → emergency needle decompression, then chest tube; recurrent or persistent → surgery (VATS or pleurodesis). VATS (video-assisted thoracoscopic surgery) repairs leaks, removes blebs or performs a pleurodesis. Pleurodesis makes the pleural layers stick together, with a chemical irritant or mechanical abrasion; the RCP 103 deck names the sclerosants talc, tetracycline or bleomycin.

The RCP 105 chest-tube deck gives the same needle landmarks and notes the 2nd ICS is a needle landmark, not where definitive chest tubes go. PPV can worsen a pneumothorax or produce tension physiology (Trauma deck). The RCP 105 Initiation deck calls untreated tension pneumothorax the only absolute (physiologic) contraindication to positive-pressure ventilation (↑ intrathoracic pressure → ↓ venous return → severe hypotension → cardiac arrest). Its NBRC scenario is severe dyspnea, hypotension, tracheal deviation and absent breath sounds on one side; the answer is needle decompression or chest tube placement before or at the same time as PPV.

Hess adds (Ch 37, cystic fibrosis patients only): asymptomatic pneumothoraces occupying < 20% of the hemithorax may be observed. Chest tubes to evacuate air "should generally be placed to water-seal rather than to suction", because suction may keep the air leak from sealing. The decks teach that suction can speed re-expansion and remove persistent leaks, although the Trauma deck notes also say suction is not always required and may prolong an air leak in some patients (see the conflicts file). For this exam, use the decks.

Gap · still not coveredhow the "> 25%" pneumothorax size in the RCP 105 chest-tube indication is measured, and what the "Oxygen to Treat Pneumothorax Protocol" contains (the RCP 103 TDP deck only names it). The Trauma deck says only "observation and oxygen" for a small, stable pneumothorax (slide 8 notes), with no flow or FiO₂ — searched the decks and Hess Ch 7, 37, 41.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 8 (and notes), 9 (notes), 11 (and notes), 12 (notes), 13 (notes) · Hess Ch 41 — Pneumothorax, Figures 41-2, 41-3 · Hess Ch 37 — Pneumothorax · RCP 105 Chest Tubes (Chang Ch 14), slides 3, 5 (notes), 6 (notes), 12 (notes) · RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 9 (notes) · RCP 103 Disorders of the Pleura & Chest Wall, slide 23 (notes)

3Glasgow Coma Scale (GCS)

The GCS "provides the most objective assessment of consciousness." It scores eye, verbal and motor responses and adds them up: E + V + M = GCS, with a maximum of 15 (fully alert) and a minimum of 3. The 203 deck calls 3 "deep coma", and Hess Ch 1 says 3 "suggests brain death". The deck's own GCS chart (RCP 105, slide 21) is an image that did not survive text extraction, so the point values below are from Hess Ch 1, Table 1-4. They agree with the deck notes' E4 / V5 / M6 maxima and its worked example.

Score Eye opening (E, max 4) Verbal response (V, max 5) Motor response (M, max 6)
6 — — Obeys commands
5 — Oriented Localizes
4 Spontaneous Confused Withdraws
3 To voice Inappropriate words Flexes (decorticate)
2 To pain Incomprehensible words Extends (decerebrate)
1 None None None

Severity bands: Mild 13–15, Moderate 9–12, Severe (coma) ≤ 8, and the deck says "GCS ≤ 8 = Intubate!" (consider the need for airway protection). Use these. One slide of the RCP 105 deck prints "mild = 13 to 14" and "< 8 = intubate", but its own notes and the 203 deck say 13–15 and ≤ 8, and Hess Ch 41 agrees (minor 15–13, moderate 12–9, severe < 9). The ≤ 8 patient is intubated not necessarily because he can't breathe, but because he cannot protect his airway, cough effectively, handle secretions or reduce aspiration risk. The deck adds that this is a guideline, not an absolute law: intoxication, post-ictal states or heavy sedation can give a temporary GCS < 8. A sedated or chemically paralyzed patient may score low without severe brain injury.

Intubated patients: verbal response cannot be assessed, so the score ranges from 2T to 10T (T = intubated). Motor response is the best predictor of neurologic outcome. The Trauma deck uses the GCS (eye opening, verbal response, motor response) in the D = Disability step of the primary survey, with pupil response and lateralizing signs: protect the airway if the GCS is low, and a declining GCS may indicate worsening brain injury. Hess calls this step "D" (deficits) and adds that you score the highest (best) response in each component.

Worked example (deck): opens eyes to pain = E2; incomprehensible sounds = V2; withdraws from pain = M4; GCS = 2 + 2 + 4 = 8 → severe, so think airway protection.

Gap · still not coveredthe deck's own GCS point chart (RCP 105 Critical Issues slide 21) is an image with no extractable text, so the table above is Hess Ch 1 Table 1-4; the Trauma deck names only the three components (slide 25 notes) — check the slide image in the PowerPoint.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 8 · RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 3 (notes), 25 (and notes) · RCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slides 20, 21 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 61 (notes) · Hess Ch 1 — Level of Consciousness, Table 1-4 · Hess Ch 41 — Deficits

4Difference between Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) and Intracranial Pressure (ICP)

MAP is the systemic driving pressure: "pressure that drives blood into tissues over the entire cardiac cycle". It is "not the arithmetic mean, but the product of CO and SVR." The formulas are MAP = [2(DBP) + SBP] ÷ 3 or MAP = CO × SVR (= HR × SV × SVR). The 203 deck writes it as MAP = Diastolic + ⅓ (Systolic − Diastolic). Worked example (the deck's numbers): BP 110/70 → MAP = 70 + ⅓(110 − 70) = 70 + 13.3 ≈ 83 mm Hg. The deck's floors: MAP 60 mm Hg is needed to maintain tissue perfusion and MAP 70 mm Hg is needed to maintain cerebral perfusion pressure. No deck prints a normal MAP range; Hess Table 3-1 gives 65–105 mm Hg. An arterial catheter gives continuous MAP (the radial artery is the first choice).

ICP is the pressure inside the skull. The Trauma deck: the brain is enveloped by the arachnoid mater, then the dura mater, then the skull. Because the skull is rigid and non-expandable, the Monro-Kellie doctrine says the total volume of its contents (brain, CSF and venous blood) must stay constant: if blood is added after injury, CSF and venous blood must leave or ICP rises. The RCP 105 deck gives a capacity of about 1,500 mL, with the brain 85–90%, blood ~10% and CSF ~3%, so the brain has very low compliance. Hess adds that once compensation is used up, ICP rises exponentially. ICP is monitored in TBI, intracranial hemorrhage and cerebral edema, for example with an intraventricular catheter connected to an external ventricular drain leveled at the eye–ear reference point.

How they connect: CPP = MAP − ICP. MAP pushes blood in and ICP resists it, so ↓ MAP or ↑ ICP → ↓ CPP → ↓ cerebral blood flow → ischemia. Deck example: MAP 90 − ICP 10 = CPP 80. If ICP rises to 30, CPP = 60. The Trauma deck: when ICP rises, MAP reflexively rises to maintain CPP; the goal of medical and surgical maneuvers is CPP > 60 mm Hg, reached by ↓ ICP, ↑ MAP, or both, and if CPP cannot be maintained, cerebral ischemia may result. The RCP 105 decks raise MAP with fluids, then vasopressors such as norepinephrine or dopamine. Normally autoregulation keeps cerebral flow constant across BP changes, but it may be lost with severe hypotension or head trauma (RCP 105); the Trauma deck says that in the injured brain autoregulation is decreased, so the brain is more susceptible to swings in MAP. Hess Ch 53 names the Cushing response: hypertension with bradycardia. Hess Ch 53 also notes that treatments that lower MAP (PPV, diuresis, vasodilators) lower CPP, while treatments that lower ICP (hyperventilation, mannitol) raise CPP.

Number Value Source
Normal ICP 8–12 mm Hg (critical value 20) RCP 105 Critical Issues slide 14; RCP 105 Weaning, slide 61
Normal ICP (other decks/Hess) 5–10 (treat sustained > 20) · 10–15 in supine healthy adults (> 20 for > 5–10 min = abnormally high; > 25 prolonged = poor outcomes) · Hess: < 10 RCP 103 Neuro slide 65; RCP 203 Lssn 1 slide 34; Hess Ch 53
CPP goal in trauma > 60 mm Hg (Trauma deck). Hess agrees: keep > 60 (Ch 41); normal > 80 (Ch 53) RCP 202 Lssn 7b slide 23; Hess Ch 41, Ch 53
CPP (RCP 105 decks) Normal 70–80 mm Hg, < 70 suboptimal; critical threshold ≥ 70, with mortality ↑ ~20% per 10 mm Hg drop and 35% lower mortality when CPP is kept > 70 RCP 105 Critical Issues slides 8, 14; RCP 105 Monitoring in MV slide 53
Hypotension to avoid SBP < 90 mm Hg RCP 105 Monitoring in MV slide 54; Hess Ch 41

What raises ICP (203 deck): ↑ brain volume (edema, tumor), ↑ blood (hemorrhage), restricted venous outflow (CVP > ICP), right heart failure, high intrathoracic pressure (PEEP, recruitment maneuvers), severe hypertension, hypercapnia and hypoxia. The procedures that raise it are Trendelenburg/positioning, neck flexion or hyperextension, intubation, suctioning or coughing, asynchrony, and high PEEP or mean airway pressure. What lowers ICP: head elevation, ↓ CSF, severe hypotension, hyperventilation/hypocapnia.

Caution · sources differWhich number to use: the decks disagree on "normal ICP" (see the conflicts file). Every deck that gives a number (the Trauma deck gives none) agrees that ICP > 20 mm Hg is abnormal and needs treatment (Hess gives only "normal ICP < 10 mm Hg"). For CPP on a trauma or head-injury question, use the Trauma deck's goal > 60 mm Hg (Hess agrees). The RCP 105 decks' 70–80 (≥ 70) is their "normal" and "critical threshold" wording; recognize it if an answer choice uses it (see the conflicts file).
Gap · still not covereda normal MAP range from a deck. The RCP 105 Hemodynamic slide 8 MAP formula/range is an image (only the MAP 60 / 70 floors survive), and the Trauma deck gives CPP = MAP − ICP and CPP > 60 but no MAP range; the 65–105 mm Hg above is Hess Table 3-1.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 23, 26 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21, slides 8, 9 · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4, slide 46 · RCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slides 6, 8 · RCP 105 Critical Issues in MV (Chang Ch 15), slides 6, 8, 9 (notes), 10 (notes), 14 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 53, 54 · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slides 65, 66 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1, slides 34, 35, 36 · Hess Ch 3 — Table 3-1 · Hess Ch 41 — Intracranial Physiology · Hess Ch 53 — Intracranial Pressures and Cerebral Perfusion Pressure

5Difference between MRI and CT scans

No deck sets CT and MRI side by side; the Trauma deck covers CT only. The RCP 103 Clinical Data deck lists each one's key traits, and Hess Ch 7 supplies the contrast.

CT (computed tomography) MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)
How it works X-rays taken from multiple angles, reconstructed into cross-sectional "slices". Gray levels = x-ray attenuation in each voxel, measured in Hounsfield units (air −1000, normal lung −700, water 0, soft tissue −100 to 100, bone 600 to > 2000). Conventional resolution 1–2 mm Nuclear magnetic resonance: in a strong magnetic field, hydrogen nuclei (odd-numbered protons) align and are excited by a radio-frequency pulse. The signal they emit as they relax builds the image. The magnet is 0.5 to 3 Tesla
Radiation Uses ionizing radiation (Hess: "MRI, unlike CT, uses no ionizing radiation"); critics cite cost and radiation exposure No radiation
Contrast IV contrast is needed for aortic dissection and pulmonary embolism. Most thoracic CT does not need it Usually no IV contrast, so it may suit renal dysfunction (contrast can be nephrotoxic)
Best for Detailed 3-D imaging; CTPA for PE. Soft tissue/bone, pleura, parenchyma/interstitial disease, mediastinum, PE, aortic dissection. Guides drainage and checks chest-tube position. Most accurate for pleural effusion Soft tissue and bone marrow pathology, vessels, tumors, complicated pleural and diaphragmatic disease, hilar/mediastinal and cardiac abnormalities. Gives information not available from other modalities
In trauma Trauma deck: chest CT is the best modality for the hemodynamically stable patient with a chest injury; it finds injuries a CXR misses (small pneumothoraces, pulmonary contusions, vascular injuries). Unstable patients should not have lifesaving treatment delayed for CT: CT means transport to the scanner, while a portable CXR is done at the bedside. After a thorough neurologic exam, a head CT is obtained as soon as clinically indicated and the patient can safely undergo imaging. Hess adds: CT with IV contrast is the gold standard for the aorta; non-contrast head CT characterizes most intracranial lesions early, and emergency head CT is the study of choice for moderate/severe brain injury once the patient is hemodynamically normal. In stroke, non-contrast CT tells ischemic from hemorrhagic. In spinal cord injury, CT gives the more definitive bony evaluation Diffuse axonal injury is hard to see on head CT and may require MRI. In stroke, MRI may be used later for further evaluation
Drawbacks / RT safety Radiation, cost, and finding clinically nonsignificant pathology Limited patient monitoring; motion artifacts from heart and breathing. Contraindicated with cardiac pacemakers. Only MRI-compatible equipment (ventilators, monitors, pumps); only aluminum O₂ cylinders, because steel cylinders become projectiles. Monitor exhaled VT on the transport ventilator
Gap · still not covereda deck that contrasts CT and MRI directly (the Trauma deck covers CT only and never mentions MRI), and any CT radiation-dose figure; the table is built mainly from Hess Ch 7 and the RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 46 list.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 18 (and notes), 25 · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 46 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slides 51, 61 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 24 (notes) · Hess Ch 7 — Computed Tomography; Magnetic Resonance Imaging; Key Points · Hess Ch 41 — Injuries Encountered During the Secondary Survey; Aortic Injury; Types of Intracranial Lesions; Diffuse Axonal Injury; Secondary Survey Issues of Head Injury · Hess Glossary (computed tomography; magnetic resonance imaging)

6Chest tubes, part 1: indications, size, site and insertion

Trauma deck: chest drains (also called chest tubes, underwater-seal drains [UWSD], thoracic catheters, tube thoracostomy or intercostal drains) remove air and fluid from the pleural space through a one-way mechanism that lets air/fluid out but keeps outside air/fluid from entering, so the lung expands and negative pressure is restored. General rule (RCP 105): place a chest tube any time air or fluid collects in the pleural space and keeps the lung from fully expanding. The goal is to restore negative intrapleural pressure so the lung re-expands. Indications: large tension pneumothorax (> 25%), hemothorax, pleural effusion, empyema (pus), chylothorax (lymphatic fluid). Only one of these is air. The 103 deck also lists mediastinal and pleural drains. Contraindications: none absolute. The relative ones are infection over the insertion site and coagulopathy or anticoagulant therapy (↑ bleeding). Complications: hemorrhage, hematoma, laceration of the lung or intra-abdominal organs, infection.

Patient Tube size (RCP 105 deck)
Large adults 36–40 Fr
Small adults / teens 28–32 Fr
Children 18 Fr
Neonates 12–14 Fr
Adult pneumothorax (air) 16–20 Fr may be adequate
Blood / thick fluid 36 Fr (range 28–40), a larger tube to reduce clotting

Remember "Air = small tube, Blood/Pus = large tube", like a milkshake versus a water straw. Site: most tubes today go through the "safe triangle": the 4th–5th intercostal space at the (anterior-to-) mid-axillary line, bounded by the lateral edge of the pectoralis major, the anterior edge of the latissimus dorsi, and the apex below the axilla. Hess Ch 41 agrees (4th–5th rib interspace anterior to the midaxillary line). The older textbook site for a pneumothorax chest tube was the 2nd or 3rd ICS anteriorly. The RCP 105 deck's update: the 2nd ICS (midclavicular) is now primarily the needle-decompression landmark, not a definitive chest-tube site. Direction: air rises → tube aimed up toward the apex; fluid falls → tube aimed down and posterior. Judge position by where the tip ends up on CXR, not where it enters the skin. Insert over the TOP of the rib, because the intercostal vein, artery and nerve run in the costal groove along the inferior border. The operative (open) tube thoracostomy (incision → blunt dissection → finger inspection of the pleural space → tube guided in with finger and Kelly clamp) is preferred. It is safer than the trocar method, which has a smaller incision but is more invasive internally: a rigid, sharp trocar goes in without finger confirmation, and it should enter only 1–2 cm. The patient is positioned in Fowler's or semi-Fowler's, and placement is confirmed with a chest x-ray.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 12 (notes) · RCP 105 Chest Tubes (Chang Ch 14), slides 3–9 (notes) · RCP 103 Chest Tubes, slides 2, 4, 5, 6 · Hess Ch 41 — Pneumothorax

7Chest tubes, part 2: drainage system, troubleshooting, removal and transport

The three-chamber system gives each chamber one job (Trauma deck slides 12–14, the same teaching as the RCP 105 deck). (1) The collection chamber collects and measures drainage. (2) The water-seal chamber holds ~2 cm H₂O and works as a one-way valve: air can leave the pleural space but cannot return. (3) The suction-control chamber regulates suction. Its water level, not the wall vacuum, sets the suction: 20 cm of water = −20 cm H₂O. The RCP 105 deck recommends −10 to −20 cm H₂O (−20 is the most common adult setting); the Trauma deck notes give −5 cm H₂O for neonates and −10 to −20 cm H₂O for children. Gentle, continuous bubbling in the suction chamber is normal. Vigorous bubbling only means the wall suction is too high: more noise and evaporation, not more suction. "Wall suction controls the bubbling. Water depth controls the suction." In the older one-chamber system, collected fluid deepens the water seal and increases work of breathing. The two-chamber system fixes this. Suction (three-chamber only) may speed re-expansion, remove persistent air leaks and improve fluid evacuation, although most pneumothoraces eventually resolve with a water seal alone; the Trauma deck adds that suction is not always required and may cause tissue trauma and prolong an air leak in some patients. A dry suction system uses a mechanical regulator instead of water. A Heimlich valve is a one-way valve without a collection chamber, used for transport, ambulation or outpatient pneumothorax.

Normal vs abnormal findings. Tidaling (swing) means the water-seal level rises on spontaneous inspiration and falls on expiration: the system is still communicating with the pleural space. The swing diminishes as the pneumothorax resolves. Unexpected loss of tidaling → blocked or kinked tube: check for kinks or dependent loops, "milk" the tubing, and if that fails, check the patient for pneumothorax and notify the physician. Intermittent bubbling in the water seal when the patient exhales or coughs is an air leak, expected while a pneumothorax is evacuating; having the patient cough can reveal a small leak. The drain grades leak severity 1 (small) to 5 (large). Continuous bubbling = a large leak between the patient and the drain, or a leak in the system: check for disconnection, dislodgement and loose connections. To locate it (Trauma deck slide 15): temporarily clamp near the chest wall. If bubbling stops, the leak is likely from the patient (within the thorax): get a CXR as ordered and notify the physician. If it continues, the leak is between the clamp and the drainage system: move the clamp progressively toward the collection system; where bubbling stops, the leak is in the tubing segment just isolated, and if it never stops, replace the collection system. Never leave a chest tube clamped in a patient with an ongoing air leak: clamping can cause a tension pneumothorax. RTs don't clamp independently unless trained and permitted (RCP 105). Stopped bubbling can mean the lung re-expanded or the tube is obstructed, so assess the patient first.

Emergencies. Drainage holes visible = the tube is partly out: notify the physician and never push it back in. Tube pulled out of the patient = cover with an occlusive (Vaseline) dressing, apply firm pressure (103 deck), and call the physician. Some institutions tape the dressing on three sides as a flutter valve, so follow policy. Disconnected from the drainage unit = clamp, connect a new unit, and keep clamping ≤ 1 min.

Removal criteria: drainage < 100 mL in 24 h, the pneumothorax resolved on CXR, and no air leak; the slide says that if all three are present the tube is usually ready (the Chang text in its notes words it as drainage < 100 mL in 24 h "or" a resolved pneumothorax with no further air leak). Test for a leak with a cough or Valsalva and watch the water seal. Many physicians first trial water seal without suction. At removal the patient does a Valsalva maneuver, petrolatum gauze goes on the site, and a follow-up CXR is taken in 4 hours. Transport (RCP 105): never clamp the tube during routine transport, keep the unit below chest level and upright, and keep the water seal intact. The Trauma deck: if the unit must be lifted above the chest, briefly double-clamp the tubing as close to the patient as possible and unclamp as quickly as possible (see the conflicts file).

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 12 (notes), 13 (notes), 14 (notes), 15 · RCP 105 Chest Tubes (Chang Ch 14), slides 10–16 (notes) · RCP 103 Chest Tubes, slides 8, 9, 11, 12 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides S106–S111

8Difference between Blunt Force Trauma and Penetrating Trauma

The Trauma deck names two major mechanisms of thoracic trauma, blunt and penetrating.

Blunt trauma Penetrating trauma
Definition (Trauma deck) An external force injures structures inside the chest without necessarily breaking or penetrating the skin: a motor vehicle collision can cause pulmonary contusions with no visible chest injury An object enters the chest, potentially damaging the lungs, pleura, heart or major blood vessels
Common causes Motor vehicle crashes (the dominant cause of blunt chest injury), falls, crush injuries Stab wounds and gunshot wounds (handguns), common in urban US trauma centers. Low velocity (stab wounds): damage localized along the object's path. High velocity (shootings, > 1500 ft/s; military assault weapons, hunting rifles): extensive damage from energy transfer and temporary cavitation. Hess adds: fragmenting or tumbling missiles cause multiple perforations
How common (chest) 25% of blunt-trauma deaths are directly due to thoracic injury (the deck notes call this textbook data, not a current universal statistic). Hess adds: blunt thoracic injury = 8% of traumatic hospital admissions < 10% of trauma admissions. Hess adds: < 20% of penetrating admissions, and almost exclusively low-velocity
Typical injuries Rib fractures, pulmonary contusions, pneumothorax. Blunt cardiac injury (myocardial bruising up to coronary, chamber or valve injury; watch for arrhythmias and instability; Hess: screen with ECG + troponin). Aortic injury from rapid deceleration, commonly at the aortic isthmus, just distal to the left subclavian artery. Large diaphragm tears, diagnosed more often on the left (the liver protects the right). Fractures of ribs 1–2, the scapula or sternum signal great force Cardiac tamponade (most commonly penetrating). Esophageal injury (most often penetrating; leakage → mediastinitis). Diaphragm injuries, about 3× more often than from blunt trauma and tiny (bullet- or knife-width). Hess adds: massive hemothorax (most commonly penetrating) and most thoracic aortic injuries, often fatal
Both mechanisms Both can cause pneumothorax, hemothorax, pulmonary contusions and respiratory failure, compromising ventilation, oxygenation and circulation; laryngotracheal injuries follow blunt or penetrating neck trauma Same

Clinical point (deck): a patient can have a significant thoracic injury without obvious external signs, especially after blunt trauma, and may look stable at first before oxygenation worsens as contusions evolve. Hess adds: aggressive crystalloid before bleeding is controlled may be detrimental to the hypotensive patient with penetrating injuries: it may prevent clotting and increase mortality.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 5 (and notes), 6 (notes), 17, 19 (and notes), 21 (and notes) · Hess Ch 41 — Thoracic Trauma; Hemothorax; Blunt Cardiac Injury; Aortic Injury; Key Points

9Primary and secondary surveys, and the other lethal chest injuries high-yield · beyond the listed asks

The primary survey (Trauma deck) uses ABCDE, ordered by rapidity and lethality: Airway with cervical-spine protection, Breathing, Circulation (including hemorrhage control and CPR), Disability (rapid neurological assessment: GCS, pupils, lateralizing signs), Exposure (fully expose while preventing hypothermia). Severe hemorrhage control may come before or alongside the sequence. Hess calls D "deficits"; use the deck's Disability. The secondary survey is a head-to-toe exam with an AMPLE history (Allergies, Medications, especially anticoagulants, Past illnesses/Pregnancy, Last meal, Events/Environment), done once the patient is sufficiently stabilized; if the patient deteriorates, return to the primary survey. The deck's lethal chest injuries: tracheobronchial injury, pneumothorax, hemothorax, pulmonary contusion, blunt cardiac injury, aortic injury, diaphragmatic injury and esophageal injury. Hess adds that the AP chest radiograph is the single most valuable diagnostic study.

Hemorrhagic (hypovolemic) shock is the most common shock state in injured patients; hypotension is often a late finding, and a normal BP or SpO₂ does not rule it out. Deck classes: I < 15%, II 15–30%, III 31–40%, IV > 40% (a teaching framework, not precise thresholds). Hess adds volumes for a 70-kg patient (I < 750 mL; II 750–1500 mL, HR > 100; III 1500–2000 mL, "30% to 40%", hypotension its hallmark; IV ~2000 mL) and the 3:1 fluid rule. Massive hemothorax is > 1500 mL of blood in the pleural space (the notes: "approximately 1,500 mL or more"): chest tube drainage to re-expand the lung, blood product resuscitation and control of bleeding, with urgent surgery for ongoing significant bleeding. It causes hemorrhagic shock; tamponade causes obstructive shock. Hess adds: > 1500 mL immediately, or > 200 mL/h for 2–4 h, points to emergent thoracotomy, and 200–300 mL must be present to show on CXR; the 103 deck confirms hemothorax when pleural-fluid Hct is ≥ 50% of the blood Hct. Cardiac tamponade shows Beck's triad: hypotension, JVD, muffled heart sounds (not always all three). Treatment is emergency pericardial decompression, usually surgical; volume resuscitation gives only temporary support; Hess says treatment "begins with volume resuscitation" (see conflicts). Hess adds: FAST accuracy ≥ 90%. Flail chest is three or more ribs fractured in two or more places (or sternal fracture/costochondral separation; Hess says two or more ribs, see conflicts), with paradoxical motion: in on inspiration, out on exhalation. Not every flail chest needs intubation. Pulmonary contusion may worsen over the first 24–48 h; Hess adds that it often blossoms at 48 h, resolves in about one week, and suggests early elective intubation, PEEP/I:E and minimal crystalloid. Pain from rib and sternal fractures causes hypoventilation, retained secretions and atelectasis; Hess adds that epidural analgesia raises MIP/MEP, VC and peak flow. Lung-protective ventilation is the standard of care: VT ~6 mL/kg PBW, pressure limit < 30 cm H₂O, lowest FiO₂. No mode is proven superior (VC or PC); the slide lists PSV, APRV and HFOV as "highly considered", but its notes say HFOV is not routinely recommended in adults. NIV is not appropriate if the patient cannot protect the airway or is hemodynamically unstable.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 3 (and notes), 4 (and notes), 16 (and notes), 17 (and notes), 19 (and notes), 20 (and notes), 21 (notes), 22 (and notes), 27 (and notes), 29 · Hess Ch 41 — The Primary and Secondary Surveys; Table 41-1 text; Hemothorax; Cardiac Tamponade; Injuries Encountered During the Secondary Survey; Pulmonary Contusion; Chest Wall Injury · RCP 103 Disorders of the Pleura & Chest Wall, slide 12 (notes)

10Head-injury priorities: lesions, hypoxia, hypotension and hyperventilation high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Lesions (Trauma deck): Epidural hematoma sits between the dura and the skull; it is often arterial, can expand rapidly, and may show a classic lucid interval before sudden deterioration (not in every patient). Subdural hematoma sits between the dura and arachnoid, usually from torn bridging veins; it is more common (~30% of severe brain injuries) and may develop more gradually. Subarachnoid blood (under the arachnoid) can induce cerebral vasospasm and worsen ischemia. Diffuse axonal injury comes from shearing and stretching forces (acceleration, deceleration or rotation) and can occur without a large visible hematoma. Hess adds: epidural hematoma is rare (< 1% of brain injuries), biconvex (lens-shaped) on CT, often from the middle meningeal artery; subdural hematoma has a curved CT appearance and the underlying injury is usually worse; DAI has no effective treatment and may need MRI to see. The RCP 105 deck's secondary brain injury concept: the initial injury can't be undone, but brain swelling → ↑ ICP → ↓ CPP afterward often can be prevented.

Primary-survey issues (Trauma deck): the priority is preventing secondary brain injury from hypoxia (in 22% of TBI patients) and hypotension (it reduces cerebral perfusion; correcting hypotension with fluid resuscitation improves neurologic outcome). Hyperventilation lowers PaCO₂ → cerebral vasoconstriction → temporarily ↓ ICP, but it also ↓ cerebral blood flow and can cause ischemia. It is reserved for impending brain herniation in severe TBI, to a PaCO₂ < 25 mm Hg to rapidly reduce ICP (profound hypocapnia can be harmful and is not routine; follow protocol). Otherwise the goal is normocapnia, PaCO₂ ~35–45 mm Hg. The deck calls the process cerebral vasoconstriction, also "therapeutic hyperventilation" or "induced hypocapnia" (formerly "permissive hypocapnia"). The 105 deck says PaCO₂ may be titrated as low as 26 mm Hg in the first 24 h (no added ICP benefit below 26), and its notes give temporary 30–35 for impending herniation; the 103 deck targets PaCO₂ 35–40 with the head of bed ≥ 30° (see the conflicts file). Hess adds: SpO₂ ≤ 90% for a median 12–20 min independently predicted death; Hess targets PaO₂ 80–100 mm Hg (the RCP 105 deck: keep PaO₂ > 60 mm Hg and SaO₂ > 90%); a single SBP < 90 mm Hg is linked to a doubling of mortality, and mortality starts climbing at 110 mm Hg. RT management: avoid hypoxemia and excessive PaCO₂ changes (Trauma deck); sedate and minimize suctioning (RCP 105).

Treatment (Trauma deck): hyperosmolar agents are the backbone of medical therapy. Mannitol shifts water out of the brain into the vessels, but this potent diuretic may worsen hypotension. Hypertonic saline draws water from swollen brain tissue and supports circulating volume rather than causing diuresis (bolus or infusion). Surgery: hematoma evacuation, CSF removal via intraventricular drains, or decompressive craniectomy. A new unilateral fixed, dilated pupil (CN III compression in transtentorial herniation) is a neurosurgical emergency (RCP 105).

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slides 24 (and notes), 25 (and notes), 26 (and notes), 28 (notes), 30 · Hess Ch 41 — Types of Intracranial Lesions; Hypoxia; Hypotension · RCP 105 Critical Issues in MV (Chang Ch 15), slides 15, 16 (notes), 18, 22 (notes) · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 65

11Postoperative VTE and Pulmonary Embolism high-yield · beyond the listed asks

From RCP 202 Lssn 8b — PostOp Respiratory Care (Hess Ch 39), added 25 Oct 2026. It sits here because this section already covers the immobilised, injured, post-surgical patient; postoperative care is not a listed topic in its own right.

  • The most common cause of PE is venous thromboembolism (VTE) — blood clots formed in the leg veins that travel to the lungs and obstruct pulmonary vessels.
  • Deep vein thrombosis in the legs and pelvis occurs as a result of venostasis in surgical patients and from other causes of immobility.
  • Signs and symptoms of acute PE: abrupt onset of cough, pleuritic chest pain, anxiety, tachycardia, hypoxemia.
  • Diagnosis: a V̇/Q̇ scan showing perfusion defects combined with areas of normal ventilation, or CT angiography with IV contrast.

Management, in the order the deck gives it

  • Prophylaxis — prevention of DVT. Intermittent pneumatic compression devices for hospitalized patients, plus early ambulation and leg exercises.
  • Anticoagulation. Therapy is considered therapeutic when the clotting time or partial thromboplastin time (PTT) is 2 to 2.5 times the control. Anticoagulants prevent further clot formation but will not dissolve clot already present. Warfarin and newer agents (e.g. apixaban) are used for patients at ongoing risk.
  • Thrombolytic therapy is what dissolves existing clot — commonly tissue plasminogen activator (tPA) — and the clinician must balance it against bleeding risk in a postoperative patient.
  • Inferior vena cava filter to prevent clots originating in the lower extremities from reaching the lungs; surgical embolectomy is rarely attempted.
Exam anchorThe two numbers are PTT 2 to 2.5 times control and the logic that separates the drugs: anticoagulant = stop new clot; thrombolytic = dissolve existing clot. A question that describes a clot already lodged and asks what will remove it is asking for a thrombolytic, not heparin.
Caution — a name only the deck usesThe deck calls the IVC filter a “bird’s nest filter.” That is a specific device design, and the deck’s own notes treat it as a synonym. Hess says “vena cava filter” and does not use the bird’s-nest name. Recognise both; answer with whichever the stem uses. The deck’s notes also claim PE is the third most common cause of death from cardiovascular disease, after heart attack and stroke — that line is in the speaker notes, not in the Hess text.
SourceHess Ch 39 — Pulmonary Thromboembolic Disease; anticoagulation and PTT (also Hess glossary, “partial thromboplastin time”) · RCP 202 Lssn 8b deck, slides 17, 18 and speaker notes (added 2026-10-25)
10

Interventional Pulmonary Procedures
