1Difference Between Diagnostic and Therapeutic Bronchoscopy2Indications for Bronchoscopy3Contraindications to Bronchoscopy4Difference Between Flexible and Rigid Bronchoscopes5Pharmacologic Agents: Anesthetic and Sedative Medications6Methods of Monitoring During the Procedure7Difference Between COPD, GERD, Emphysema, and Lung Cancer8Difference Between Lavage, Washing, Brushing, and Biopsy9Hess Ch 9 "Key Points" (study guide: "Study the 'Key Points' p.205")10Complications of Bronchoscopy and Their Management11Preparation, Post-Bronchoscopy Care, and Scope Reprocessing

1Difference Between Diagnostic and Therapeutic Bronchoscopy

The Lssn 7a deck (and Hess) calls bronchoscopy the most commonly used invasive procedure in pulmonary medicine, a safe and effective tool that performs both diagnostic and therapeutic procedures; Hess adds that about 500,000 are done each year in the United States.

  • Diagnostic = "concerned with the diagnosis of illness": it identifies the cause of an airway abnormality. Examples: airway inspection, BAL, biopsies, identification of tumors or infections (an ILD diagnosis requires a tissue sample).
  • Therapeutic = it treats: removal of mucus plugs or foreign bodies, control of bleeding, airway dilation and stent placement. It is most commonly used for central airway obstruction of malignant or benign cause, which can be intrinsic (inside the airway), extrinsic (compression from outside) or mixed; an endobronchial tumor may be debulked, while external compression may need dilation and/or stenting.

The RCP 105 deck gives the one-line difference:

  • Diagnostic means "Find out what's wrong", or Look.
  • Therapeutic means "Fix a problem", or Treat.

A diagnostic bronchoscopy "is done to gather additional information or to confirm preliminary diagnosis following history, physical, and other laboratory results." Biopsy and cytology samples are obtained for diagnostic purposes. A therapeutic bronchoscopy treats the airway problem instead of just diagnosing it. The RCP 105 deck's NBRC tip lists both sides:

  • Diagnostic: biopsy, BAL, washing, airway inspection.
  • Therapeutic: remove mucus plugs, remove foreign bodies, control bleeding, relieve airway obstruction.

The RCP 203 deck says the same thing: "Diagnostics relate to finding a suspected disease, while Therapeutics relate to treatment." RCP 105 deck pearl: if a question asks which procedure is both diagnostic and therapeutic, fiberoptic bronchoscopy is "an excellent answer."

Hess adds:

  • Glossary definition. Hess defines therapeutic bronchoscopy as "a bronchoscopic procedure aimed at relieving central airways obstruction."
  • How common the obstruction is. Central airway obstruction affects 20% to 30% of patients with primary lung cancer and 7% to 18% of patients after lung transplantation.
  • Role of the rigid scope. Rigid bronchoscopy gives definitive control of the airway, so general anesthesia can be used. The rigid scope can itself dilate airway stenoses and core out an airway tumor.
  • Therapeutic tools:
    • rapid heat therapy (endobronchial laser, with Nd:YAG the most commonly used; electrocautery; argon plasma coagulation)
    • inflatable balloons or rigid dilators for stenosis
    • delayed-effect therapies (cryotherapy, photodynamic therapy, brachytherapy)
    • airway stents (self-expandable metallic, silicone, and hybrid metal–silicone)
  • FiO₂ during heat therapy. With any heat therapy in the airway, FiO₂ should be reduced to 40% or less so that flammable components in the airway do not ignite.
  • Laser success rate. Laser can relieve as many as 70% of central airway obstructions.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 4 (and notes), 22 (and notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 3 (notes), 5 (notes), 6 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slide 12 (notes) · Hess Ch 9 — Overview of Bronchoscopy; Therapeutic Bronchoscopy; Therapeutic Procedures; Airway Stenting; Glossary

2Indications for Bronchoscopy

Lssn 7a deck (slides 18–21) lists nine indications: lung collapse/atelectasis, hemoptysis, cough, suspected malignancy, infection, foreign body aspiration (FBA), interstitial lung disease (ILD), lung transplant, and trauma.

  • Atelectasis / lobar collapse / retained secretions: common in the ICU; bronchoscopy removes mucus plugs and is most valuable when a bronchus is obstructed. Not every atelectasis needs it: try noninvasive airway clearance first (bronchodilators when indicated, CPT/PEP, coughing, humidification, suctioning).
  • Hemoptysis: identify the cause, localize the bleeding source, and evaluate for endobronchial malignancy.
  • Chronic cough: the most common causes are asthma, postnasal drip/allergic rhinitis and GERD; bronchoscopy may be considered when a persistent cough stays unexplained despite appropriate evaluation and treatment, particularly when an underlying airway abnormality is suspected (the notes: it is not routinely indicated for uncomplicated chronic cough).
  • Suspected malignancy: visualize and sample the lesion. Infection: define the causative organism (BAL, washings).
  • FBA: common and serious in children. Classic triad: sudden paroxysmal coughing, wheezing, and diminished breath sounds on one side. Rigid bronchoscopy is often preferred for large objects and remains the instrument of choice in children (RCP 105: "Large foreign objects in the airway are removed by rigid bronchoscope.").
  • ILD: BAL and/or transbronchial biopsy may be considered, but tissue biopsy is not always necessary. Lung transplant: BAL and/or transbronchial biopsy evaluate allograft complications. Blunt chest trauma: tracheal and bronchial injuries can be life threatening.

The other decks list indications in two columns. The RCP 105 deck lists the "tissue and fluid samples" indication as the most important concept on the slide.

Diagnostic indications Therapeutic indications
RCP 105 deck: tumors in airways and lungs; airway obstruction; hemoptysis, inflammation, and infection; interstitial pulmonary disease; staging of lung cancer before surgery; vocal cord paralysis; tissue and fluid samples from airways or lungs RCP 105 deck: removal of retained secretions, mucus plugs, or polyps; removal of foreign objects (common in children); removal of endobronchial tissues; intubation of difficult airway; drainage of abscess; bronchial toilet; local administration of thrombin for bloody sputum or hemoptysis; electrosurgical treatment
RCP 203 deck: lesions of unknown etiology on chest x-ray; recurrent atelectasis or pulmonary infiltrates; patency of the upper airway; source of hemoptysis; unexplained cough, localized/unilateral wheeze, or stridor; suspicious or positive sputum cytology; obtaining lower respiratory tract secretions, cell washings, or biopsies; location and extent of injury from toxic inhalation or aspiration; problems with artificial airways (tube placement or tracheal damage) RCP 203 deck: facilitating ETT insertion during difficult intubations; selective intubation of a mainstem bronchus; clearing mucus plugs causing lobar or segmental atelectasis; removing abnormal endobronchial tissue or foreign bodies; placing and assessing an airway stent; airway balloon dilatation for tracheobronchial stenosis
RCP 103 Clinical Data deck: also suspected bronchiectasis and evaluation of airway trauma or smoke inhalation RCP 103 Clinical Data deck: also selective lavage and controlling massive hemoptysis

RCP 202 Burn deck. Bronchoscopy has traditionally been the standard diagnostic approach for inhalation injury; the notes call it the "most important diagnostic procedure." It shows airway erythema/edema, soot or carbonaceous material, secretions, and mucosal injury/sloughing.

Hess adds:

  • Indications for rigid bronchoscopy (Box 9-3): massive hemoptysis, foreign body removal, dilation of airway stenosis, airway stent placement, and resection of central airway tumor.
  • Atelectasis
    • Reported success rates range from 19% to 89%.
    • Bronchoscopy for lobar collapse seems more beneficial than bronchoscopy for subsegmental atelectasis.
    • In general, conservative management should be tried first.
    • An air bronchogram predicts delayed resolution.
  • Hemoptysis
    • The most common reason for bronchoscopy is suspected malignancy.
    • About 5% to 6% of patients with hemoptysis and a normal chest radiograph have an endobronchial malignancy.
    • The highest lung-cancer yield comes with a smoking history of more than 40 pack-years, male sex, and age greater than 40 years.
    • In massive hemoptysis, rigid bronchoscopy is often preferred because its suctioning and ventilation are superior. If a flexible scope is used, intubate first.
  • Lung transplant: transbronchial biopsy is the gold standard for diagnosing acute rejection.
  • Blunt trauma: bronchoscopy remains the standard for diagnosing tracheobronchial injury.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 18 (and notes), 19 (and notes), 20, 21 (notes), 33 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 3, 5, 6 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slides 12, 13 · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 62 · RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slides 25, 28 (notes) · Hess Ch 9 — Indications for Bronchoscopy; Box 9-3

3Contraindications to Bronchoscopy

Lssn 7a deck (slide 7). Patient-selection risk factors: asthma and bronchospasm; cardiovascular risk; head trauma and elevated ICP; hypoxemia and high O₂ requirement; anticoagulant and antiplatelet therapy (check PT, PTT, INR and platelet count); thrombocytopenia; uremia and renal dysfunction (uremia impairs platelet function even with a normal count). The contraindication box comes down to three concerns: oxygenation, hemodynamic stability and bleeding risk. Absolute = do not proceed unless the problem is corrected or an exceptional emergency justifies it; relative = higher risk, but may proceed when benefits outweigh risks. Severe, uncorrectable hypoxemia is a major contraindication to elective bronchoscopy, although an emergency airway obstruction may still require it.

Normal coagulation values (slide 7 notes): PT 11–13.5 s; INR 0.8–1.1 (not on anticoagulants; warfarin therapeutic range 2.0–3.0; INR > 4 → increased bleeding risk); PTT 25–35 s. Check them when indicated, especially before biopsy.

AARC criteria (RCP 203 deck). You should recommend against bronchoscopy in a patient who:

  1. cannot be adequately oxygenated during the procedure;
  2. has a bleeding disorder that cannot be corrected;
  3. has severe obstructive airway disease;
  4. has severe refractory hypoxemia;
  5. is hemodynamically unstable.

For BAL, the RCP 203 deck says the "only major contraindication… is a predisposition for bleeding."

Hess adds Box 9-1, Contraindications to Fiberoptic Bronchoscopy and Transbronchial Biopsy:

Absolute Relative
Inability to maintain adequate oxygenation; severe hypoxemia Active ischemic heart disease
Operator inexperience Active cardiac arrhythmia
Inadequate facilities Refractory hypoxemia
Lack of informed consent Bleeding diathesis
Status asthmaticus Uncooperative patient
Unstable cardiovascular conditions Active or uncontrolled bronchospasm

Numbers and rules (the oxygenation, asthma and ICP points also appear in the Lssn 7a slide 7 notes; the rest are Hess only)

  • Oxygenation: severe hypoxemia with PaO₂ < 70 mm Hg despite supplemental oxygen is generally considered a contraindication.
  • Myocardial infarction: the British Thoracic Society recommends deferring bronchoscopy a minimum of 6 weeks after an MI.
  • Asthma: avoid bronchoscopy entirely in status asthmaticus. Defer an elective procedure until bronchospasm is controlled.
  • ICP: increased intracranial pressure has been cited as a relative contraindication, because bronchoscopy-related cough can raise ICP. Studies found transient ICP rises but no acute clinical deterioration, so Hess says to use bronchoscopy with caution in these patients.
  • Platelets:
    • minimum 20,000–50,000/mm³ for fiberoptic bronchoscopy;
    • >50,000/mm³ for transbronchial biopsy;
    • with thrombocytopenia, the oral route is preferred.
  • Holding anticoagulant and antiplatelet drugs:
Drug Hold before bronchoscopy Detail
Aspirin Need not be held, even for TBB —
Clopidogrel and ticagrelor ≥5 days before biopsy With clopidogrel, significant bleeding after TBB was 89% vs 3.4% in controls
Prasugrel 7 days —
Ticlopidine 10–14 days —
Warfarin 3–5 days (BTS) An INR of 1.5 is safe for most surgical procedures
Unfractionated heparin Stop 4–6 hours before —
LMWH Hold 24 hours before —
New oral anticoagulants 24–48 hours 3–5 days if creatinine clearance is < 50 mL/min
  • Rigid bronchoscopy:
    • relative contraindications are the same as for diagnostic bronchoscopy (uncontrolled coagulopathy, high O₂ requirement), plus limited cervical neck extension;
    • the absolute contraindication is inability to provide informed consent.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slide 7 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slides 14, 15 · Hess Ch 9 — Box 9-1; Asthma and Bronchospasm; Cardiovascular Risk; Head Trauma and Elevated Intracranial Pressure; Hypoxemia and High Oxygen Requirement; Anticoagulant and Antiplatelet Therapy; Thrombocytopenia; Rigid Bronchoscopy — Indications

4Difference Between Flexible and Rigid Bronchoscopes

Feature Flexible (fiberoptic) bronchoscope Rigid bronchoscope
Build Control unit (head) plus a soft, flexible shaft of 3.5–6 mm external diameter; the tip rotates up to 210° (Lssn 7a notes, from Hess). RCP 105 deck: the scope bends about 180° up and 130° down. Straight, hollow, stainless steel tube with a beveled distal tip (Lssn 7a). Hess: adult scopes are 9–13 mm in diameter and 40 cm long; tracheoscopes are 25 cm.
Channels / ports Operating channel for suction and specimens; working channel for saline, lidocaine and instruments. RCP 105 deck: handle, eyepiece, bending mechanism, channel outlet, insertion tube. Proximal ports for ventilation, suction catheters, grasping tools, a telescope or a flexible scope (Lssn 7a). Hess: sidewall fenestrations keep ventilating the opposite lung.
Anesthesia Lssn 7a: usually local anesthesia with sedation (general anesthesia if needed). Hess: topical anesthesia alone or with IV (procedural) sedation. Lssn 7a: general anesthesia (size and less comfortable design; gives comfort, airway control and safety). Hess: a muscle relaxant is optional.
Ventilation The patient keeps breathing; an intubated patient stays on the ventilator through an adapter. Hess: spontaneous assisted ventilation or jet ventilation (Sanders' jet ventilator).
Route Nose (transnasal is most common; it anchors the scope for stability and comfort), mouth (bite block required), ETT or tracheostomy (Lssn 7a; the notes add that the preferred route depends on the patient and setting). Hess: the trach must be ≥6 mm ID, and the ETT must be ≥1.5 mm larger than the scope (typically ≥8 mm ID). Hess: through the mouth with the neck hyperextended.
Reach / view Lssn 7a: navigates smaller, more distal airways, with a more detailed view of large and small airways. Hess: to the fourth-order bronchi. RCP 103 deck: third or fourth airway generation. Lssn 7a: a more limited view, mainly the larger central airways.
Best for Lssn 7a: routine diagnostic work (biopsies, BAL, smaller airways) and some therapy (bleeding, smaller obstructions); better tolerated. Foreign bodies can be removed without general anesthesia. Lssn 7a: complex therapeutic procedures needing airway control: severe central airway obstruction, large foreign bodies, significant airway bleeding, airway stents (silicone stents require rigid bronchoscopy), laser resection, dilation. Instrument of choice for pediatric foreign body.
Limits Lssn 7a: lower trauma risk, but bleeding, infection and pneumothorax, especially with biopsies. Hess: limited ability to suction blood quickly. Lssn 7a: higher risk of trauma (airway perforation) and the risks of general anesthesia. Hess: needs an operating room; limited cervical extension is a relative contraindication.
History Lssn 7a: flexible scope introduced in the 1960s in Tokyo, Japan. Hess: Shigeto Ikeda, "late 1960s" and "1967". RCP 105 deck: "development in 1966", introduced to the United States in 1970. Lssn 7a: first bronchoscopy, Germany, 1897, with a rigid scope. Hess: Gustav Killian (removed a pork bone); refined by Chevalier Jackson in the early 1900s.

Two kinds of flexible scope. Flexible scopes are either fiberoptic or video chip.

  • In a fiberoptic scope, images travel from the distal lens to a proximal eyepiece.
  • In a videobronchoscope, digital images are captured at the tip and sent to a remote monitor. Its disadvantages are the need for extra equipment and space.
  • Lssn 7a: both do the same jobs; only image capture differs. Modern flexible bronchoscopes are predominantly video-based, including many disposable ones.

Dates. The Lssn 7a deck says only "1960's in Tokyo, Japan". The RCP 105 deck (Chang) says the flexible scope was developed in 1966 and introduced to the United States in 1970. Hess says Ikeda (Tokyo) developed it in the late 1960s and introduced it in 1967. The sources are consistent (all 1960s). If asked for a year it was developed, answer 1966 (RCP 105 deck).

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 4 (and notes), 5 (notes), 12 (and notes), 19 (notes), 23 (and notes), 25 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 3 (notes), 5 (notes), 7 (notes), 9 · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 61 (notes) · Hess Ch 9 — Overview of Bronchoscopy; The Flexible Bronchoscope; Airway Examination; Hemoptysis; Suspected Foreign Body Aspiration; Rigid Bronchoscopy (Indications, Equipment, Insertion, Anesthesia and Ventilation); Airway Stenting

5Pharmacologic Agents: Anesthetic and Sedative Medications

Lssn 7a deck (slides 8–10). IV sedation relieves anxiety, improves comfort and cooperation, and gives amnesia; outpatients get procedural (conscious) sedation. Sedation is a continuum: a moderate level (responds to voice, breathes spontaneously) can slide into deep sedation (not easily aroused, may need airway help). The deck says to focus on these drugs and the reversal agents:

Drug Class Effect Reversal
Midazolam (Versed) Benzodiazepine Sedation, anxiolysis, amnesia; no analgesia Flumazenil (Romazicon); can precipitate seizures, so it is not given routinely to speed recovery
Fentanyl (Sublimaze) Opioid Analgesia; often combined with midazolam, which raises the risk of respiratory depression Naloxone (Narcan)
Propofol (Diprivan) Sedative-hypnotic (neither opioid nor benzo) Rapid sedation/anesthesia; respiratory depression and hypotension; no analgesia None

Memory trick: Fentanyl = pain = opioid = naloxone; Versed = sedation/amnesia = benzo = flumazenil; Propofol = neither = no reversal. The deck calls the Table 9-1 doses textbook reference information, not current standard dosing. Premedications are given as ordered (not every patient gets the same): lidocaine spray, a sedative such as midazolam, an anticholinergic (atropine) may be given to dry secretions, and antibiotics if prescribed.

RCP 105 deck (slide 8). The goal is a patient who is comfortable, coughs less, has fewer secretions and tolerates the scope. The NBRC purposes are:

  • Lidocaine: local anesthetic that reduces mucosal irritation and suppresses cough. Dose: 5–10 mL of a 1% to 4% solution via aerosol, or instilled down the working channel starting at the vocal cords.
  • Atropine sulfate IM: reduces vagal response, oral secretions, and bronchospasm.
  • Morphine sulfate IM: suppresses coughing and relieves pain.
  • Diazepam (Valium) or another benzodiazepine IV: provides sedation and reduces anxiety.

The notes add that many hospitals now use midazolam (Versed), propofol (Diprivan) or fentanyl. The goal is not to put the patient fully to sleep; the patient keeps breathing on their own.

Other decks

  • The RCP 203 deck lists the bronchoscopy cart medications as lidocaine, lidocaine-epinephrine, acetylcysteine, and thrombin.
  • The RCP 104 deck says lidocaine blocks Na⁺ channels and is used during intubation and bronchoscopy. Watch for a suppressed cough, which raises aspiration risk.

Hess adds: the regimen and its rules (the regimen and the atropine point are also in the Lssn 7a slide 9 notes)

  • Regimen:
    • topical lidocaine for local anesthesia;
    • anticholinergics to reduce secretions and inhibit vagal tone;
    • codeine for antitussive effect;
    • benzodiazepines for sedation, amnesia and anxiolysis;
    • opioids for analgesia and cough suppression.
  • Lidocaine:
    • It is the most commonly used topical agent because of its favorable safety profile and short half-life. Other topical agents are tetracaine, benzocaine and cocaine.
    • Topical use can produce serum levels of up to 50% of an IV bolus, so the total dose should not exceed 5–7 mg/kg. Lower it with significant hepatic or cardiac disease.
    • Through the scope it is given in aliquots of 2–3 mL of 1% to 2% lidocaine.
  • Nasal vasoconstrictors: 4% cocaine or 0.5% phenylephrine.
  • Atropine: many centers have abandoned it, because more bradycardia or secretions have not been reported without it.
  • Benzodiazepines plus opioids: together they potentiate respiratory depression. Keep naloxone (for opioids) and flumazenil (for benzodiazepines) ready.
  • Propofol: often requires a trained anesthesia practitioner.

Hess Table 9-1 gives the doses below.

Agent Dose and route Onset Duration Effect Side effects
Atropine IM 0.4–1.0 mg 30–60 min Variable ↓ airway secretions, ↓ vagal tone Tachycardia, tachydysrhythmias, AV dissociation, urinary retention, dry mouth
Lidocaine 1–10%, topical or inhalation; max 5–7 mg/kg 5–10 min 30–60 min Cough suppression, local anesthesia Early bronchospasm, dizziness, seizures in high doses; toxic above 5 μg/mL plasma
Midazolam IV 2.5–10.0 mg (0.05–0.075 mg/kg) 1–3 min 2 h Amnesia, sedation Respiratory depression
Diazepam IV 2–7 mg (0.1 mg/kg); PO 5–10 mg 1–3 min; 15–30 min 2–8 h Amnesia, sedation Respiratory depression, thrombophlebitis, pain on injection
Meperidine IV/IM 20–75 mg (1 mg/kg) IV 1–3 min, IM 15–30 min 2–4 h Analgesia Respiratory depression, nausea
Codeine IM 20–120 mg 30 min 2–4 h Cough suppression Urinary retention
Morphine IV/IM 2–10 mg (0.1 mg/kg) IV 5 min, IM 15–30 min 2–6 h Analgesia Respiratory depression, nausea, itching, bronchospasm, bradycardia, biliary spasm
Fentanyl IV 50–100 μg (1 μg/kg) 2 min 30–60 min Analgesia Respiratory depression, nausea, chest wall rigidity, bradycardia
Alfentanil IV 250–1000 μg (10 μg/kg) 1 min 15–30 min Analgesia Respiratory depression, nausea, chest wall rigidity
Propofol IV "50 μg/mg/kg (10–30 mg)" (as printed) 30 s 8–10 min Sedation Respiratory depression, hypotension
Naloxone IV 40 μg titrated q 2–3 min 1 min 20–60 min Reverses opioids Tachycardia, hypertension, dysrhythmias
Flumazenil 0.4–1 mg 2 min 1 h Reverses benzodiazepines CNS excitation, nausea, residual sedation
Caution · sources differWhere the sources differ:
  • Lidocaine concentration. The RCP 105 deck gives 5–10 mL of 1–4% by aerosol (the Lssn 7a deck gives no dose). Hess gives 2–3 mL aliquots of 1–2% through the scope, and Table 9-1 gives 1–10%. Use the RCP 105 deck's 5–10 mL of 1–4% on this exam.
  • Atropine. The RCP 105 deck lists atropine as a premedication, and the Lssn 7a notes say it "may be given" to dry secretions. Hess (quoted in the same Lssn 7a notes) says many centers have dropped it. For "what does atropine do," use the deck answer.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 8 (and notes), 9 (notes), 10 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 8 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide S118 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 20 — Drugs Affecting the CNS, slide 35 (and notes) · Hess Ch 9 — Patient Sedation; Table 9-1 (table text

6Methods of Monitoring During the Procedure

During the procedure (Lssn 7a deck, slides 8 and 10 notes)

  • During sedation, monitor SpO₂ and respiratory status, HR and BP, level of consciousness, and capnography when indicated or required by the sedation protocol. Sedation raises the risk of respiratory depression and airway obstruction.
  • The RT sets up continuous monitoring of SpO₂, ECG/heart rate, respiratory rate and blood pressure, watches ventilation and patient responsiveness, and is ready to give supplemental oxygen or assist ventilation.
  • Transient hypoxemia occurs in up to 35% of patients and can often be relieved with supplemental oxygen; its causes are partial airway obstruction by the scope, suctioning, sedation-related hypoventilation and BAL (slide 17).

During the procedure (RCP 105 deck)

  • Continuous monitoring: the RT watches ECG, SpO₂, BP, and ETCO₂ (if used), plus ventilator pressures, tidal volumes, heart rate and the patient's appearance.
  • Why hypoxemia matters: hypoxemia is the complication most likely to be seen. Patients are often preoxygenated. If SpO₂ falls, the RT raises oxygen, tells the physician, and the procedure may be paused.
  • Supplemental oxygen:
    • Spontaneously breathing patients without an artificial airway may get oxygen up to 6 L/min.
    • Through an ETT or trach, an aerosol setup may be used.
    • SpO₂ is titrated with a pulse oximeter.
  • On the ventilator (the slide's "most valuable" note), the scope inside the ETT raises airway resistance. Therefore:
    • FiO₂ may be raised, often to 100%;
    • delivered VT may decrease;
    • peak pressure may increase;
    • the high-pressure limit/alarm may need temporary adjustment;
    • a minor leak can be offset by increasing VT.
  • Capnography: the RCP 203 deck's slide 38 notes name sidestream capnography for non-intubated, consciously sedated patients, such as those having bronchoscopy (the slide text itself answers "When would a Sidestream type be used?" with "Vented pts").

After the procedure (RCP 105 deck)

  • Monitor vital signs for at least 2 hours or until stable: HR, BP, RR, SpO₂ and level of consciousness.
  • Use a continuous pulse oximeter during recovery.
  • Oxygen may usually be stopped 4 hours after bronchoscopy.

Hess adds

  • Monitoring: use telemetry, pulse oximetry, and noninvasive blood pressure throughout the procedure and the recovery period.
  • Oxygen: give 2–3 L/min of supplemental O₂ to every patient to prevent transient hypoxemia.
  • Capnography: the American Society of Anesthesiologists recommends capnography to check ventilation during moderate or deep sedation. It identifies early respiratory compromise and reduces hypoxemia and apnea.
  • Procedural sedation: requires continuous monitoring and nursing until the patient has recovered completely.
  • Discharge: outpatients are usually safe for discharge 1–2 hours after the procedure. They should not drive or operate heavy machinery for 24 hours.

Supplemental oxygen: where the sources differ. The RCP 105 deck says up to 6 L/min for a spontaneously breathing patient without an artificial airway. Hess says 2–3 L/min for all patients. The Lssn 7a deck gives no flow. Use the RCP 105 deck's number on this exam.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 8 (notes), 10 (notes), 17 (and notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 7 (notes), 9 (and notes), 10 (notes), 13 (and notes), 14 (notes), 15 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 5b — Ch 8 Quality Control, slide 38 (notes) · Hess Ch 9 — Minimizing Complications; Patient Sedation

7Difference Between COPD, GERD, Emphysema, and Lung Cancer

COPD Emphysema GERD Lung cancer
Definition "Preventable, treatable, progressive, irreversible airflow limitation." Caused by an abnormal inflammatory response to harmful particles or gases, mainly cigarette smoke. An umbrella over chronic bronchitis and emphysema. "Permanent enlargement of air spaces distal to terminal bronchioles with alveolar wall destruction." One of the two conditions under COPD. Hess: a pathologic, not clinical, term, diagnosed by histopathology or imaging (HRCT). "Regurgitation of stomach contents into esophagus", the backward flow of stomach contents. Abnormal, uncontrolled multiplication of cells (neoplasm) arising from the epithelium of the tracheobronchial tree. A tumor from bronchial mucosa is bronchogenic carcinoma; the terms are used interchangeably.
Key features Dyspnea on exertion, chronic cough, sputum, hypoxemia, hypercapnia, ↑ lung compliance, ↓ elastic recoil. Diagnosis needs spirometry: post-bronchodilator FEV₁/FVC < 0.70. "Pink puffer": thin, barrel chest, pursed-lip breathing, minimal sputum, hyperresonance, ↓ DLCO. Panacinar form is linked to alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency; centriacinar is the most common and smoking-linked. 3× more common in asthma. An often-overlooked cause of chronic cough (about 10–20% of cases). Microaspiration → bronchoconstriction and airway inflammation. Smoking causes about 80% of deaths; radon is the second leading cause. Two groups: NSCLC (squamous, adenocarcinoma, large cell) and SCLC (oat cell).
Link to bronchoscopy (where a source says so) Hess Ch 33 lists cancer among COPD's nonpulmonary consequences. Hess Ch 47: lung cancer is "more closely linked to COPD with airflow compromise." RCP 203 deck: severe obstructive airway disease is an AARC contraindication to bronchoscopy. Hess Ch 9: diffuse edema and inflammation are seen in chronic bronchitis. Lssn 7a deck (slide 13 notes, from Hess Ch 9): a scabbard (sabre-sheath) trachea can be seen in emphysema. Lssn 7a slide 31: one-way endobronchial valves placed bronchoscopically let trapped air out but not back in, reducing hyperinflation in selected patients with advanced emphysema; the major complication is pneumothorax. The FDA approved the Zephyr valve. Lssn 7a slide 19: GERD is one of the most common causes of chronic cough (with asthma and postnasal drip/allergic rhinitis); the notes' chronic-cough workup includes empiric reflux treatment and a 24-hour pH probe (for silent reflux) before bronchoscopy is considered. Hess Ch 9: an inflamed hypopharynx on airway exam may indicate severe GERD. Lssn 7a slides 15–16: endobronchial biopsy is commonly used to diagnose lung cancer, and EBUS-TBNA is particularly valuable for lung cancer diagnosis and mediastinal lymph node staging; bronchoscopic appearance alone cannot make the diagnosis (tissue is needed). Squamous cell carcinoma is a type of NSCLC, in which adenocarcinoma is the most common. Hess Ch 47: bronchoscopy is "the procedure most commonly used to diagnose lung cancer": washing, brushing, biopsy, and EBUS-TBNA for diagnosis and staging. RCP 105 deck: staging of lung cancer is a diagnostic indication. RCP 103 deck: shows a small cell tumor in the right mainstem and a wire stent placed to keep the airway open.

How they relate. COPD is the umbrella; emphysema is the alveolar-destruction half of it. The other half, chronic bronchitis, is a productive cough for ≥3 months in each of 2 consecutive years. GERD is reflux of stomach contents into the esophagus that may trigger bronchospasm and chronic cough. Lung cancer is a malignant neoplasm; the COPD deck lists a higher likelihood of lung cancer among COPD's health risks (slide 3).

Gap · still not coveredwhy the instructor groups these four diseases under bronchoscopy. The Lssn 7a deck mentions GERD (chronic cough), emphysema (scabbard trachea, endobronchial valves) and lung cancer (biopsy, EBUS staging) but never defines COPD or contrasts the four; the table answers from the definitions in the other decks and Hess — ask the instructor or check Hess Ch 9, 33, 47.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 13 (notes), 15 (notes), 16 (notes), 19 (and notes), 31 (and notes) · RCP 103 COPD PP, slides 2, 3, 6, 7, 14, 16 · RCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slide 23 (and notes) · RCP 103 Lung Cancer, slides 2, 4, 6, 8, 33 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 14 · RCP 105 Procedures, slide 3 · RCP 103 Asthma PP, slide 13 · Hess Ch 33 — Definitions · Hess Ch 9 — Airway Examination; Cough; Endobronchial Valves · Hess Ch 47 — Diagnosis (bronchoscopy, EBUS) · Hess Glossary (bronchogenic carcinoma, emphysema, chronic bronchitis)

8Difference Between Lavage, Washing, Brushing, and Biopsy

The Lssn 7a deck's takeaways (slides 11–16):

  • BAL = scope wedged (segmental or subsegmental bronchus), saline in and out → distal airways and alveolar region (pneumonia, opportunistic infections, some ILDs). Washing = saline in and out without wedging → larger conducting airways.
  • Standard brush = cells (cytology), mainly for suspected malignancy. Protected brush = organisms (microbiology), with less upper-airway contamination (less used today because BAL is widely available).
  • Endobronchial biopsy = forceps tissue from a visible airway lesion. TBLB = tissue from the lung beyond the bronchial wall, often with imaging guidance (ILD, infections, peripheral lesions). TBNA = a needle samples beyond the airway wall, including lymph nodes, guided in real time by EBUS.
  • All of these are done at PRMC and AVMC.

The RCP 105 deck's rule. "Biopsy = Piece of tissue; Cytology = Cells." Forceps = tissue, needle = cells (aspiration), brush = surface cells. Biopsies go to histology (tissue sections). Brushing, with or without lavage, goes to cytology (individual cells).

Technique What it samples How it's done Key numbers / use
Bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) The alveolar space: cells and microbiology Lssn 7a: wedged in a segmental or subsegmental bronchus, saline instilled and suctioned back. Hess: a third- or fourth-order bronchus; room-temperature sterile buffered saline; suction kept < 120 cm H₂O so the bronchus does not collapse. Hess: total ≤200 mL (most patients need < 100 mL); about 60% is returned; aliquots are 20–60 mL; in DAH, the return gets bloodier with each aliquot, and >20% hemosiderin-laden macrophages suggests DAH. Other decks: diagnoses PCP (RCP 103), VAP and nonresolving pneumonia (RCP 203); therapeutic large-volume lavage is used for alveolar proteinosis (RCP 103).
Bronchoscopic washing The airway, not the alveoli Lssn 7a: saline in and out without wedging. Hess: saline in 5–10 mL aliquots is placed near a lesion and suctioned into a trap. Hess: cytology of an endobronchial mass, abnormal mucosa or obstructed orifice; upper-airway flora can contaminate microbiology.
Brushing Surface cells or microorganisms Lssn 7a: standard brush → cytology; protected brush → microbiology. Hess: the brush is moved back and forth in short strokes to disrupt the mucosa; the protected specimen brush is a double telescoping catheter plus a biodegradable plug, so it samples lower-tract pathogens. Hess: slightly higher bleeding risk than BAL or washing; slides are fixed in 95% alcohol.
Endobronchial (forceps) biopsy Tissue from visible lesions in the airway Forceps within the visual range of the scope (RCP 105). Hess: 3–6 samples, placed in formalin; the major complication is bleeding.
Transbronchial lung biopsy (TBB/TBLB) Lung tissue (parenchyma) beyond the airway Lssn 7a slide 15 notes (from Hess): fluoroscopy is not necessary in diffuse disease, although it may reduce the risk of significant pneumothorax; in focal disease seen on CXR it may significantly raise the yield. The RCP 105 deck: taken during a forced exhalation maneuver. Hess recommends a minimum of 6 pieces, 1–2 mm. Pneumothorax 1–6% and significant bleeding (>50 mL) in 2–9% (Lssn 7a slide 17 and Hess).
Transbronchial needle aspiration (TBNA) Cells from outside the airway (mediastinal/hilar lymph nodes) A needle is passed through the airway wall (RCP 105; Lssn 7a: guided in real time by EBUS). Hess: do it before other sampling to avoid contamination; gauges 19, 20, 21 and 22.

Hess adds diagnostic sensitivity.

  • Endobronchial (central) disease:
    • biopsy 74%
    • brushing 59%
    • washing 49%
    • all three combined up to 88%
  • Peripheral lesions:
    • brushing 52%
    • TBB 46%
    • BAL/washing 43%
    • all modalities 69%
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 11 (and notes), 12 (and notes), 14 (and notes), 15 (and notes), 16 (and notes), 17 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 11, 12 (and notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 63 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5, slide 15 · Hess Ch 9 — Bronchoalveolar Lavage; Bronchoscopic Washing; Bronchoscopic Brushing; Endobronchial Biopsy; Transbronchial Biopsy; Transbronchial Needle Aspiration; Complications of Bronchoscopy; Suspected Malignancy · Hess Glossary

9Hess Ch 9 "Key Points" (study guide: "Study the 'Key Points' p.205")

Every Key Point, as the book gives it:

  1. Bronchoscopy is the most commonly used invasive procedure in pulmonary medicine and can be used for both diagnostic and therapeutic procedures.
  2. The flexible fiberoptic bronchoscope consists of a control unit (or head) and a soft, flexible shaft.
  3. Bronchoscopy is a safe and effective tool for diagnosing and treating a wide variety of pulmonary processes.
  4. Factors necessary for a successful bronchoscopy are proper patient selection, patient preparation, and appropriate anesthesia.
  5. The ideal patient for fiberoptic bronchoscopy is awake, able to understand and cooperate, and free of other conditions that could raise the risk of the procedure.
  6. Bronchoscopy can be performed with topical anesthesia alone, but many physicians and patients prefer the judicial use of adjunctive IV sedation.
  7. Common techniques: airway examination, bronchoalveolar lavage, bronchoscopic washing, bronchial brushing, endobronchial biopsy, transbronchial lung biopsy, and transbronchial needle aspiration.
  8. Indications: atelectasis, hemoptysis, cough, suspected malignancy, infection, foreign body aspiration, interstitial lung disease, lung transplant, and trauma.
  9. Rigid bronchoscopy can be used for any bronchoscopic indication. Because it requires general anesthesia, most centers limit it to therapeutic indications (relief of central airway obstruction, foreign body removal) and investigation of massive hemoptysis.
  10. Modern airway stenting uses self-expandable metallic stents, silicone stents, and hybrid metal–silicone stents.
  11. Pleural effusions may be caused by a variety of medical conditions.
  12. Indications for thoracentesis: diagnosis of a new pleural effusion of unknown etiology, and relief of dyspnea from a large effusion causing significant loss of lung volume.
  13. Pleural fluid is exudative if the pleural fluid : serum protein ratio is ≥ 0.5 or the pleural fluid : serum LDH ratio is ≥ 0.6.
  14. The traditional treatment for recurrent pleural effusions is pleurodesis. It is done by mechanical abrasion during thoracotomy or thoracoscopic surgery, or by giving a sclerosing agent via tube thoracostomy.
  15. Endoluminal lung-volume reduction treats advanced emphysema with one-way endobronchial valves.

The Lssn 7a deck's own Key Points (slides 32–33) repeat Key Points 1–3, 6–8, 10–12 and 15 nearly word for word, with these differences (use the deck's wording):

  • KP 4: adds that anesthesia needs vary: topical anesthesia with moderate sedation for some, deep sedation or general anesthesia for others.
  • KP 5: an awake, cooperative patient is ideal under moderate sedation, but bronchoscopy can also be done on unconscious, intubated or mechanically ventilated patients.
  • KP 9: rigid bronchoscopy is primarily for complex therapeutic procedures (central airway obstruction, foreign body removal, airway stenting) and typically requires general anesthesia.
  • KP 14: the traditional approach to recurrent symptomatic malignant pleural effusions is pleurodesis or a sclerosing agent.
  • KP 13 (exudate ratios) is not among the deck's Key Points. The deck's slide 29 instead teaches transudate = pressure problem (hydrostatic/oncotic) and exudate = inflammation, infection or malignancy; its notes give a protein cutoff (transudate < 25 g/L with normal serum protein; exudate > 35 g/L). Know both this and Hess's ratios (see the conflicts file).
  • Slides 28–30 add: ultrasound guidance is recommended for thoracentesis, and a tunneled pleural catheter is the alternative to pleurodesis (home drainage).

Hess adds, from the chapter body behind Key Points 11–14:

  • Thoracentesis:
    • Removing 1500 mL of fluid at a time is safe.
    • Pleural fluid with pH < 7.20 and glucose < 60 mg/dL may indicate a complicated parapneumonic effusion.
  • Pleurodesis: talc is the most efficacious sclerosing agent, with 80% 30-day success, vs 60% for doxycycline or bleomycin.
SourceHess Ch 9 — Key Points; Respiratory Recap "Thoracentesis"; Management of Recurrent Pleural Effusions · RCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 28, 29 (and notes), 30 (and notes), 32, 33

10Complications of Bronchoscopy and Their Management high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Lssn 7a deck (slides 17, 27): the three major complications

  • Pneumothorax: 1–6% of patients, mainly after transbronchial lung biopsy (not routine diagnostic bronchoscopy). Suspect it with sudden dyspnea, chest pain and decreased breath sounds after TBB (the slide also lists hemoptysis).
  • Significant hemorrhage (> 50 mL): 2–9%, procedure-dependent and more likely after biopsy. Stop it with epinephrine or thrombin (pharmacologic) or iced saline, all given through the working channel; airway patency and oxygenation come first.
  • Transient hypoxemia: in up to 35%, often relieved with supplemental oxygen (see item 6 for causes).
  • Not on slide 17: bronchospasm and sedation-related complications (respiratory depression, hypotension, prolonged sedation).
  • Cardiovascular effects (slide 7 notes, from Hess): bronchoscopy can raise HR by 43%, MAP by 30% and cardiac index by 28% compared with before the procedure.

RCP 105 deck

  • Infection: typical nosocomial microbes, and cross-contamination of healthy lung segments. Outbreaks of M. tuberculosis and Pseudomonas aeruginosa have been traced to the flexible bronchoscope.
  • Hypoxemia: "the complication you're most likely to see." Causes:
    • increased airflow resistance;
    • hypoventilation from premedication;
    • air leaks;
    • pooled secretions;
    • excessive suctioning.
  • Most common complications: "Hypoxemia and arrhythmias are the most common complications during and after bronchoscopy."
  • Ways to stop hemorrhage:
    • topical vasopressor (1:1000 epinephrine; the notes give 1 mL of 1:1000 with 9 mL normal saline in 2-mL portions);
    • wedging with the scope tip;
    • wedging with a 6-Fr Fogarty balloon catheter;
    • a built-in coagulation electrode.
  • Pneumothorax:
    • Biopsy instruments are sharp and long.
    • On mechanical ventilation, a lung puncture can become a tension pneumothorax. Signs: worsening cyanosis despite high O₂, diaphoresis, tachypnea, tachycardia, thready pulse.
    • Treatment is a chest tube, so a complete chest tube setup should be on the supply list.
    • With sudden deterioration, get a CXR.
  • Rates: major complications 0.08% (survey of over 24,000 procedures); mortality 0.1–0.2%.

Hess adds

  • Rates:
    • major complications (pneumothorax, pulmonary hemorrhage, infection, respiratory failure): 0.6%;
    • serious complications with TBB: 1–6%;
    • mortality: about 0.01%.
  • Minor complications: fever, cough, bronchospasm, transient hypoxia, hemoptysis.
  • Bleeding sequence for significant hemorrhage:
    1. wedge and suction for 5 minutes;
    2. 20 mL iced saline;
    3. 20 mL of 1:20,000 epinephrine;
    4. place the patient bleeding side down;
    5. occasionally an endobronchial blocker.
Caution · sources differWhere the sources differ:
  • Epinephrine strength. The RCP 105 deck's slide says topical 1:1000 (the Lssn 7a deck names epinephrine without a strength). Its notes dilute 1 mL of 1:1000 with 9 mL NS, which makes 10 mL of 1:10,000, given in 2-mL portions. Hess gives 20 mL of 1:20,000. If asked what the slide lists, answer 1:1000.
  • Complication rates. The RCP 105 deck gives 0.08% major complications and 0.1–0.2% mortality. Hess gives 0.6% and ~0.01%. The Lssn 7a deck gives no overall rates.
  • Pneumothorax frequency. The RCP 105 deck says it occurs "in extremely rare cases"; the Lssn 7a deck (and Hess) give 1–6%, mainly after TBB. Use 1–6%.

Use the RCP 105 deck's overall rates on this exam. Note that its mortality (0.1–0.2%) is higher than its own major-complication rate (0.08%), so one of the two is probably a misprint in the source. If an answer choice matches Hess (0.6% major, ~0.01% mortality), recognize it too.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 7 (notes), 17 (and notes), 27 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 13, 14 (and notes) · Hess Ch 9 — Cardiovascular Risk; Procedure Risks and Complications; Complications of Bronchoscopy (Pneumothorax, Bleeding Complications)

11Preparation, Post-Bronchoscopy Care, and Scope Reprocessing high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Before the procedure

  • RT preparation (Lssn 7a deck, slide 10 notes): verify informed consent (obtained by the physician before sedatives are given) and reinforce education; review vital signs, oxygenation, allergies, NPO status and bleeding risks; check the scope, suction, oxygen delivery, monitoring and emergency equipment; give premedications as ordered; position the patient supine or semi-Fowler's.
  • RT setup (RCP 105 deck): test the scope (light source and camera, suction, bending, flushed channel), and coat the insertion tube with water-soluble lubricant.
  • Fasting: the Lssn 7a deck says NPO, typically no food or liquids for 6–8 hours, to reduce aspiration risk. Hess: the patient fasts after midnight before a morning procedure, with a light liquid breakfast allowed before an afternoon procedure. Use the deck's 6–8 hours.
  • Five risk factors that prompt further pre-op evaluation (Hess):
    1. prior anticoagulant therapy
    2. liver disease
    3. bleeding history
    4. active bleeding or recent transfusion
    5. an unreliable historian

Post-bronchoscopy care (RCP 105 deck slide 15)

  • Monitor vital signs ≥2 hours or until stable.
  • Use an aerosol bronchodilator for bronchospasm or wheezing.
  • Use racemic epinephrine for stridor. The rule is "stridor = upper airway → racemic epi; wheezing = lower airway → bronchodilator."
  • Maintain SpO₂ with oxygen.
  • Withhold food or drink until the gag reflex returns. Lidocaine numbs the throat, which creates an aspiration risk.

After the procedure (Hess adds)

  • Fever: transient fever in up to 10%, resolving within 24 hours. Isolated fever needs no antibiotics.
  • Hemoptysis: a small amount, subsiding over 24 hours.
  • Activity: no driving for 24 hours.

Reprocessing

  • RCP 105 deck (Chang):
    • The part of the scope that touches mucosa must be sterilized.
    • Scopes used on patients with TB or hepatitis B should also be sterilized.
    • Ethylene oxide is used for parts that cannot be steam-autoclaved.
    • The notes add that proper cleaning and high-level disinfection are essential.
  • RCP 203 Lssn 5a deck (slides 18 notes, 19): the bronchoscope is semicritical equipment, so it needs sterilization or high-level disinfection (HLD). Glutaraldehyde (Cidex) gives HLD in 20 minutes at room temperature; true sterilization takes 10 hours. The biopsy forceps passed through the scope into tissue are critical → sterilization.
  • Where the decks differ: RCP 105 says "sterilized"; RCP 203 accepts sterilization or HLD. For an NBRC-style question, know that HLD is acceptable for a semicritical bronchoscope.
  • RCP 203 deck:
    1. Flush the working channel at the bedside for 20 seconds.
    2. Do a leak test.
    3. Soak in enzymatic cleaner for 5 minutes.
    4. Brush the channels.
    5. After disinfection, rinse and dry the channel with 70% alcohol and compressed air.
    6. Hang the scope vertically.
  • Light sources: the most common problem is no light output.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 7a — Interventional Pulmonary Procedures (Bronchoscopy), slides 8 (notes), 10 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 9 (notes), 15 (and notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides S118, S120, S121 · RCP 203 Lssn 5a — Ch 7 Infection Control, slides 18 (notes), 19 · Hess Ch 9 — Patient Selection; Minimizing Complications; Postprocedure Care and Education

Perioperative Management of Lung Transplants

The deck arrived on 25 Oct 2026. This section used to open by saying no deck in the vault covered it. That is no longer true: RCP 202 Lssn 8a — PeriOp Management of Lung Transplants (Hess Ch 46), 34 slides with speaker notes is now in the vault, and it is the PowerPoint the final study guide names. Items 1–7 were written from Hess Ch 46 and have been checked against the deck line by line — they agree. Items 8–10 are what the deck adds: the donor-procurement sequence, the two operations step by step, and how lungs are actually allocated today. Supporting lines still come from Hess Ch 9, 20, 23, 33, 34, 35 and 37, and from the RCP 202 ECMO deck.

The study guide’s own instruction still stands: for the dates of the first transplants, use the book answer, not the PowerPoint (item 2).

1Disease States Considered for Lung Transplant

Hess names four major groups of lung-transplant recipients:

  1. COPD/emphysema
  2. cystic fibrosis (CF)/bronchiectasis
  3. idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis (IPF)
  4. pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH)

The Key Points repeat this: the majority of cases involve patients with COPD/emphysema, bronchiectasis/cystic fibrosis, IPF, and pulmonary hypertension.

How the mix has shifted over time

  • Late 1980s and early 1990s: most transplants were for COPD/emphysema. There was no LAS yet, and COPD had lower short-term mortality than PH or IPF.
  • PAH: the introduction of IV epoprostenol in 1995 cut idiopathic PAH (IPAH) transplants by nearly 75% by 1998, and by another 50% by 2005.
  • After the LAS (2005): COPD transplants fell by nearly 10%, and IPF transplants rose by nearly the same amount.

Choice of procedure

  • IPF: single-lung transplant was long preferred. Over the past 15 years, bilateral transplant for IPF has risen nearly 60%.
  • COPD: similar data exist, but only for patients younger than 60 years.

Hess Ch 46, Table 46-1: when to refer for transplant

Disease Indications for referral
Interstitial lung disease Histopathologic or radiographic evidence of UIP or fibrosing NSIP, regardless of lung function. FVC < 80% predicted or DLCO < 40% predicted. Dyspnea or functional limitation. Any supplemental O₂ requirement.
Cystic fibrosis FEV₁ decline to 30% or rapid decline despite optimal therapy. PaCO₂ > 50 mm Hg and/or PaO₂ < 55 mm Hg. 6-minute walk < 400 m. Secondary pulmonary hypertension without a hypoxic exacerbation. Clinical decline: acute respiratory failure needing NIV, rising antimicrobial resistance, pneumothoraces, life-threatening hemoptysis.
COPD Progressive disease despite maximal medication, pulmonary rehab and O₂. Not a candidate for endoscopic or surgical lung volume reduction. PaCO₂ > 50 mm Hg and/or PaO₂ < 60 mm Hg. FEV₁ < 25% predicted. BODE index of 5 to 6.
Pulmonary vascular disease NYHA class III or IV during escalating therapy. Use of parenteral targeted therapy. Known or suspected pulmonary veno-occlusive disease. 6-minute walk < 350 m. Cardiac index < 2 L/min/m². Progressive right heart failure or syncope.

Ch 46 text: the most common referral triggers

  • CF: FEV₁ < 30%, PaCO₂ > 50, PaO₂ < 55, massive hemoptysis, recurrent pneumothoraces.
  • IPF: resting hypoxemia, DLCO < 40%, group III pulmonary hypertension.
  • COPD: FEV₁ < 20%, PaCO₂ > 45 mm Hg, and a homogeneous distribution that precludes LVRS.
  • Pulmonary hypertension is the most debated indication. Good candidates have:
    • NYHA class III or IV;
    • uncompensated right heart failure;
    • syncope;
    • 6-minute walk < 350 m;
    • cardiac index < 2 L/min/m²;
    • right atrial pressure > 15 mm Hg.

Hess, disease-by-disease (other chapters)

  • COPD (Ch 33)
    • "COPD is the most common indication for lung transplantation."
    • Candidates have advanced COPD with an estimated survival of less than 2 years. Near-term mortality markers include FEV₁ < 25% to 30% predicted, rapid decline, severe hypoxemia, hypercapnia and secondary pulmonary hypertension.
    • Coexisting bronchiectasis with purulent secretions requires double-lung transplantation.
  • ILD (Ch 34): respiratory failure from ILD is the primary indication in approximately one-half of all U.S. transplant recipients.
  • PAH (Ch 35): transplant is appropriate in carefully selected patients who fail medical therapy.
  • CF (Ch 37): the preferred operation is sequential bilateral transplantation rather than heart–lung.

Where Hess disagrees with itself:

  • COPD referral thresholds.
    • Ch 46 text: FEV₁ < 20%, PaCO₂ > 45.
    • Table 46-1: FEV₁ < 25%, PaCO₂ > 50, PaO₂ < 60.
    • Ch 33: FEV₁ < 25–30%.
    No deck breaks the tie. Table 46-1 is the book's formal referral table, so give its numbers if asked, but know the others exist.
  • Most common indication. Ch 33 says COPD is the most common indication. Ch 34 says ILD accounts for about one-half of U.S. recipients.
SourceHess Ch 46 — Introduction; Disease States for Transplantation; Table 46-1; Respiratory Recap; Key Points · Hess Ch 33 — Lung Transplantation · Hess Ch 34 · Hess Ch 35 · Hess Ch 37 — Lung Transplantation

2Dates of the First Single- and Double-Lung Transplants (book answer)

Year Event (exactly as Hess Ch 46 gives it)
1963 First lung transplant ever performed, by Dr. James Hardy, on a 58-year-old prisoner with metastatic lung cancer. He survived only 18 days (progressive renal failure), but his arterial oxygenation improved markedly and the anastomosis was confirmed successful.
1983 The Toronto Lung Transplant Group performed the "first successful lung transplant" on a 58-year-old male with pulmonary fibrosis. With cyclosporine and azathioprine, the patient survived 7 years, which set the basis for current immunosuppression. The chapter opens: "Since the first successful lung transplant in 1983…"
1986 "The first successful double-lung transplant for emphysema."
Caution · sources differAnswer for the exam.
  • First successful lung transplant: 1983 (Toronto, pulmonary fibrosis).
  • First successful double-lung transplant: 1986 (emphysema).
  • First lung transplant ever performed: 1963 (Hardy).

Hess's wording is "first successful lung transplant" for 1983. The book does not literally say "single-lung" for 1983. It is the transplant set against the 1986 double-lung transplant.

Do not confuse heart–lung with double-lung. Hess Ch 58, Table 58-3, also lists Hardy's first human lung transplant and the first human heart–lung transplant (Bruce Reitz, 1981). A heart–lung transplant is not a double-lung transplant (1986). For Hardy's date, use Ch 46's explicit 1963, because the year beside Hardy's row in Table 58-3 is unclear in the text.

Growth and survival after that:

  • About 45 transplants had been done by 1987, and more than 400 worldwide just 3 years later.
  • The 1990s averaged about 1400 per year.
  • Average survival rose from about 4 years (1980s–1990s) to 5 to 6 years now.
SourceHess Ch 46 — Introduction; Lung Transplant History · Hess Ch 58 — Table 58-3

3Lung Allocation Score (LAS) Details

Before the LAS (before May 2005). Patients were selected by listing time. That favored slowly progressive diseases such as COPD over diseases with rapid decline and high waitlist mortality, such as IPF or severe pulmonary hypertension.

What the LAS is

  • Who and when: the Organ Procurement and Transplant Network (OPTN) introduced the LAS in May 2005. Its goal is to maximize overall survival benefit for patients on the waiting list and those being transplanted.
  • What goes into it: the algorithm uses clinical data (respiratory function, hemodynamics, and diagnosis). It balances waitlist mortality against post-transplant survival. Key Point: "The LAS helps to balance waitlist mortality and post-transplant survival."
  • How priority works: when donor lungs become available, the patients with the highest scores within the Organ Procurement Organization (OPO) region get priority.
  • Who it applies to: all recipients aged 12 and older.
  • The scale: each patient is assigned a number from 0 to 100.
  • Data rules: UNOS requires most data to be current (within the past 6 months) or a zero value. Exceptions are made when a patient's condition prevents a test.
  • Petitions: if the calculated LAS does not reflect the patient's situation, a petition may be submitted to UNOS for modification and adjudication.
  • Glossary definition: "A numerical value used by the United Network for Organ Sharing to assign relative priority for distributing donated lungs for transplantation within the United States. It estimates the severity of each candidate's illness and his or her chance of success following a lung transplant."

Table 46-4: LAS variables

Predicts waitlist survival Predicts post-transplant survival
Diagnosis Diagnosis
Age Age
Body mass index Creatinine
Diabetes NYHA functional class
NYHA functional class Forced vital capacity (PAH and ILD groups only)
Forced vital capacity (% predicted) Pulmonary capillary wedge pressure ≥ 20 mm Hg (ILD group only)
Oxygen requirement at rest Continuous mechanical ventilation
Continuous mechanical ventilation
Pulmonary artery systolic pressure (all groups except PAH)
6-minute walk distance < 150 feet (as printed)

Beyond the LAS. A high score does not guarantee an organ. These also matter:

  • blood-type compatibility
  • HLA profile
  • chest cavity size (lungs too small to oxygenate the patient, or too large to fit, may rule out a transplant)
  • donor age
  • distance from the transplant center within the OPO region

Effects of the LAS (Hess)

  • After it began in 2005, COPD transplants fell nearly 10% and IPF transplants rose.
  • In the pre-LAS era, single-lung transplant was the standard approach. Today single-lung is only 25% of all lung transplants.
Gap · still not coveredthe LAS formula/weighting and any score cutoff. Hess gives only the variables, the 0–100 range and the rules above (Hess Ch 46).
SourceHess Ch 46 — Lung Allocation Score; Table 46-4; Disease States for Transplantation; Surgical Procedure; Key Points · Hess Glossary — lung allocation score

4Lung-Protective Strategies During Mechanical Ventilation for Lung Transplant Patients

Recipient after transplant (Hess Ch 46)

  • Settings: typically, a low tidal volume (lung-protective) approach of ≤6 mL/kg, with PEEP at a maximum of 5 cm H₂O and FiO₂ ≤ 0.5.
  • Single vs double lung: the ventilatory technique varies with the surgical approach.
  • When FiO₂ must go higher: with severe primary graft dysfunction (PGD) or postoperative pneumonia needing higher FiO₂, ECMO may be needed.
  • Key Point: "Following transplantation, lung-protective ventilation with minimization of PEEP and FIO₂ are the cornerstones of ventilator management."

During surgery (Hess Ch 46)

  • Single-lung transplant: the contralateral lung receives single-lung ventilation. The pulmonary artery to the lung being removed is clamped to prevent shunt.
  • Bilateral transplant:
    1. The native lung with the worst function on V/Q scan is removed first, while the other lung gets single-lung ventilation.
    2. Single-lung ventilation is then moved to the new allograft.
    3. The second native lung is removed and the second graft implanted.
  • Extracorporeal support:
    • In 2015, an estimated 30% to 40% of lung transplants needed extracorporeal support (CPB or ECMO).
    • Compared with CPB, ECMO had fewer transfusions, less PGD, fewer complications and a shorter duration of intubation, with no difference in 30-day or 1-year survival.
    • ECMO may be VV (vena cava/right atrium → right atrium) or VA (right atrium → arterial system, bypassing heart and lungs).

Donor lungs before procurement

  • Pressure limits (Ch 46): plateau pressure, and ideally peak airway pressure, < 30 cm H₂O.
  • Fluid and PEEP (Ch 46): prevent pulmonary interstitial fluid overload, using diuretics with vasopressors to hold MAP. Set PEEP to optimize oxygenation while limiting harm to venous return and cardiac output.
  • Ch 20: "Lung-protective ventilation is also recommended for brain-dead patients who are potential donors for lung transplantation." The same chapter says accumulating evidence supports a target VT of 6 mL/kg PBW even in patients with normal lungs.

Bridge to transplant (RCP 202 ECMO deck and Hess Ch 23)

  • ECMO indication: both list "end-stage lung disease as a bridge to transplant." The deck's notes (slide 14) explain the term: here we're not necessarily waiting for the patient's lungs to recover; ECMO may temporarily maintain gas exchange while the patient waits for donor lungs.
  • Awake ECMO: the RCP 202 ECMO deck (slide 19, notes) says a CF patient on ECMO as a bridge to lung transplant may be awake, eating, and actively participating in physical therapy, "sometimes called 'awake ECMO'." The deck also lists lung transplantation as one of the things ECMO bridges to (slide 22, notes). Hess adds (Ch 23): an awake and ambulatory ECMO strategy improves outcomes in these patients and avoids the effects of sedation and immobility.
SourceHess Ch 46 — Donor Management; Surgical Procedure; Postoperative Management; Key Points · Hess Ch 20 — lung-protective ventilation for potential donors · Hess Ch 23 — Indications; Outcomes · RCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slides 14 (and notes), 19 (and notes), 22 (notes)

5Recipient Candidacy and Exclusion Criteria high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Window and candidacy. The lung transplant window is "the time between activation on the waiting list and the transplantation procedure." The ideal window is when tangible disease progression is seen but exertional capacity is not so limited that rehabilitation is no longer an option.

How candidacy is decided. It needs a multispecialty evaluation. The work-up includes:

  • labs and imaging;
  • right and left heart catheterization;
  • esophageal pH and manometry.

Two disease-specific additions:

  • cardiac MRI for sarcoidosis or severe pulmonary hypertension;
  • sinus CT for CF.

Where Hess disagrees with itself (work-up). The text says the catheterizations and esophageal studies are done on all patients. Table 46-2 makes them age-based:

  • right heart catheterization if age < 50;
  • CT coronary angiography or LHC at ages 40–50;
  • right and left heart catheterization if age > 50;
  • 24-hour pH probe/esophageal manometry if age ≥ 18, and a BRAVO study if < 18.

Table 46-3: exclusion criteria

  • Malignancy in the last 2 years. Localized malignancies with expected 5-year survival >80% are the exception. High-recurrence cancers need 5 years cancer-free.
  • Organ dysfunction: untreatable advanced dysfunction of another major organ, unless the patient is a multiple-organ candidate.
  • Age > 60 and in need of a multiple-organ transplant.
  • Vascular disease: uncorrected atherosclerotic disease, or coronary disease not amenable to revascularization.
  • Bleeding: uncorrectable bleeding diathesis.
  • Infection:
    • chronic infection with virulent/resistant microbes (e.g., genomovar 3 Burkholderia cenocepacia);
    • active Mycobacterium tuberculosis.
  • Body:
    • significant chest wall/spinal deformity;
    • obesity or malnutrition: < 70% or > 130% of ideal body weight.
  • Behavior and adherence:
    • psychiatric inability to cooperate or adhere;
    • repeated nonadherence;
    • active substance addiction (alcohol, tobacco, narcotics, illicit drugs);
    • chronic, active use of narcotics or benzodiazepines.
  • Function and other conditions:
    • poor rehabilitation potential;
    • severe or symptomatic osteoporosis;
    • severe esophageal dysmotility;
    • multiple comorbidities;
    • disease too early for transplantation.

Key Point. Malignancy, chronic infection, or substance abuse is a standard absolute contraindication.

Correctable contraindications. Lack of pulmonary rehab and obesity (> 130% IBW) can be corrected.

Age limits by center. Some programs treat age ≥ 65 as an absolute contraindication; others have no upper limit.

HLA. Antibodies to human leukocyte antigens may preclude transplant or require augmented immunosuppression.

Where Hess disagrees with itself. The Ch 46 text says malignancy "in the previous 2 to 3 years." Table 46-3 says "in the last 2 years."

SourceHess Ch 46 — Transplant Recipient Criteria; Table 46-2; Table 46-3; Key Points · Hess Glossary — lung transplant window

6Primary Graft Dysfunction and Postoperative Care high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Primary graft dysfunction (PGD)

  • Definition: "a severe form of ischemia/reperfusion acute lung injury that is a major cause of early morbidity and mortality after lung transplantation."
  • Grading: grade 0 to 3, by the PaO₂/FiO₂ ratio. Greater severity goes with decreased lung compliance and elevated pulmonary arterial pressures.
  • Treatment: an inhaled vasodilator is often given, but supportive care is the most common standard treatment.
  • Prognosis: Grade 3 at 72 hours correlates with higher 90-day mortality. The book says "an absolute mortality increase of 18"; no unit is printed.
PGD grade PaO₂/FiO₂ Infiltrates consistent with pulmonary edema
0 > 300 Absent
1 > 300 Present
2 200–300 Present
3 < 200 Present

Measured on arrival to the ICU from the OR, then daily for 72 hours.

Other postoperative care (Hess Ch 46)

  • Chest tubes:
    • Single-lung transplant: three chest tubes.
    • Bilateral transplant: three on each side plus two mediastinal tubes.
    • Removal order: mediastinal and anterior first; posterior once the patient is ambulatory; basilar after several days of output of 100 to 150 mL or less.
    • Most patients have all tubes out within 7 to 10 days.
  • Infection prophylaxis:
    • intraoperative cefepime + vancomycin (or a similar broad-spectrum regimen);
    • antifungals (inhaled amphotericin, nystatin, a triazole);
    • trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole within one week for Pneumocystis jirovecii;
    • ganciclovir → oral valganciclovir for CMV.
  • Nutrition: a bedside swallow study, then FEES, before a regular diet. Severe dysphagia may need a gastrojejunal tube.
  • VTE prophylaxis: low-dose subcutaneous heparin starting as early as postoperative day 1 and continued until discharge, plus early ambulation.

Hess Ch 9 adds (bronchoscopy after transplant)

  • Transbronchial biopsy is the gold standard for acute rejection.
  • Surveillance bronchoscopy is controversial.
  • Transplant recipients have higher bleeding risk from bronchoscopy.
  • Central airway obstruction occurs in 7–18% after transplant.
SourceHess Ch 46 — Surgical Procedure; Postoperative Management; Table 46-5 · Hess Glossary — primary graft dysfunction · Hess Ch 9 — Lung Transplant; Uremia and Renal Dysfunction; Therapeutic Bronchoscopy

7Donor Evaluation and Management high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Donation after brain death (most lung transplants)

The donor must first meet these preconditions:

  • core temperature ≥ 36 °C (97 °F);
  • systolic BP ≥ 100 mm Hg;
  • eucapnia (PaCO₂ 35–45 mm Hg);
  • no hypoxia;
  • euvolemia.

The clinical exam must show:

  • absent brain-originating motor response;
  • absent corneal and oculovestibular reflexes;
  • absent gag and cough with tracheal suctioning;
  • a positive apnea test.

The apnea test. The donor is disconnected from the ventilator for up to 8 minutes. The test is positive (consistent with brain death) when PaCO₂ > 60 mm Hg, or > 20 mm Hg above baseline, with no breathing activity.

Donation after circulatory determination of death (DCDD)

  • How common: DCDD rose from 1% to 4% of U.S. lung transplants from 2011 to 2016.
  • Declaring death: requires at least 2 minutes of observation.
  • Time limit: 60 to 120 minutes after withdrawal of support is the usual upper limit for organ recovery.
  • Outcomes: DCDD has more PGD and BOS, but no significant survival difference.

Typical donor criteria

  • age < 55;
  • ABO compatibility;
  • PaO₂ > 300 mm Hg on FiO₂ 1.0 and PEEP 5 cm H₂O;
  • no significant chest trauma, aspiration or prior cardiopulmonary surgery.

Extended donors. Donors older than 55 or with > 20 pack-years may be accepted.

Size match. Size is matched mainly by height. Oversized lungs may lower the risk of grade 3 PGD compared with undersized lungs.

Preservation

  • Flush: a chilled low-potassium dextran (Perfadex) flush.
  • Storage: at 4–8 °C.
  • Ischemic time: warm ischemic time correlates directly with perfusion injury and PGD.
  • Ex vivo perfusion: normothermic ex vivo lung perfusion (EVLP) can keep lungs at least 12 hours at body temperature.
SourceHess Ch 46 — Donor Evaluation and Selection; Respiratory Recap "Apnea Test"; Donor Management

8Donor Management and Procurement — What Happens Before the Lungs Leave high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Managing the donor (Hess Ch 46, Donor Management)

  • The governing goal is prevention of pulmonary interstitial fluid overload, with close attention paid to PaO₂.
  • Ventilator criteria in the donor: a plateau pressure, and ideally a peak airway pressure, of less than 30 cm H₂O.
  • What meeting those criteria costs: it may require diuretics with concurrent vasopressors to hold mean arterial pressure, plus reconciliation of PEEP — optimising oxygenation while minimising the harm to venous return and cardiac output.
  • Brain injury with diabetes insipidus → desmopressin is often implemented.
  • Hemodynamic instability and/or ejection fraction < 45% → thyroid hormone treatment has been suggested as beneficial.
  • How much of this applies depends on the type of donation expected — DCDD versus donation after brain death.

Procurement after brain death — Hess’s sequence, in order

  1. Lungs are procured via median sternotomy, with the pulmonary arteries dissected from the ascending aorta and the superior vena cava dissected to the innominate bifurcation.
  2. The donor is anticoagulated with intravenous heparin, and a perfusion cannula is placed within the pulmonary artery.
  3. Intravenous prostaglandin is given into the pulmonary artery before transection of the inferior vena cava.
  4. When blood pressure begins to decrease, the surgeon cross-clamps the aorta.
  5. Cardioplegia is perfused into the heart while a chilled low-potassium dextran solution (Perfadex) is flushed into the pulmonary artery — to cool lung tissue and remove blood from the pulmonary vascular bed, preventing thrombosis and endothelial injury.
Exam anchorTwo things are worth carrying out of this. The order — sternotomy → heparin → prostaglandin → cross-clamp → flush — and the fact that the Perfadex flush does two jobs at once: it cools the tissue and clears blood out of the vascular bed. Item 7 already gives the storage temperature (4–8 °C) and the warm-versus-cold ischemic time distinction that follows from it.
Why it mattersSize matching is done on donor and recipient height, with some weight given to lung-volume estimates from chest x-rays. A close match is preferred, but Hess notes a useful asymmetry: oversized donor lungs may reduce the risk of grade 3 PGD compared with undersized lungs. Patients with advanced IPF often have small chest cavities, so the perfect size is frequently unavailable.
SourceHess Ch 46 — Donor Management; Donor Evaluation and Selection (size matching); Surgical Procedure · RCP 202 Lssn 8a deck, slides 20, 22, 23 (added 2026-10-25)

9The Two Operations, Step by Step — and CPB versus ECMO high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Single-lung transplant

  • Incision: a posterolateral or anterolateral thoracotomy.
  • The contralateral lung receives single-lung (one-lung) ventilation while the pulmonary artery supplying the explanted lung is clamped to prevent shunt.
  • If the diseased lung cannot support the patient, extracorporeal oxygenation may be employed.
  • Anastomoses run posterior to anterior, and the order is the thing to memorise: bronchus → pulmonary artery → pulmonary veins to the left atrium.
  • Air is purged from the pulmonary circulation, then three chest tubes go into the pleural space at the anterior, posterior and basilar portions.
  • Single-lung transplant was the standard approach in the pre-LAS era. Today it is only 25% of all lung transplantations.

Bilateral (double-lung) transplant

  • Incision: a transverse thoracosternotomy — the clamshell incision — with the lungs implanted sequentially.
  • The native lung with the worst function, as evaluated by a ventilation–perfusion scan, is removed first, with single-lung ventilation on the other side.
  • Single-lung ventilation is then transferred to the allograft; the remaining native lung comes out and the second graft goes in.
  • Closure: the upper and lower chest wall are approximated with pericostal sutures; the sternum is secured with wires.
  • Three chest tubes bilaterally, plus two tubes in the mediastinal region at the site of the sternum.
  • Hess Ch 34 adds that bilateral transplantation may require cardiopulmonary bypass in 20% of patients.

Extracorporeal support: which one, and why

  • In 2015, an estimated 30% to 40% of lung transplants required extracorporeal support. In severe pulmonary vascular disease, either CPB or ECMO is standard of care. Secondary pulmonary hypertension, severe pulmonary fibrosis or an enlarged right ventricle raise the risk of unanticipated intraoperative ECMO.
  • CPB: venous blood is drained from the right atrium into a venous reservoir; the arterial pump drives it through a heat exchanger and an oxygenator and returns it through an arterial cannula in the ascending aorta.
  • VV ECMO: blood is taken from the vena cava or right atrium and returned to the right atrium — typically a catheter in the common femoral vein and one in the right internal jugular vein.
  • VA ECMO: blood is taken from the right atrium and returned to the arterial system, bypassing the heart and lungs — venous cannula in the right internal jugular, arterial cannula typically to the right femoral artery.
Exam anchorIf the question asks which is better, the evidence runs one way. A 2014 comparison found CPB associated with a higher rate of blood product transfusion and more postoperative PGD than ECMO, with no difference in 30-day or 1-year survival; further studies found ECMO had fewer postoperative complications and a shorter duration of intubation. The deck compresses all of that to one line: “Lower complications in ECMO patients vs. CPB.”
SourceHess Ch 46 — Surgical Procedure · Hess Ch 34 (bilateral transplant and CPB) · RCP 202 Lssn 8a deck, slides 24, 25, 26 (added 2026-10-25)

10How Lungs Are Allocated Today — the LAS, and What Has Replaced It high-yield · beyond the listed asks

What the LAS is computed from — the input list the deck supplies. Item 3 covers what the LAS is, who it applies to and the 0–100 scale; this is what feeds it.

  • Age · body mass index · diagnosis (e.g. COPD, pulmonary fibrosis)
  • Pulmonary function (e.g. forced vital capacity, oxygen requirement)
  • Functional status (ability to perform daily activities)
  • Measures of heart and lung function — pulmonary artery pressure and cardiac output
  • Serum creatinine, as an indicator of kidney function
Gap — narrowed, not closedThis guide previously flagged the LAS formula and its weights as still not covered. The deck closes half of that: it names which variables go in. It still does not publish the weights, and neither does Hess, which simply points at Table 46-4, “LAS variables.” If an exam item asks you to compute an LAS, no source in the vault supports that.

What replaced it — and this is not in Hess at all

  • Continuous distribution went live in March 2023, replacing the binary geographic zones previously used to allocate organs.
  • Lungs are now distributed on a composite allocation score (CAS), which still uses the LAS as a component but adds factors such as proximity to the donor hospital.
  • Since the change: lung transplant rates increased 16% — 327 transplants per 100 patient-years after the policy versus 281 before — and waiting-list mortality decreased 29%.

Volume, for scale (deck speaker notes, citing OPTN and SRTR)

  • 2010: about 1,739 US lung transplants → 2020: about 2,539. Across that decade, roughly 21,000 in total.
  • Then 2,569 (2021), 2,743 (2022), 3,026 (2023) and a record 3,340 in 2024.
  • For comparison, item 2 gives Hess’s historical figures: about 45 transplants by 1987, more than 400 three years later, and an average of about 1,400 per year through the 1990s.
Caution — sources differ by eraNone of the continuous-distribution or recent-volume material appears in Hess. It comes from the deck’s speaker notes, which cite OPTN and SRTR; the textbook predates the 2023 change. If a question says “Lung Allocation Score,” answer the LAS — that is what both the study guide and the book ask for. Treat continuous distribution and the CAS as current-practice context your instructor put on the slide, not as the keyed answer.

Three candidacy details the deck adds

  • Relocation. Once a patient is inside the transplant window and accepted, they are typically relocated to within a set time and/or distance of the transplant and rehabilitation center, and benchmarks must be met before listing — that is, before the patient can actively accept organs. (Hess Ch 46.)
  • The evaluation panel includes a DEXA scan (dual-energy x-ray absorptiometry, for bone density and body composition) and GFR staging: normal ≥ 90; mild 60–89; moderate 30–59; severe 15–29; kidney failure < 15 mL/min/1.73 m². (Deck slide 8 notes — not in Hess.)
  • Retransplant is itself one of the listed indication categories on the deck’s disease-state slide, alongside ILD, IIP, CF, A1-ATD and COPD — a second transplant after failure of the first, which the notes describe as more complex because the recipient may be more sensitised to foreign tissue. (Deck slide 5.)
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 8a deck, slides 5, 8, 12, 13 and speaker notes (OPTN / SRTR) · Hess Ch 46 — Transplant Recipient Criteria; Lung Allocation Score; Table 46-4 (added 2026-10-25)

Respiratory Anatomy (Hess Ch 49 — "EVERYTHING!")

18 items · Everything in Hess Ch 49
