1What the respiratory system does: gas exchange, gas distribution and the five processes of respiration

Hess opens Ch 49 by saying the respiratory system is a gas exchanger as well as a gas distributor, and the Key Points restate it: the primary functions of the respiratory system are gas distribution and gas exchange. Gas exchange happens through respiration, which has five distinct processes: ventilation (movement of air from the atmosphere to the lung), perfusion (movement or circulation of blood through the cardiovascular system), diffusion (movement of O₂ and CO₂ from a relatively high pressure to a low pressure across the alveolar-capillary membrane), external respiration (gas exchange between the alveoli and the blood) and internal respiration (gas exchange between the blood and the tissues). Gas exchange occurs exclusively at the acinus or alveolar level, the functioning portion of the lung at the very end of the respiratory tract. Gas distribution is gas moving through the upper airway and the conducting airways of the lower respiratory tract.

The chapter's basic terms: anatomy = parts, physiology = function, pathophysiology = functional changes in an abnormal condition/disease. You have to know the parts (anatomy) and their function (physiology) before you can understand respiratory disease (pathophysiology).

Term Hess Ch 49 definition
Ventilation movement of air from atmosphere to the lungs
Perfusion movement or circulation of blood
Diffusion movement of gas across the alveolar-capillary membrane
Respiration gas exchange
External respiration gas exchange between alveoli and blood
Internal respiration gas exchange between blood and tissues
SourceHess Ch 49 — Introduction; Respiratory Recap "Basic Terminology" and "Key Respiratory Concepts"; Key Points

2Growth and development: life stages and the five stages of fetal lung development

Table 49-1 (life stages): Embryo = conception to the end of the 8th week of gestation; Fetus = 9th week to birth; Neonate = birth to the end of week 4; Infant = end of week 4 to 1 year; Child = 1 year to puberty; Adolescent = puberty to adulthood; Adult = approximately 18 years to old age; Senescence = old age to death. The lung goes from a single bud, to an infant with some 50 million alveoli, to an adult with a surface area of approximately 70 m² and 300 million alveoli.

Table 49-2, the five stages of lung development (memorize the order: Embryonic → Pseudoglandular → Canalicular → Saccular → Alveolar):

Stage Gestational age (Table 49-2) What develops / why it matters
Embryonic 4–6 weeks Proximal airways (trachea, major bronchi, early segmental bronchi). Begins about 21 days after conception with a lung bud from the foregut; the bud forms the trachea and two bronchial buds (future main stem bronchi); the pharynx becomes the esophagus.
Pseudoglandular 6–16 weeks Conducting airways (smooth muscle, cilia, mucous glands, goblet cells, respiratory bronchioles); near-complete development of the diaphragm. A fetus here is highly unlikely to survive premature delivery.
Canalicular 17–26 weeks Vascular bed and framework of the respiratory acini; a capillary network forms around the air passages; type I and type II alveolar cells appear (alveolar-capillary membrane + surfactant production). Limited gas exchange becomes possible, so a premature fetus can survive with intensive, advanced care.
Saccular 27–36 weeks Gas-exchange units (surfactant present, early alveoli); a marked increase in potential gas-exchanging surface area; terminal airways become saccules that later become alveoli.
Alveolar 36 weeks–beyond birth Rapid alveolar development. Alveoli at birth range 20 to 150 million (average ≈ 50 million); the number peaks at ≈ 300 million by age 8, when alveolar development is considered complete.

Watch the book's inconsistencies. The prose says the embryonic stage "spans approximately 2 months", while Table 49-2 says 4–6 weeks. The Hess glossary also gives different ranges (embryonic "26 days after conception to the end of the fifth week"; pseudoglandular "week 17 to week 26", which copies the canalicular range; saccular "week 26 of gestation to birth"; alveolar "birth to 8 years of age"). For the exam, use Table 49-2, the chapter's own table.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Growth and Development of the Respiratory System, Table 49-1, Table 49-2, Embryonic/Pseudoglandular/Canalicular/Saccular/Alveolar Stage sections, Respiratory Recap "Five Stages of Lung Development" · Hess Glossary (stage entries)

3Birth transition and infant vs adult airway differences

Before the first breath the lung is filled with fluid, which keeps it expanded and helps it grow. The fluid is replenished at approximately 250 to 300 mL per day and flows to the oropharynx, where it is swallowed or expelled into the amniotic fluid. The first breath must be deep and forceful: an exceedingly high transpulmonary pressure gradient is needed, with reports estimating approximately 40 to 80 cm H₂O and a volume of approximately 40 mL to overcome alveolar surface tension and the viscosity of the remaining fluid. Later breaths need less pressure because more alveoli stay inflated. In a normal term infant, fluid moves to the interstitium within several breaths, but capillaries and lymphatics may take several hours to clear it.

Infant vs adult anatomy (Hess): the infant's head is larger and heavier relative to the body, the nasal passages are proportionately smaller, the tongue is much larger relative to the oral cavity, the jaw is rounder, the larynx sits higher in the neck, and the cricoid ring is the narrowest part of the upper airway in the infant vs the glottis in the adult. These differences make the infant a preferential nose breather and increase susceptibility to airway obstruction.

The RCP 105 Airway deck makes the same point and adds why it matters: adults → narrowest at the glottis (vocal cords); children → narrowest at the cricoid ring. The adult airway is cylindrical below the cords, the pediatric airway is funnel-shaped, and 1 mm of circumferential edema in an infant can reduce airway diameter by more than 50% (croup, epiglottitis, post-extubation edema, airway trauma). The deck's notes add that newer MRI/CT studies suggest the pediatric glottis may be as narrow as the cricoid (one reason cuffed ETTs are now routinely used in pediatrics), but for boards: adults = glottis, children = cricoid ring.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Postnatal Lung Development · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 22 (and notes)

4Upper respiratory tract: nose, nasal cavity, sinuses, pharynx

The upper respiratory tract begins at the nose and mouth and ends at the larynx: nose, mouth, nasal and oral cavities, pharynx, tongue, epiglottis, soft and hard palates, and larynx. The lower tract runs from the trachea to the alveoli. (Hess's Respiratory Recap lists the upper-tract components as nasal cavity, oral cavity, pharynx [nasal, oral, laryngeal], larynx. The Key Points list nose, nasopharynx, oropharynx, laryngopharynx and larynx.)

  • Nose / nasal cavity. Air enters through the nostrils (external nares) into a nasal cavity made of bone and cartilage. It is lined by mucus-secreting epithelium called the respiratory mucosa. The nasal conchae (turbinates) are bony ridges projecting laterally into the cavity. The main job of this area is to filter, warm and humidify inspired air. The frontal and sphenoid sinuses lie above the nasal cavity and the hard and soft palates lie below it. Sensory neurons give smell (via the olfactory nerve) and start the sneeze reflex or a sense of breathlessness.
  • Paranasal sinuses. These are hollow spaces in skull bones that act as resonant chambers and help reduce skull weight. They are lined with ciliated epithelium (maxillary, frontal, sphenoid and ethmoid bones). Postnasal drip from infected sinuses irritates the larynx and is a common cause of chronic cough.
  • Pharynx (the throat), three regions. The nasopharynx is lined with ciliated epithelial and goblet cells and carries air only. The oropharynx is lined with stratified squamous epithelium continuous with the oral cavity. The laryngopharynx (hypopharynx) is the most inferior portion. Below the nasopharynx the pharynx carries air and food. Air goes to the larynx because of negative thoracic pressure generated mainly by the diaphragm, and food goes posterior into the esophagus during swallowing.
  • Tonsils, adenoids and uvula. The tonsils are the two masses at the back of the throat. The adenoids are a single pharyngeal tonsil high behind the nose and soft palate, not visible through the mouth. The uvula is the visible projection of the middle of the soft palate. Enlargement of these structures may impede breathing, especially during sleep.
  • Epiglottis. A flap-like cartilage at the back of the tongue that covers the opening to the trachea during swallowing, preventing aspiration.

The RCP 105 Airway deck divides the airway into five regions: nose and nasopharynx, oral cavity and oropharynx, hypopharynx, larynx, and tracheobronchial tree. It lists the oral cavity/oropharynx as the teeth, tongue, buccal mucosa, faucial pillars, hard palate, soft palate, uvula, tonsils and posterior pharyngeal wall.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Gross Anatomy of the Respiratory System, Table 49-3, Upper Respiratory Tract, Mucociliary Clearance (sinuses), Key Points · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slides 20, 99

5Larynx and glottis

The larynx is a cartilaginous passageway for air between the pharynx and trachea (the "voice box"; it houses the vocal cords). It produces sound and helps filter, warm and moisten air. Its landmarks (Hess gives no full vertical order; the hyoid is above the larynx, the epiglottis is the uppermost cartilage, and the cricoid lies below the thyroid cartilage):

Structure Key fact (Hess Ch 49)
Hyoid bone Seated above the larynx
Epiglottis Uppermost cartilage of the larynx; valve-like, closes the entry to the larynx to prevent aspiration
Thyroid cartilage Largest laryngeal cartilage; more prominent in men (Adam's apple)
Cricoid cartilage The only complete ring of cartilage around the airway; located below the thyroid cartilage
Cricothyroid membrane Between the thyroid and cricoid cartilages; can be opened in an emergency to reach the lower airway
Corniculates and arytenoids Small paired cartilages that complete the posterior larynx
Vocal cords / glottis Cords = mucosal folds supported by elastic ligaments; the opening between them is the glottis (glottic opening), a triangular slit. The glottis closes during swallowing, and the cords vibrate to make sound.

The Hess glossary defines the glottis as the laryngeal opening where the vocal cords reside, which separates the upper and lower airways. Deck link: the RCP 105 Airway deck states that creating an airway through the cricothyroid membrane is a cricothyrotomy.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Upper Respiratory Tract, Table 49-3 · Hess Glossary ("glottis") · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 63

6Trachea, carina and the main stem bronchi (angles)

The trachea ("windpipe") bifurcates at the carina into the two primary (main stem) bronchi and marks the beginning of the conducting system. Because it looks like an inverted tree, it is called the tracheobronchial tree.

Trachea fact Number (Hess Ch 49)
Length 10 to 13 cm
Cartilage support 16 to 20 C-shaped cartilages, which keep it open during the negative pressure of inspiration
Adult diameter approximately 2.0 to 2.5 cm
Lining Pseudostratified ciliated columnar epithelium (trachea and most of the tree); smoking destroys the cilia
Right main stem angle 20 to 30 degrees from vertical (smaller angle than the left)
Left main stem angle 45 to 55 degrees

The bifurcation is asymmetric. Because the right main stem leaves at the smaller angle, foreign bodies are more likely to be aspirated into the right bronchus, and suction catheters more commonly advance into the right lung.

Deck links. For an ETT, the RCP 203 deck says the tip should ideally sit 4–6 cm above the carina (head neutral), and that too deep usually enters the right mainstem bronchus. Hess adds: Hess Ch 7 also says "approximately 4 to 6 cm above the carina", but Hess Ch 17 says "3 to 7 cm" (neck neutral) for adults. Use 4–6 cm on this exam, the instructor's deck. The RCP 105 Monitoring deck notes that uneven bilateral breath sounds without lung pathology may indicate main-stem intubation. On bronchoscopy, the scope goes trachea → carina → right and left mainstem → lobar → segmental bronchi, and a "sharp carina" is a normal finding.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Lower Respiratory Tract · RCP 203 Lssn 2a — Ch 2 Clinical Assessment, slide 57 (and notes) · Hess Ch 7 — Evaluation of Tubes and Catheters · Hess Ch 17 (tube position) · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 16 (notes) · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 10 (notes)

7Bronchial tree branching, generations, and the conducting vs respiratory zones

The main bronchi divide by dichotomous branching: each airway splits into two progressively smaller airways. The order is lobar bronchi → segmental bronchi → approximately 40 subsegmental bronchi. The lobar bronchi correspond to the 5 lobes and the segmental bronchi to the 18 segments. Hundreds of smaller bronchi then branch into thousands of bronchioles. Up to the bronchioles, airways are supported by cartilage. Bronchioles are < 1 mm in diameter and contain no cartilage; they stay open during exhalation through the retractile (elastic) forces of the lung parenchyma. Terminal bronchioles number approximately 30,000 to 40,000, are approximately 0.65 mm in diameter, and are the last component of the conducting zone. The respiratory bronchioles, alveolar ducts, alveolar sacs and alveoli form the acinus or respiratory zone, surrounded by a rich capillary network where gas exchange occurs.

Zone Structures Generations Function
Conducting zone Trachea → bronchi → lobar → segmental/subsegmental → bronchioles → terminal bronchioles Figure 49-6: 0 (trachea) through 16 (terminal bronchioles) Moves (distributes) air; no gas exchange
Respiratory zone (acinus) Respiratory bronchioles, alveolar ducts, alveolar sacs, alveoli Text: "16th to 23rd generations". Figure 49-6: respiratory bronchioles 17–19, alveolar ducts 20–22, alveolar sacs 23 Gas exchange

Book conflict (generation numbers). Hess's prose puts the respiratory zone at the 16th–23rd generations, but the book's Figure 49-6 description puts terminal bronchioles at 16 and the respiratory zone at 17–23. The prose sentence that introduces Figure 49-6 even says "conducting zone (trachea to respiratory bronchioles)". No deck gives zone generation numbers, so the instructor has not settled this. Know both: the text's "16th to 23rd" is the phrase most likely to be quoted, and the figure's 0–16 / 17–23 split matches the text's statement that terminal bronchioles are the last conducting airway. Table 49-4 (diameters and cross-sectional areas by generation) is an image only in the text file, so its numbers cannot be reproduced here.

Deck links. The RCP 104 Aerosol deck gives the same split: conducting zone = air movement only (warms, humidifies, filters; no gas exchange), from nose/upper airway to bronchioles "up to terminal bronchioles"; respiratory zone = gas exchange (respiratory bronchioles, alveolar ducts, alveolar sacs). The RCP 104 Mucus deck's notes add that alveolar ducts plus respiratory bronchioles account for around 10% of gas exchange and the alveoli for the remaining 90%. It also places submucosal glands in the cartilaginous airways (generations 1–14). The RCP 105 Principles of MV deck: anatomic dead space ≈ 1 mL per lb of body weight (150-lb person ≈ 150 mL). Worked example: a 180-lb patient has about 180 × 1 mL = 180 mL of anatomic dead space.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Lower Respiratory Tract, Figure 49-5, Figure 49-6 (and its description text), Table 49-4 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 3 — Aerosolized Drugs, slide 13 (notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slides 9, 10 (notes) · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 15

8The lungs: lobes, fissures, segments and gross facts

The lungs are cone-shaped, with a broad, concave base on the diaphragm. There are 5 lobes and 18 segments. Normal adult lungs weigh about 800 g and are about 90% gas and 10% tissue. The apices extend from above the clavicle to the first vertebra. At quiet end-expiration, the anterior lung borders the sixth rib. The hilum, on the medial (mediastinal) surface, is where the bronchi and pulmonary vessels enter (glossary: vessels and nerves). The right lung is larger and heavier because the left shares its hemithorax with the heart.

  • Right lung: 3 lobes (upper, middle, lower). The horizontal fissure separates the middle and upper lobes, and the oblique fissure separates the middle and lower lobes.
  • Left lung: 2 lobes (upper, lower), separated by an oblique fissure.
Lung Lobe Segments (Table 49-5)
Right (10) Upper Apical, Anterior, Posterior
Middle Lateral, Medial
Lower Superior, Anterior basal, Posterior basal, Lateral basal, Medial basal
Left (8) Upper Apical posterior, Anterior, Superior lingular, Inferior lingular
Lower Superior, Anterior medial basal, Lateral basal, Posterior basal

(The extracted Table 49-5 text is misaligned. This grouping follows the book's Figure 49-10 description, which places "Middle lobe; Medial and lateral" and the right inferior lobe's "Superior" segment, and the RCP 105 deck's "RML medial and lateral segments" and "RLL superior segment".) Knowing lobes and segments matters for bronchoscopy and for positioning patients for postural drainage.

Deck links (RCP 105 Monitoring in MV): proper identification of the lung segments is essential for charting and for correct chest percussion and postural drainage. The LUL lingula segments look like the RML medial and lateral segments on the lateral view. A lesion in the lingula or the LLL superior segment can look alike on a PA film, and the lateral view isolates the location.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Lungs, Table 49-5, Figure 49-10 (description text) · Hess Glossary ("hilum", "lobes") · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slides 16 (notes), 17 (notes), 18 (notes), 19, 20

9Alveoli, the alveolar-capillary membrane, and alveolar cell types (type I vs type II)

Adults have approximately 300 million alveoli, each 200 to 300 microns in diameter. Pulmonary capillaries cover 85% to 90% of the alveolar surface area. The alveolar sacs arising from a single terminal bronchiole make up a primary lobule. The lungs have approximately 130,000 primary lobules, each containing 2000 alveoli. Capillary blood and alveolar gas are separated by the vascular endothelium, interstitial space and alveolar epithelium, and gas crosses the alveolar-capillary membrane by diffusion.

Cell Shape / coverage Function and facts
Type I (squamous) pneumocyte Flat; smallest and most abundant. Covers 90% to 95% of the alveolar surface (another passage says approximately 95%; glossary says 90%). Thickness 0.1 to 0.5 micron Major site of alveolar gas exchange; gases cross easily. Sensitive to injury: high O₂, bacteria, bleomycin, cyclophosphamide, particulates
Type II (granular) pneumocyte Cuboidal; one passage calls them small and cuboidal, another says larger and more irregularly shaped than type I; covers the remaining 5% to 10% (another passage says only 5%). Sits in the corners of alveolar sacs Primary source of surfactant. Differentiates into type I cells when those are damaged. Transports sodium and water toward the endothelium/blood. Forms tight junctions with type I cells to keep fluid out
Alveolar macrophage ("type III cell") (see item 17) Defensive: removes bacteria from the acini

Surfactant: 90% phospholipids and 10% protein. The main surface-tension-lowering agent is dipalmitoyl phosphatidylcholine (DPPC), which has both hydrophobic and hydrophilic parts. Surfactant lowers surface tension at end-expiration and prevents alveolar collapse. Alveolar surface tension ranges from 5 to 15 dynes/cm (small alveolus) to 50 dynes/cm (large, fully distended). Hess states that as the radius gets smaller, surface tension decreases, and as it grows larger, surface tension increases; as alveolar area decreases, the surfactant molecules crowd together and begin to repel one another. Absence of surfactant → atelectasis. Conditions with surfactant deficiency or type II instability: neonatal RDS, congenital surfactant deficiency, pulmonary alveolar proteinosis.

Collateral ventilation: adjacent alveoli communicate through the pores of Kohn and the channels of Lambert. In the glossary, the channels of Lambert connect alveoli and adjacent terminal bronchioles.

Safety factors that keep fluid out of the alveoli: (1) tight junctions between alveolar cells; (2) water transport from the alveoli into the pulmonary vessels, because pulmonary hydrostatic pressure is low; (3) surfactant and its proteins, which repel water; (4) an active defense system in the alveolar sacs; (5) an extensive lymphatic system draining the interstitium.

Deck links. RCP 103 ILD: type I cells form the thin gas-exchange barrier; type II cells produce surfactant and can proliferate during injury. RCP 103 ARDS: damaged or dying type II cells → ↓ surfactant → worse alveolar flooding and collapse. RCP 103 COPD: in emphysema, collateral ventilation through the pores of Kohn and canals of Lambert helps compensate.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Lungs; Alveolar Cells; Respiratory Recap "Alveolar Cells" · Hess Glossary ("type I cells", "channels of Lambert", "pores of Kohn") · RCP 103 ILD, slide 4 (notes) · RCP 103 Diffuse Alveolar Disease — ARDS, slides 5, 6 (notes) · RCP 103 COPD PP, slide 6

10Interstitium and pulmonary lymphatics

Interstitium. This is the space between the alveolar epithelium and the endothelial cells of the vasculature. It is a continuum from the visceral pleura to the hilum, where it connects to the mediastinum, and it gives mechanical support and helps keep fluid balance. Its cells are fibroblasts, myofibroblasts, smooth muscle cells, pericytes, lymphatic endothelial cells, and inflammatory/immune cells (lymphocytes, mast cells, macrophages). Its gel has two compartments. Tight space lies between the alveolar epithelium and the capillary endothelium. Loose space surrounds the bronchioles, respiratory bronchi, alveolar ducts and alveolar sacs. The matrix holds elastin and collagen fibers, fibronectin, proteoglycans and basement-membrane constituents. Collagen limits alveolar distention "beyond hazardous limits" and makes up 15% to 20% of the lung's dry weight. Elastin forms a 3-D network that gives elastic recoil. Elastases or alpha-1 antitrypsin deficiency can cause considerable lung damage. Interstitial lung disease = more cells in the interstitium and a thicker interstitium. (The RCP 103 ILD deck: the thickened barrier reduces diffusion capacity, causing hypoxemia.)

Lymphatics. The lungs and pleura are well endowed with lymphatics. The open-ended lymphatic vessels lie just beneath the visceral pleura and mainly remove excess fluid and protein leaking from capillaries; they also remove bacteria and foreign products. They exit at the hilum. Lymph (a clear or milky fluid called chyle) drains to the mediastinal lymph nodes, which store it until it returns to the circulation via the thoracic duct.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Interstitial Space; Lungs (lymphatic paragraph); Key Points · RCP 103 ILD, slide 4 (notes)

11Pleurae, pleural space and mediastinum

Structure Facts (Hess Ch 49)
Visceral pleura Covers the lung surface and extends into the fissures. Innervated by the vagus nerve. No pain sensation (lacks nerves of general sensation)
Parietal pleura Lines the chest wall; at the medial surface the visceral pleura reflects onto the mediastinum to become part of the parietal pleura. Innervated by the intercostal and phrenic nerves. Extremely sensitive to irritation; in pleurisy, pain may be referred to the root of the neck and over the shoulder
Lining cells Ciliated mesothelial cells line both pleurae
Pleural space Between the two pleurae; a small amount of thin fluid, < 20 mL, with low protein
Fluid balance Leakage from systemic and pulmonary capillaries vs drainage by pleural lymphatics, which can handle approximately 700 mL per day
Function of the fluid Frictionless sliding plus a tight lung–chest wall junction (like a drop of water between two glass slides)
Pressure The lungs' tendency to collapse and the chest wall's tendency to expand keep pleural pressure negative (below atmospheric). It was first measured by a catheter in the pleural space and is now estimated with an esophageal balloon

The pleurae isolate the lungs from the heart, which sits in its own container, the pericardium.

Mediastinum. The area between the two pleural sacs, divided into superior and inferior regions and anterior, middle and posterior regions. Everything in the thorax except the lungs and pleurae is in the mediastinum. The superior mediastinum holds the thymus, great vessels, esophagus, trachea, thoracic duct, and nerves including the vagus and esophageal plexus. The other regions hold the heart (in the pericardium), main bronchi, great vessels, phrenic nerves, and parts of the thymus and esophagus. The thorax as a whole has three regions: the mediastinum, a right pleural cavity and a left pleural cavity.

Deck link (RCP 103 Pleura). Each pleural cavity normally holds about 0.26 mL of fluid per kg of body weight. The deck's model: hydrostatic pressure is higher in the parietal pleura (pushes fluid into the space), oncotic pressure is equal on both pleurae, and lymphatic drainage, mainly from the parietal pleura, removes excess fluid. The deck's per-kg figure and Hess's "< 20 mL" do not contradict each other: for a 70-kg adult, 0.26 × 70 ≈ 18 mL.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Anatomy of the Thorax; Pleurae; Figure 49-12 · RCP 103 Disorders of the Pleura & Chest Wall, slide 3 (and notes)

12Bony thorax and chest-wall mechanics

The bony thorax = sternum, ribs, thoracic vertebrae, clavicles and scapulae. It supports and protects the heart and lungs, helps expand and relax the chest, and stabilizes the chest wall during changes in intrapleural pressure. The chest wall (skin, ribs, intercostal muscles) protects the lungs. The vertebrae allow movement, rotation and elevation of the ribs.

  • Sternum, 3 parts: manubrium (connects to ribs 1–2), body (connects directly to ribs 3–7), and xiphoid process (the tip).
  • 12 pairs of ribs, matching their vertebrae of origin. Ribs 1–7 are important in ventilation. They lift like bucket handles (outward and upward) while the sternum rises like a pump handle. The lower ribs rotate toward the back.
  • AP diameter. Normally the AP diameter is about half the width of the chest. In obstructive disease (chronic bronchitis, emphysema), air trapping increases the AP diameter, producing a "barrel chest". The RCP 103 decks say the same: barrel chest occurs in COPD due to chronic air trapping, and the emphysema "pink puffer" has a barrel chest (↑ AP diameter).
SourceHess Ch 49 — Anatomy of the Thorax; Bony Thorax; Respiratory Recap "Bony Elements of the Thorax" · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 24 (notes) · RCP 103 COPD PP, slide 6

13Muscles of ventilation: diaphragm (primary) and accessory muscles of inspiration and expiration

Diaphragm (the major/primary muscle). It provides the primary force for the work of breathing. It is normally dome-shaped and attaches to the large vertebrae, the ribs and the xiphoid process; its fibers join a broad sheet called the central tendon. On inspiration it contracts and flattens: the central tendon is drawn down, intrathoracic pressure decreases, and a pressure difference with the atmosphere is created. Its rib attachments create a zone of apposition, which moves the thorax outward during inspiration. Exhalation = elastic recoil of the lungs + relaxation of the diaphragm. Excursion is ≈ 1.5 cm in normal tidal breathing and up to 6 to 10 cm per breath under high stress and work of breathing.

Phrenic nerve. The diaphragm's major nerve supply exits the cervical region at C3–C5. A hiccup is a reflex spasm of the diaphragm from phrenic nerve irritation. Hess says cervical fractures between C1 and C5 are likely to affect the phrenic nerve and impair breathing. Deck link (RCP 103 Neuro): C3 through C5 innervate the diaphragm via the phrenic nerve, and injury at or above this level → diaphragmatic paralysis. Thoracic cord injuries impair intercostal and accessory muscle function and reduce cough. After spinal cord injury, ventilation becomes diaphragm-dependent, giving rapid, shallow breathing and paradoxical abdominal breathing (chest wall moves inward on inspiration).

Group Muscles (Hess Ch 49) Role
Primary Diaphragm Main inspiratory muscle
Accessory muscles of inspiration Scalene, sternocleidomastoid, pectoralis major, trapezius, external intercostals Expand the chest wall. External intercostals lift the ribs. Recruited with exercise and increased effort
Accessory muscles of expiration Internal intercostals, rectus abdominis, external abdominis oblique, internal abdominis oblique, transverse abdominis Exhalation is normally passive. These work when expiratory resistance is significant or ventilatory demand is high (exercise), and in coughing or sneezing. Internal intercostals fix the chest wall

Clinical signs: retraction at the notch above the clavicles during inspiration is one way to spot respiratory distress, and contraction of the abdomen = active exhalation. In severe obstructive disease, both inspiratory and expiratory accessory muscles are often active. Deck link (RCP 103 Clinical Data): accessory muscle use (sternocleidomastoid, scalene, intercostal) occurs when normal breathing is insufficient, commonly with increased airway resistance or respiratory distress. (The Hess glossary lists additional accessory muscles, including pectoralis minor, serratus anterior and erector spinae. The chapter text uses the five listed above.)

SourceHess Ch 49 — Respiratory Muscles; Respiratory Recap "Muscles of Ventilation"; Figure 49-9 · Hess Glossary ("accessory muscles", "phrenic nerve") · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slides 49, 50 (notes) · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 26 (notes)

14Innervation of the respiratory system (summary)

Nerve / supply What it serves (source)
Phrenic nerve (C3–C5) Motor supply to the diaphragm (Hess Ch 49; glossary: "exit the third through fifth cervical vertebrae")
Vagus nerve Visceral pleura, which has no pain sensation (Hess Ch 49). The vagus and esophageal plexus also run in the superior mediastinum
Intercostal and phrenic nerves Parietal pleura, which is very pain-sensitive (Hess Ch 49)
Olfactory nerve Sense of smell from the nasal cavity; nasal sensory neurons also start sneezes (Hess Ch 49)
Nerve endings in the pulmonary vasculature Release acetylcholine and norepinephrine, which affect vascular tone (Hess Ch 49)
Parasympathetic (cholinergic) Submucosal glands are under parasympathetic control and increase mucus with cholinergic stimulation. Surface goblet cells do not seem to be directly innervated (RCP 104 Mucus deck)
SourceHess Ch 49 — Respiratory Muscles; Pleurae; Upper Respiratory Tract; Airway and Vascular Smooth Muscle · Hess Glossary ("phrenic nerve") · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slides 14, 15

15Mucociliary apparatus (mucociliary clearance)

Normal tidal volume is ≈ 400 to 500 mL, but breathing 12,000 to 24,000 liters of air per day exposes the lungs to pollutants, viruses, bacteria and antigens. The body responds with coughing, sneezing, bronchoconstriction and more mucus. The primary defense in the conducting airways is the pseudostratified ciliated columnar epithelium, which works as an escalator of mucus. Goblet cells secrete mucus that catches debris. The cilia beat in a watery sol layer, and a mucous gel layer sits on top. The proportion of ciliated cells increases farther down the bronchi. Bronchioles also contain cuboidal cells and secretory Clara cells. Under the epithelium lie fibroblasts, nerves, lymphatics and smooth muscle (nose to bronchioles). Mucus boluses move upward and are expelled or, most often, swallowed.

The apparatus's most important role is maintaining fluid homeostasis, and dehydration prevents patients from adequately producing secretions. Mucoglycoproteins cause the viscosity. Things that impair mucociliary transport: smoking, cystic fibrosis, COPD, asthma, plus drugs (narcotics, ethyl alcohol, atropine, aspirin, β-adrenergic antagonists, inhaled and IV anesthetics).

Number Hess Ch 49 RCP 104 Mucus deck Use
Ciliated : goblet cell ratio about 4:1 approximately 5:1 Conflict; see conflicts file. The deck is the instructor's → 5:1
Cilia per ciliated cell 200 to 250 200 Compatible (200 is in Hess's range)
Cilium length about 6 microns (trachea); diameter about 2 microns 7 µm in larger airways, 5 µm or less in smaller bronchioles Conflict → deck 7 µm (large) / ≤ 5 µm (small)
Ciliary beat frequency 1000 to 1500 per minute, faster in larger airways not given Hess
Secretion volume 10 to 100 mL/day expelled 100 mL/24 hours secreted Close; the deck says 100 mL/24 h
Goblet cell density not given 6,000 goblet cells/mm² Deck

Deck adds (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9). The airway mucus has two phases: a gel layer (0.5–20 µm) that is propelled toward the larynx by the cilia, and a periciliary (sol) layer about the height of a fully extended cilium (≈ 7–10 micrometers per the notes). A functional surfactant layer at the cilia tips separates the periciliary fluid from the mucus gel. The cilia have an effective (power) stroke and a recovery stroke. The mucociliary system's structures are pseudostratified columnar ciliated cells, surface goblet cells, club (formerly Clara) cells in the distal airway, and submucosal glands (with mucous [thick] and serous [thin] cells). The deck's bracket glosses μm as "micromoles", but its notes say "micrometers", so read µm = micrometers.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Mucociliary Clearance; Figure 49-13; Respiratory Recap "Mucociliary Apparatus" · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 9 — Mucus-Controlling Agents, slides 9 (and notes), 14, 18, 23

16Airway and vascular smooth muscle; the pulmonary circulation's tone

Both the airways and the pulmonary vasculature are lined with smooth muscle, and its control is involuntary. Contracting smooth muscle cells become shorter and wider. Airways, pulmonary vasculature, mast cells and epithelial cells can alter tone.

The pulmonary vasculature has less smooth muscle than systemic arteries, especially the pulmonary artery bed. Pulmonary vascular tone, pressure and resistance are low compared with the systemic circulation. Smooth muscle directs regional blood flow by changing vessel caliber. Vasoactive chemicals come from endothelial cells, nerves, mast cells or other organs. Nerve endings release acetylcholine and norepinephrine, and mast cells produce histamine and leukotrienes. Hypercapnia, a decrease in pH and, particularly, hypoxia cause pulmonary vasoconstriction, and hypoxia acts directly on smooth muscle cells. This response exists to match alveolar ventilation and perfusion and maximize gas exchange.

Ch 49 does not describe the gross anatomy of the pulmonary arteries/veins or the bronchial circulation; see the gaps file.

SourceHess Ch 49 — Airway and Vascular Smooth Muscle

17Mast cells, macrophages and dendritic cells

Cell Hess Ch 49 facts Recap label
Mast cell 10 to 20 microns, oval or irregular; found in respiratory mucosal surfaces and alveolar septa; abundant granules. Mediators in 3 classes: (1) granule-associated (histamine, serotonin); (2) membrane-derived lipid (leukotrienes); (3) cytokines. In asthma, mast cell numbers are elevated, as are protease and histamine levels in BAL fluid. Mast-cell stabilization and anti-mediator drugs help prevent asthma symptoms Cells that release mediators
Macrophage (alveolar macrophage, "type III cell") Derived from bone marrow stem cells. Three functions: phagocytosis, antigen presentation, cytokine production. The most significant cytokines are IL-1 and TNF. Removes bacteria from the acini Phagocytic cells
Dendritic cell Also bone-marrow derived; mobile, irregular shape with long processes; few in number in the airway epithelium and parenchyma. Main role: antigen-presenting cell. It takes up antigens, migrates to lung-draining lymph nodes and presents them to T lymphocytes, activating them. Numbers rise dramatically with chronic irritant exposure, and inhaled or systemic steroids reduce them Antigen-presenting cells
SourceHess Ch 49 — Mast Cell, Macrophages, and Dendritic Cells; Lungs (type III cells); Respiratory Recap "Mast Cells, Macrophages, and Dendritic Cells"

18Hess Ch 49 Key Points (reproduced; high-yield, beyond the listed asks)

  1. The primary functions of the respiratory system are gas distribution and gas exchange.
  2. The five stages of fetal lung development are embryonic, pseudoglandular, canalicular, saccular and alveolar.
  3. Postnatal development leads to significant anatomic differences between the infant/child and the adult.
  4. Key upper-airway structures: nose, nasopharynx, oropharynx, laryngopharynx and larynx.
  5. Key lower-airway structures: trachea, main stem bronchi, bronchioles and alveoli.
  6. The lungs consist of the airways and alveoli.
  7. The bony thorax consists of the sternum, ribs, thoracic vertebrae, clavicles and scapulae.
  8. The respiratory muscles are the diaphragm, accessory muscles of inspiration and accessory muscles of expiration.
  9. The visceral pleurae surround the lungs, the parietal pleurae line the chest wall, and the pleural space lies between them.
  10. The mucociliary apparatus clears inhaled agents from the lower respiratory tract.
  11. Important lower-tract cells include mast cells, macrophages and dendritic cells.
  12. The alveolus is composed of type I and type II cells.
  13. The interstitial space lies between the alveolar epithelia and the vascular endothelia.
SourceHess Ch 49 — Key Points
13

Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (CPR) ("EVERYTHING!")

17 items · Everything · pulse checks (adult, child, infant) · best chance of survival · head-tilt–chin-lift vs jaw thrust · MOANS

1Check pulses where: adult, child (pediatric) and infant2What ensures the best chance of survival3Head-tilt–chin-lift vs jaw-thrust maneuver4MOANS: predicting difficult bag-mask ventilation (deck and Box 18-3)5Chain of survival6C-A-B (CABD) sequence and how it evolved7High-quality chest compressions: rate, depth, hand position and recoil by age8Compression-to-ventilation ratios (1 and 2 rescuers) and rescue-breathing rates, by age9Ventilation during CPR (without and with an advanced airway)10Bag-valve-mask (manual resuscitator) technique11AED and defibrillation: shockable rhythms, energy, pads, and when to shock12Drugs during CPR (epinephrine, amiodarone and the rest) and routes13Capnography (PETCO₂) during CPR: targets and ROSC14Advanced airways during CPR, and the impedance threshold valve15Post–cardiac arrest care16Choking (foreign body airway obstruction) by age17Definitions, risk factors, newborns and ethics

Main source: Hess Ch 18 (2015 AHA Guidelines). The RCP 203 Ch 9 deck covers airway opening and MOANS (items 3–4) and leads there. Heuer Ch 15 is not in the vault (see gaps). Several numbers below come from the descriptions of Hess's algorithm figures, which are printed at the back of the book's text file. They are cited as "Algorithm 18-x (description)".

1Check pulses where: adult, child (pediatric) and infant

Patient (Hess age definition) Where to check the pulse How long Who gets compressions
Adult (at and beyond puberty) Carotid artery, on the side of the neck nearest the rescuer No more than 10 seconds No pulse, or uncertain → start 30 compressions immediately
Child (1 year to puberty, about 12–14 yr) Carotid or femoral artery (Hess's general circulation paragraph says "adult or child → carotid") Less than 10 seconds Start CPR when the pulse is < 60 beats/min (with poor perfusion despite oxygenation and ventilation, per Algorithm 18-3)
Infant (neonates through ≈ 1 year) Brachial artery: index and middle fingers on the inside of the upper arm. The infant's short neck makes the carotid hard to palpate (same < 10-s rule as Algorithm 18-3) Heart rate absent or < 60 beats/min with signs of poor perfusion (cyanosis, pallor) despite oxygenation and ventilation = cardiac arrest

Other pulse facts: the effectiveness of compressions is judged by a second person palpating the carotid or femoral pulse during compressions. In the adult BLS algorithm the provider checks breathing and pulse simultaneously, asking "is a pulse definitively felt within 10 seconds?" Agonal gasps are not adequate breathing. Compressions given to someone later found not to be in arrest rarely cause significant injury, so do not hesitate when unsure. In the unresponsive choking adult or child, start compressions and do not check for a pulse (Table 18-3).

SourceHess Ch 18 — Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (age definitions); Circulation; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for a Child; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant; Figures 18-2, 18-22; Respiratory Recap "Child CPR"; Algorithm 18-2 and 18-3 (description); Table 18-3; The Initial Steps

2What ensures the best chance of survival

Hess's direct answer: "When a patient experiences a cardiopulmonary arrest, the most important determinant of the patient's survival is the presence of a trained rescuer who is ready, willing, and able to do basic life support." Speed is what makes the rescuer matter:

  • Early CPR and early ALS. The highest hospital discharge rates occur when CPR is started within 4 minutes of arrest and advanced life support within 8 minutes.
  • Early defibrillation. The time from collapse to defibrillation is one of the most important determinants of survival. Rapid defibrillation is the cornerstone of treatment for VF and pulseless VT. Hess: survival falls 7% to 10% with each passing moment of untreated VF. The RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 deck gives the time unit: survival decreases by 7–10% for every minute from symptom onset to defibrillation, and with CPR only 3–4% per minute. CPR buys time; defibrillation is MOST important for VF and pulseless VT, and "drugs don't fix dead — CPR and shock do."
  • High-quality, uninterrupted compressions. Any interruption lowers coronary and cerebral perfusion pressure and raises the death rate. No adjunct or device other than a defibrillator has consistently improved long-term out-of-hospital survival.
  • Factors that determine outcome (Key Point): initial rhythm, time to CPR, time to defibrillation of VF/pulseless VT, duration of CPR, and whether the arrest was witnessed.

Survival numbers (Hess). About 326,000 out-of-hospital arrests per year in the US (15% of all deaths), with out-of-hospital survival 1% to 16% in one study. In-hospital: 209,000 adults and > 6000 children per year, with 22.3% of adults and 39.4% of children (excluding neonates) surviving to discharge. The AHA median hospital survival is 18% for adults and 36% for children. A 2015 IOM report: 5.5% survival outside the hospital vs 24% in hospital. The RCP 105 Critical Issues deck (WHO 2002 data) gives ROSC in 17–49% of resuscitated patients, but about 80% of initial survivors stay comatose for a time, about 40% remain in a persistent vegetative state, and 80% are dead at one year.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Summary of Basic Life Support; Chain of Survival; Automated External Defibrillation; Current Guideline Developments; Circulation; Incidence and Epidemiology; History; Key Points · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 — Vasopressors, Inotropes, slide 46 (and notes) · RCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slide 9 (notes)

3Head-tilt–chin-lift vs jaw-thrust maneuver

Deck answer (RCP 203 Ch 9, slides 5–6 notes): head-tilt/chin-lift opens the airway by moving the tongue away from the posterior pharynx and is used when there is no suspected C-spine injury. Jaw thrust is preferred when cervical spine injury is suspected because it minimizes neck movement; it opens the airway without extending the neck. The deck's NBRC pearl: suspected C-spine injury → jaw thrust.

Hess adds: Posterior displacement of the tongue is the most common cause of airway obstruction in the unconscious person. Loss of control of the submandibular muscles lets the tongue drop and block the oropharynx. Because the tongue is attached to the lower jaw, moving the jaw forward with either maneuver lifts the tongue away from the posterior pharynx. Which maneuver you use depends on whether the patient may have a cervical spine injury. About 2% of blunt trauma victims have a spinal cord injury, and the risk triples with a craniofacial injury.

Head-tilt–chin-lift Jaw-thrust
When Routine airway opening in CPR (no suspected spine injury) Possible cervical spine injury (avoids further injury)
Rescuer position To the side of the patient Behind the top of the patient's head
Hands Palm of one hand on the forehead tilts the head backward; fingers of the other hand under the bony part of the lower jaw near the chin lift the chin forward Fingers of both hands under the angles of both jaws; move the mandible forward and upward
If it fails Reposition If the airway is still obstructed after a jaw thrust, gently tilt the head backward until the airway opens

Infants: open the airway with the head-tilt–chin-lift, without pressing the fingers deeply into the soft tissue under the chin. Tilting the head back beyond a neutral or "sniffing" position may block the airway. Improper opening of the infant airway is the most common cause of inadequate ventilation during infant resuscitation. After opening: inspect for foreign objects, vomit or blood, and remove visible objects with a finger sweep or suction; never perform a blind finger sweep. A breathing patient goes into the recovery position. Deck link (RCP 103 Neuro, head injury): airway assessment first, with cervical spine precautions; temporary airway support = jaw thrust, BVM, supraglottic airway.

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 5 (notes), 6 (notes) · Hess Ch 18 — Airway; Figures 18-5, 18-6, 18-7; Respiratory Recap "Primary Airway Management During CPR"; Breathing; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant · RCP 103 Neuro-Respiratory Disorders, slide 64

4MOANS: predicting difficult bag-mask ventilation (deck and Box 18-3)

Letter Deck: RCP 203 Ch 9, slide 11 notes (use this) Hess Box 18-3
M Mask seal: facial hair, facial trauma/deformity, secretions/vomit, or poor mask fit Mask seal / Male gender / Mallampati Class III or IV
O Obesity/Obstruction: obesity increases upper-airway collapsibility Obesity / Obstruction
A Aged: changes in facial anatomy/tissue and airway mechanics (no age number) Age > 55 years
N No teeth: cheeks collapse inward, hard to get a tight mask seal No teeth
S Stiff lungs: higher pressures needed for an adequate VT (ARDS, pulmonary edema, severe bronchospasm, decreased compliance) Stiff lung / Snoring

Which tool predicts what (deck, slides 11–12): Mallampati → difficulty with INTUBATION ("look in the MOUTH"); MOANS → difficulty with MASK VENTILATION ("can I MASK ventilate?"); LEMON → "can I INTUBATE?" A difficult intubation and difficult BVM ventilation are NOT necessarily the same thing. In the deck, Mallampati is a separate tool, not part of the M, and "Aged" carries no number; Hess's > 55 and snoring are textbook additions. Once bagging starts, the deck's signs of difficulty are absent/inadequate chest movement or breath sounds, gastric air entry or dilatation, cyanosis or inadequate SpO₂, and absent or inadequate exhaled CO₂. Early hypoxemia can cause tachycardia and hypertension; worsening hypoxemia can eventually produce bradycardia, hypotension, and cardiac arrest.

Hess adds: Difficult mask ventilation = being unable to keep SpO₂ > 90% with 100% O₂ and positive-pressure ventilation. Other signs include cyanosis, absent exhaled CO₂, absent breath sounds or chest movement, auscultatory signs of severe obstruction, gastric air entry, and hemodynamic changes of hypoxemia/hypercarbia (hypertension, tachycardia, arrhythmias). Physical risk factors: age > 55, BMI > 30, history of snoring, male gender, Mallampati III–IV, lack of teeth, presence of a beard. Predictors include an inadequate mask seal (excess leak) and excess resistance to gas entry. Table 18-4: upper-airway obstruction causes = large tongue, small pharyngeal space, tonsillar hyperplasia, airway edema, excess oropharyngeal tissue, oropharyngeal collapse (trauma, morbid obesity, neck/oral tumors). Lower-airway causes = tracheal/bronchial/mediastinal tumors, severe bronchospasm, noncompliant lungs, bronchopleural fistula. Equipment problems = wrong mask size or a partially deflated mask cushion, poor head/neck position, or the need for a properly sized oropharyngeal airway. Practical fixes: keep dentures in and place an OPA for a tight seal. With multiple predictors, switch to two-person ventilation and possibly move quickly to an advanced airway (fiberoptic intubation).

SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 11 (and notes), 12 · Hess Ch 18 — Difficult Bag-Mask Ventilation; Box 18-3; Table 18-4

5Chain of survival

The 2015 Guidelines replaced the single chain with two chains: in-hospital (IHCA) and out-of-hospital (OHCA). The IHCA chain's primary providers are the code team, cath lab and ICU; the OHCA chain runs lay rescuers → EMS → ED → cath lab → ICU. The links are interdependent: each link's success depends on the ones before it.

The five in-hospital links:

  1. Surveillance and prevention
  2. Recognition and activation of the emergency response system
  3. Immediate high-quality CPR
  4. Rapid defibrillation
  5. Advanced life support and post-arrest care

High-quality CPR in this section means a rate of 100 to 120/min, depth at least 2 inches (adults) or at least one-third of the AP chest diameter (infants and children; about 1½ in for infants, 2 in for children), complete recoil, minimal interruptions and no excessive ventilation. The 2015 goals are to double the bystander CPR rate and double survival. Rapid response teams (= medical emergency teams) bring critical-care expertise to the bedside and can prevent deterioration to the point of needing BLS or ACLS.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Chain of Survival; Figure 18-1 (and description); Respiratory Recap "Chain of Survival"; History, Evolution, and Milestones; Basic Life Support

6C-A-B (CABD) sequence and how it evolved

In 2010 the sequence changed from ABCD (airway, breathing, circulation, defibrillation) to CABD (circulation, airway, breathing, defibrillation) for adults and children. "Look, listen and feel" was eliminated. Compressions come first so the victim gets them early: in the first minutes of VF arrest, airway and ventilation matter less than compressions. Untrained rescuers do hands-only (compression-only) CPR. Healthcare providers add rescue breathing, AED/defibrillation and teamwork. Deck link (RCP 103 Clinical Data): in the NBRC world the priority is normally ventilation, then oxygenation, circulation, perfusion, "however, if someone is in cardiac arrest CPR and rescue breaths start together."

Box 18-2, Primary CABD (BLS) sequence: (1) check responsiveness and breathing or gasping; (2) activate the emergency response system and call for a defibrillator; (3) check the pulse for no more than 10 s, and if absent start compressions; (4) 30 compressions, ≥ 2 in deep, 100–120/min; (5) open the airway; (6) give two slow breaths; (7) resume compressions immediately; (8) attach the AED when available and use it if indicated; (9) resume compressions. Secondary (ACLS) sequence: continue compressions right after defibrillation; give O₂, IV, monitor, fluids; place an advanced airway as soon as possible (but do not interrupt compressions if BVM ventilation is adequate), confirm tube placement with two methods (physical exam plus qualitative/quantitative ETCO₂), and secure it (purpose-made holders beat tie-and-tape; cervical collar and backboard if a C-spine injury is suspected and the patient must be moved); give 2 breaths after every 30 compressions; confirm oxygenation and ventilation with quantitative capnography and pulse oximetry; search for and treat the cause.

Milestones (Hess): 1960 Maryland Medical Society introduces compressions plus rescue breathing. 1966 first CPR guidelines. The Resusci Anne mannequin (Asmund Laerdal). 1970s Seattle mass citizen training (> 100,000 people in 2 years). 1979 ACLS introduced. 1983 pediatric conference, leading to PALS/NALS (the Recap labels this the "1980s"). 1990s Public Access Defibrillation. 2005 Family & Friends CPR Anytime Kit (core skills in 20 minutes) and a new compression-to-ventilation ratio. 2010 A-B-C → C-A-B. 2015 two chains of survival, and the 5-year revision cycle replaced by a continuously updated website.

SourceHess Ch 18 — CABD Sequence; Box 18-2; Current Guideline Developments; History, Evolution, and Milestones; Respiratory Recap "Milestones in Contemporary CPR" · RCP 103 Clinical Data, slide 10 (notes)

7High-quality chest compressions: rate, depth, hand position and recoil by age

Adult Child (1 yr–puberty) Infant (< 1 yr)
Rate 100–120/min 100–120/min ("push fast and hard") 100–120/min (Algorithm 18-3: "at least 100/min")
Depth At least 2 inches (5 cm) One-third of the AP diameter ≈ 2 inches (5 cm) in most children One-third of the AP diameter ≈ 1½ inches (4 cm)
Hand position Lower half of the sternum, between the nipples / above the notch where the ribs meet the lower sternum. Heel of one hand, second hand on top, fingers off the chest, arms straight, elbows locked, shoulders directly over the sternum Mid-sternum between the nipples Two fingertips "between the nipple line", or the two-thumb technique (hands encircle the chest, compress with the thumbs)
Recoil Complete recoil after each compression. Do not lift the hands off the chest. Compression and relaxation times should be equal Complete recoil Keep fingertips on the chest but release the pressure for recoil

For every age: minimize interruptions, keeping them < 10 seconds (for intubation, defibrillation or moving the patient). Rotate the compressor every 2 minutes or sooner if fatigued. Put the patient on a firm surface, but do not delay compressions waiting for one. Chest compressions produce only 25% to 33% of normal cardiac output. Rate wording: Hess's text uses 100 to 120/min in most places, but two older passages and the pediatric algorithm say "at least 100/min". Use 100–120.

Complications: compressing the xiphoid can lacerate the liver and cause severe bleeding. Off-midline hands or fingers on the ribs cause rib fractures and costochondral separation, and broken rib ends can lacerate the lungs. The elderly, patients on chronic steroids and patients with calcium deficiencies are most susceptible. If you feel cracking, check hand position and keep compressing. Fat emboli can come from marrow microfractures even without overt fractures, and cerebral fat emboli can cause mental deterioration after successful resuscitation.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Chain of Survival; Circulation; Figures 18-3, 18-4; Respiratory Recap "Primary Circulation Management During CPR"; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for a Child; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant; Figure 18-23; Breathing; Algorithm 18-3 and 18-5 (description)

8Compression-to-ventilation ratios (1 and 2 rescuers) and rescue-breathing rates, by age

1 rescuer 2 rescuers Victim has a pulse but is not breathing normally
Adult 30:2 30:2 (Hess gives 30:2 as the adult ratio without a separate 2-rescuer number; the ACLS algorithm says "if no advanced airway, 30:2") 1 breath every 5–6 s (≈ 10–12 breaths/min); recheck the pulse about every 2 min
Child 30:2 15:2 1 breath every 3 s; add compressions if the pulse stays < 60/min with poor perfusion
Infant 30:2 15:2 (pediatric algorithm, as for the child)
Adult with an advanced airway (Hess ACLS / Algorithm 18-5) 1 breath every 6 s (10 breaths/min) with continuous compressions, no pause for breaths
Gap · still not coveredadvanced-airway breath rate for children and infants — no source in the vault gives one (Hess Ch 18 pediatric sections and Algorithm 18-3 are silent).

Two-rescuer CPR (Key Point): one person compresses, and the other ventilates and assesses cardiopulmonary function. Five cycles of 30:2 take about 2 minutes. For a child, 2 minutes = 5 cycles of 30:2 (one rescuer) or 10 cycles of 15:2 (two rescuers). Hess also notes that one retrospective study found improved survival with a 50:2 ratio, but data are limited, and hyperventilation is not beneficial.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Circulation; Respiratory Recap "Primary Circulation Management During CPR" and "Child CPR"; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for a Child; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant; Airway Management; Summary of Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support; Key Points; Algorithms 18-2, 18-3, 18-5 (description)

9Ventilation during CPR (without and with an advanced airway)

  • Breaths: give 2 breaths of 1 second each, just enough for visible chest rise. Box 18-2 calls them "two slow breaths", and the ALS section says each breath "takes less than 1 second" during a brief pause. Tidal volume 6 to 7 mL/kg (≈ 500 to 600 mL) is sufficient. Opening the airway and giving the 2 initial breaths should take 5 to 10 seconds. If the first breath does not raise the chest, reposition the airway and try again.
  • Avoid excessive ventilation. Providers commonly over-ventilate and give compressions that are too few, too shallow or interrupted, which lowers blood flow to vital organs. Hyperventilation is common and discouraged. Breaths that are too fast or too forceful go to the stomach (gastric inflation, when pharyngeal pressure exceeds esophageal opening pressure), causing vomiting, aspiration and later pneumonia.
  • With an advanced airway: 1 breath every 6 seconds (10/min) with no pause in compressions.
  • Air trapping (especially obstructive lung disease): if hypotension develops during bagging, stop ventilating for 20 to 30 seconds. If the BP comes back, air trapping is present, so resume less aggressively at a slow rate of 6 to 8 breaths/min.
  • Oxygen: give the highest O₂ concentration possible during arrest (Key Point). Hess Ch 14: BVMs are designed to deliver 100% O₂ during CPR, then titrate O₂ to target SpO₂ after ROSC. Exhaled rescuer gas contains 14% to 17% O₂ and 4% CO₂.
  • Barrier/rescue methods: mouth-to-mouth (pinch the nose), mouth-to-nose (when the mouth cannot be opened or sealed), mouth-to-stoma, face shields (reduce contact but may increase resistance; switch to mask or BVM ASAP), and mouth-to-mask (a one-way valve diverts exhaled gas; position at the head; use the bridge of the nose as a guide). The most common cause of difficulty ventilating is an improperly opened or occluded airway.
  • Cricoid pressure (Sellick maneuver) is no longer recommended routinely. It may help visualize the cords in special circumstances.
SourceHess Ch 18 — Breathing; Box 18-2; Airway Management; Current Guideline Developments; Circulation; Respiratory Recap "Primary Breathing Management During CPR"; Key Points; Algorithm 18-5 (description) · Hess Ch 14 (O₂ during CPR)

10Bag-valve-mask (manual resuscitator) technique

Parts: self-inflating bag, non-jamming gas intake valve, nonrebreathing valve, O₂ inlet nipple, O₂ reservoir. Use a transparent mask so you can see regurgitation. Squeezing sends gas through the nonrebreathing valve to the patient. On release, exhaled gas leaves through the exhalation port while the bag reinflates. O₂ flow to the bag: 10 to 12 L/min.

Technique: one-person BVM may not give an adequate tidal volume, and two-person ventilation improves VT: one person holds the mask with two hands and opens the airway, and the other squeezes the bag with two hands, both watching for chest rise. EC grip: thumb and index finger form a "C" on the mask, and the other three fingers along the mandible form an "E" (two-handed = EC grip with both hands). Thenar eminence (TE) grip: the thenar eminences rest on the mask's lateral borders, thumbs point toward the chest, and the fingers wrap the mandible. Using a self-inflating BVM to give O₂ to a spontaneously breathing patient is discouraged (increased work of breathing through the nonrebreathing valve). Infants: choose the right bag and mask size. The mask covers the mouth and nose without covering the eyes or extending past the chin; lift the jaw into the mask rather than pressing the mask onto the face. A T-piece resuscitator (Neopuff) gives consistent pressure and reliable 100% O₂ but needs a gas source and a tight seal, so keep a backup self-inflating bag.

Deck adds (RCP 203 Lssn 4, Equipment): BVM systems are the standard and should be your first choice. Use an O₂ reservoir at least equal to the bag stroke volume. Set the O₂ flowmeter to the maximum the manufacturer allows, but never delay ventilation to get O₂. Source difference: Hess gives a number (10–12 L/min); the instructor's deck says the maximum the manufacturer allows (no number). For this exam, follow the deck's wording if asked how to set the flowmeter, and know Hess's 10–12 L/min if a number is asked. Add a PEEP valve if needed, and monitor airway pressure with a manometer in children and infants. For the highest FiO₂: slow bag refill, a reservoir attached, and adequate input flow. Pre-use checks: (1) occlude the patient connector and squeeze: the pop-off should open, or the bag should be impossible to compress (failure = missing/leaking nonrebreathing or inlet valve); (2) squeeze, then occlude: the bag should reinflate via the inlet valve and the reservoir should deflate (failure = inlet valve jammed or mispositioned). A failed bag gets replaced, or use mouth-to-valve mask. The RCP 105 Airway deck adds that an OPA may make BMV more effective but should not be used in semi-comatose or alert patients.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Breathing; Figures 18-10, 18-12, 18-13; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 58–60 · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 4

11AED and defibrillation: shockable rhythms, energy, pads, and when to shock

Rhythms. In a witnessed sudden cardiac arrest, the most common initial rhythm is VF. Untreated or unsuccessfully treated VF deteriorates to asystole. Shockable = VF and pulseless VT (RCP 203 deck: AEDs give early defibrillation to pulseless patients with pulseless V-tach or V-fib). Pulseless, non-shockable = asystole or PEA. The pulseless arrest rhythms in Hess: VF, pulseless monomorphic VT, PEA, asystole. Defibrillation passes current through the heart to eliminate the chaotic, asynchronous activity of VF, so the cells depolarize and repolarize uniformly.

P-A-I-D (Hess's AED mnemonic):

  • Power on.
  • Attach leads: right-size pads; dry a wet chest; one pad on the upper-right chest, right of the sternum just below the clavicle, and one left of the nipple, just below the left armpit.
  • Interpret/analyze: no one touching the patient; analysis takes 5 to 15 seconds.
  • Defibrillate: say "clear", check visually, shock, then immediately restart compressions. After 2 minutes (five sets of 30:2), reanalyze. The RCP 203 deck adds: switch rescuer positions at each 2-minute analysis, and if no shock is advised, resume CPR because the patient is still pulseless.

Energy (ACLS Algorithm 18-5 description): biphasic = manufacturer's recommendation (e.g., initial 120–200 J); if unknown, use the maximum available; second and subsequent doses should be equivalent, and higher doses may be considered. Monophasic = 360 J.

Children and infants: for children < 8 years, use child pads and the child shock dose. For > 8 years, use adult pads and the adult dose. If child pads are not available, adult pads may be used without overlapping or touching. For an unwitnessed or out-of-hospital pediatric arrest, attach the AED after about 2 minutes of CPR (10 cycles of 15:2 or 5 cycles of 30:2), because most pediatric arrests are not caused by ventricular arrhythmias. For an in-hospital or witnessed pediatric arrest, use the AED as soon as it is available. No recommendation exists for AED use in infants < 1 year. Adults: for a lone rescuer, doing CPR before getting the AED does not improve outcomes. With two or more rescuers, one does CPR while the other gets and attaches the AED. Give 1 shock, then resume CPR immediately for about 2 minutes.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Automated External Defibrillation; Figure 18-15; Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for a Child; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant; Key Points; Algorithm 18-2 and 18-5 (description) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 62 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 — Vasopressors, Inotropes, slide 46

12Drugs during CPR (epinephrine, amiodarone and the rest) and routes

Drug Indication in arrest/ACLS Dose (Hess Table 18-6 / Algorithm 18-5 unless noted)
Epinephrine Cardiac arrest: refractory VF, pulseless VT, asystole, PEA; also shock and symptomatic bradycardia 1 mg IV/IO push (10 mL of 1:10,000) every 3–5 minutes. ET dose 2–2.5 mg diluted in 10 mL NS. Bradycardia infusion 2–10 mcg/min. Deck: α₁ stimulation → vasoconstriction, which ↑ coronary and cerebral perfusion ("Epi makes CPR work better"); less effective in acidosis, so fix oxygenation and ventilation
Amiodarone VF and pulseless VT unresponsive to shock delivery, CPR and a vasopressor; unstable VT First dose 300 mg IV/IO push, second dose 150 mg. Deck: Class III; pulmonary toxicity (fibrosis/pneumonitis) in up to 20%; many drug interactions
Lidocaine VF/pulseless VT; stable VT with a pulse VF/pVT: 1 to 1.5 mg/kg, refractory 0.5–0.75 mg/kg in 3–5 min, total 3 mg/kg
Magnesium sulfate Arrest only if torsades de pointes or suspected low Mg 1–2 g (deck: may repeat; caution in renal impairment)
Sodium bicarbonate Preexisting hyperkalemia, drug overdose, known ketoacidosis, prolonged arrest with adequate ventilation Hess: 1 mmol/kg IV bolus. Deck: 0.5–1 mEq/kg, keep pH > 7.15; it raises CO₂, so ventilation must be adequate (see conflicts)
Atropine Symptomatic bradycardia (first-line; Hess Table 18-6). Atropine is not among the drugs in the ACLS cardiac-arrest algorithm (Algorithm 18-5 lists epinephrine and amiodarone) 0.5 mg every 3–5 min, max 3 mg (0.04 mg/kg)
Vasopressin (deck only) Formerly could substitute for the 1st or 2nd epinephrine dose 40 units IV once (prior to 2015); removed from the algorithm for lack of benefit
Adenosine / dopamine SVT / second-line for bradycardia or hypotension 6 mg rapid IV then 12 mg / 2–20 mcg/kg/min

Routes. In ACLS, advanced airway, vascular access and drugs must not delay compressions or defibrillation. IV/IO is first. IO (deck): recommended for children and adults when IV access is too difficult; needle not left > 3–4 hours; most common site proximal tibia (also humerus). Endotracheal (Hess Key Point): when IV access is delayed, some drugs can be given through the tracheal tube. Deck "NAVEL" lists the drugs that can go down the ETT: Naloxone, Atropine, Vasopressin, Epinephrine, Lidocaine. Give 2–2.5 × the IV dose diluted in 10 mL NS or sterile water through a catheter past the tube tip, then 5–10 rapid ventilations. In modern ACLS the ET route is rarely used and IO is preferred.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Table 18-6 Summary of ACLS Pharmacology; Advanced Cardiovascular Life Support; Key Points; Algorithm 18-5 (description) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 — Vasopressors, Inotropes, slides 38 (amiodarone), 47 (and notes), 49, 50, 51, 53, 54, 56

13Capnography (PETCO₂) during CPR: targets and ROSC

PETCO₂ finding Meaning / action Source
0 No pulmonary blood flow Hess Ch 2
< 10 mm Hg Poor or absent pulmonary blood flow → improve CPR quality Hess Algorithm 18-5; deck: "PetCO₂ levels remain low (< 10 torr)" with poor flow
Rising Adequate CPR (good flow from compressions or restored cardiac function) Hess Ch 2; RCP 203 deck
Abrupt, sustained increase (typically 40 mm Hg) Return of spontaneous circulation (ROSC) Hess Algorithm 18-5; Hess Ch 2 ("abrupt increase … indicates ROSC")
Not > 10 mm Hg after 20 minutes of CPR (intubated) Poor prognosis; may be part of a multimodal decision to end resuscitation Hess Ch 18; Hess Ch 2
After ROSC: 35–40 mm Hg Post-arrest ventilation target (start at 10 breaths/min) Hess Algorithm 18-8

The AHA recommends quantitative waveform capnography to confirm and monitor ETT placement, monitor CPR quality and detect ROSC. During CPR, PETCO₂ correlates well with cardiac output (RCP 203 deck). Hess Ch 2 Figure 2-20 contrasts the capnogram of ineffective cardiac compressions or rescuer fatigue with that of ROSC. Hess adds: with an arterial line, a relaxation-phase (diastolic) pressure < 20 mm Hg also means CPR quality should be improved. Other ROSC signs: pulse and blood pressure, and spontaneous arterial pressure waves.

SourceHess Ch 18 — Airway Management; Algorithm 18-5 and 18-8 (description) · Hess Ch 2 — capnography in CPR, Figure 2-20, Respiratory Recap · RCP 203 Lssn 3b — Ch 5 Recommend Diagnostics, slides 23, 24

14Advanced airways during CPR, and the impedance threshold valve

  • Timing. BVM ventilation in the first minutes allows uninterrupted compressions and defibrillation. Prehospital advanced airways have not been shown to improve survival. In adult IHCA, intubation during the first 15 minutes is associated with decreased survival to discharge, so early intubation during CPR is usually not necessary. In OHCA, an advanced airway is not associated with survival for shockable rhythms but is associated with better survival for non-shockable rhythms. (Hess's Key Points still state "there is no substitute for tracheal intubation" in airway management.)
  • ETT. Indications: can't ventilate with a BVM, patient can't protect the airway (unconscious), and a trained rescuer is present. Advantages: protects from aspiration, keeps the airway patent, allows suctioning, and is a drug route. Complications: oropharyngeal trauma, interrupted compressions and ventilation, unrecognized misplacement. Postmortem studies found ≈ 6% to 14% of ETTs misplaced, so confirm with several techniques (waveform capnography). Interrupt compressions only as long as needed to see the cords. If the first attempt fails, stop, ventilate and oxygenate, then retry.
  • Laryngeal tubes / esophageal–tracheal tubes (Combitube, King airway): placed blindly with the tip in the esophagus, easier than an ETT, and better than a face mask at isolating the airway, but not useful for clearing tracheal secretions. Usually prehospital only, converted to an ETT once stable. Initial laryngeal tube use in OHCA was associated with greater 72-hour survival than initial intubation.
  • Laryngeal mask airway: more secure than a BVM; easy insertion, simpler training (no need to see the cords), better aspiration protection than a face mask, and usable with a possible unstable neck injury.
  • Impedance threshold valve (ITV). It limits air entry during chest recoil, which makes intrathoracic pressure more negative (decreases it by 6 to 8 mm Hg) and increases venous return and cardiac output. With active compression–decompression CPR, it gave > 50% higher coronary perfusion pressure and better 24-hour survival and neurologic function. It works with an LMA, cuffed ETT or tight face mask. Contraindicated in pulmonary edema or CHF.
SourceHess Ch 18 — Airway Management; Figure 18-24; Respiratory Recap "Equipment for Emergency Airway Management"; Impedance Threshold Valve; Key Points

15Post–cardiac arrest care

Most post-resuscitation deaths occur in the first 24 hours. These patients need respiratory support, arrhythmia management, hemodynamic support (mechanical and/or drugs), and correction of metabolic problems. Key goals: optimize cardiopulmonary function and vital-organ perfusion; transfer to a capable unit; identify and treat ACS and other reversible causes; control temperature; and avoid excessive ventilation and hyperoxia.

Post-ROSC target (Algorithm 18-8 description unless noted) Value
SpO₂ 94% (titrate FiO₂ to the minimum needed)
Ventilation Do not hyperventilate. Start at 10 breaths/min, titrate to PETCO₂ 35–40 mm Hg
Hypotension Treat SBP < 90 mm Hg: IV/IO bolus ≈ 1–2 L NS or LR, vasopressor infusion (epinephrine 0.1–0.5 mcg/kg/min, dopamine 5–10 mcg/kg/min, norepinephrine 0.1–0.5 mcg/kg/min)
12-lead ECG STEMI or high suspicion of AMI → coronary reperfusion
Not following commands Targeted temperature management (TTM)
Blood gases Normal values (text). Hyperventilation, high airway pressure and auto-PEEP → cerebral vasoconstriction, ↑ ICP, ischemic brain injury

TTM / therapeutic hypothermia (Hess): cool to 32 °C to 36 °C for at least 24 hours after VF, PEA or asystole arrest. All comatose adults with ROSC should have TTM. TH is likely beneficial after adult TBI but cannot be recommended in children. Internal cooling (cold saline, endovascular catheters) reaches target faster with better outcomes, and surface pads with circulating water are also used. Complications: below-range temperature → coagulopathy and arrhythmias; more pneumonia, sepsis and hyperglycemia. Set the ventilator humidifier to the patient's target core temperature, and monitor temperature continuously. Deck (RCP 105 Critical Issues): TTM generally 32 °C–36 °C reduces brain metabolism and improves neurologic outcome. After ROSC, RTs maintain oxygenation and ventilation and support TTM while avoiding hypotension and hypoxemia. Arrest → MAP ≈ 0 → CPP ≈ 0 → no cerebral blood flow. (The same deck's notes also say "32 to 34 °C", which is older; use 32–36 °C.)

Reversible causes (H's and T's, Algorithms 18-5/18-8): Hypovolemia, Hypoxia, Hydrogen ion (acidosis), Hypo-/hyperkalemia, Hypothermia; Tension pneumothorax, Tamponade (cardiac), Toxins, Thrombosis (pulmonary), Thrombosis (coronary).

SourceHess Ch 18 — Post–Cardiac Arrest Care; Algorithm 18-8 (description); Algorithm 18-5 (description); Key Points · RCP 105 Critical Issues in MV — HIE/TBI (Chang Ch 15), slide 9 (notes)

16Choking (foreign body airway obstruction) by age high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Mild obstruction Severe obstruction
Signs Good air exchange, can cough forcefully, may wheeze between coughs Universal choking sign (clutching the throat), can't speak or cry, poor or no air exchange, weak or no cough, high-pitched inspiratory noise or none, increasing distress, possible cyanosis
Action Encourage coughing, don't interfere, stay and monitor, and activate EMS if it persists or worsens Ask "Are you choking?" If the victim nods and can't talk, give abdominal thrusts
  • Adult / child (≥ 1 yr): coughing is the most effective way to clear the airway. Use abdominal thrusts (Heimlich): from behind, thumb side of the fist at the midline, slightly above the navel and well below the breastbone, quick upward thrusts, each a separate movement. Use chest thrusts for pregnant or obese victims. If the victim becomes unresponsive: lower to the ground, activate EMS, start CPR with compressions (no pulse check), look in the mouth each time you open the airway and remove only a visible object (never a blind finger sweep), and activate EMS after ≈ 5 cycles / 2 minutes if alone. When abdominal thrusts are given to a child who is or becomes unconscious, use the heel of one hand rather than a two-handed fist (Hess Ch 18). Common causes in children: balloons, small objects, hot dogs, round candies, nuts, grapes.
  • Infant: the most common cause is liquids. No abdominal thrusts. Give 5 back blows (heel of the hand, between the shoulder blades, infant prone and head-down on your forearm), then 5 chest thrusts (supine, head lower, same location as compressions), and repeat until the object comes out or the infant becomes unresponsive, then begin CPR and look for the object before each breath.
SourceHess Ch 18 — Choking Relief for Adults and Children; Table 18-1; Table 18-2; Table 18-3; Relief for a Choking Child; Choking Infant Relief; Respiratory Recap "Choking Adult"

17Definitions, risk factors, newborns and ethics high-yield · beyond the listed asks

  • CPR = a series of lifesaving actions that improve survival after cardiac arrest. Cardiac arrest = cessation of cardiac mechanical activity, confirmed by absent signs of circulation. Indications for CPR: cardiac arrest, respiratory arrest or both. Cardiac arrest usually follows VF. Respiratory arrest comes from an obstructed airway, depressed drive (spinal cord/head injury), pulmonary disorders (COPD, pulmonary edema, PE) or gas-transport problems (anemia, shock). Infants and children usually arrest secondary to pulmonary disease or respiratory arrest. Major pediatric causes: respiratory failure, SIDS, sepsis, neurologic disease, trauma.
  • Age groups: infant = neonates (first 28 days) to ≈ 1 year; child = 1 year to puberty (about 12–14); adult = at and beyond puberty.
  • Newborns: ≈ 10% need help to start breathing at birth. Ask three questions: term gestation? breathing or crying? good muscle tone? If all three are yes, no resuscitation is needed. Algorithm 18-4: PPV if HR < 100, gasping or apnea; compressions if HR < 60 despite ventilation.
  • CHD risk factors (Box 18-1): cannot change: heredity, gender, increasing age. Can change: cigarette smoking (the most important; CHD death rate 70% greater in smokers), hypertension, inactivity, obesity, stress, diabetes, cholesterol.
  • Ethics: CPR is started without a physician's order (implied consent), but withholding CPR requires a physician's order. Do not start CPR if there is a valid DNR, obvious signs of irreversible death (rigor mortis, decapitation, decomposition, dependent lividity), danger to the rescuer, or no possible physiologic benefit. Not starting and stopping resuscitation are ethically equivalent. The decision to terminate rests with the treating physician, based on time to CPR, time to defibrillation, comorbidity, pre-arrest state and initial rhythm. RTs must know each patient's resuscitation status.
SourceHess Ch 18 — Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation; Basic Life Support; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for a Child; Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for an Infant; Algorithm 18-4 (description); Risk Factors, Box 18-1; Ethical Concerns; Key Points
14

Mechanical Ventilation (29 questions on the exam)

1How to Tell Any Two Modes Apart (the Framework Behind Every Mode on the List)

The decks open with a warning: mode names are manufacturer-dependent. "Ventilator mode names are not always standardized between manufacturers", so the slide says to "Learn how the mode works – not just the name!" (example on the slide: Servo = PRVC; Dräger = AC/VC with AutoFlow; PB840 = VC+). Hess adds: one book lists almost 300 mode names for about three dozen ventilators, and identical modes carry different names. For example, Hamilton Galileo's Pressure-Control Ventilation Plus Adaptive Pressure Ventilation is the same mode as Pressure Regulated Volume Control on the Servo 300.

To describe any mode, the RCP 105 deck has you ask three questions:

  1. What is controlled: volume or pressure?
  2. What starts and ends the breath (trigger and cycle)?
  3. What type of breaths occur: CMV, IMV or CSV?

The golden rule: you can "Control" only one item; the other is the "Variable." Control the volume and the pressures go up and down; control the pressure and the volumes go up and down. Hess says the same thing: "A ventilator can control only one variable at a time — either pressure or volume."

The deck ranks the three delivery methods for VT as VC = 1st choice, PC = 2nd choice, PS = "help" (used in SIMV and in an SBT).
