Breath types and breath sequences.

  • A spontaneous breath is one the patient both triggers and cycles.
  • A mandatory breath is one where the machine triggers or cycles it, or both.
  • The deck's quick rule: "If the patient controls both = Spontaneous. If the ventilator controls either one = it's Mandatory." So patient-triggered ≠ spontaneous.
  • There are only three breath sequences:
    • CMV: all breaths mandatory
    • IMV: mandatory plus spontaneous
    • CSV: all spontaneous, e.g. CPAP or PS

Hess adds:

  • The control variable and breath sequence combine into five ventilatory patterns: VC-CMV, VC-IMV, PC-CMV, PC-IMV, PC-CSV. VC-CSV is not valid, because presetting the tidal volume implies machine cycling.
  • Modes are further split by seven targeting schemes: setpoint, dual, biovariable, servo, adaptive, optimal and intelligent. The 105 deck says targeting schemes are covered in RCP 202/203.

Open-loop vs closed-loop. An open-loop ventilator "delivers what you set and doesn't adjust" (e.g. VC). A closed-loop ventilator "watches the patient and automatically adjusts." The deck's closed-loop examples are PSV, MMV, ASV, PAV, volume-assured pressure support, PRVC and NAVA.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 2, 4; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 4, 5 (notes), 7 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 82 (notes), 83, 84 · Hess Ch 19 — Understanding Ventilator Technology; Ten Fundamental Maxims (#6, #8, #9); Key Points

2VC vs PC: the Difference and When to Use Each

Volume Control (VC)

  • VT is guaranteed and pressure varies.
  • The deck's example: set VT = 500 mL and the patient develops ARDS. VT is still 500 mL, but the pressure increases.
  • Hess: with VC, flow and tidal volume are the independent variables and pressure is the dependent variable. The VT is delivered regardless of effort, resistance or compliance, unless the high-pressure alarm limit is reached.
  • Box 20-4 lists what raises PIP in VC: decreased compliance and greater airway resistance, among others.
  • When to use it (Hess): "VC should be used whenever a constant tidal volume is important in the maintenance of a desired PaCO₂."
  • Its synchrony weakness: because inspiratory flow is fixed, VC can cause patient–ventilator asynchrony, particularly if the inspiratory flow is set too low.
  • Waveforms: VC usually uses a constant (square) flow, and its pressure waveform "looks like a mountain" because pressure climbs throughout inspiration.

Pressure Control (PCV)

  • Pressure is guaranteed and VT varies.
  • Set PC = 20 cm H₂O and the patient develops ARDS: pressure is still 20, but VT decreases.
  • PCV breaths are time-triggered, pressure-limited and time-cycled, and may be patient-triggered for additional breaths.
  • Hess: the pressure set is the independent variable, and flow and VT are dependent. Peak alveolar pressure cannot exceed the set pressure. VT rises with greater patient effort and falls with decreased compliance.
  • Indication on the slide: severe ARDS (decreased lung compliance), because airway pressures ≥ 50 cm H₂O during VC can cause barotrauma.
  • Switching to PC lets a lower PIP be used while still providing oxygenation and ventilation. The notes add that, as in the control mode, the patient should be sedated.
  • Waveforms: PC has a descending ramp flow, and its pressure waveform "looks like a table": a square pressure waveform with a plateau.
  • Worked example: PC 20 + PEEP 10 → PIP = 30 cm H₂O (PIP = pressure control + PEEP).

Which one first? The RCP 202 deck ranks VC 1st choice, PC 2nd choice (slide 3). The RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slide 31) says that "in the NBRC hospital" either is satisfactory, as long as VT is 6–8 mL/kg PBW (8 mL/kg if not at risk for ALI/ARDS), and a patient needing Pplat > 30 cm H₂O in VC should probably be switched to PC at ≤ 30. The Trauma deck (slide 22) adds that in chest trauma there is no evidence the choice of VC vs PC changes mortality or morbidity; lung protection matters more than the mode.

Advantages (Hess Table 20-1) Disadvantages (Hess Table 20-1)
VC Constant VT despite changes in resistance and compliance; familiar ↑ Pplat as compliance falls (overdistention); fixed flow may cause flow mismatch
PC Reduced risk of overdistention; variable flow improves flow matching in some patients VT changes with resistance, compliance and effort; less familiar
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 4 (notes), 30, 31 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 3, 20 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 31; RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 22 (and notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 4 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Volume Control Versus Pressure Control, Box 20-4, Box 20-5, Table 20-1

3CMV (and How It Differs from Assist/Control)

Watch the wording. The decks use "CMV" in two different ways (see the conflicts file).

Don't decide by the spelled-out name. The Chang control-mode slide itself reads "Continuous mandatory ventilation (CMV), control mode," while the Principles deck uses "Continuous Mandatory Ventilation (CMV), (aka: Assist/Control)." Decide by whether the stem lets the patient trigger: patient cannot trigger / sedated-paralyzed → control mode; patient triggers and every breath gets full support → A/C.

Meaning 1: CMV = the control mode (RCP 105 Op Modes slide 14; RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 4).

  • The ventilator delivers a preset VT at a time-triggered rate, and patient triggering and spontaneous breathing are not possible.
  • The slide's one-line difference: A/C allows patient-triggered breaths; CMV does not.
  • The ventilator "controls everything, so pt effort is not used to initiate breaths." Adequate sedation is often required, and neuromuscular blockade may be needed.
  • If the patient wakes up, severe dyssynchrony and "fighting the ventilator" develop. That is why CMV is reserved for deeply sedated, paralyzed or apneic patients.
  • Indications: tetanus or seizures, complete rest, crushed chest injury, paradoxical chest wall movement.
  • Complications:
    • problems from prolonged sedation
    • apnea and hypoxia if the patient is disconnected
    • rapid disuse atrophy of the respiratory muscles: 18 to 69 hours of complete diaphragm inactivity produces marked myofiber atrophy
  • The most important alarms in the control mode are low exhaled volume and low inspiratory pressure.

Assist/Control (A/C).

  • Assist = a patient-triggered breath, seen as a negative deflection below baseline before inspiration.
  • Control = a time-triggered breath, with no negative deflection.
  • Every breath gets the full preset support. If A/C is set at 12 and the patient triggers 24, the patient receives 24 full breaths.
  • Indications: full ventilatory support; minimize atelectasis.
  • Advantages: reduced WOB; the patient can increase minute ventilation.
  • Complications: high frequency with respiratory-center dysfunction → alveolar hyperventilation (respiratory alkalosis).

Meaning 2: CMV = Assist/Control as a breath sequence (Hess Ch 19 maxim #7; RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 84). Here CMV is A/C, as a breath sequence. Every patient trigger in the trigger window produces a machine-cycled (mandatory) breath, so spontaneous breaths are not possible between mandatory breaths. The set rate is a minimum: the actual mandatory rate can be higher, never lower. Hess adds:

  • When ventilation is first started, CMV (A/C) is often best, because it produces nearly complete respiratory muscle rest.
  • Its disadvantages are high mean airway pressure, respiratory alkalosis and auto-PEEP if the patient triggers rapidly, and possible respiratory muscle atrophy.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 14, 15 (notes), 16, 17; RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 4 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 84 · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#7); Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2

4IMV vs SIMV (and Why SIMV Usually Comes with Pressure Support)

IMV

  • Mandatory breaths come at a precise, fixed time interval, and the patient may breathe spontaneously in between.
  • The problem is breath stacking: a mandatory breath is delivered before the patient has completely exhaled a spontaneous breath.
  • This leads to auto-PEEP, hyperinflation, ↑ airway pressures, ↑ WOB and barotrauma risk.
  • No new adult ventilators offer IMV; all current ventilators provide SIMV instead.

SIMV

  • SIMV is IMV with synchronization. Mandatory breaths come at a slightly variable interval.
  • The ventilator waits within a small "synchronization window". If the patient starts to inhale, it delivers the mandatory breath with that effort, so breath stacking is reduced or avoided.
  • Worked example (SIMV RR 10, VT 450, FiO₂ 30%, PS 8, PEEP +5):
    • 60 ÷ 10 = a mandatory breath every 6 seconds.
    • If the patient makes no effort, the breath is time-triggered.
    • If the patient inhales at second 5.8, the breath is delivered at once as an assisted (patient-triggered) mandatory breath. It still counts as mandatory because it delivers the preset VT.
  • Between mandatory breaths the patient breathes to "any VT" (200, 350 or 600 mL). The PS of 8 applies only to those spontaneous breaths.

The key contrast with A/C: with a set rate of 10 and a patient who triggers 20:

  • A/C gives 20 mandatory breaths.
  • SIMV gives 10 mandatory + 10 spontaneous.
  • Hess: in IMV the mandatory rate can never be higher than the set rate, but may be lower (the set rate is a maximum).

When to use SIMV:

  • Indications: partial ventilatory support; maintaining respiratory muscle strength and avoiding atrophy; reducing V/Q mismatch; facilitating weaning (e.g. SIMV 12 → 8 → 4 → CPAP/PS).
  • Complication: muscle fatigue and weaning failure without pressure support, which is why you see SIMV + PS.
  • The deck's pearl: A/C = let the ventilator do the work; SIMV = let the patient do some of the work; SIMV + PS = let the patient work, but give them some help.

Hess adds:

  • SIMV's disadvantages are asynchrony if the rate is set too low and high WOB with older ventilators. Its advantages are lower mean airway pressure and preventing respiratory muscle atrophy.
  • Considerable inspiratory effort occurs with both the mandatory and the spontaneous breaths. As SIMV support is reduced, WOB rises for both; adding pressure support ameliorates this.
Exam anchorWord-play note: the Principles deck's NBRC pearl is "When you see SIMV, your brain should immediately think: IMV." The instructor's own study-guide word-play list also says "SIMV = IMV." On this exam, treat SIMV and IMV as the same breath sequence unless the question is plainly about the old unsynchronized IMV and breath stacking.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 18, 19, 20, 21; RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 8; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 85 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#5 synchronization window, #7); Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2 · Instructor's Final Exam Study Guide, word-play list

5Pressure Support (PS/PSV): the Breath Type Inside SIMV, Volume Support and VV+

The defining phrase: PSV is patient-triggered, pressure-limited and flow-cycled. The deck notes say this phrase "appears on NBRC-style exams repeatedly."

  • PS is only active during spontaneous (patient-triggered) breaths and is inactive during mechanical breaths.
  • VT is determined by the patient: effort, compliance and resistance.
  • Inspiration ends when inspiratory flow falls to a predetermined percentage of peak flow, often around 25%.
  • PSV cannot be used in an apneic patient.

When to use it:

  • Indications: low spontaneous VT, high spontaneous frequency, increased WOB.
  • Titration targets: VT 8–10 mL/kg IBW and spontaneous frequency < 25/min.
  • Worked example (5′10″ male): IBW = 50 + 2.3 × 10 = 73 kg, so 73 × 8 = 584 mL and 73 × 10 = 730 mL.
  • The notes add that modern lung-protective practice often targets 6–8 mL/kg IBW, especially in ARDS.

Hess adds (Table 20-2):

  • PS requires spontaneous respiratory effort and is not appropriate for patients without an adequate respiratory drive.
  • Set too low, it causes fatigue and tachypnea. Set too high, it causes activation of expiratory muscles.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 22, 23 (notes), 24 (and notes), 25 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2

6MMV (Mandatory, or Minimum, Minute Ventilation)

What the RCP 202 deck says (use this on the exam). MMV is an "additional function/mode related to SIMV" and is ventilator-dependent. It automatically ensures the patient receives a preset minimum minute ventilation (aka Minimum Minute Ventilation).

  • When spontaneous V̇E falls below the preset minimum, the ventilator automatically increases the mandatory breath frequency, using volume-cycled mandatory breaths.
  • Worked example: target V̇E = 8 L/min.
    • The patient makes 9 L/min on their own → no extra mandatory ventilation.
    • The patient drops to 6 L/min → the ventilator adds mandatory breaths to make up the deficit.
  • The catch: MMV "doesn't guarantee that every breath has an adequate VT – it guarantees a minimum total minute ventilation."
  • When to use it:
    • weaning, encouraging spontaneous breathing without hypoventilation
    • a transition mode from full support toward spontaneous breathing
    • patients with variable drive (neuromuscular disease, recovering from sedation)
  • Analogy on the slide: MMV = a "minimum ventilation safety net". If you breathe enough, the ventilator stays out of the way; if not, it adds mandatory breaths.
  • The notes cut two ways on apnea.
    • Under limitations they say MMV "requires a reliable patient effort (not ideal if apnea is likely)."
    • Their example is that an apnea episode drops V̇E below the preset level, and the mandatory frequency is increased automatically to compensate.
  • The Weaning deck agrees: MMV is a form of SIMV in which minute ventilation is guaranteed. The ventilator adjusts the frequency automatically, and lowering the MMV level makes the patient take over more spontaneous breathing.

How MMV differs from ASV and PAV (the deck's takeaway):

  • MMV guarantees a minimum V̇E by increasing the mandatory rate; the patient sets the pattern and the ventilator "just fills the gap."
  • ASV: the ventilator chooses the optimal rate and VT and adapts continuously.
  • PAV: the ventilator amplifies the patient's own effort proportionally.

Hess, and the RCP 105 Principles deck (slide 85), frame MMV as a type of IMV (conflict, see conflicts file). MMV is the third variety of IMV, in which mandatory breaths are delivered only when the measured minute ventilation drops below a preset threshold. Both framings agree that low V̇E → more mandatory breaths; the 202 slide simply doesn't mention a ceiling. Hess's examples are Dräger's Mandatory Minute Volume Ventilation and Hamilton's Adaptive Support Ventilation. As with all IMV, the mandatory frequency can never be higher than the set rate (the set frequency is a maximum). Hess also describes minute ventilation as a trigger signal: inspiration is triggered if V̇E drops below a preset threshold.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 9 (and notes), slides 14 (notes), 15 (notes); RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 24 (notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 85 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#5, #7)

7PRVC (Pressure-Regulated Volume Control) and Automode

PRVC is a "hybrid": pressure-controlled breaths with automatic pressure adjustment to achieve a target VT.

Watch the wording (conflict, see conflicts file). The PRVC slide itself says PRVC "provides Volume-Controlled breaths" while keeping PIP as low as possible, and the notes call it "Volume Control with an automatic pressure adjustment." Hess says the breath type is actually PC. All sources agree on the function: target VT delivered at the lowest PIP possible. Answer by function; if forced to classify, use the 202 slide 4 wording (pressure-controlled breaths targeting a VT).

  • Settings: the clinician sets RR, VT, FiO₂ and PEEP, and the ventilator adjusts the inspiratory pressure.
  • Goal: deliver the volume while keeping PIP at the lowest level possible. It does this by altering peak flow and inspiratory time:
    • ↓ flow = ↓ resistance = ↓ pressure
    • ↓ flow = ↑ I-time, so the target volume is still delivered
  • Compliance worsens (ARDS, pulmonary edema, atelectasis) → PRVC raises inspiratory pressure.
  • Compliance improves → it lowers inspiratory pressure.
  • Board pearl: "Which mode automatically adjusts inspiratory pressure to maintain a target VT while minimizing airway pressure?" → PRVC.
  • Same idea, other names: Adaptive Pressure Control (Servo-i), AutoFlow (Dräger), Adaptive Pressure Ventilation, Volume Control+ (Puritan Bennett), Volume Targeted Pressure Control, Pressure Controlled Volume Guarantee.
  • When to use it: when you want the volume guarantee of VC with the lower pressures of PC.

Hess adds:

  • In these "volume feedback" modes, the breath type is actually PC or PS.
  • PRVC was the first adaptive-targeting mode.
  • Two cautions:
    • A volume guarantee negates the pressure-limiting feature: worsening mechanics raise the applied pressure.
    • If demand rises inappropriately (e.g. pain) and VT increases, the ventilator lowers the pressure, which may be wrong for a patient in respiratory failure.
  • Table 20-2 lists the disadvantages as "does not precisely control tidal volume" and "support is taken away if the patient's tidal volume consistently exceeds the target."

Automode (Servo 300A) combines PRVC and Volume Support:

  • No spontaneous trigger for 12 s (adult), 8 s (pediatric) or 5 s (neonatal) → PRVC: time-triggered, preset VT, variable PIP up to the high-pressure limit.
  • Two consecutive patient-triggered breaths → Volume Support: patient-triggered, pressure-limited, flow-cycled.
  • Memory trick: sleeping patient → PRVC; awake patient → Volume Support.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 4 (and notes), 16, 17; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 26 (and notes), 27 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#9, adaptive); Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2

8VV+ (Volume Ventilation Plus, Puritan Bennett 840)

VV+ is an option on the PB 840 that combines two dual-mode, volume-targeted breath types: Volume Control Plus (VC+) and Volume Support (VS). The notes call VC+ "Puritan Bennett's version of PRVC." Hess likewise lists "volume control plus (Puritan Bennett)" among the PRVC names.

VC+ (the mandatory breaths, in A/C and SIMV):

  • Preset VT and I-time
  • Variable pressures to keep VT stable
  • a test breath to determine relative compliance
  • variable flow to meet flow demand
  • excess pressure from breathing or coughing is vented through the active exhalation valve, which maintains synchrony
  • It is intended to provide a higher level of synchrony than standard VC. The notes put it this way: traditional VC says "Sorry, you're getting 60 L/min", while VC+ gives more flow when the patient wants it.

Volume Support (the spontaneous breaths): "Guarantees volume and lets pressure vary."

  • Settings: target VT only. The clinician does not set I-time or mandatory frequency.
  • Less patient effort → higher pressure support; more effort → lower PS.
  • Frequency and V̇E are set by the patient's triggering effort, and I-time by the patient's respiratory demand.
  • Common use: post-anesthesia recovery / weaning from anesthesia.

PSV vs Volume Support:

  • PSV guarantees pressure and lets volume vary.
  • VS guarantees volume and lets pressure vary.
  • Both are patient-triggered, pressure-limited and flow-cycled.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 28 (and notes), 29 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 18 (notes), 19 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes

9PAV (Proportional Assist Ventilation)

What it is (RCP 202 deck). "With PAV there is no target flow, volume, or pressure." The pressure is variable and proportional to the patient's pulmonary characteristics (elastance and airflow resistance) and ventilatory demand (flow or volume).

  • Flow assist overcomes airflow resistance.
  • Volume assist overcomes restrictive (elastic) defects.
  • In short: patient pulls harder → more assist; less effort → less assist; no effort → no assist.
  • Settings: the clinician sets a % assist (e.g. 50%, 70%). The Chang text in the notes says PAV is set to overcome 80% of the elastance and airflow resistance.
  • Cautions:
    • Overshoot of pressure support may occur.
    • PAV requires intact spontaneous effort (not for apneic patients).
    • It needs accurate compliance and resistance measurements, and leaks reduce accuracy.
  • When to use it:
    • spontaneously breathing patients who need variable assistance
    • weaning
    • patients with variable drive (COPD, neuromuscular disease, coming off sedation)
    • In ventilator-dependent COPD patients, it improves ventilation and reduces neuromuscular drive and WOB.
  • Advantage: improved patient–ventilator synchrony.

Hess adds:

  • Mechanism: PAV is a positive-feedback mode that supports in proportion to the neural output of the respiratory center.
    • It recalculates pressure from the equation of motion every 5 ms.
    • It estimates resistance and elastance with 300-ms pauses every 4 to 10 s.
  • Settings: % support is 5% to 95%, typically set so that patient WOB is 0.5 to 1.0 J/L.
  • Risks:
    • Too high a percentage → "runaway" (excessive volume and pressure).
    • Too low → excessive patient WOB.
    • PAV provides no minimal support level, so alarms and backup modes are important.
  • How it compares with other modes:
    • PS and PC keep the applied pressure constant regardless of demand.
    • VC pressure decreases as demand increases.
    • PAV varies its pressure breath to breath with the patient's compliance, resistance and flow demand.
  • Cycle and requirements: PAV is flow-cycled and requires intact drive and a functional neuromuscular system.
  • Where it's available: PAV+ on the PB 840/980, and on the V60 for NIV.
  • Possible benefits: better synchrony and sleep; outcome benefit is unproven.
  • Disadvantages (Table 20-2): not useful with weak drive or weak muscles; the clinician has little control over VT or rate.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 15 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2; Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#9, servo)

10APRV (Airway Pressure Release Ventilation)

Definition (RCP 202 deck): "APRV is inverse ratio, pressure controlled, intermittent mandatory ventilation with unrestricted spontaneous breathing."

Settings and timing:

  • Settings: FiO₂, Phigh, Plow, Thigh and Tlow. There is no VT setting and no set RR; Thigh and Tlow determine the release frequency.
  • Time at each level (deck): the patient spends most of the time at Phigh and less than 1.5 s at Plow.
  • Time at each level (Hess):
    • 80% to 95% of the cycle is spent at Phigh.
    • Tlow is 0.2 to 0.8 s in adults, ended when expiratory flow falls to 50% to 75% of peak expiratory flow.
    • Because Tlow is so short, exhalation is often incomplete, which produces intrinsic PEEP.
  • Starting settings (RCP 203 Ch 11 deck, slide 26): Plow 0–8 cm H₂O; Phigh set for 4–8 mL/kg PBW but kept below 30–35 cm H₂O; Thigh 3–5 s; Tlow 0.2–0.8 s (same as Hess), giving about 10–20 release cycles/min. That deck says APRV should only be used on patients who can spontaneously trigger enough breaths for an adequate minute ventilation.

How it works:

  • Pressure rising from Plow to Phigh simulates inhalation; the release to Plow simulates exhalation and CO₂ removal.
  • VT is set by the Phigh − Plow gradient and varies directly with compliance and inversely with airway resistance.
  • The patient can breathe spontaneously at both levels. APRV allows spontaneous breathing but doesn't require it, which is why the deck says it is seen in organ-donor (One Legacy) cases.

When to use it: the classic patient has significant hypoxemic respiratory failure/ARDS and still has an intact respiratory drive, without being deeply paralyzed. The stated benefits are:

  • improved oxygenation
  • reduced VILI and atelectrauma
  • alveolar recruitment with lower peak airway pressures
  • possibly reduced sedation needs

The Trauma deck (slide 22 notes): APRV may be considered in selected patients with severe oxygenation problems, but it is not a routine first-line recommendation.

APRV in the Weaning deck (Chang):

  • Pressures: higher pressure e.g. 10 cm H₂O; release pressure usually 0 cm H₂O.
  • Weaning: decrease the release frequency. At zero, the patient is essentially on CPAP.

Hess adds:

  • Classification: APRV is time-cycled and pressure-controlled, and an active exhalation valve allows spontaneous breathing throughout. Because the inflation period is long, most spontaneous breaths occur at Phigh, which is why Hess calls it "upside-down SIMV."
  • Without spontaneous breathing, it is functionally pressure-controlled inverse ratio ventilation.
  • Disadvantages (Table 20-2):
    • may be uncomfortable
    • may give large VT depending on the Phigh − Plow difference
    • large transpulmonary pressure swings with spontaneous breaths
  • Evidence: survival evidence is inconsistent. Ch 38 adds that no large trials show clinical benefit.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 22 (and notes), 23; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 26; RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 22 (notes); RCP 105 Weaning & Extubation (Chang Ch 13, 16), slide 24 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes, Table 20-2; Hess Ch 38 — Mechanical Ventilation

11HFOV (High-Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation)

What it is. HFOV delivers extremely small volumes at high frequency ("Tiny VT + very fast RR").

  • The VT may be around or even below anatomic dead space.
  • Main application: to minimize development of lung injury while providing mechanical ventilation.
  • HFOV actively pushes gas in and actively pulls gas out, unlike conventional ventilation, where exhalation is passive.

How to adjust it:

  • Primary settings: amplitude (ΔP/power), frequency (Hz), mean airway pressure, FiO₂. Percent inspiration and bias flow are secondary.
  • To improve ventilation (CO₂): ↑ amplitude or ↓ frequency. Lowering Hz is "BACKWARDS from conventional ventilation": ↓ Hz → larger oscillatory VT → ↑ CO₂ removal. The L4 slide 27 notes add ↑ inspiratory time or ↑ bias flow (with an intentional cuff leak).
  • To improve oxygenation: ↑ mean airway pressure or ↑ FiO₂.
  • Frequency (MgmtMV deck): usually 3–15 Hz = 180–900 breaths/min (the RCP 203 Ch 11 slide 82 notes say 3–10 Hz). HFOV was traditionally used in neonates when conventional ventilation fails and has since been used in adult acute respiratory failure.
  • When to use it (RCP 203 Ch 11 deck, slides 61, 63): HFV runs at 150–900 breaths/min with VT as small as 1–2 mL/kg. It is indicated primarily for hypoxemic respiratory failure that has not responded to conventional methods (severe IRDS or ARDS, with or without air leak). Relative contraindications: hypotension/unstable cardiovascular status, air trapping/dynamic hyperinflation, and (neonates) intracranial hemorrhage.
  • Not routine in adults (Trauma deck, slide 22 notes): the slide text says "PSV, APRV, and HFOV are highly considered", but its notes say HFOV "is not routinely recommended for adult patients with acute respiratory failure or ARDS because it has not demonstrated a clinical advantage and may cause harm."
  • Adult weaning steps:
    1. meet weaning criteria
    2. wean FiO₂ to 40%
    3. reduce mPaw by 2–3 cm H₂O every 4–6 h to 22–24 cm H₂O
    4. switch to PCV at f 20–25, I:E 1:1, PEEP 12
    5. titrate PCV to VT 6–8 mL/kg, keeping Pplat < 35 and mPaw < 20 cm H₂O
  • The RCP 203 Ch 11 weaning steps differ (slide 96): lower FiO₂ to 0.40, then cut Pmean 2–3 cm H₂O every 4–6 h to ≤ 20 cm H₂O, then switch to PC at a pressure limit ≤ 30 with PEEP 10–12, then a standard SBT (conflict, see conflicts file).
  • FDA approval: neonates 1991, children 1995, adults 2001.
  • The Principles deck gives HFOV as an example of time control.
  • The NMBA deck lists HFOV, PC and inverse ratio among the modes where paralysis is beneficial.

Hess adds:

  • Frequencies run up to 900 breaths/min in adults (Ch 20). Ch 38 states 1 to 15 Hz (1 Hz = 60 breaths/min).
  • The high mean pressure works like high-level CPAP.
  • Its routine use in ARDS is not supported (the same message as the Trauma deck). The Ch 38 practice guideline gives a strong recommendation against routine HFOV in moderate or severe ARDS: one large RCT showed no mortality difference and another showed increased mortality. Ch 20 says it might be considered for refractory hypoxemic respiratory failure in experienced centers.
Gap · still not coveredstarting (initiation) settings for adult HFOV — amplitude, Hz and mPaw. The Management of MV slide is titled "Initiate HFOV for adults" but lists only the weaning steps — look in Chang Ch 12.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 26 (and notes), 27 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 22 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 61, 63, 82 (notes), 96; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 20 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 75 (notes); RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 — NMBAs, slide 28 · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes; Hess Ch 38 — Mechanical Ventilation, CPG 38-1

12All the Listed Modes Side by Side

Every entry below is restated from items 2–11, with the same sources. Use the table to drill the differences.

Mode What is set / guaranteed Trigger → cycle; breath sequence When to use it (per the sources)
VC VT guaranteed, pressure varies; constant (square) flow Patient or time → volume; used in CMV or IMV 1st choice; when a constant VT is needed for a desired PaCO₂
PC Pressure guaranteed, VT varies; decelerating flow Time (or patient) → time; pressure-limited 2nd choice; severe ARDS / when limiting pressure matters more than VT
CMV (202/Chang "control") Preset VT and rate; no patient trigger Time → machine Tetanus/seizures, complete rest, crushed chest, paradoxical chest wall; needs sedation ± paralysis
CMV (Hess) = A/C Every breath full support; set rate = minimum Patient or time → machine Full support at initiation; minimize atelectasis
IMV Mandatory breaths at fixed intervals + spontaneous breaths Time-triggered mandatory Historical; breath stacking; not offered on new adult ventilators
SIMV Mandatory breaths synchronized; spontaneous at any VT (± PS) Time or patient → machine (mandatory); patient → flow (PS breaths) Partial support, preserve muscle strength, weaning
MMV Minimum V̇E guaranteed Low V̇E → ↑ mandatory (volume-cycled) breaths Weaning / transition / variable drive
PRVC Target VT at the lowest PIP; pressure auto-adjusted breath to breath Time or patient → time (time-triggered when the patient makes no effort, per the Automode slide); Hess: the breath type is PC Volume guarantee of VC with the lower pressures of PC
VV+ (VC+ / VS) VC+: preset VT + I-time, variable pressure and flow; VS: target VT only VC+: mandatory in A/C or SIMV; VS: patient → pressure-limited → flow Better synchrony than VC; VS for post-anesthesia recovery / weaning
PAV No target flow, volume or pressure; % assist ∝ effort Patient → flow; spontaneous Spontaneous patients needing variable assist (COPD, NM disease, coming off sedation); not apneic
APRV Phigh/Plow, Thigh/Tlow; no VT, no RR Time-cycled PC; spontaneous breathing at both levels Hypoxemic failure/ARDS with intact drive; Trauma deck: not routine first-line
HFOV Amplitude, Hz, mPaw, FiO₂; VT around or below dead space Oscillator-timed ("time control") Minimize lung injury; hypoxemic failure unresponsive to conventional ventilation; Trauma deck and Hess: not routine in adult ARDS
Sourceas cited in items 2–11

13What Causes Patient–Ventilator Asynchrony: Definition, Consequences, and Trigger Asynchrony

Definition and consequences. Hess: synchrony means the ventilator's breath delivery is in phase with the patient's effort, and asynchrony means it is out of phase. At worst, asynchrony looks like the patient is "fighting or bucking the ventilator", but it is often subtle. It:

  • decreases comfort
  • increases WOB and the O₂ cost of breathing
  • increases sedation needs
  • is associated with longer mechanical ventilation

The Waveforms deck adds that dyssynchrony also increases myocardial work and prolongs weaning. Its examples are double-triggering, breath stacking, missed triggers and ineffective efforts.

Hess sorts asynchrony into three forms: trigger, cycle and mode. The RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slide 78) uses trigger, flow and cycle, and links asynchrony to longer mechanical ventilation and ICU stay.

Trigger asynchrony, type 1: missed or ineffective triggering (the patient can't trigger).

  • Causes:
    • an insensitive trigger setting. The MgmtMV deck says that with sensitivity "too high (not sensitive enough)" such as −5 cm H₂O, the patient may be unable to trigger and becomes uncomfortable and fatigued.
    • respiratory muscle weakness
    • a common cause (Hess), auto-PEEP in obstructive disease. Auto-PEEP raises the baseline, so it is harder to reach the trigger level below it.
  • Signs:
    • Hess: a patient respiratory rate greater than the ventilator rate means auto-PEEP is compromising triggering.
    • On waveforms, inspiratory efforts during exhalation show as slowed expiratory flow plus a negative pressure dip.
    • Deep negative deflections before a breath mean excessive triggering effort.
  • Fixes (Hess):
    • make the trigger more sensitive. The usual pressure trigger is 1 to 2 cm H₂O below PEEP/CPAP.
    • for auto-PEEP: lower V̇E, shorten the I:E, give bronchodilators and clear secretions
    • in COPD, applied PEEP to counterbalance auto-PEEP can be effective, but it rarely works when the auto-PEEP comes from a high V̇E and too little expiratory time. Watch PIP: if it rises more than the PEEP increase, suspect overdistention.
    • NAVA triggering is not affected by auto-PEEP.

Trigger asynchrony, type 2: auto-triggering (auto-cycling). The ventilator triggers on an artifact. Causes:

  • cardiac oscillations (fix: adjust sensitivity)
  • water condensation in the circuit (fix: drain it)
  • circuit leaks (fix: correct the leak). With PEEP, a leak lowers circuit pressure below the sensitivity threshold, and the ventilator reads that as a patient effort.
  • an overly sensitive trigger, e.g. −0.5 cm H₂O or a 0.5 L/min flow trigger (the RCP 203 Ch 11 notes say even −1 cm H₂O or 1 L/min may be too low; fix by decreasing sensitivity slightly, e.g. −1 → −2 cm H₂O or 1 → 2 L/min). Sensitivity direction: a number closer to zero = more sensitive (−3 → −1), farther = less sensitive (−3 → −5).

Think "leak" when breaths appear in a patient making no obvious efforts.

Reverse triggering (Hess). A ventilator-triggered breath triggers neural effort in the patient. If that effort is large enough, it triggers a second breath, and the combined VT may contribute to VILI.

SourceRCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 38 (and notes), 43, 48, 51 (notes), 54 (and notes); RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slide 33 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 78, 79 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction; Breath Triggering; Physical Assessment

14Flow Asynchrony (Flow Starvation) and Cycle Asynchrony (Premature vs Delayed)

Flow mismatch / flow starvation. Hess: the ventilator does not meet the patient's inspiratory flow demand.

  • Waveform: the pressure waveform differs breath to breath, PIP varies, and "a vigorous patient effort literally pulls the airway pressure graphic downward."
  • Clinical signs: tachypnea, retractions, chest–abdominal paradox.
  • It is more common with VC but can occur with PC.
  • Fixes:
    • increase the flow or change the flow pattern in VC
    • switch VC → PC or PS
    • increase the pressure or the rise-time setting in PC/PS
    • sedation or analgesia if drive is high from anxiety or pain
  • In PS, a too-low initial flow causes flow starvation, and a too-high flow causes a pressure overshoot.

Reading the dip in the Waveforms deck:

  • A pressure dip early in inspiration = "I need more flow" (inadequate peak flow).
  • A dip late in inspiration = "I want more volume": the breath ended before the patient's effort did.
  • During PSV, arrows a–c show inadequate flow.
  • Rise time: too fast gives a pressure spike; a slower rise gives more comfort.
  • The RCP 203 Ch 11 notes (slide 27) say an appropriate flow rate prevents "discomfort, air hunger, and asynchrony". Its slide 80 names the inadequate-flow sign "scalloping" (concavity) of the pressure waveform of assisted breaths (and of the flow waveform in VC): the patient's effort is added on top of the ventilator's.

Cycle asynchrony (Hess). The patient's neural inspiratory time doesn't match the ventilator's inspiratory time.

Premature cycling (I-time too short):

  • The patient's effort continues after the breath ends, so the patient may double-trigger: a second breath almost immediately after the first.
  • Double triggering → breath stacking. The patient can receive up to twice the set VT.
  • One study found double triggering more common with PC than VC.
  • A high initial flow can prematurely cycle a PS breath, because PS cycles at a fraction of peak flow.
  • On Figure 20-10, premature termination = continued inspiratory efforts after the breath.
  • Double triggering can also come from reverse triggering (Hess).
  • Fixes (from the sources): give the breath more volume or a longer inspiratory delivery (the late-dip reading on Waveforms slide 39). In PS, the flow-termination (cycle) criterion can be adjusted, which changes the inspiratory time (Hess Figure 20-11); a high initial flow can cycle a PS breath early, and rise time should be adjusted to maintain patient comfort (Hess).

Delayed (prolonged) cycling (I-time too long):

  • The patient actively exhales against the breath.
  • Signs:
    • abdominal (expiratory) muscle activation, felt by palpation or seen as rounding of the abdomen
    • on graphics, a pressure spike at end-inspiration: the ventilator is pressure-cycling instead of flow-cycling
    • on Figure 20-10, delayed termination shows as a bump in pressure above the plateau (the patient actively exhales)
  • Causes: PS in obstructive disease (flow decays slowly and never reaches the cycle threshold) and leaks larger than the termination flow.
  • Fixes (Hess):
    • lower the PS level
    • raise the termination-flow (cycle) setting
    • switch to PC (time-cycled)
    • with a leak, use PC, a ventilator that allows adjusting termination flow, or a set maximum inspiratory time (secondary time cycle)
  • Hess: for patient-triggered breaths, keep I-time short (≤ 1.5 s).

Air trapping / incomplete exhalation. Expiratory flow doesn't return to baseline before the next breath (PSV arrows f–g; slide 50, arrow e). This leads to breath stacking, auto-PEEP, ↑ WOB and worsening synchrony. Active expiratory effort during exhalation (faster expiratory flow, higher airway pressure; Waveforms slide 49) suggests air trapping/auto-PEEP or settings that don't match the patient. Auto-PEEP itself is covered in item 23.

SourceRCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 33, 39 (and notes), 40 (and notes), 42, 44 (and notes), 49, 50 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 27 (notes), 80 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction (Figures 20-27, 20-28, 20-29); Ventilator Modes (Figures 20-10, 20-11); Inspiratory Time

15Mode Asynchrony and the Fix Sequence (Settings → Mode → Sedation → Paralysis)

Mode asynchrony (Hess). The mode itself can cause asynchrony when the ventilator delivers different breath types, as with SIMV: the respiratory center cannot adapt to varying breaths. It also happens with adaptive pressure control (PRVC-type modes), which inappropriately reduce support when excessive effort (pain, metabolic acidosis) pushes VT above target.

Other mode-related culprits (decks, plus Hess Table 20-2):

  • the control mode (CMV) in a patient who wakes up
  • IRV, which "may cause significant patient-ventilator dyssynchrony → often requires increased sedation"
  • the old IMV (breath stacking)
  • SIMV with too low a rate (Hess Table 20-2)
  • PS set too low (fatigue, tachypnea) or too high (expiratory-muscle activation) (Hess Table 20-2)

Hess says PAV and NAVA may improve synchrony. In ARDS, spontaneous efforts can contribute to asynchrony, and VT may be raised to 8 mL/kg for marked asynchrony provided Pplat ≤ 30 cm H₂O.

The fix sequence:

  1. Find the cause on the waveform first. Then adjust settings: VT, rate, peak flow, PEEP, plus trigger sensitivity, rise time and cycle criteria.
  2. Reconsider the mode. "Some patients may synchronize better with pressure-targeted modes than volume-targeted modes". The deck names PCV and APRV, and VC+ was designed for better synchrony than VC.
  3. Sedation/analgesia. Hess: "Anxiety is a common cause of failure to breathe in synchrony with the ventilator." Options are analgesics (narcotics), sedatives (benzodiazepines, propofol, dexmedetomidine), or rarely paralytics. Propofol suits short-term sedation to restore synchrony.
  4. Neuromuscular blockade, last. NMBAs are used to improve ventilator–patient synchrony, ventilation and oxygenation and to lower ventilating pressures. But the NMBA deck warns: "DO NOT jump to paralytics."
    • Troubleshoot first: kinked tube, secretions, wrong vent settings, circuit issue.
    • "Paralytics don't fix problems – they hide them."
    • Paralysis always requires ventilation plus sedation.
SourceRCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 38 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 24; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 14 (notes), 18, 28; RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 — NMBAs, slides 28, 34 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction; Sedation; Table 20-2; Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome

16ASV, ATC, and IRV: the Other "Advanced Modes" Slides high-yield · beyond the listed asks

ASV (Adaptive Support Ventilation) is a closed-loop mode that targets a minute ventilation.

  • The RT enters the IBW and the desired minute ventilation.
  • The ventilator measures dynamic compliance and the expiratory time constant. It then picks the rate, I-time, I:E and pressure limit for both mandatory and spontaneous breaths, using the Otis equation to find the frequency with the lowest WOB.
  • More patient effort → less support; less effort → more support.
  • Hess adds:
    • The target is 0.1 L/min/kg, set as 25% to 350% of target, with an estimated dead space of 2.2 mL/kg.
    • With patient triggering, ASV resembles volume support.
    • VT may exceed 10 mL/kg IBW in near-normal lungs.

ATC (Automatic Tube Compensation; PB840 "Tubing Compensation") automatically offsets the resistance of the artificial airway so the patient breathes as if the tube weren't there.

  • Unlike PSV, it compensates during both inspiration and expiration and varies dynamically.
  • ↓ ETT diameter or ↑ flow demand → ↑ pressure compensation. A 6.0 ETT needs more compensation than an 8.0.
  • Hess adds: the clinician can set the fraction compensated (e.g. 50%), but TC has not been shown to improve outcomes.

IRV (Inverse Ratio Ventilation)

  • Normal I:E is 1:1.5 to 1:3 (sometimes 1:4); IRV uses 2:1 to 4:1. (The RCP 203 Ch 11 slide text prints normal as 1:2 to 1:3; its notes say 1:1.5 to 1:3.)
  • How it helps: longer I-time → ↑ mean airway pressure → more recruitment → better oxygenation.
  • Adverse effects:
    • ↓ venous return
    • auto-PEEP from the short expiration
    • overdistension/barotrauma
    • pulmonary edema
    • agitation
    • significant dyssynchrony needing more sedation, sometimes neuromuscular blockade
  • Link to APRV: APRV has an inverse-ratio timing pattern but permits spontaneous breathing.
  • Hess: unless IRV is coupled with spontaneous breathing, this "extreme (and potentially hazardous) form of ventilation is seldom necessary."
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 14 (and notes), 24 (and notes), 25 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 30 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes; Inspiratory Time

17Ventilator Variables: Pressure, Volume, Flow and Time

The three variables of every breath. The Principles deck says every ventilator breath has three variables: pressure, volume and flow. The RCP 203 Ch 11 deck notes (slide 27) define them:

  • Volume = the amount of air (tidal volume) delivered with each breath, measured in mL or L.
  • Pressure = the force exerted by the ventilator to deliver air into the lungs, measured in cm H₂O. If it is too high it can cause lung injury (barotrauma).
  • Flow (V̇) = the rate at which air is delivered, measured in L/min. The Principles deck adds: "Volume tells us how much air is delivered. Flow tells us how fast it gets there." (slide 3 notes) and normal adult inspiratory flow = 40–60 L/min (slide 2).
  • Pressure, flow and resistance are linked by the Principles deck's Raw = ΔP ÷ V̇ (item 24).

Time is the fourth variable. Hess:

  • Inspiratory time runs from the start of inspiratory flow to the start of expiratory flow. It equals inspiratory flow time plus inspiratory hold (pause) time.
  • Expiratory time runs from the start of expiratory flow to the start of inspiratory flow.
  • Total cycle time = TI + TE = 60/f, and I:E = TI:TE.
  • Worked example (Waveforms deck): with constant flow, VT = flow × TI. A flow of 60 L/min = 1 L/s; given for 1 s, it delivers a 1 L tidal volume.

Control variables (what the ventilator holds fixed during inspiration). Per the Principles deck, "What are the three control variables?" → volume, pressure and time. The two most commonly used in the ICU are VC and PC.

  • Volume control: both volume and flow are preset before inspiration.
  • Pressure control: inspiratory pressure as a function of time is predetermined.
  • Time control: inspiratory flow, volume and pressure all depend on respiratory system mechanics. The deck's example is HFOV (and some jet ventilators). In Hess's mode algorithm, a mode is time-controlled when neither volume/flow nor pressure is preset and pressure is not proportional to effort. Hess's example is Intrapulmonary Percussive Ventilation.

Phase variables (what happens within one breath). The deck lists the "mechanical breath variables (control, trigger, cycle)."

  • Trigger = what starts inspiration (item 19).
  • Limit = the value held during inspiration. The decks name it for pressure breaths: PSV point "B = Limited by Pressure," and for PCV "Limit – What is controlled? = Pressure."
  • Cycle = what ends inspiration (item 20).
  • Baseline = the level everything is measured from. The Principles deck: "Pressure, volume, and flow are all measured relative to their baseline values."
    • Volume baseline = end-expiratory lung volume. The volume added above it during inspiration is the VT.
    • Pressure baseline = PEEP / the pressure at the start of inspiration.
    • Flow baseline = zero flow (positive during inspiration, negative during expiration).
    • Worked example: PEEP 5 and PIP 25 → the pressure causing inspiration is 25 − 5 = 20 cm H₂O (the notes call it "Driving Pressure or Pressure above PEEP"; see item 24 for why Pplat − PEEP is the true driving pressure).
  • Hess says the same of the equation of motion: all variables are "measured relative to their end-expiratory values," and the pressure baseline is normally the set PEEP.
  • A naming trap (Hess): in PC, the operator sometimes sets the pressure relative to atmospheric pressure (called peak inspiratory pressure) and sometimes relative to PEEP (called "inspiratory pressure"). The deck's version: PC 20 + PEEP 10 → PIP 30 cm H₂O.
Gap · still not covereda named "limit variable" for volume-control breaths. The decks name the limit only for pressure breaths (PSV, PCV = pressure), and Hess Ch 19 does not use "limit" as a phase variable; the Waveforms deck's "time- and flow-limited ventilation" (slides 18–19) describes flow patterns, not a phase variable.
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 2, 3 (notes), 62 (notes), 75 (and notes), 78 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 21 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 27 (notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5 (and notes), 23 (notes), 30 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 6 (notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#1, #3), Table 19-1, Figure 19-10 (description)

18Independent vs Dependent Variables

The definition (Principles deck): "Whatever the ventilator guarantees is called the 'independent variable.'" The variable left to float is the dependent variable.

Independent (set, guaranteed) Dependent (varies with the patient) Deck example when the patient develops ARDS
Volume control Volume (Hess: flow and tidal volume) Pressure VT 500 mL at PIP 20 → VT still 500 mL, PIP rises to 35 cm H₂O
Pressure control Pressure (Hess: the pressure set) Volume (Hess: flow and tidal volume) 20 cm H₂O with VT 500 mL → pressure still 20, VT falls to 300 mL
Time control (HFOV) Inspiratory time Flow, volume and pressure all depend on mechanics —
Why it mattersWhy only one can be independent. The decks' golden rule is that you can "Control" only one item; the other is the "Variable." Hess: "A ventilator can control only one variable at a time—either pressure or volume." (The full golden-rule discussion is in item 1.)

What moves the dependent variable (Hess).

In VC, PIP is the dependent variable (Box 20-4). PIP rises with:

  • a higher peak flow setting
  • constant (rather than descending ramp) flow: PIP is lower with descending ramp flow
  • ↑ PEEP
  • auto-PEEP
  • ↑ VT
  • ↑ airways resistance
  • ↓ compliance

Hess's key point: during VC, "changes in respiratory system mechanics affect mean and peak inspiratory pressure."

In PC, VT is the dependent variable (Box 20-5).

  • VT rises with:
    • a higher driving pressure (PIP − PEEP)
    • longer inspiratory time, but only while flow is still present
    • greater patient effort
  • VT falls with:
    • ↑ auto-PEEP
    • ↓ compliance
    • ↑ resistance (after flow reaches zero, resistance no longer affects VT)

Hess's key point: during PC, "changes in respiratory system mechanics affect peak inspiratory flow and tidal volume."

The RCP 203 deck says the same thing at the bedside.

  • Falling compliance:
    • in VC: ↑ PIP, ↑ Pplat, no change in PIP − Pplat
    • in PC: ↓ VT
  • Rising airway resistance:
    • in VC: ↑ PIP, ↑ PIP − Pplat, no change in Pplat
    • in PC: ↓ VT, but only if flow continues to the end of the breath
  • Improvements in compliance or resistance produce the opposite changes.

A Hess subtlety for PRVC-type modes. Setting a tidal volume is "necessary but not sufficient" to call a mode volume control, because some pressure control modes let the operator set a target tidal volume but let the ventilator determine the flow. True VC means both volume and flow are preset.

SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 33 (notes), 75 (and notes), 79 (and notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 4; RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 52, 53, 54, 55 · Hess Ch 20 — Volume Control Versus Pressure Control, Box 20-4, Box 20-5; Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#3); Key Points

19Trigger: What Starts the Breath

Definition. Hess: "Starting inspiration is called triggering, or the trigger event."

  • Common trigger signals are time, airway pressure, volume or flow changes. An electrical signal from the diaphragm (NAVA) can also trigger inspiration.
  • Sensitivity = the amount the trigger (or cycle) signal must change before inspiration starts (or stops).
  • The deck's version: "Trigger = Start". Ask, "Who started the breath?" The answer is the patient or time.

Machine (time) triggering. The ventilator starts the breath from a clinician-set rate or timer.

  • Rate 12 → a breath every 5 seconds (OpModes notes). A time-triggered breath shows no negative deflection before inspiration.
  • Hess: the ventilator can also use minute ventilation as a trigger: inspiration is triggered if V̇E drops below a preset threshold.

Patient triggering. The patient's effort produces a negative pressure deflection below baseline before the breath. There are three ways the ventilator senses it:

  1. Pressure trigger detects a drop in airway pressure. Hess: it is set so trigger effort is minimal but auto-triggering is unlikely, typically 1 to 2 cm H₂O below the PEEP or CPAP setting.
  2. Flow trigger detects a drop in flow through the circuit. Hess's worked example:
    • base flow 10 L/min, flow sensitivity 3 L/min
    • the ventilator triggers when expiratory-circuit flow drops to 7 L/min
    • the assumption is that the patient inhaled 3 L/min
  3. Electrical activity of the diaphragm (NAVA).

Hess adds that on current ventilators the differences between pressure and flow triggering are minor.

Setting sensitivity (MgmtMV deck notes).

  • Too high, i.e. not sensitive enough (e.g. −5 cm H₂O): the patient may not be able to trigger and becomes uncomfortable and fatigued.
  • Too low, i.e. too sensitive (e.g. −0.5 cm H₂O or a flow trigger of 0.5 L/min): the ventilator may deliver unwanted breaths (autotrigger).
  • The Waveforms deck's A/C example uses a pressure-trigger threshold of −2 cm H₂O, consistent with Hess's 1 to 2 cm H₂O below PEEP.
  • An excessive sensitivity setting is one listed cause of the high-frequency alarm.

The asynchrony side (missed triggers, auto-triggering from leaks, water and cardiac oscillations) is in item 13.

Auto-PEEP and triggering. Auto-PEEP raises the baseline pressure and makes triggering to the sensitivity level (below baseline) more difficult. Hess: auto-PEEP "functions as a threshold pressure that must be overcome" before airway pressure or flow falls enough to trigger. "Neither pressure triggering nor flow triggering may be effective if significant auto-PEEP is present." The work of triggering is an "imposed load for the patient."

Trigger window vs synchronization window (Hess).

  • A trigger window is the entire expiratory time minus a short refractory period. A patient signal inside it starts a patient-triggered breath.
  • A synchronization window is a short period at the end of a preset expiratory time (or inspiratory time).
    • A patient signal in an expiratory window starts inspiration, but the breath counts as machine-triggered: it would have been time-triggered anyway.
    • A patient signal in an inspiratory window starts expiration, which counts as machine-cycled.
  • Trigger windows are used only in CMV; synchronization windows only in IMV.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5 (notes), 14 (notes), 16; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 30, 33 (and notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 11, 12 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#4, #5, #7); Hess Ch 20 — Breath Triggering; Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (Figure 20-22)

20Cycle: What Ends the Breath (and How Trigger–Limit–Cycle Labels Each Mode)

Definition. Hess: "Stopping inspiration is called cycling, or the cycle event." The deck's version: "Cycle = Stop". Ask, "What ends the breath?" The answer is volume, time or flow.

The four ways to cycle (Hess).

  • Volume cycling: inspiration ends at a preset tidal volume.
  • Time cycling: inspiration ends at a preset inspiratory time (or inspiratory pause time).
  • Flow cycling: inspiration ends when inspiratory flow decreases to the operator-set value, called the flow cycle threshold or expiratory trigger sensitivity. Pressure then drops back to PEEP. This is the method used for pressure support.
    • The decks: PS ends when flow falls to a predetermined percentage of peak flow, "e.g., 25% of peak flow" ("commonly around 25%").
    • Flow cycling is a form of patient cycling, because patient mechanics decide how fast flow decays.
  • Pressure cycling: inspiration ends when a preset pressure is reached. Hess counts it as a form of patient cycling, and it "most often occurs as an alarm condition (high-pressure alarm)." It is also routine in automatic resuscitators.
    • Hess's example: a cough during VC-CMV can pressure-cycle a breath via the pressure alarm limit. This is not normal operation, and the sequence does not become IMV.
Exam anchorThe deck's memory line (Waveforms): "Volume Control is volume-cycled. Pressure Control is time-cycled. Pressure Support Ventilation is flow-cycled."
Why it mattersWhy trigger and cycle matter together.
  • Spontaneous breath = the patient both triggers and cycles it.
  • Mandatory breath = the ventilator triggers or cycles it, or both.

Patient-triggered ≠ spontaneous (item 1).

Trigger / limit / cycle labels that appear in the decks.

Breath / mode Trigger Limit Cycle
PSV Patient Pressure Flow (~25% of peak flow)
PCV Time (or patient, if allowed) Pressure Time
VC (A/C) Patient (assist) or time (control) — Volume
Control mode (CMV, Chang) Time only (preset VT) — —
Volume Support Patient Pressure Flow
Dual control (VCV + PCV) — Pressure (volume-targeted) Time
MMV mandatory breaths Low measured V̇E raises the mandatory rate — Volume

Hess's patient vs machine lists (Figure 19-13).

  • Patient trigger variables: airway pressure change, inspiratory or expiratory flow change, a bioelectrical signal, other signals of patient effort.
  • Patient cycle variables: airway pressure change, inspiratory flow change, a bioelectrical signal, other signals of patient effort.
  • Machine trigger variables: time (preset frequency), minute ventilation, other machine signals independent of patient mechanics.
  • Machine cycle variables: time (preset inspiratory time), volume, other machine signals independent of patient mechanics.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5 (notes), 14, 16, 22, 23 (notes), 30 (and notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 9 (notes), 17, 19 (notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 11; RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 31 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 80 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#4, #5, #6, #7), Figure 19-13 (description)

21PEEP: What It Is, What It Does, and How Much to Use

Definition (RCP 202 deck). "Positive End-Expiratory Pressure (PEEP) increases the end-expiratory or baseline airway pressure. PEEP keeps alveoli from collapsing at end-expiration, which increases FRC and improves oxygenation."

  • It is "not commonly regarded as a stand-alone mode". It is used in conjunction with another ventilator mode.
  • Normally airway pressure returns to 0 cm H₂O at end-expiration. With PEEP it stays elevated (e.g. 5, 8 or 10 cm H₂O).
  • The notes: "PEEP is primarily an oxygenation intervention, not a ventilation intervention."
  • Pearl: "What is the number one ventilator adjustment for improving oxygenation? Answer: PEEP."
  • CPAP is PEEP applied to a patient who is breathing entirely on their own: all spontaneous breaths, no mechanical breaths. On BiPAP, EPAP = PEEP (L4 slide 29: EPAP is "similar to CPAP or PEEP").

What PEEP does (the slide's chain). ↓ the pressure threshold for alveolar inflation → ↑ FRC → improves ventilation → ↑ V/Q → improves oxygenation → ↓ WOB. It recruits collapsed alveoli and reduces shunt.

Major indications (decks).

  • Intrapulmonary shunting and refractory hypoxemia. The MgmtMV deck: consider PEEP if refractory hypoxemia is present (e.g. PaO₂/FiO₂ < 200 mm Hg), and keep PaO₂ < 80 mm Hg (< 60 for COPD) or FiO₂ < 60% to prevent oxygen toxicity.
  • Decreased FRC and lung compliance. "Reduced lung compliance greatly increases the alveolar opening pressure."
  • Auto-PEEP not responding to adjustments of ventilator settings.

Hess adds other uses:

  • maintain alveolar recruitment
  • counterbalance auto-PEEP
  • reduce cardiac preload and afterload (left heart failure)
  • reduce microaspiration around the cuff
  • pneumatic splinting of the airway (malacia)
  • facilitate leak speech

How much PEEP.

  • Initial setting (Initiation deck): 5 cm H₂O initially for refractory hypoxemia (post-resuscitation, smoke inhalation, ARDS). The slide adds "NBRC range 2–6". Then adjust based on FiO₂ requirement, tolerance of PEEP and cardiovascular responses.
  • Hess:
    • Low-level PEEP (3 to 5 cm H₂O) is often used in all mechanically ventilated patients to prevent atelectasis.
    • ARDS may need more.
    • Use PEEP cautiously in unilateral disease: it may overdistend the more compliant lung and shunt blood to the less compliant one.
  • Optimal PEEP (MgmtMV deck) = "the lowest pressure level that offers the most benefits with the least side effects."
    • It is titrated with PaO₂, compliance, O₂ saturation and ventilator waveforms.
    • The deck's worked example: 10 cm H₂O is optimal because the next step (12 cm H₂O) decreased both PaO₂ and compliance.
    • On a pressure–volume loop, the RCP 105 Waveforms notes say lower inflection point = best PEEP (example: optimum PEEP = 10 cm H₂O), and its slide 58 notes set PEEP "at or slightly above" the LIP. The RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slide 46) sets PEEP 2–3 cm H₂O above the LIP, and Hess sets it slightly greater than the LIP (Box 20-6). Answer "just above the LIP" (conflict, see conflicts file).
  • RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slides 39, 40, 87): if an acceptable PaO₂/SaO₂ holds on < 50% O₂, adjust FiO₂; if not, adjust PEEP. As oxygenation improves, lower FiO₂ to ≤ 50% first, then PEEP. "Optimal" PEEP is the level that keeps SpO₂ ≥ 88–90% without lowering BP. In a decremental study, set PEEP 2 cm H₂O above the level that gave the highest Crs.
  • In ARDS:
    • The Procedures deck: FiO₂ and PEEP rise together. Its examples: FiO₂ 30% → PEEP about 5; 60% → about 10; 100% → 20–24. A decremental approach opens the lung with high PEEP, then lowers PEEP to find the lowest PEEP that keeps the alveoli open. The notes add that recruitment maneuvers and decremental PEEP trials are used in selected patients, not routinely in every ARDS patient.
    • Hess: aggressive (at least 13 to 15 cm H₂O) and conservative (at least 7 to 9 cm H₂O) PEEP strategies had comparable outcomes. Higher PEEP may benefit moderate and severe ARDS (P/F ≤ 200) but may harm mild ARDS (P/F > 200). The PEEP/FiO₂ tables target PaO₂ 55–80 mm Hg or SpO₂ 88%–95%.
  • Mean airway pressure (Hess): set by PIP, PEEP, I:E ratio, respiratory rate and inspiratory flow pattern. Most patients can be managed with a mean airway pressure of 10 to 20 cm H₂O.
Gap · still not covered"physiologic PEEP" (the small end-expiratory pressure normally kept by the glottis). The term and any value for it appear in no deck and nowhere in Hess (searched "physiologic/physiological PEEP" and glottis + PEEP). Look in Egan's or a Chang PEEP chapter.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slides 6 (and notes), 7, 29; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 39, 40, 46 (and notes), 87; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 9 (notes), 11 (notes), 13 (notes); RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 11, 16, 17 (and notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 18; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 37 (notes), 39 (and notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 58 (notes), 62 (notes), 64 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Positive End-Expiratory Pressure; Mean Airway Pressure; Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome; Box 20-6

22PEEP Complications and the "Dangerous Level" (> 10 cm H₂O)

Dangerous levels start at (the RCP 202 slide, starred on the slide itself): "PEEP > 10 cmH₂O (or P̄aw > 30 cmH₂O; or PIP > 50 cmH₂O) is associated with an increased incidence of alveolar rupture or barotrauma." The Chang lung-protection list says the same: the "safe" airway pressures are PIP < 50, Pplat < 35, mean airway pressure < 30, PEEP < 10 cm H₂O (item 24). Use > 10 cm H₂O as the exam answer. There is a second danger pairing in the Principles deck: a high level of PEEP (> 15 cm H₂O) plus increased intra-abdominal pressure (> 20 cm H₂O) "can adversely affect the cardiovascular, renal, and pulmonary systems." The notes say the lungs get squeezed from both directions.

The caveat (conflict, see conflicts file). The Procedures deck notes call "PEEP < 10" older practice. "Many ARDS patients require PEEP levels of 10–15 cm H₂O or even higher," so PEEP should be individualized. Hess likewise reports aggressive PEEP of at least 13 to 15 cm H₂O with outcomes comparable to conservative PEEP. Hess limits PEEP by its effect instead: avoid PEEP that results in Pplat > 30 cm H₂O (except with abnormal chest wall mechanics) or a driving pressure greater than 15 cm H₂O.

The complications (decks):

  1. Decreased venous return → hypotension (the major downside). "Pleural pressure becomes less negative, hence pressure gradient to right atrium decreases. Results in decreased C.O. and Hypotension." The chain is ↑ PEEP → ↑ intrathoracic pressure → ↓ venous return → ↓ preload → ↓ cardiac output → ↓ BP.
  2. Barotrauma: "lung injury resulting from hyperinflation of alveoli past the rupture point", i.e. pneumothorax or alveolar rupture.
  3. Increased ICP: PEEP raises intrathoracic pressure, which can impede venous drainage from the brain and potentially increase ICP. The OpModes slide says this applies "in pts with normal lungs." The RCP 203 deck lists high PEEP/high mean airway pressure among the ventilator causes of ↑ ICP.
  4. Hemodynamic numbers with PPV + PEEP (Principles deck): ↑ pulmonary artery pressure, ↑ CVP, ↓ aortic pressure, ↓ cardiac output.
    • PAP rises because expanded alveoli compress the pulmonary capillaries (↑ PVR).
    • CVP rises "even though venous return is actually decreasing": "High CVP does not always mean lots of preload."
  5. Liver: "hepatic perfusion decreases only when PEEP is added to PPV." The kidneys are more sensitive to falling cardiac output than the liver.
  6. Dead space: excessive PEEP can compress pulmonary capillaries (overdistended alveoli → alveolar dead space).

Hess adds:

  • PEEP is "a two-edged sword." If more PEEP recruits alveoli and raises compliance, lung stress falls. If it raises end-inspiratory transpulmonary pressure, stress rises.
  • With ARDS, clinicians should monitor the hemodynamic effects of the higher intrathoracic pressure.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 6 (and notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 9 (and notes); RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 16 (notes), 37 (and notes), 41 (and notes), 44 (and notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 32; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 34 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 1 — Ch 1 Evaluate Data, slide 36 · Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator-Induced Lung Injury; Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome

23Auto-PEEP (Intrinsic, Inadvertent, Occult PEEP)

What it is. Unintentional PEEP: gas trapped in the lungs because the patient never finished exhaling before the next breath.

  • Other names:
    • The decks call it intrinsic PEEP, inadvertent PEEP and occult PEEP. The RCP 203 deck adds "Dynamic Hyperinflation."
    • Hess calls it intrinsic PEEP or air trapping.
  • Hess's definition: it is "the result of the lungs not returning to the baseline proximal airway pressure at end-exhalation." The trapped gas increases end-expiratory lung volume (dynamic hyperinflation).
  • Why it hides: the notes say distal airway pressure can be as high as 15 cm H₂O while the ventilator's proximal manometer shows zero (or just the set PEEP).
  • Total PEEP = set PEEP + auto-PEEP.

Causes. The MgmtMV notes list:

  • excessive pressure support ventilation
  • significant airway obstruction
  • high frequency (above 20/min)
  • insufficient inspiratory flow rates
  • a relatively equal (about 1:1) or inversed I:E ratio
  • a history of air trapping

The high-PEEP alarm causes add insufficient inspiratory flow (long I-time), insufficient expiratory time and extreme high compliance.

Hess: the determinants are high minute volume, a long inspiratory-to-expiratory time relationship, and a long expiratory time constant (obstructed airways and high-compliance units). Ch 51 adds that auto-PEEP increases with ↑ resistance and compliance, ↑ rate or ↑ inspiratory time (both shorten expiratory time), and ↑ VT. The RCP 203 deck puts it as the patients at greatest risk: high Raw and/or high Crs on modes that limit expiratory time.

Other deck causes:

  • breath stacking in IMV
  • IRV (short expiration)
  • continuous-flow tracheal gas insufflation
  • an excessive rate in COPD (e.g. RR 30)

What it does.

  • ↑ WOB; raises the baseline, so triggering is harder (item 19).
  • Hess: it increases intrathoracic pressure, which can affect gas delivery, hemodynamics, end-inspiratory distention (and thus VILI), and breath triggering. It is difficult to recognize and should generally be avoided.
  • The RCP 203 deck:
    • hemodynamic compromise (↓ venous return, ↓ CO, hypotension)
    • barotrauma
    • hyperinflation
  • In VC, auto-PEEP raises PIP. In PC, it reduces VT (Box 20-4, Box 20-5).
  • The Principles deck vignette: a COPD patient on RR 20, VT 700, flow 40 L/min develops rising PIP, auto-PEEP and hypotension. The good answers are to lower VT, lower RR, increase flow and increase expiratory time.

How to detect it.

  • The flow–time waveform: expiratory flow does not return to zero (baseline) before the next breath.
  • Hess:
    • exhalation is still occurring when the next breath is delivered
    • inspiratory efforts that do not trigger the ventilator
    • a patient respiratory rate greater than the ventilator rate means auto-PEEP is compromising triggering
    • narrow or collapsing small airways can hide regional auto-PEEP even when the flow signal reads zero
  • The high-PEEP alarm sounds when actual PEEP exceeds the preset PEEP limit.
  • Volume clue (Waveforms): with air trapping, expired VT < set VT for 2 to 3 breaths, and then a breath with expired VT > set VT as the trapped air comes out. A leak is low on every breath (item 25).

How to measure it.

  • Apply an end-expiratory pause (occlude the expiratory port just before the next inspiration).
  • The pause pressure minus the set PEEP = auto-PEEP. Worked example (RCP 203): 12 − 5 = 7 cm H₂O of auto-PEEP.
  • Pause length: 0.5 to 1.5 s (Hess Ch 20) or 0.5 to 2 seconds (Hess Ch 51).
  • The patient must be relaxed. The deck says "sedated or paralyzed." Active breathing invalidates the measurement.
  • Hess: the pause method can underestimate auto-PEEP when airways close during expiration (severe asthma).

How to fix it: give the lungs more time to empty.

  • MgmtMV deck:
    • ↑ flow rate to decrease I-time
    • ↓ VT to decrease I-time
    • ↓ f to increase E-time
    • keep PS frequency < 20/min
    • ↓ air trapping with a bronchodilator
  • RCP 203 deck: decrease the RR (VC or PC); increase inspiratory flow (VC); decrease VT (VC); decrease TI (PC).
  • Hess: decrease minute ventilation, increase expiratory time, decrease airways resistance (bronchodilators, clearing secretions).
  • Therapeutic (applied) PEEP comes last: "Therapeutic PEEP should be used after making ventilator adjustments." It counters auto-PEEP from small-airway air trapping. How much (conflict, see conflicts file):
    • Hess: set PEEP up to 75% to 85% of auto-PEEP in obstructive disease to facilitate triggering. Monitor PIP and Pplat, and do not use it if it raises total PEEP.
    • RCP 203 deck notes: up to 80–90% of measured auto-PEEP, "only for spontaneously breathing patients."
SourceRCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 32 (and notes), 33 (notes), 34 (and notes), 35 (and notes), 69; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 34; RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slides 37, 41, 42 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 57 (notes), 58; RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 52, 66 (notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 12 (notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 18 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 24 · Hess Ch 20 — Auto-PEEP; Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP; Physical Assessment; Obstructive Lung Disease; Hess Ch 51 — Alveolar Pressure

24High Pressures and Their Causes (PIP vs Plateau)

The two pressures.

  • PIP = the pressure needed to overcome both airflow resistance and lung compliance.
  • Plateau pressure (Pplat, "peak alveolar pressure") = the pressure needed to overcome lung (and chest wall) compliance only.
    • It is measured with an end-inspiratory pause: 0.5 to 1.5 s (Hess Ch 20) or 0.5 to 2 seconds (Hess Ch 51). The RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slide 41) says bedside mechanics need complete respiratory-muscle relaxation and at least 2–3 seconds of zero flow for pressures to equilibrate (its requirement for the respiratory-mechanics methods of PEEP titration).
    • Because flow has stopped, it does not include airway resistance.
  • PIP − Pplat = transairway pressure (PTA) = flow × resistance.
  • The rule to memorize: "When PIP and Plateau move together, think lungs. When only PIP moves, think airways."
Pattern Meaning Causes listed in the decks
↑ PIP, Pplat unchanged (↑ PIP − Pplat) ↑ Airway resistance (or ↑ flow setting) Inside the ETT: secretions, mucus plug, blood clot, kinked tube. Airways: asthma, bronchospasm, COPD exacerbation, airway edema. Circuit: water in tubing, obstructed filter, partially closed HME. Also biting the ETT, higher inspiratory flow settings.
↑ PIP and ↑ Pplat together (PIP − Pplat unchanged) ↓ Compliance ARDS, pulmonary edema, pneumonia, atelectasis, fibrosis, pneumothorax. RCP 203 adds tension pneumothorax and pleural effusion, overdistention/hyperinflation, ET tube slippage into a mainstem bronchus, mucus plug, ascites/abdominal distention.

The high-pressure alarm.

  • Setting (Initiation deck): 10 to 15 cm H₂O above the observed PIP. The RCP 203 Ch 11 notes (slide 19) give 35 cm H₂O initially (before any PIP is measured), then 10 cm H₂O above the observed PIP once the patient is stable (conflict, see conflicts file). It detects ↑ resistance or ↓ compliance: water in the circuit, secretions, kinking of the ET tube, tension pneumothorax, coughing. The "High inspiratory pressure alarm should not be raised without justification."
  • The Principles notes: high-pressure alarm causes are secretions, kinked tubing, patient biting the ETT, bronchospasm, pneumothorax.
  • The MgmtMV airway-care slide says to monitor for and avoid ET tube kinking, biting, and malfunction (e.g. herniated cuff). All of these obstruct the tube.
  • Hess:
    • In VC, the peak airway pressure alarm is the one that detects changes in resistance and compliance. In PC or PS, it is the low exhaled volume alarm.
    • In VC the set VT is delivered "provided that the high-pressure alarm limit is not reached". Reaching the limit pressure-cycles the breath, an alarm condition (item 20).
  • Circuit obstruction (RCP 203): it is always associated with low-volume and high-pressure alarms. Expiratory obstruction is the more serious: it can cause rebreathing, asphyxia or barotrauma.

How high is too high? (conflict on Pplat, see conflicts file)

Pressure Chang "safe"/threshold (Procedures slide 34; Initiation slide 32) Current target / other figures (deck notes, Hess)
PIP < 50 cm H₂O. PCV is indicated because VC pressures ≥ 50 cm H₂O can cause barotrauma. The notes call PIP limits historical. Pplat matters more.
Pplat < 35 cm H₂O (also MgmtMV low-VT slide; HFOV weaning) < 30 cm H₂O (ARDSNet; Initiation slide 13; Burn deck ≤ 30; Hess "below 30 … and lower if possible"); RCP 203 Ch 11 slide 31: Pplat > 30 in VC → probably switch to PC at ≤ 30
Mean airway pressure < 30 cm H₂O Hess: most patients 10 to 20 cm H₂O
PEEP < 10 cm H₂O Individualized (item 22)
Driving pressure (Pplat − PEEP) — < 15 cm H₂O

Use Pplat < 30 cm H₂O. The Procedures notes say "Today, the accepted goal is a Plateau Pressure (PPlat) below 30 cm H₂O whenever possible."

Driving pressure: say it correctly (conflict).

  • The true driving pressure is Pplat − PEEP. That is how the Procedures and ECMO decks and the RCP 203 Lssn 2b notes define it (resistive = PIP − Pplat; elastic = Pplat − PEEP, "the true driving pressure"), and Hess uses it for the < 15 cm H₂O target.
  • PIP − PEEP is the "total pressure delivered": it includes resistive (PIP − Pplat) plus elastic (Pplat − PEEP) pressure.
  • Four places call PIP − PEEP the "driving pressure":
    • the Principles slide 78 notes (25 − 5 = 20, "Driving Pressure or Pressure above PEEP")
    • the RCP 203 slide 37 text ("PIP – PEEP … Driving Pressure"), which its own notes correct to Pplat − PEEP
    • Hess Box 20-5, for PC
    • the RCP 203 Ch 11 deck (slide 38, and slide 44's ΔP), for PC modes: ΔP (PIP − PEEP), "typically 15–25 cm H₂O" in its PEEP-titration method
  • The two numbers are the same only when flow reaches zero at end-inspiration, as it usually does in PC: then PIP = Pplat (Hess Ch 20; Waveforms slide 25 notes). In VC with flow still present at end-inspiration, PIP − PEEP is larger because it includes the resistive pressure.
  • On the exam, driving pressure = Pplat − PEEP (< 15 cm H₂O).

Worked numbers (Raw formula from the Principles deck, slide 2; Cstat formula from the Waveforms deck, slide 25 notes, and Hess Ch 51; data from Hess Ch 51).

  • Raw = ΔP ÷ V̇, where ΔP = PIP − Pplat. Hess's example: PIP 43, Pplat 40, flow 0.33 L/s → (43 − 40) ÷ 0.33 = 9 cm H₂O/L/s.
  • Static compliance = VT ÷ (Pplat − PEEP). Hess: 320 mL ÷ (40 − 26) = 23 mL/cm H₂O.

Resistance norms depend on the population.

Source Who Value
Principles deck population not stated normal Raw 0.6–2.4 cm H₂O/L/sec (≈ 1–3); > 5 → think airway obstruction; > 10 → think severe bronchospasm, mucus plugging, kinked ETT
RCP 203 deck intubated adults 5–12 cm H₂O/L/sec
Hess intubated, mechanically ventilated RI should be < 10 cm H₂O/L/s
Gap · still not covereda "normal" adult PIP or Pplat range. Every source gives only ceilings (above). The only anchors are the deck's illustration of a patient who starts at VT 500 mL with PIP 20 cm H₂O before developing ARDS and the Waveforms example of a Pplat of 20 rising to 40 as compliance falls. Look in Egan's.
