SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 2, 13 (and notes), 33 (notes), 65 (notes), 78 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 8 (notes), 9 (and notes), 10, 16 (and notes), 17 (and notes), 25 (notes); RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slides 13 (notes), 27, 32; RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 34 (and notes); RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 20, 41, 64; RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 30; RCP 202 Lssn 2 — Burn & Inhalation Injury, slide 32 (notes); RCP 202 Lssn 5 — ECMO, slide 20 (notes); RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 37 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 53, 54, 55; RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 72; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 19 (notes), 31, 38, 41, 44 · Hess Ch 20 — Volume Control Versus Pressure Control; Box 20-5; Alarms; Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP; Tidal Volume; Mean Airway Pressure; Hess Ch 51 — Respiratory System Compliance; Airways Resistance; Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#5)

25Low Pressures and Their Causes (Leaks, Disconnection, Loss of PEEP)

The low-pressure alarm. Low inspiratory pressure alarm = 10 to 15 cm H₂O below the observed PIP, set to detect and alert leaks or disconnection. Its partner is the low exhaled volume alarm, set 100 mL lower than the expired VT, which also detects leaks or disconnection. The RCP 203 Ch 11 notes (slide 19) differ: low PIP alarm 10 cm H₂O below the observed PIP (or the PI setting in PC) and VT alarms 150 mL above and below the set VT (conflict, see conflicts file). Hess: "The most important alarm is the patient-disconnect alarm," which can be a low-pressure alarm or a low exhaled volume alarm (or both). Exhaled CO₂ can also serve as a disconnect alarm, and a sensitive alarm should catch leaks as well as disconnection. In the control mode, the most important alarms are low exhaled volume and low inspiratory pressure, because the patient depends entirely on the ventilator (item 3).

Causes of low pressure (from the decks):

  • circuit disconnection
  • circuit leaks, which are "among the most common problems causing loss of ventilator volume and pressure"
  • ET tube cuff leaks
  • ventilator malfunction can look the same. Run a circuit leak test: if it is negative, the ventilator may not be delivering the preset volume.
  • Low PEEP alarm = actual PEEP drops below the preset low PEEP limit. "Failure of the ventilator circuit to hold the PEEP is usually due to leakage in the circuit or ET tube cuff."
  • Hess: an end-inspiratory squeak over the trachea usually means insufficient air in the artificial airway cuff.

How a leak looks on the waveforms (vs air trapping).

  • Expired VT is less than set VT on every breath. With air trapping, it is low for only 2 to 3 breaths and then a breath exceeds set VT.
  • PIP is lower than the previous PIP on every breath, while pressure still returns to baseline normally.
  • With PEEP, a leak can drop circuit pressure below the sensitivity level and auto-trigger the ventilator.
  • Hess (Figure 20-23):
    • inspiratory-limb volume > expiratory-limb volume = a leak in the circuit or patient
    • inspired > expired volume measured at the patient = the patient is the source (cuff leak, bronchopleural fistula)

Pressure falling for good reasons. In VC, improving compliance or resistance lowers PIP (the reverse of item 24). Hess: if airway pressure falls below baseline during inspiration, the patient is doing work on the ventilator and the breath is called "loaded" rather than assisted (flow starvation: item 14).

The other alarms in the decks (Initiation slides 26–28; MgmtMV slides 30–32; Principles slide 93).

Alarm Setting What it means / causes
High PEEP — Auto-PEEP, air trapping, insufficient inspiratory flow (long I-time), insufficient expiratory time, extreme high compliance
Low PEEP — Circuit or ET tube cuff leaks
Apnea / low frequency 15- to 20-sec time delay Circuit disconnection, oversedation, prolonged neuromuscular block, respiratory center dysfunction, respiratory muscle fatigue
High frequency 10/min over observed total frequency Patient's need to ↑ V̇E (rapid shallow breathing), insufficient flow or pressure support, excessive sensitivity. Do not raise the limit without clear justification.
High/low FiO₂ 5% to 10% over/below analyzed FiO₂ —
Input power — Loss of electrical power/battery; loss of air or O₂ source (< 50 psig)
Control circuit — Settings out of range; failed self-test (SST) → ventilator inoperative

The rule from the Principles notes: Alarm → look at the patient → look at the ventilator → determine the cause → fix it. Never silence an alarm and walk away.

SourceRCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slides 26, 28; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 30, 31, 32 (and notes); RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 15 (notes); RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slides 52, 53 (and notes), 54; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 65 (notes), 93; RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 72; RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slides 53, 55; RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 19 (notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Alarms (Figure 20-23); Physical Assessment; Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#2)

26Ventilator Circuits: Single-Limb vs Double-Limb (Dual-Limb "Wye")

The patient circuit is the tubing that connects the ventilator to the patient. There are three basic configurations:

Circuit Where the exhalation valve is Typical use How it works
Single-limb WITH exhalation valve Near the patient end, in-line Home care or transport ventilators A pneumatically controlled exhalation valve is switched by a pressure signal through small-bore tubing. Signal high → valve closes → gas goes to the patient. Signal low → valve opens → the patient exhales to the atmosphere. A residual pressure in the valve often sets the PEEP level.
Double-limb (dual-limb, "wye") Built into the ventilator ICU ventilators Has an inspiratory limb (fresh gas to the patient), a standard 15-mm patient connector/swivel adapter, and an expiratory limb that carries exhaled gas to the ventilator's expiratory valve or PEEP/CPAP valve.
Single-limb WITHOUT exhalation valve (a leak port, "aka: Port") None. A small fitting with a carefully sized opening gives a known leak (sometimes built into the mask). Noninvasive ventilators The ventilator measures circuit pressure, calculates the leak flow and subtracts it to estimate the patient's flow and volume. With the NIV interface leak, the port prevents rebreathing most expired gas. PEEP/CPAP is created by continuous regulation of pressure and flow/leak via the demand valve. Leak ports on NIV: see the Noninvasive section.

The RCP 203 deck adds:

  • Tubing:
    • adult/pediatric circuits use large-bore corrugated tubing
    • neonatal ventilators use smaller-diameter, low-compliance tubing to minimize compressed volume loss
  • What the circuit does: it usually includes active or passive humidification, can provide bacterial filtration and monitoring, and often provides the mechanism to create PEEP/CPAP.
  • The two single-limb types: a single-limb circuit may have a true expiratory "mushroom" valve or a leakage-type exhaust port.
  • Mechanical deadspace:
    • in a dual-limb circuit, the rebreathed volume is the space between the circuit wye and the patient's airway, so any device added there (e.g. an HME) increases deadspace
    • in a single-limb circuit with a valve, it is the space between the expiratory valve and the airway
    • Hess: mechanical dead space "becomes an extension of the patient's anatomic dead space" and matters most with lung-protective small VT

Compressible volume (gas "lost" stretching the tubing).

  • Hess:
    • The circuit compresses as much as 3 to 5 mL/cm H₂O. At 25 cm H₂O above PEEP, about 100 mL of the delivered gas never reaches the patient.
    • Most modern ventilators correct for this.
  • Worked example (MgmtMV deck): at a PIP of 40 cm H₂O:
    • a circuit compliance of 5 mL/cm H₂O holds 200 mL
    • 3 mL/cm H₂O holds only 120 mL
    • so circuit compliance should be as low as possible: high circuit compliance reduces delivered VT
  • Initiation deck: corrected VT = expired VT − circuit compressible volume. Its example:
    • circuit factor 150 mL ÷ 50 cm H₂O = 3 mL/cm H₂O
    • with PIP 60 and PEEP 10, 3 × 50 = 150 mL is lost
    • this matters most in neonates and pediatrics

Circuit care (MgmtMV deck).

  • Condensation:
    • it occurs due to temperature drop and is the most common threat to circuit patency
    • it increases flow resistance
    • manage it with a heated-wire circuit, inline water trap, or HME (temporary use)
  • HME:
    • efficiency is 70% to 90% relative humidity and 30°C to 31°C
    • for an MDI, place the MDI between the HME and the patient's airway
  • Temperature:
    • the probe goes in the inspiratory limb as close to the patient as possible (e.g. the wye)
    • the target is 37°C
  • Circuit changes:
    • do not change routinely for infection control
    • weekly has been shown to be optimal
    • change it when visibly soiled
  • Hess:
    • all ventilator circuits include a filter in the inspiratory limb and an active or passive humidifier
    • condensate in the inspiratory circuit or proximal ETT shows the gas is fully saturated
    • Key Point: use the manufacturer's recommended circuit configuration. The wrong configuration can cause ventilator malfunction.

Troubleshooting (RCP 203).

  • If a major problem is suspected, remove the patient from the ventilator and ventilate manually with a resuscitator plus PEEP valve on O₂. If that fixes it, the ventilator or circuit was the cause.
  • The most common circuit problems are:
    • leaks
    • obstructions
    • expiratory/PEEP valve problems
    • humidification and temperature problems
    • infection/cross-contamination
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 63 (and notes); RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 66, 67, 68, 70, 71, 72; RCP 105 Management of MV (Chang Ch 12), slides 36 (notes), 37 (and notes), 38, 39, 40; RCP 105 Initiation of MV (Chang Ch 8), slide 14 (notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Patient Circuits; Key Points; Hess Ch 20 — Circuit; Humidification

27Exhalation (Expiratory) Valves: What They Do and What Goes Wrong

Job 1: separate inspiration from expiration. Hess (Figure 20-2): "The expiratory valve is closed during the inspiratory phase, and the inspiratory valve is closed during the expiratory phase." The RCP 203 deck: most critical care ventilators have an internal electromechanical expiratory valve that closes when the machine triggers to inspiration and opens to allow exhalation. In a single-limb circuit with a valve, a separate pneumatic line pressurizes the valve so that it blocks gas outflow during inspiration (item 26).

Job 2: set PEEP. Hess: "The expiratory valve controls positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP)." In the ICU ventilator schematic (Hess Figure 19-1, also on the Principles slide):

  • the expiratory valve is a proportional valve used to adjust the pressure in the patient circuit
  • the exhalation circuit consists of the expiratory valve, an expiratory pressure sensor (with its calibration valve) and an expiratory flow sensor
  • pressure in the patient circuit is measured with two independent pressure sensors

In a home or transport single-limb circuit, a residual pressure in the exhalation valve often sets PEEP.

Safety valves in the same schematic.

  • When the safety valve opens, the patient can breathe atmospheric air spontaneously through the emergency breathing valve.
  • The emergency expiratory valve gives a second channel for expiration when the expiratory valve is blocked.

The "active" exhalation valve.

  • Some ventilators let the patient breathe during a mandatory pressure breath. Hess: in some PC modes "on ventilators with an active exhalation valve, spontaneous breaths may occur during mandatory breaths." This is also how APRV allows spontaneous breathing throughout (item 10).
  • VC+ (VV+): "Excessive pressure caused by breathing or coughing is vented (active exhalation valve), thus maintaining synchrony." The notes: when the patient coughs, airway pressure spikes, the exhalation valve opens and the excess pressure is released.

What goes wrong.

  • Expiratory/PEEP valve problems are among the most common ventilator circuit problems.
  • Of circuit obstructions, expiratory obstruction is "the more serious." It can cause rebreathing, asphyxia or barotrauma, and it comes with low-volume and high-pressure alarms. The most dangerous form is an obstructed exhalation port on a single-limb (NPPV) circuit.
  • Aerosolized ribavirin can crystallize in the circuit. The result is "occlusion and impairment of expiratory valves and sensors with ventilator use" and ETT blockage from drug precipitate. The deck's checklist:
    • check expiratory valve function
    • watch for ↑ peak pressures
    • assess for decreased ventilation
    • inspect the circuit for crystal buildup
  • An expiratory HEPA filter must be heated to prevent blockage from condensation.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 66, 67, 68, 70, 71, 72; RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 59 (notes), 63; RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 18 (and notes); RCP 104 Pharm Ch 13 — Aerosolized Anti-infectives, slide 33 (and notes) · Hess Ch 20 — Introduction (Figure 20-2); airway pressure release ventilation paragraph; Hess Glossary — airway pressure release ventilation; Hess Ch 19 — Basic Concepts (Figure 19-1), Patient Circuits, Ten Fundamental Maxims (#7)

28The Equation of Motion

What it is. The Principles deck: "The Equation of Motion describes the relationship between the forces that generate airflow into the lungs and the mechanical properties of the respiratory system (compliance and resistance). It helps us understand how pressure, flow, and volume interact during breathing — whether spontaneously or on a ventilator."

  • The deck's instruction: "You do NOT need to memorize this equation, just understand what it means."
  • The translation: the pressure needed to deliver a breath must overcome (1) airway resistance and (2) lung/chest wall stiffness.
  • Ten Fundamentals #3: a ventilator assists breathing with either PC or VC "based on the equation of motion for the respiratory system."
  • The model: the respiratory system is a single flow-conducting tube connected to a single elastic compartment. Pressure, volume and flow are variables (functions of time), while resistance and compliance are constants.

The equation, in the three forms Hess uses.

  1. Ch 19 (the basic form): Pvent(t) = E × V(t) + R × V̇(t).
    • Pvent(t) = the transrespiratory pressure generated by the ventilator over time. Transrespiratory pressure = pressure at the airway opening minus pressure on the body surface.
    • E = respiratory system elastance (lungs + chest wall). It is the inverse of compliance.
    • V(t) = volume; R = resistance; V̇(t) = flow.
    • E × V = the elastic load; R × V̇ = the resistive load. Both are in units of pressure.
    • "Breath unloading" means the ventilator supplies some of the work against these loads.
    • All variables are measured relative to their end-expiratory values.
  2. With the patient's muscles added (Hess Figure 19-9 and Glossary): Pvent + Pmus = E·V + R·V̇. Pmus = the pressure generated by the respiratory muscles. Pressure can come from the patient's muscles (Pmus), the ventilator (Pvent), or both.
  3. Full bedside form:
    • Ch 20: Paw + Pmus = (flow × resistance) + (volume/compliance) + applied PEEP + auto-PEEP
    • Ch 51: Paw + Pmus = VT/C + R × V̇ + PEEP + PEEPi (+ inertance)
    • Inertance and tissue resistance have only small effects and are usually ignored.

What it tells you (Hess Ch 20).

  • Spontaneous breathing: Paw = 0. All the pressure comes from the muscles.
  • Full ventilatory support: Pmus = 0. All the pressure comes from the ventilator.
  • Partial support: both contribute.
  • VC fixes flow and volume, so Paw varies with resistance and compliance.
  • PC fixes Paw, so flow and volume vary.
  • Auto-PEEP is one of the loads on the right-hand side, next to applied PEEP. It must be counteracted by Pmus and Paw to affect flow and volume delivery, which is why it makes triggering harder (item 19).

The deck's clinical translation.

  • Asthma (high resistance, normal compliance): the ventilator must generate extra pressure to move air through narrowed airways. The R × V̇ term rises.
  • ARDS (low compliance, normal resistance): the ventilator needs extra pressure to inflate stiff lungs. The elastic (V/C) term rises.
  • "Both can increase peak pressures, but for different reasons." This is the PIP vs Pplat logic of item 24.
  • The notes call the equation "the basis of ventilator graphics." Hess's idealized waveforms were drawn by substituting VT, resistance and compliance into the equation of motion and solving it.

"Stop and Think" (deck and Hess), answered from Boxes 20-4 and 20-5.

  • How can VT rise or fall in PC? Pressure is fixed, so VT falls if compliance falls (E rises), if resistance rises while flow is still present, or if auto-PEEP rises. It rises with more patient effort (Pmus), a higher driving pressure or a longer inspiratory time while flow continues.
  • How can airway pressure rise or fall in VC? Volume and flow are fixed, so Paw rises if compliance falls, resistance rises, flow or VT is raised, or PEEP/auto-PEEP rises.

Worked example (Hess Ch 51 patient data).

  • Plateau pressure is the equation at zero flow: Pplat = VT/C + PEEP.
    • VT 320 mL, compliance 23 mL/cm H₂O, PEEP 26 → 320 ÷ 23 ≈ 14, and 14 + 26 ≈ 40 cm H₂O (the measured Pplat).
  • Add the resistive term to get PIP: R 9 cm H₂O/L/s × flow 0.33 L/s ≈ 3, and 40 + 3 = 43 cm H₂O (the measured PIP).

Hess also uses the equation to:

  • calculate R, C and auto-PEEP by least-squares fitting. This assumes Pmus is zero, so it is less valid if the patient is actively breathing.
  • drive PAV, which recalculates the needed pressure from the equation of motion every 5 ms (item 9).
SourceRCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 62 (notes), 71, 72 (and notes), 73 (and notes), 74 (and notes) · Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims (#2, #3, #5), Figure 19-2, Figure 19-9 (and description), Key Points; Hess Ch 20 — The Equation of Motion; Box 20-4; Box 20-5; Proportional assist ventilation; Hess Ch 51 — Equation of Motion; Alveolar Pressure; Respiratory System Compliance; Airways Resistance; Hess Glossary — equation of motion

29Ventilation Around Surgery — Intraoperative Settings and Postoperative Failure high-yield · beyond the listed asks

From RCP 202 Lssn 8b — PostOp Respiratory Care (Hess Ch 39), added 25 Oct 2026. Postoperative care is not one of the eighteen listed topics, so only the parts that overlap a listed topic are folded in; this is the ventilator half.

Settings in the operating room — the deck poses this as a Stop-and-Think: “An anesthesiologist asks if you think lung-protective ventilation has a role in the operating room.”

  • The deck’s own hint and answer: ventilator settings employed during surgery may affect postoperative outcomes, and the targets are VT 6–8 mL/kg of PBW with low levels of PEEP.
  • The notes add the rest of the package: PEEP to prevent alveolar collapse during exhalation, plateau pressure < 30 cm H₂O, and the lowest FiO₂ that maintains adequate oxygenation.
  • The stated mechanism: minimising volutrauma (overdistension) and atelectrauma (repeated collapse) — the two mechanisms of VILI the slide names.
Why it mattersCompare this with the numbers already in this section and in ARDS. 6–8 mL/kg PBW is the intraoperative figure on a patient without lung injury; the ARDS target is 6 mL/kg and can go to 4–6. The plateau ceiling of 30 cm H₂O does not move. And note what the deck is really claiming: lung-protective ventilation is not just an ICU idea — it has been associated with reduced postoperative pulmonary complications, including respiratory failure and pneumonia.

Why postoperative patients end up on a ventilator

  • Postoperative mechanical ventilation is occasionally required, most commonly due to the residual respiratory depressant effects of anesthesia.
  • Mechanical ventilation places the patient at high risk for pneumonia — and the use of NIV may reduce VAP.
  • Most patients having thoracic or upper abdominal surgery lose pulmonary function after surgery through decreased lung volumes (atelectasis), diaphragmatic dysfunction, and V̇/Q̇ mismatch or gas exchange abnormalities; anesthesia may additionally depress the postoperative respiratory drive, and inhibited cough and impaired airway clearance add infection risk.

The risk numbers worth carrying

  • PPCs occur in approximately 7% of patients with normal preoperative lung function. In patients with risk factors, pulmonary complications have been reported as high as 70%.
  • Intraoperative risk factors: surgical incision site (upper abdominal and thoracic surgeries carry a 20% to 70% pulmonary complication rate), duration of surgery, type of surgery, type of anesthesia, monitoring during anesthesia, intraoperative fluid management, and the ventilator settings — VT and PEEP.
  • Postoperative respiratory failure is rare without preexisting cardiopulmonary or neuromuscular disorders.
Caution — this contradicts a habitThe deck’s Key Points slide states flatly: “Available evidence does not support the use of IPPB or incentive spirometry.” The same deck still lists incentive spirometry, PEP and CPAP under airway-clearance therapy for preventing atelectasis. Both lines are on the instructor’s slides, so know the Key Point as written — it is the kind of sentence that gets keyed verbatim — while recognising IS is still named as a prevention option. Neither IPPB nor incentive spirometry appears anywhere else in this guide, because no other 202 topic asks about them.
SourceHess Ch 39 — Introduction; Intraoperative Risk Factors; Mechanical Ventilation; Key Points · RCP 202 Lssn 8b deck, slides 3, 10, 11, 16, 20, 21 and speaker notes (added 2026-10-25)

Noninvasive Support (NIPPV and HFNC)

1Describe Vapotherm (HFNC): what it is and how it is set up

Exam anchorWord-play first. The instructor's own study guide equates the names: "HFNC = VapoTherm or Optiflow (Fisher & Paykel) (NOT HFNC up to 15L)." So "Vapotherm" on this exam means heated, humidified high-flow nasal cannula, not an ordinary cannula turned up high. The RCP 203 Ch 11 notes (slide 36) call it "HiVNI (aka: VapoTherm)": a patient who had started with a PaCO₂ of 110 and became altered on a 3 L/min cannula was placed on HiVNI "at 45L and 45%", preferred it to BiPAP, and had an ABG of pH 7.39, PaCO₂ 81, PaO₂ 144, HCO₃⁻ 46.1 afterward. No source describes the Vapotherm device itself or spells out HiVNI (Hess names Vapotherm only in a reference title). Hess does name the Fisher & Paykel version: the home My Airvo system (a flow generator, air/O₂ blender and heated humidifier in one unit) used with the Optiflow nasal cannula. Its flow generator delivers up to 63% O₂ at 15 to 45 L/min. That is a home-device figure, not the HFNC maximum (see below: FiO₂ up to 1.0, flows up to 60 L/min).
Why it mattersWhy it exists (Hess Ch 21). A conventional nasal cannula is limited to 6 to 8 L/min, because higher flows are uncomfortable. With heated humidification, higher flows become comfortable, and HFNC delivers flows of up to 60 L/min. The gas source is an air/O₂ blender, a ventilator or a turbine flow generator. It is connected through an active heated humidifier to a nasal cannula, and FiO₂ is adjusted independently of the flow, over a range of 0.21 to 1.0. The prongs come in two types. Large-bore prongs carry up to 60 L/min and should partly occlude the nares, which raises pharyngeal pressure. Small-bore prongs carry up to 40 L/min and give "high-velocity nasal insufflation." Hess says it is not known whether one style gives better outcomes.

What the RCP 203 deck says. An HFNC system includes:

  • a blender to mix air and O₂
  • a flowmeter (high-flow for adults)
  • a heated humidification system
  • an O₂ analyzer
  • a heated delivery system to prevent condensation
  • a custom nasal cannula

Because the gas is warm and humidified, HFNC overcomes the discomfort of standard cannulas at high flows. The deck's flow ranges are adults 1–40+ L/min, children 1–20 L/min and infants 1–8 L/min. It also says a blending system is "a better way" to give high FiO₂s (> 0.60) at high flows (> 40 L/min).

The deck's setup points:

  • Calibrate the analyzer.
  • "ALWAYS connect O₂ 'First'" (older machines).
  • Plug in the humidifier last.
  • Allow the unit to reach the set temperature before connecting the cannula.
  • Check the FiO₂, flow and temperature against the order.
  • Evaluate the response using SpO₂, comfort and relief of hypoxemia.

Other deck mentions:

  • RCP 103 TDP: use pulse oximetry if FiO₂ ≥ 0.44, and high-flow systems can reach FiO₂ ≥ 0.90.
  • RCP 104: "HFNC very common in pediatrics."
Gap · still not covereda description of the Vapotherm device itself, or what HiVNI stands for. The RCP 203 Ch 11 slide 36 notes only name it ("HiVNI (aka: VapoTherm)") in a patient case at "45L and 45%"; the answer above uses the HFNC material, since the instructor's word-play list equates HFNC with VapoTherm or Optiflow.
Sourcethe final study guide (instructor word-play list) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 18, 19 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 36 (and notes) · RCP 103 TDP Program & Communication, slide 19 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 1 — Intro, slide 5 (notes) · Hess Ch 21 — High-Flow Nasal Cannula, HFNC Prongs, Figure 21-2 · Hess Ch 25 — My Airvo / Optiflow paragraph

2How HFNC works, who it is for, and when it is failing (ROX index)

Mechanisms (Hess Respiratory Recap "Physiologic Mechanism of HFNC"):

  • Precise FiO₂. High flow minimizes room-air entrainment, so any FiO₂ from room air to 100% is possible.
  • Flushing of anatomic dead space. CO₂ is washed out of the upper airway during exhalation.
  • It overcomes upper-airway resistance.
  • Increased pharyngeal pressure and lung volume. The flow opposes exhalation and gives a modest CPAP effect. With the mouth closed, pharyngeal pressure rises about 1 cm H₂O for every 10 L/min of flow, but the CPAP effect can be lost if the patient opens their mouth.
  • Heated humidity might improve airway clearance.

Lower dead space and lower upper-airway resistance reduce the work of breathing. A common study finding is a lower respiratory rate. On comfort, 31 °C was more comfortable than 37 °C, and comfort was similar at 30 and 60 L/min.

The decks agree:

  • RCP 105 Airway: HFNC "provides flow-dependent PEEP and allows apneic oxygenation during laryngoscopy," at 40–60 L/min.
  • RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema: HFNC "provides mild positive pressure" and is "often used for patients who cannot tolerate CPAP or BiPAP."

Indications (Hess):

  • The most common indication is acute hypoxemic respiratory failure. HFNC decreases the intubation rate compared with conventional oxygen.
  • The Recap lists the risk of post-extubation hypoxemic respiratory failure as supported. However, the body text calls post-extubation use controversial, with conflicting trials.
  • Evidence is not sufficiently robust for COPD, asthma or cardiogenic pulmonary edema.
  • Benefit in immune compromise is controversial.

When HFNC is failing (Hess): use the ROX index = (SpO₂/FiO₂) ÷ respiratory rate.

  • ROX ≥ 4.88 is associated with success.
  • ROX < 3.85: consider endotracheal intubation.
  • ROX 3.85–4.77: monitor closely, for example in an ICU.

Worked example (numbers chosen for illustration): SpO₂ 92% on FiO₂ 0.50 at a respiratory rate of 20 gives 92 ÷ 0.50 = 184, and 184 ÷ 20 = 9.2, which is ≥ 4.88 and so predicts success. The same SpO₂ and FiO₂ at a rate of 40 gives 184 ÷ 40 = 4.6, which falls in the 3.85–4.77 "watch closely in ICU" band.

Hess adds:

  • Sequential NIV and HFNC (alternating the two) may improve comfort in patients with low NIV tolerance.
  • HFNC has been used for oxygenation during intubation. Sequential use is another option for intubation: NIV during preparation, then HFNC during the procedure itself.
SourceHess Ch 21 — Mechanism of Action, Respiratory Recap "Physiologic Mechanism of HFNC", Clinical Indications, ROX index, Respiratory Recap "Indications for HFNC", Sequential Use of NIV and HFNC · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 55 (notes) · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 35 (+ notes)

3Patient–ventilator asynchrony on NIPPV: causes and fixes

No deck uses the word "asynchrony" for NIV. The deck rules that bear on it are:

  • Rise is the speed at which pressure climbs from EPAP to IPAP. It is "set according to the pts WOB," and you "want to MATCH the pts WOB!"
  • I-Time is set according to the backup rate, "NOT what the pt is actually doing."
  • Total leak is normally 7–25 and should never exceed 60, "otherwise pt is NOT ventilating."
  • A leak reading of "---" or "0" means the mask is too tight.
  • RCP 105 Waveforms shows how a leak creates false triggering. When PEEP is in use, auto-triggering can occur when the leak lets circuit pressure fall below the sensitivity setting. The ventilator reads the pressure drop as a patient effort.

Hess Ch 21 is the main source. Patient–ventilator asynchrony is listed among the usual minor complications of NIV. Leak is the central cause:

  • With a significant leak, the patient may breathe from the leak without making the flow or pressure change that triggers a breath (missed trigger).
  • The leak itself can cause a pressure or flow drop that auto-triggers the ventilator.
  • Leaks also interfere with cycling to exhalation. In pressure support, ventilators have a maximum inspiratory time, typically 3 s.

Other causes Hess names:

  • Rise time. A faster rise may unload COPD patients better but can bring substantial leaks and poor tolerance. Neuromuscular patients often tolerate a slower rise. Set rise time for patient comfort.
  • The helmet interface has been associated with patient–ventilator asynchrony and is listed with "poorer patient–ventilator synchrony."
  • Apnea or periodic breathing on pressure support, which has no backup rate. A backup rate (S/T mode) is recommended, especially at night.

Box 21-3, Strategies to Improve Synchrony with NIV:

Problem Fixes (Hess Box 21-3)
Trigger Adjust trigger sensitivity to balance trigger effort against auto-triggering · Increase PEEP (EPAP) to counterbalance auto-PEEP · Minimize unintentional leak by fitting the interface · Treat the disease (e.g., bronchodilators)
Cycle Minimize unintentional leak · Use time-cycled (pressure control) rather than flow-cycled (pressure support) · Adjust the flow-cycle setting · Reduce the pressure support setting · Treat the disease
Mode Use a backup rate if apnea or periodic breathing occurs
Flow mismatch Use pressure- or volume-targeted ventilation per comfort · Adjust inspiratory pressure (pressure-targeted) or flow and VT (volume control) · Adjust rise time per comfort · Minimize unintentional leak · Reduce respiratory drive (e.g., increase ventilation to treat acidosis)

Hess also notes that ventilator waveform displays of pressure, flow and volume help when titrating settings to improve synchrony.

Gap · still not coveredno deck uses the words asynchrony/synchrony for NIV; the answer above uses the decks' rise, I-time and leak rules and the RCP 105 Waveforms leak auto-trigger slide (an invasive-ventilator slide). The RCP 203 Ch 11 asynchrony slides (77–80) are about invasive ventilation.
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1b — BiPAP Basics, slide 3 · RCP 105 NIV/NPPV (Chang Ch 4), slides 5, 6 · RCP 105 Ventilator Waveform Analysis (Chang Ch 11), slide 54 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 21 — Ventilators for Noninvasive Positive Pressure Ventilation, Table 21-1, Clinical Application, Box 21-3

4NIPPV guideline: who should and should not get it

Hess gives the guideline: CPG 21-2 (Official ERS/ATS guideline, 2017).

NIV should be used for NIV should NOT be used for No recommendation
COPD exacerbation · Acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema · Postoperative respiratory failure · Chest trauma with acute respiratory failure · Prevention of post-extubation respiratory failure in high-risk patients Hypercapnia without acidosis in COPD exacerbation · Prevention of post-extubation failure in non-high-risk patients · Established post-extubation respiratory failure Acute respiratory failure due to asthma · De novo acute respiratory failure (e.g., ARDS)

Patient selection is a two-step process (Hess Box 21-1).

Step 1: does the patient need mechanical ventilation? Look for any of these:

  • Respiratory distress with dyspnea, accessory muscle use or abdominal paradox
  • Respiratory acidosis with pH < 7.35 and PaCO₂ > 45 mm Hg
  • Respiratory rate > 25/min
  • A diagnosis known to respond to NIV (COPD, cardiogenic pulmonary edema)

Step 2: are there any exclusions? The exclusions are:

  • Airway protection problems: respiratory arrest, unstable hemodynamics, high aspiration risk, copious secretions
  • Unable to fit a mask: facial surgery, craniofacial trauma or burns, upper-airway lesion
  • An uncooperative or anxious patient
  • Patient wishes

The decks' version:

  • The patient must be spontaneously breathing (RCP 202).
  • RCP 202 indications: help prevent intubation in end-stage COPD, support chronic ventilatory failure, and restrictive disease, neuromuscular disease and nocturnal hypoventilation.
  • RCP 105 notes list the common indications: acute respiratory failure, severe hypoxemia, severe asthma, COPD exacerbation, acute heart failure, post-extubation support, moderate acidemia, moderate hypercarbia, nocturnal hypoventilation, obesity hypoventilation syndrome and sleep apnea.
  • RCP 105 contraindications: lack of cooperation, cardiac arrest, respiratory arrest, excessive secretions, inability to fit a mask, inability to protect the airway, severe facial burns or trauma.
  • RCP 203 Ch 11 (slides 49, 51) adds the indications avoid reintubation after extubation, sleep apnea (especially central) and breathlessness in the terminally ill, and the contraindications pH < 7.20, upper airway obstruction, uncontrolled arrhythmias, active upper GI bleeding and nausea and vomiting, alongside the arrest, secretion, airway-protection, hemodynamic and facial-trauma exclusions above.
  • Trauma deck (slide 27): HFNC and NIV may suit selected hypoxemic trauma patients who do not need immediate intubation, but NIV is not appropriate if the patient cannot protect the airway or is hemodynamically unstable.

Chronic NIV (Hess Box 21-2) covers restrictive thoracic disorders and COPD. Restrictive-disorder criteria include PaCO₂ ≥ 45 mm Hg, nocturnal SpO₂ ≤ 88% for 5 consecutive minutes, MIP > −60 cm H₂O or FVC < 50% predicted. The COPD criteria are PaCO₂ ≥ 55 mm Hg, or PaCO₂ 50–54 mm Hg plus either nocturnal SpO₂ ≤ 88% for 5 consecutive minutes on O₂ ≥ 2 L/min or hospitalization for recurrent hypercapnic respiratory failure.

SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1b — BiPAP Basics, slide 1 · RCP 202 Lssn 7b — Trauma, slide 27 (and notes) · RCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slide 4 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 49, 51 · Hess Ch 21 — CPG 21-2, Box 21-1, Box 21-2

5Goals of NIPPV, how success is judged, and when to quit

Goals (RCP 105 NIV deck). Expect a "which is NOT a goal" question. The listed goals are:

  • improve oxygenation
  • improve ventilation
  • reverse hypoxemia
  • reverse acute respiratory failure
  • provide relief for respiratory distress
  • reverse respiratory muscle fatigue
  • improve pulmonary mechanics
  • prevent or reverse atelectasis
  • improve lung compliance

The notes add two points:

  • The primary goal of BiPAP is to reverse acute respiratory failure without inserting an artificial airway.
  • NIV will NOT cure the underlying disease; it supports the patient until the disease is treated.
Why it mattersWhy choose NPPV over intubation (deck FAQ):
  • It gives greater flexibility in starting and stopping support.
  • It preserves airway defense, speech and swallowing, so the patient can eat, drink and talk.
  • It avoids the trauma of intubation and the complications of artificial airways, including VAP.
  • It may reduce the need for monitoring.

Hess adds:

  • The primary goal when initiating NIV is patient comfort, not ABG improvement. ABGs usually improve once comfort and respiratory-muscle unloading are achieved.
  • In appropriately selected patients with acute respiratory failure, NIV decreases the need for intubation, decreases nosocomial pneumonia and improves survival.
  • Goals of chronic NIV are to improve symptoms (fatigue, morning headache), decrease PaCO₂ and decrease nocturnal desaturation.

Hess's application steps:

  • Choose the correct interface and avoid one that is too large.
  • Silence alarms and start with low settings.
  • Hold the mask in place while starting, then secure it without a tight fit.
  • Titrate inspiratory pressure to comfort.
  • Titrate FiO₂ to SpO₂ > 90%.
  • To minimize gastric insufflation, avoid inspiratory pressure > 20 cm H₂O.
  • Titrate expiratory pressure to trigger effort and SpO₂.

RCP 203 Ch 11 application points (slide 52): the patient must be alert with intact upper airway function/secretion control; use high semi-Fowler's and usually an oronasal (full) face mask for acute failure; keep IPAP < 20–25 cm H₂O; adjust ventilation (PaCO₂, pH) with IPAP and ΔP and oxygenation with FiO₂ and EPAP. If a contraindication develops or ventilation/oxygenation worsens, consider intubation (the deck gives no time limit).

Weaning from NPPV (RCP 203 Ch 11, slide 95): there are no standard guidelines. Readiness: the underlying condition is mostly resolved, RR < 25, HR < 110, IPAP < 15, EPAP < 10, compensated pH > 7.35, SpO₂ > 90% on < 50%. Wean in the daytime first, extending time off, then give at least one more night of support.

Success. The deck defines success as overall improvement of the ABG: PaO₂ ↑ and PaCO₂ ↓. Hess adds two predictors:

  • A VT > 10 mL/kg predicts NIV failure.
  • A faster fall in PaCO₂ is seen when NIV is succeeding.

Failure: the two sources give different time limits.

  • RCP 105 NIV deck: a patient who "continues to deteriorate 30 minutes after the initiation of NIV" should be intubated and started on mechanical ventilation.
  • Hess: if there is no improvement within 1 to 2 hours, consider intubation.
Exam anchorFor this exam, use the deck's 30 minutes; know that Hess says 1–2 hours.
SourceRCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slides 3 (+ notes), 4 (notes), 6 (notes), 11, 12 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slides 52, 95 · Hess Ch 21 — Acute Care Applications, Chronic Applications, Clinical Application

6Cardiogenic pulmonary edema: what it is and how it presents

Cardiogenic pulmonary edema is a "pressure problem." The most common cause is left-sided heart failure (CHF). The left ventricle fails, so pressure backs up into the lungs; the problem starts in the heart, not the lungs.

Starling forces (deck numbers):

  • Hydrostatic pressure, 10–15 mm Hg, pushes fluid out of the capillaries.
  • Oncotic pressure, 25–30 mm Hg, holds fluid in.
  • When hydrostatic pressure exceeds 25–30 mm Hg, fluid leaks into the interstitium and alveoli: "PUSH > PULL = EDEMA."
  • The fluid is a transudate: thin, low in protein, and caused by pressure rather than inflammation.

Ejection fraction (deck): normal LVEF is 55–70%; < 40% means heart failure; < 35% is severe, with ↑ arrhythmia risk.

Clinical signs (deck):

  • Classic pink, frothy sputum
  • Tachypnea
  • Cheyne-Stokes respirations
  • PND and orthopnea
  • Crackles

ABG progression (deck): early respiratory alkalosis with hypoxemia, then respiratory acidosis as the patient tires.

Hemodynamics (deck): CVP, RAP, PA and PCWP ↑, CO and CI ↓, SVR ↑ and PVR ↑.

BNP (deck):

  • normal < 100 pg/mL
  • 100–300: suggests heart failure may be present
  • > 300: mild heart failure
  • > 600: moderate heart failure
  • > 900: severe heart failure

Chest X-ray (deck):

  • cardiomegaly (> 50% of thoracic width)
  • Kerley A & B lines
  • central fluffy "bat's wing"/"butterfly" opacities
  • transudative pleural effusion

Noncardiogenic edema differs: normal heart size, a patchy infiltrate, and usually no effusion.

Management (deck): the order is ↓ preload → ↓ afterload → ↑ contractility.

  • Preload: nitroglycerin (rapid venodilation), loop diuretics (furosemide), and morphine (rare).
  • Afterload: ACE inhibitors and nitroprusside.
  • Contractility: dobutamine, dopamine, norepinephrine and milrinone.
  • Supportive care adds O₂, non-invasive or invasive ventilation if needed, and arrhythmia control.

Hess adds (Ch 40): pulmonary edema raises lung elastance and airway resistance and causes lung collapse. The large negative intrathoracic pressure swings of labored breathing may increase LV afterload. Hess sums up the cycle: "cardiac failure and respiratory distress begets more cardiac failure."

SourceRCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slides 6, 7, 8, 14, 15, 16, 18, 20, 22, 24, 27, 30, 31, 32, 33 · Hess Ch 40 — Ventilatory Support of the Patient with Heart Failure

7CPAP/NIV for cardiogenic pulmonary edema: why it works and what level

Hess Ch 21: the strongest evidence for mask CPAP is in acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema. The increase in intrathoracic pressure from CPAP:

  • decreases preload
  • decreases afterload
  • improves lung compliance
  • decreases intrapulmonary shunt
  • increases PaO₂

The typical level is CPAP 5 to 10 cm H₂O. Mask CPAP, like NIV, decreases the intubation rate and improves survival in this condition. In CPG 21-2, NIV is a "should be used" for acute cardiogenic pulmonary edema. In contrast, the evidence for HFNC is "not sufficiently robust" here.

The decks:

  • RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema: CPAP 5–10 cmH₂O is the "key therapy" for shunt physiology.
  • RCP 105 NIV FAQ: "What is the first line therapy for Acute Pulmonary Edema? CPAP 8–12 cmH₂O with 100% oxygen."
  • RCP 203 Equipment: for adult CPAP in cardiogenic pulmonary edema, use a CPAP/NPPV device capable of providing 100% O₂, and switch to bi-level (BiPAP) with a backup rate if hypopnea or hypercapnia appears.
  • RCP 203 Ch 11 (slide 16): CPAP needs an awake, cooperative patient with upper airway control; avoid if SBP < 90 mm Hg, MI, GCS ≤ 8 or need for intubation. Start at 10 cm H₂O and 100% O₂, titrate pressure/FiO₂ to SpO₂ > 90%, avoid > 20 cm H₂O (gastric insufflation: opens the UES and LES), and switch to BiPAP with a backup rate for hypopnea or hypercapnia.
  • RCP 105 Operating Modes: names the CHF patient as a "good CPAP patient." In its example, a CHF patient with SpO₂ 84% and a normal PaCO₂ has an oxygenation problem, and the fix is to increase EPAP.

The CPAP level conflicts between sources:

Source CPAP level for cardiogenic pulmonary edema
Hess Ch 21 5 to 10 cm H₂O
Hess Ch 40 (text) 5 to 10 cm H₂O
RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema deck 5–10 cmH₂O
RCP 105 NIV (13-slide) FAQ 8–12 cmH₂O with 100% O₂
RCP 203 Ch 11, slide 16 start at 10 cm H₂O and 100% O₂; avoid > 20
Hess Ch 40 (case-study answer) 5 to 15 cm H₂O, or NIV with inspiratory 8–20 / expiratory 4–10 cm H₂O
Caution · sources differWhat to use: this exam names Hess Ch 21 for this topic, and Hess agrees with the RCP 103 deck, so 5–10 cm H₂O is the best-supported answer. For a single starting value, 10 cm H₂O (the RCP 203 Ch 11 start) sits inside every range above. Also memorize "8–12 cmH₂O with 100% O₂" as the RCP 105 FAQ answer to "first-line therapy for acute pulmonary edema."

Hess adds (Ch 40): positive pressure recruits collapsed lung, unloads the respiratory muscles, and may improve forward cardiac output. In fragile patients, sudden loss of positive pressure in the thorax (e.g., at extubation) can lead to acute edema and rapid failure, so NIV may be applied immediately after extubation.

SourceHess Ch 21 — Continuous Positive Airway Pressure / Acute Care Applications, CPG 21-2, Respiratory Recap "Indications for HFNC" · Hess Ch 40 — Noninvasive Ventilation; Discontinuing Mechanical Ventilation; Case Study · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 35 (+ notes) · RCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slide 12 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 43 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 16 · RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 11, 13 (notes)

8Hemodynamics: Mean Arterial Pressure (MAP) range, formula, and why NIV can drop it

No deck gives a normal MAP range; the number comes from Hess. Hess Table 3-1 gives normal MAP as 65–105 mm Hg. The decks give the minimums:

  • MAP 60 mm Hg is needed to maintain tissue perfusion.
  • MAP 70 mm Hg is needed to maintain cerebral perfusion pressure.

Formulas:

  • RCP 203: MAP = Diastolic + ⅓(Systolic − Diastolic)
  • RCP 104: MAP = [2(DBP) + SBP] ÷ 3, or CO × SVR. That deck stresses that MAP is "not the arithmetic mean"; it is the pressure that drives blood into the tissues over the whole cardiac cycle.

Worked example (deck): for BP 110/70, MAP = 70 + ⅓(110 − 70) = 70 + 13.3 ≈ 83 mm Hg. The other formula gives the same answer: [2(70) + 110] ÷ 3 = 250 ÷ 3 ≈ 83.

Value RCP 105 Hemodynamic deck Hess Table 3-1
Systolic BP 100−140 mm Hg 90–140 mm Hg
Diastolic BP 60−90 mm Hg 60–90 mm Hg
MAP not given (only the 60 and 70 minimums) 65–105 mm Hg
Why it mattersWhy it matters on NIPPV. Positive pressure can lower MAP:
  • RCP 202/105: ↑ PEEP → ↑ intrathoracic pressure → ↓ venous return → ↓ preload → ↓ cardiac output → ↓ BP.
  • RCP 105 NIV: decreased cardiac output is a listed NIV complication.
  • Hess lists hemodynamic compromise among NIV complications, unstable hemodynamics as an NIV exclusion (Box 21-1), and hemodynamics as a parameter to monitor during NIV (Box 21-4).

Hess adds: in septic shock requiring vasopressors, the initial MAP target is usually > 65 mm Hg (Ch 43).

Gap · still not covereda normal MAP range from a deck. The decks give only the 60 mm Hg (tissue perfusion) and 70 mm Hg (cerebral perfusion) minimums and the formulas; the RCP 203 Ch 11 deck gives only MAP ≥ 60–65 mm Hg as the stability limit for recruitment maneuvers (slide 84). The 65–105 mm Hg range is Hess Table 3-1 only.
SourceRCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slide 8 · RCP 105 Monitoring in MV (Chang Ch 9), slide 38 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 3a — Ch 4 Evaluate Results, slide 46 · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 — Vasopressors, Inotropes, slide 8 · RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 6 · RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 9 · RCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slide 4 (notes) · Hess Ch 3 — Table 3-1 · Hess Ch 21 — Box 21-1, Clinical Application, Box 21-4 · Hess Ch 43 — fluid resuscitation paragraph

9Leak ports on NIV (vented masks, single-limb circuits, leak monitoring)

Bilevel ventilators (Hess Ch 21):

  • They are blower devices designed to function in the presence of a leak.
  • They use a single-limb circuit, and the leak port serves as the patient's passive exhalation port.
  • The leak port sits either in the circuit near the patient or in the interface (mask).
  • Masks for bilevel or CPAP machines may incorporate a leak port and an anti-asphyxia port that opens if flow from the ventilator is lost.
  • Masks used with a conventional (critical care) ventilator have a standard elbow without a leak port.
  • CPAP machines for OSA are likewise designed to operate with a built-in leak, "usually found in the mask or between the tubing and mask."
  • Small leaks are common and do not necessarily compromise CPAP or NIV.

Rebreathing is the hazard of a passive port. If the patient's expiratory flow exceeds what the port can vent, the patient exhales into the hose and rebreathes CO₂. Rebreathing is reduced when:

  • the leak port is in the mask rather than the hose
  • O₂ is titrated into the mask rather than the hose
  • expiratory pressure is higher
  • a plateau exhalation valve is used

Higher expiratory pressure needs more flow, and that extra flow flushes the hose. This is why the minimum expiratory pressure on many bilevel ventilators is 3 to 4 cm H₂O. Some ventilators compensate for unintentional leak, and an unintentional leak < 0.5 L/s is usually well tolerated.

The decks:

  • Vented masks "require a vent for exhalation and use only one corrugated tube" to connect to the ventilator (RCP 105 NIV).
  • RCP 203 Equipment describes two kinds of single-limb circuit. One has a built-in expiratory valve (home-care/transport vents). The other has a leakage-type exhaust port, "a small orifice or set of slotted vent holes." Combined with the leak common to all NPPV interfaces, the exhaust port "prevents rebreathing most expired gas!" The circuit without an exhalation valve is the one used in noninvasive ventilators.
  • The most dangerous expiratory obstruction is an obstructed exhalation port on a single-limb (NPPV) circuit, which can cause rebreathing, asphyxia or barotrauma (RCP 203).
  • CPAP setup for sleep apnea (RCP 203): position the exhaust port so vented air is directed away from the patient, make sure nothing blocks the exhaust port, and if a full oronasal mask is used, be sure it has a safety inlet valve.
Exam anchorLeak numbers to memorize (RCP 202, V60):
  • Normal Total Leak is 7–25.
  • Never exceed 60, or the patient is not ventilating.
  • "---" or "0" = mask too tight.
  • Use Total Leak after a Circuit Test with after-market equipment (Vyaire, Fisher & Paykel).
  • Patient Leak applies to factory-compensated Respironics circuits and masks, or when no Circuit Test was done ("Patient Leak = No Good").
SourceHess Ch 21 — Interfaces for CPAP and NIV (Figure 21-9 text), CPAP Equipment for OSA, Ventilators for Noninvasive Positive Pressure Ventilation · RCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slide 13 · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slides 42, 68, 72 · RCP 202 Lssn 1b — BiPAP Basics, slide 3 (+ notes)

10Difference between PEEP, PAP, and pharyngeal pressure

No single source sets these three terms side by side. The contrast below is built from what each source says about each term.

Term What the sources say Key numbers
PEEP (positive end-expiratory pressure) "Increases the end-expiratory or baseline airway pressure." It is the "pressure left in the lungs at the end of exhalation"; without PEEP, airway pressure returns to 0 cmH₂O. It keeps alveoli from collapsing, ↑ FRC and ↑ oxygenation. It is not a stand-alone mode and is applied with another mode. In NIV the equivalent is EPAP ("EPAP = PEEP"; Hess Box 21-3 says "PEEP (expiratory positive airway pressure)"). CPAP is "PEEP applied to a patient who is breathing entirely on their own." Barotrauma risk ↑ with PEEP > 10 cmH₂O (or Paw > 30, or PIP > 50)
PAP (positive airway pressure) The family of mask therapies (RCP 103 Sleep Apnea notes; RCP 105 NIV): CPAP = one continuous pressure; BiPAP (bi-level PAP) = two pressures, IPAP in inspiration and EPAP throughout; APAP = auto-PAP that self-adjusts the pressure. Hess's CPG 21-1 calls them "positive airway pressure (PAP) devices." IPAP is the peak inspiratory pressure and includes EPAP, whereas on a critical-care ventilator pressure support is added on top of PEEP (Hess Figure 21-18). So PS = IPAP − EPAP. APAP range 4 to 20 cm H₂O; home CPAP units 3 to 20 cm H₂O
Pharyngeal pressure The pressure in the pharynx (upper airway), not set on any machine. With HFNC it rises about 1 cm H₂O per 10 L/min of flow with the mouth closed and is lost when the mouth opens. That rise is HFNC's "modest CPAP effect" (the RCP 105 Airway deck calls it "flow-dependent PEEP"). It also matters for gastric inflation: a breath that is too large produces "a pharyngeal pressure that exceeds the esophageal opening pressure," and gas goes to the stomach (Hess Ch 18). PAP vs pharyngeal pressure in OSA: the airway collapses during sleep because of decreased muscle tone and negative pressure during inspiration, and PAP "splints the airway open" (RCP 103 Sleep Apnea notes). Hess: "PAP acts as a pneumatic splint in the upper airway, counteracting the negative inspiratory pressure and preventing airway collapse" (Ch 45). On a bilevel device, the EPAP is set to "maintain airway splinting and patency" (Ch 21). Hess Ch 21: aerophagia on NIV is usually benign because airway pressures are below the esophageal opening pressure; to minimize gastric insufflation, keep inspiratory pressure ≤ 20 cm H₂O. RCP 203 Ch 11 (slide 52): keep IPAP < 20–25 cm H₂O, the "esophageal opening pressure (UES and LES)"

Worked example: BiPAP IPAP 15 / EPAP 5 gives PS 10, with a "PEEP" of 5. On a critical-care ventilator the same breath would be set as PS 10 above PEEP 5, giving a peak of 15.

PAP can also mean pulmonary artery pressure, and that is where PEEP and PAP meet in the decks. In the hemodynamics decks, PAP = pulmonary artery pressure, measured with the PA-catheter balloon deflated.

  • Deck normal: 15–25 / 6–12 mm Hg.
  • Pulmonary hypertension: PAP systolic > 35 mm Hg, or mean PAP > 25 mm Hg at rest (> 30 on exertion).
  • Hess Table 3-1 gives slightly different normals: PASP 15–30, PADP 4–12, mean PAP 9–16 mm Hg. Use the deck's 15–25 / 6–12 on this exam.
  • PEEP → ↑ PAP. "Use of PPV with positive end-expiratory pressure (PEEP) can lead to ↑ pulmonary artery pressure." The reason: PEEP expands the alveoli, the expanded alveoli compress the nearby pulmonary capillaries, and PVR rises. The Hemodynamic deck likewise lists mechanical ventilation and PEEP among the causes of increased PAP.
  • PPV without PEEP tends to ↓ PAP (RCP 105 Principles of MV, slide 36).
  • The RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema deck uses PAP the same way ("the right ventricle has to push against that pressure, so PAP increases").

Read the question stem for context: positive airway pressure (a mask therapy) vs pulmonary artery pressure (a PA-catheter measurement).

Gap · still not coveredno source sets all three terms side by side in one place (the contrast above is assembled from the decks' PEEP→PAP link and Hess's PAP-as-pneumatic-splint description), and Hess Ch 18/21 only name the esophageal opening pressure; the one number (IPAP < 20–25 cm H₂O, RCP 203 Ch 11 slide 52) is an RCP 203 deck limit, not a measured pharyngeal pressure.
SourceRCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slides 9 (+ notes), 11 (notes), 12, 13 (notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 1b — BiPAP Basics, slide 2 · RCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slides 4, 6 · RCP 103 Sleep Apnea, slide 35 (notes) · RCP 105 Airway Management (Hess Ch 17), slide 55 (notes) · RCP 105 Hemodynamic Monitoring (Chang Ch 10), slide 19 · RCP 105 Principles of MV, slides 36, 37 (+ notes) · RCP 103 Pulmonary Edema & PVD, slide 21 (notes) · RCP 202 Lssn 4 — Advanced Modes of MV, slide 6 · Hess Ch 21 — Mechanism of Action, CPAP Equipment for OSA, bilevel for OSA, Improving Patient Adherence (APAP), CPG 21-1, Figure 21-18, Box 21-3, Clinical Application · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 52 · Hess Ch 45 — Positive Airway Pressure Therapy · Hess Ch 3 — Table 3-1 · Hess Ch 18 — gastric inflation paragraph

11BiPAP settings, adjustment rules and alarms high-yield · beyond the listed asks

The two pressures:

  • IPAP is applied only in inspiration. Think ventilation (VT): raising IPAP without changing EPAP raises VT (more PS).
  • EPAP is applied continuously in inspiration and exhalation. It is "similar to CPAP or PEEP". Think oxygenation: it improves FRC and shunting.

The rules:

  • PS = IPAP − EPAP, and it is "Never < 4." In the deck example, IPAP 15 / EPAP 5 gives PS 10.
  • You can always raise IPAP alone, but never raise EPAP without raising IPAP by the same amount. Otherwise PS shrinks.
  • Both pressures are increased by increments of 2.
  • S/T mode: the backup rate is set two to five breaths below the patient's spontaneous RR.
  • High CO₂ → increase IPAP or PS, which raises VT and then minute ventilation.
  • Low CO₂ → decrease PS or IPAP.
  • Oxygenation → change the FiO₂ or the EPAP.
  • NBRC examples (RCP 203 Ch 11, slide 54), starting at 10/5: oxygenation only → raise both equally (12/7), ΔP unchanged; ventilation only → raise IPAP only (15/5); both → raise both, IPAP more (15/7).
  • Raising EPAP increases FRC.

Starting values (the decks differ):

  • RCP 105 NIV: IPAP 8–12, EPAP 4–6, backup rate 10–12.
  • RCP 105 Operating Modes slide: IPAP 8 / EPAP 4, with notes saying "most clinicians start around" 8–10 / 4–5.

Rise and Ramp:

  • A lower Rise number means faster pressurization.
  • Ramp can be set Off or 5–45 minutes. Hess adds: the role of ramp in NIV is unclear, and in acute care it "may be undesirable because it delays application of the therapeutic pressure setting."

V60 alarms (RCP 202):

  • Lo Rate: set one higher than the set RR (12 → 13), or the V60 shows "Alarm disabled…"
  • HIP: 5 to 10 above IPAP
  • LIP: 2 to 3 below IPAP
  • LIP T: similar to apnea time
  • Hi/Lo VT and Hi RR: set from actual patient measurements
  • Lo V̇E: normally 4 or 5 L/min

The RCP 105 deck's alarm settings differ:

  • high/low pressure ±5 in S/T mode
  • VT alarms 150 mL above and below the observed or desired VT (based on IBW)
  • low minute ventilation adjusted to 10–20% below the patient's baseline
SourceRCP 202 Lssn 1b — BiPAP Basics, slides 1, 2, 3, 4 · RCP 203 Lssn 7 — Ch 11 Support Ventilation & Oxygenation, slide 54 · RCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slides 7, 8, 12, 13 · RCP 105 Operating Modes of MV (Chang Ch 4), slide 13 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 21 — Ventilators for NIPPV (ramp)

12NIV interfaces: advantages and disadvantages high-yield · beyond the listed asks

The RCP 105 deck lists four primary interfaces:

  • Nasal mask: less aspiration risk, easier secretion clearance and speech, less claustrophobia and dead space. The biggest complication is leakage in mouth-breathers. Choose the smallest mask that does not obstruct the nostrils.
  • Oronasal mask: a better seal, but aspiration, regurgitation, and asphyxiation if the machine, electricity or gas fails (most now have safety valves).
  • Nasal pillows: the smallest and most comfortable, but "would NOT be recommended for BiPAP."
  • Full-face mask: the largest and least comfortable. It is indicated when there is significant mouth-breathing, as in an acute COPD exacerbation.

RCP 203 adds that for hypercapnic respiratory failure, nasal appliances are preferred because of their lesser dead space, and that "the best interface is the one that the patient prefers and uses!"

Hess adds (Table 21-1 and text):

  • The most commonly used interfaces are oronasal and nasal masks.
  • The oronasal mask has increased dead space and aspiration risk.
  • The total face mask cannot deliver aerosolized medications.
  • The helmet has rebreathing, poorer synchrony and asphyxia risk.
  • Headgear: you should be able to pass one or two fingers under the headgear.
  • Pressure sores on the bridge of the nose are common. Reduce headgear tension, try a different style (hybrid or total face), or apply a hydrocolloid dressing.
  • For mouth leak with a nasal mask, try a chin strap.
  • For acute respiratory failure, the oronasal or total face mask is usually better tolerated than a nasal interface.
SourceRCP 105 NIV (13-slide), slide 10 (notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 4 — Ch 6 Equipment, slide 69 · Hess Ch 21 — Interfaces for CPAP and NIV, Table 21-1
16

Pneumonia (PNA)

10 items · Signs, symptoms and diagnosis · atypical PNA · Legionella · categories · chlorhexidine · + postoperative PNA and atelectasis (Lssn 8b)

1Signs and symptoms of pneumonia2How pneumonia is diagnosed3Categories of pneumonia4Atypical pneumonia: how it differs from typical pneumonia5Legionella infections6Chlorhexidine7Ventilator-associated pneumonia: incidence, diagnosis, organisms, treatment8Aspiration pneumonia vs aspiration pneumonitis9CAP severity scoring (CURB-65, PSI) and prevention10Postoperative Pneumonia — and the Atelectasis It Gets Confused With

1Signs and symptoms of pneumonia

Definitions.

  • RCP 103 deck: pneumonia is "an infectious inflammatory process that primarily affects the gas exchange area of the lungs causing capillary fluid to pour into the alveoli," leading to alveolar inflammation, consolidation and atelectasis. It spreads mainly by aerosolized droplets from coughing, sneezing or talking. It often mimics a cold or the flu. Causes include bacteria, viruses, fungi or aspiration.
  • Hess: pneumonia is "inflammation and consolidation of lung tissue, most frequently due to infection." Pathogens reach the lower airway by aspiration of gastric, oropharyngeal or nasopharyngeal secretions, by inhalation of droplets or aerosols, by hematogenous spread, or by direct spread from the pleural space.

Bedside picture (RCP 103 "Patient Assessment"):

Finding What the deck says
Appearance Diaphoretic, cyanotic
Breathing Tachypnea: hypoxemia stimulates the peripheral chemoreceptors; poor lung compliance gives shallow, rapid breathing (the notes add J-receptors, pain and anxiety)
Breath sounds Crackles, bronchial breath sounds, whispered pectoriloquy
Percussion Dull over consolidation
Cough and sputum Starts dry and hacking, then becomes productive (yellow/green); may be rust-colored or blood-streaked (hemoptysis)
Vital signs Fever, bacterial > 101 °F, viral < 101 °F; ↑ HR, RR, BP

Hess Box 36-1, Clinical Signs and Symptoms of Pneumonia:

  • Constitutional: fever, chills, night sweats, fatigue and/or myalgias
  • Respiratory: productive cough, shortness of breath and/or pleuritic chest pain
  • Laboratory: leukocytosis, ↑ % bands (immature neutrophils), or leukopenia
  • Radiology: infiltrate(s) on chest radiograph and/or CT

Hess's text adds sputum that is "purulent, green, or rust-colored," plus tachycardia, ↑ work of breathing and hypoxemia. Typical (lobar) pneumonia shows consolidation signs: bronchial breath sounds, tactile fremitus, whispered pectoriloquy and inspiratory crackles.

SourceRCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slides 2 (+ notes), 3, 26 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 36 — Definition and Classification of Pneumonia, Box 36-1, Etiology

2How pneumonia is diagnosed

Hess: "Pneumonia is a clinical diagnosis... no single test can diagnose pneumonia." The diagnosis rests on signs and symptoms, imaging and microbiology. A causative pathogen is isolated in only 40% of inpatient and outpatient cases.

The RCP 103 deck's diagnostic tests:

  • Chest X-ray: ↑ density from consolidation or atelectasis, air bronchograms, and possibly an abscess (air-fluid level) or pleural effusion/empyema.
  • ABG: early respiratory alkalosis with hypoxemia (hyperventilation); in severe disease, respiratory acidosis with hypoxemia (acute ventilatory failure).
  • PFT: a restrictive pattern, with ↓ volumes and capacities.
  • CBC: ↑ WBC in bacterial pneumonia and ↓ WBC in viral pneumonia (the notes say viral WBC "may be normal or decreased").
  • Sputum: culture and sensitivity.

Hess's toolkit (Ch 36):

Test Key points and numbers
Chest imaging Done routinely; an infiltrate is a necessary part of the diagnosis. It also excludes other causes (e.g., pneumothorax). Some infiltrates show only on CT. S. pneumoniae → lobar consolidation with air bronchograms; H. influenzae → bronchopneumonia; M. pneumoniae → diffuse interstitial opacities.
Sputum Gram stain and culture "The cornerstone" of microbiologic diagnosis. Positive = ≥ 10⁶ CFU/mL. Recent antibiotics give false negatives; oropharyngeal contamination gives false positives.
Urine antigens Used when sputum is inadequate. They are unaffected by antibiotics and give results in minutes. Pneumococcal: sensitivity 50%–80%, specificity ≥ 90%, detectable up to 3 months. Legionella: sensitivity 70%–90%, specificity nearly 99%, but it tests only serogroup 1.
Blood cultures Not recommended for outpatient CAP. Bacteremia occurs in 5%–16% of hospitalized patients. Draw from two sites before antibiotics. Sensitivity is low; specificity is high.
PCR / rapid flu PCR from a nasopharyngeal swab detects viruses (influenza, RSV, hMPV, adenovirus). The rapid influenza test has only 50%–60% sensitivity (Hess's influenza section gives 40%–50% in adults).
Serology Paired acute and convalescent titers; a 2- to 3-fold rise confirms infection. Of limited value because results arrive late.
Thoracentesis Rules out empyema. Send pH, protein, LDH, glucose, Gram stain and culture.
Bronchoscopy / BAL Not standard for CAP. Used in severe pneumonia requiring intubation, suspected unusual organisms, immunocompromised patients, or those unable to produce sputum. BAL positive at ≥ 10⁴ CFU/mL.
Biomarkers CRP is neither sensitive (60%) nor specific (80%) and is not routine. Procalcitonin rises in bacterial but not viral infection; its main use is guiding when to stop antibiotics.
SourceRCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slide 27 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 36 — Diagnostic Workup (Radiology, Microbiology, Diagnostic Procedures, Biomarkers)

3Categories of pneumonia

The two decks' approaches. RCP 103 gives two main classification approaches:

  • by pathogen (bacterial, viral, fungal)
  • by clinical setting (CAP, HAP, VAP)

Setting is used because "identifying the exact organism is often difficult." Hess lists five ways to classify:

  • site of acquisition (community vs hospital)
  • pathogen (viral vs bacterial)
  • clinical features (typical vs atypical)
  • type of insult (infectious vs noninfectious)
  • immune status (immunocompetent vs immunocompromised)

By setting:

Category Definition Usual organisms
CAP (community-acquired) Deck: "acquired from normal social contact." Hess: occurs in patients who lack risk factors for nosocomial pneumonia S. pneumoniae is the most common bacterium. The deck also lists Staph, H. influenzae, Legionella, Klebsiella (lobar), Moraxella, Pseudomonas. Hess: pathogens are detected in only ~38% of CAP cases, and most of those are viruses
HAP (hospital-acquired, nosocomial) ≥ 48 hours after admission and not present (incubating) at admission. Hess adds non-mechanically ventilated patients Deck: Pseudomonas aeruginosa, MSSA, MRSA
VAP (ventilator-associated) A subtype of nosocomial pneumonia in intubated, ventilated patients. Timing differs by source (see the conflict below) Deck: P. aeruginosa, Enterobacter, Klebsiella, Acinetobacter, S. maltophilia, S. aureus
HCAP (healthcare-associated) Deck: hospitalized within 90 days, nursing-home/long-term care resident, or IV antibiotics, chemotherapy or wound care within 30 days. Hess: the term was "retired in 2016"; it is no longer a separate entity from HAP Deck notes: these patients acquire pneumonia outside the hospital but have risk factors for multidrug-resistant organisms from prior health-care exposure

Other categories in the decks:

  • Typical vs atypical (item 4).
  • Aspiration pneumonia vs aspiration pneumonitis (item 8).
  • Chronic pneumonia: a granulomatous localized lesion; TB is the "most important chronic pneumonia pathogen," along with fungi such as Histoplasma and Coccidioides.
  • Pneumonia in the immunocompromised host: CMV; Pneumocystis jirovecii (PCP), a major AIDS infection; MAC; Aspergillus; Candida.
  • Anatomic patterns: bronchopneumonia (bronchi plus nearby alveoli, patchy); lobar (one or more lobes, often the end result of severe bronchopneumonia); interstitial (diffuse, the tissue between alveoli).
  • Lay terms: "double pneumonia" = both lungs; "walking pneumonia" = a mild case, often Mycoplasma.
Caution · sources differConflict on the VAP time definition:
Source VAP timing
RCP 103 deck "more than 48 to 72 hours after endotracheal intubation"
RCP 105 Procedures slide "within 48 hours" after intubation
RCP 105 Procedures notes ("update") "48 hours or more" after intubation
RCP 203 Ch 9 deck (slide 59) patients receiving mechanical ventilation "for more than 48 hours"
Hess text "after 48 hours of endotracheal intubation"
Hess Table 36-1 "after 2–4 days"

Use "≥ 48 hours after intubation." The "within 48 hours" wording on the RCP 105 slide is contradicted by its own updated notes.

SourceRCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slides 6, 7, 8 (notes), 9, 10, 14, 16, 17 (+ notes), 18, 25, 39, 40 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slide 43 (+ notes) · RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slide 59 · Hess Ch 36 — Definition and Classification of Pneumonia, Table 36-1, Etiology, Nosocomial Pneumonia, Hospital-Acquired Pneumonia, Ventilator-Associated Pneumonia

4Atypical pneumonia: how it differs from typical pneumonia

Why it mattersWhy "atypical"? (RCP 103):
  • The organism escapes identification by standard bacteriologic tests.
  • There is only a moderate amount of sputum.
  • There is no alveolar consolidation and no alveolar exudate.
  • The white cell count is only moderately elevated.

The onset is subacute, with pulmonary plus extrapulmonary findings such as headache, fatigue or diarrhea. The cough "tends to come in violent attacks, producing only a moderate amount of white mucus."

Feature Typical (Hess Ch 36) Atypical (Hess Ch 36)
Onset and symptoms Abrupt shaking chills (rigors), high fever, pleuritic chest pain, cough with rusty or purulent sputum Flu-like: dry cough, rhinorrhea, myalgias, malaise, moderate fever; rigors generally absent
Who (not specified) More common in young adults / younger patients
WBC Usually elevated Usually normal (the RCP 103 deck says "moderate elevation")
Chest X-ray Lobar or segmental consolidation with air bronchograms Diffuse interstitial or peribronchial opacities
Exam Consolidation signs (bronchial breath sounds, fremitus, whispered pectoriloquy, crackles) No consolidation (deck)
Organisms S. pneumoniae (most common), H. influenzae, S. aureus, Klebsiella, M. catarrhalis Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydia psittaci, Chlamydophila pneumoniae, Coxiella burnetii, Legionella pneumophila, respiratory viruses
Lab detection Seen on Gram stain and culture Not detectable on sputum Gram stain and do not grow on standard media

Organism details:

  • Mycoplasma pneumoniae is the most common cause of atypical pneumonia. It causes "walking pneumonia" (patients stay ambulatory) and is common in children and young adults in crowded settings (schools, dorms, boot camps). It produces a dry, hacking cough in violent attacks. Complications include hemolytic anemia from cold hemagglutinins.
    • Hess Ch 56 adds: it is the smallest free-living organism and lacks a cell wall, so penicillin and other β-lactams don't work. Cold hemagglutinins are positive in 50%–70% after 10 days.
  • Coxiella burnetii causes Q fever, from livestock dust or aerosols.
  • Chlamydia psittaci comes from bird droppings and causes psittacosis ("parrot fever").
  • Viruses account for about 50% of all pneumonias (RCP 103). Influenza A & B are the most common viral causes in adults; RSV, parainfluenza and human metapneumovirus are the main ones in children.

Treatment (RCP 104).

  • Macrolides (e.g., azithromycin) are the "GO-TO for atypical pneumonia": "Atypical pneumonia? → Think macrolide." The deck says this covers Mycoplasma, Chlamydia and Legionella.
  • Tetracyclines (doxycycline) are the "atypical coverage kings."
  • Hess adds: fluoroquinolones are also used. Doxycycline is the drug of choice for C. psittaci.
SourceRCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slides 11, 12, 13, 14, 15 (+ notes) · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 14 — Antimicrobials, slides 26 (notes), 29 (+ notes) · Hess Ch 36 — Etiology; Atypical Organisms; Age-Specific Angle · Hess Ch 56 — Mycoplasma pneumoniae; Chlamydophila pneumoniae

5Legionella infections

Hess Ch 36:

  • Most Legionella infections are caused by L. pneumophila, an atypical organism that colonizes water systems: showers, fountains, grocery-store mist machines.
  • Aerosolized bacteria cause outbreaks of Legionnaires disease.
  • Symptoms include cough, fever, dyspnea, confusion, abdominal pain and diarrhea.
  • Of the atypical organisms, Legionella is the most likely to lead to ARDS.
  • Complications include pericarditis, myocarditis, encephalomyelitis, Guillain-Barré syndrome, rhabdomyolysis, acute renal failure, paralytic ileus and pancreatitis.
  • First-line treatment is a fluoroquinolone or a macrolide.
  • Hess Table 36-2 lists summer/fall as the time-of-year risk factor for Legionella.

Diagnosis. Legionella does not grow on standard sputum culture and needs specialized testing. The Legionella urinary antigen has 70%–90% sensitivity and nearly 99% specificity, but it detects only serogroup 1. Up to 20% of Legionella pneumonias are other serogroups and test falsely negative. The antigen can stay detectable for weeks. Current guidelines recommend urine antigen testing to improve the diagnostic yield in CAP. A positive Legionella test is one of the criteria for possible VAP (PVAP) in the ventilator-associated event definitions.

Hess adds (Ch 56):

  • L. pneumophila is a flagellated, aerobic gram-negative bacillus that produces β-lactamase.
  • It is transmitted by inhaling mist droplets.
  • It was named after a 1976 American Legion convention outbreak in Philadelphia.
  • It is usually not harmful except in the elderly or immunocompromised.
  • Hospital-acquired Legionella pneumonia has a mortality as high as 28%.
  • Therapy is quinolones and newer macrolides (azithromycin, clarithromycin, roxithromycin).

Hess adds (Ch 31, Ch 36), infection control:

  • Legionella has been isolated from hospital water supplies and has caused epidemic VAP.
  • It is common practice to fill ventilator humidifiers with sterile water to keep out heat-resistant organisms such as Legionella. Hess notes that the efficacy of this practice has not been validated, but it "appears prudent given the demonstrated danger of Legionella presence in hospital tap water."
  • Hess Ch 31 lists legionellosis as an airborne-transmitted disease.

Where the decks put it:

  • RCP 104 lists Legionella as an atypical organism treated with macrolides.
  • RCP 103 lists Legionella pneumophila on its "Community-Acquired Typical Pneumonia" slide, "known for causing Legionnaires' disease."

Use "atypical": Hess Ch 36, Hess Ch 56 and the RCP 104 deck all say so. Both views agree it is a CAP organism.

SourceHess Ch 36 — Atypical Organisms / Legionella Species, Table 36-2, Microbiology (urine antigen), Pathogenesis of VAP, Ventilator-Associated Events · Hess Ch 56 — Legionella pneumophila; Respiratory Tract Culture · Hess Ch 31 — Table (Transmission Routes), Ventilator-Related Issues · RCP 104 Pharm Ch 14 — Antimicrobials, slide 29 (notes) · RCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slide 10 (+ notes)

6Chlorhexidine

What chlorhexidine is in these sources: an antiseptic.

  • As a skin antiseptic: before arterial-line insertion, "Scrub the insertion site with chlorhexidine, and cover area with sterile drape" (RCP 203). Before thoracentesis, the site is cleaned "with an appropriate antiseptic such as chlorhexidine or Betadine" (Hess Ch 9).
  • As oral care in the VAP bundle: this is where the sources disagree.
Source What it says about oral chlorhexidine
RCP 203 Ch 9 deck, CDC "VAP bundle" (slide 60) "Providing daily oral care with chlorhexidine (aka: mouthwash)" listed as a key component of the bundle
Hess Ch 36, ventilator-bundle text "reducing aspiration of contaminated secretions (e.g., oral care with chlorhexidine, endotracheal cuff pressure 20–30 cm H₂O, continuous aspiration of subglottic secretions, elevation of the head of the bed up to 45 degrees...)"
Hess Ch 36, Box 36-7 Ventilator-Care Bundle "Oral care with chlorhexidine (no longer recommended)"
Hess Ch 36, following text "daily oral care with chlorhexidine and stress ulcer prophylaxis may prove harmful in some patients"
RCP 105 Procedures deck (notes) Lists "use of oral chlorhexidine" among studied VAP-prevention strategies. The deck's "Current Practice" bundle says only "Oral care with antiseptic solution (follow your hospital's protocol)"
Caution · sources differWhat to use: the instructor's RCP 203 Ch 9 deck is the exam answer: the CDC-recommended VAP bundle includes daily oral care with chlorhexidine (mouthwash). Know the textbook contrast too: Hess's current bundle (Box 36-7) marks it "no longer recommended," and Hess's text adds that daily oral chlorhexidine "may prove harmful in some patients." The RCP 105 deck's current bundle avoids naming chlorhexidine.

The RCP 203 Ch 9 deck's CDC VAP bundle (slides 59–60). VAP is infection in patients receiving mechanical ventilation for more than 48 hours. Key components:

  • head of bed 30° to 45° (unless contraindicated)
  • daily "sedation vacations" and SBTs
  • peptic ulcer disease prophylaxis (sucralfate rather than H₂ antagonists)
  • daily oral care with chlorhexidine
  • subglottic ETTs that continuously remove secretions (listed under airway management techniques)

Not bundle components: the deck calls these "other strategies potentially helpful" in preventing VAP (general infection control, airway management, equipment maintenance, oral care): avoid intubation whenever possible; if needed use the oral route and avoid nasal intubation (sinus infections); cuff pressure 20–30 cm H₂O, avoid the minimal leak technique; aspirate subglottic secretions; flush suction catheters with sterile water or saline.

Hess adds, the rest of the Box 36-7 bundle (note: Hess calls peptic ulcer prophylaxis and continuous aspiration of subglottic secretions controversial, while the deck lists PUD prophylaxis and subglottic ETTs as key bundle components):

  • head of bed 30–45°
  • daily awakening trial and assessment of readiness for extubation
  • DVT prophylaxis
  • peptic ulcer prophylaxis (controversial)
  • continuous aspiration of subglottic secretions (controversial)

Head-of-bed elevation, sedation interruption, SBTs and thromboembolism prophylaxis appear beneficial.

The RCP 105 deck's "four things" to remember:

  • cuff pressure 20–30 cm H₂O
  • HOB 30–45°
  • hand hygiene
  • extubate as early as safely possible
Gap · still not covereda chlorhexidine concentration or oral-care technique. The RCP 203 Ch 9 deck gives the frequency only (daily); Hess Ch 36 (Box 36-7) and Ch 31 name the agent only.
SourceRCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9 Airway Care / Artificial Airways, slides 59, 60 · Hess Ch 36 — Prevention of VAP / Ventilator-Care Bundles, Box 36-7 · Hess Ch 9 — thoracentesis procedure · RCP 203 Lssn 2b — Ch 3 Procedures, slide 24 · RCP 105 Procedures Related to MV (Chang Ch 14/15), slides 46 (+ notes), 47 (notes)

7Ventilator-associated pneumonia: incidence, diagnosis, organisms, treatment high-yield · beyond the listed asks

Incidence and outcome:

  • RCP 105 deck: VAP affects 10–25% of ventilated patients and causes 25% of all ICU infections. Mortality is 33–50%. Risk is highest right after intubation: the incidence is 3% for the first 5 days, then 2% per day for the next 5 days, then 1% per day thereafter. Trauma, neurosurgical and burn units have higher rates.
  • Hess: VAP occurs in 15% to 26% of intubated patients, with 7 to 28 episodes per 1000 ventilator-days. Attributable mortality "might not be as high as was assumed in the past."
  • The ETT is the risk factor, "NOT the ventilator itself" (deck). It bypasses upper-airway defenses, and microaspiration of secretions pooled above the cuff is "the primary mechanism". Hess agrees: "The number one risk factor for nosocomial pneumonia is an endotracheal tube."
  • The deck's clinical pearl names a different "single greatest" factor: "The single greatest risk factor for VAP is prolonged mechanical ventilation."
