13Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (CPR) ("EVERYTHING!")

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Sodium bicarbonate dose in cardiac arrest1 mmol/kg IV bolus (Hess Ch 18, Table 18-6)0.5–1 mEq/kg in cardiac arrest; keep pH > 7.15 (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21, slide 51)Deck (instructor): 0.5–1 mEq/kg. Both agree it is not first-line and requires adequate ventilation.
Chest compression rate100 to 120 compressions/min (Hess Ch 18: Chain of Survival, Box 18-2, child section, Algorithm 18-5)"at least 100 compressions per minute" (Hess Ch 18, Circulation paragraphs; Algorithm 18-3 pediatric description "Rate at least 100/min")Use 100–120/min (the 2015 wording used everywhere else in the chapter). No deck gives a rate.
Rate of survival decline without defibrillation7% to 10% "with each passing moment" of untreated VF (Hess Ch 18, Chain of Survival)7–10% for every minute from symptom onset to defibrillation; only 3–4% per minute with CPR (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21, slide 46)Deck (instructor): 7–10% per MINUTE without CPR, 3–4% per minute with CPR.
Targeted temperature management range32 °C to 36 °C for at least 24 hours (Hess Ch 18, Post–Cardiac Arrest Care); TTM "between 32°C and 36°C" (RCP 105 Critical Issues, slide 9 notes)"Therapeutic hypothermia (32 to 34ºC)" (RCP 105 Critical Issues, slide 9 notes, older paragraph)Use 32–36 °C (Hess and the deck's "current practice" bullet).
Duration of rescue breaths"2 breaths of 1 second each" (Hess Ch 18, Breathing)"Give two slow breaths" (Box 18-2); each breath "takes less than 1 second" (Hess Ch 18, Airway Management)Use 1 second per breath, just enough for visible chest rise; avoid fast or forceful breaths (gastric inflation).
Pulse check site for a childCarotid artery "for an adult or child" (Hess Ch 18, Circulation)"carotid or femoral arteries" (Hess Ch 18, Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation for a Child)For a child answer carotid OR femoral (the child-specific paragraph); adult = carotid; infant = brachial.
Early tracheal intubation during CPRKey Point: "In airway management, there is no substitute for tracheal intubation" (Hess Ch 18)"early intubation during CPR is usually not necessary"; intubation in the first 15 min of adult IHCA associated with decreased survival (Hess Ch 18, Airway Management)Not a numeric conflict. During CPR: compressions plus effective BVM first; intubate without interrupting compressions more than needed to see the cords.
O₂ flow to the manual resuscitator (BVM)10 to 12 L/min (Hess Ch 18, Breathing)O₂ flowmeter set to the maximum level allowed by the manufacturer; never delay ventilation to obtain O₂ (RCP 203 Lssn 4, slide 59)Deck (instructor) wording for how to set the flowmeter; know Hess 10–12 L/min if a number is asked.
MOANS letters (difficult mask ventilation)M = Mask seal only (Mallampati is a separate tool predicting intubation); A = "Aged", no number; S = Stiff lungs (RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9, slide 11 notes)M = Mask seal / Male gender / Mallampati III or IV; A = Age > 55 years; S = Stiff lung / Snoring (Hess Ch 18 — Box 18-3)Use the deck letters (mask seal, obesity/obstruction, aged, no teeth, stiff lungs); Hess > 55 and snoring are textbook extras

14Mechanical Ventilation (29 questions on the exam)

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
MMV: how mandatory breaths are addedVentilator 'automatically increases the mandatory breath frequency' (volume-cycled) when spontaneous VE falls below the preset minimum; 'form of SIMV ... adjusts the frequency automatically' (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 9 + notes; RCP 105 Weaning slide 24 notes)MMV is IMV variation 3: mandatory breaths delivered ONLY when measured VE drops below a preset threshold; with IMV the mandatory frequency can never exceed the set rate (set frequency = maximum); Hess gives Hamilton ASV as an example of this MMV-type IMV, whereas the deck contrasts ASV vs MMV (Hess Ch 19 — Ten Fundamental Maxims #7); the RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 85 slide text carries the same IMV framing ('delivered only when the measured minute ventilation drops below a preset threshold'; 'mandatory breath frequency can never be higher than the set rate'), and its notes name it MMVDeck (RCP 202, instructor's): low VE -> ventilator increases the mandatory rate to restore the preset minimum VE. Both framings agree that low VE brings more mandatory breaths; they differ only on whether the set rate is a ceiling (202 slide doesn't mention one). Know the 'set rate is a maximum' framing (Hess and Principles slide 85) for a question quoting it.
CMV: what the abbreviation means'Controlled Mandatory Ventilation' = control mode: time-triggered, patient trigger and spontaneous breathing NOT possible; 'A/C allows patient-triggered breaths. CMV does not.' (RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 14; RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 4 lists CMV and AC separately); note the same Chang slide also labels it 'Continuous mandatory ventilation (CMV), control mode''Continuous mandatory ventilation, commonly known as Assist/Control' — patient triggers allowed, every breath mandatory, set rate is a minimum (Hess Ch 19 maxim #7; Hess Ch 20 Ventilator Modes; also RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 84 'CMV (aka: Assist/Control)')Don't decide by the spelled-out name (Chang slide 14 says 'Continuous mandatory ventilation (CMV), control mode'). Decide by whether the stem lets the patient trigger: patient cannot trigger / sedated-paralyzed -> control mode (Chang/202 slide 4); patient triggers and every breath gets full support -> A/C (Hess; Principles slide 84).
PRVC: volume control or pressure control?'PRVC provides Volume-Controlled breaths' / 'PRVC = Volume Control with an automatic pressure adjustment' (RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 26 + notes; RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 16)Breath type is 'actually PC or PS' with volume feedback; adaptive targeting (Hess Ch 20 Ventilator Modes; Hess Ch 19 maxim #9); RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 4 itself says 'Pressure-controlled breaths with automatic pressure adjustment to achieve a target VT'Answer by function, which all sources agree on: target VT delivered with automatically adjusted inspiratory pressure at the lowest PIP possible. If forced to classify, use the 202 slide 4 wording ('Hybrid': pressure-controlled breaths targeting a VT).
HFOV frequency range3–15 Hz = 180–900 breaths/min (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 20 notes)1 to 15 Hz (1 Hz = 60 breaths/min) (Hess Ch 38 — Mechanical Ventilation); 'up to 900 breaths/min in the adult' (Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes); 3–10 Hz (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 82 notes)RCP 105 deck: 3–15 Hz (180–900/min). Upper limit 15 Hz/900 per min agrees across the deck and Hess Ch 20/38. Hess Ch 22 (neonatal) gives 400–2400/min generically, with 3–15 Hz on the SensorMedics 3100A. Use the RCP 105 Management of MV deck's 3–15 Hz (no RCP 202 deck gives a Hz range); the RCP 203 deck's 3–10 Hz lies inside it.
HFOV: when to use itMain application is to minimize development of lung injury (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 26); traditionally neonates when conventional ventilation fails, 'used successfully' for adult acute respiratory failure (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 20 notes)'Not routinely recommended for adult patients with acute respiratory failure or ARDS because it has not demonstrated a clinical advantage and may cause harm' (RCP 202 Lssn 7b Trauma slide 22 notes, although the same slide's text says 'PSV, APRV, and HFOV are highly considered'); strong recommendation AGAINST routine HFOV in moderate/severe ARDS (Hess Ch 38 CPG 38-1); might be considered for refractory hypoxemia in experienced centers (Hess Ch 20); HFV indicated primarily for hypoxemic respiratory failure unresponsive to conventional methods (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 61)Value A is the RCP 202 Advanced Modes deck plus the RCP 105 Management deck; value B is the RCP 202 Trauma deck notes plus Hess and an RCP 203 slide. They fit together: the Advanced Modes deck gives HFOV's purpose (minimize lung injury); the Trauma notes and Hess say it is not routine for adult ARDS/acute respiratory failure. The Trauma slide text lists HFOV as 'highly considered' but its notes hedge; use the notes. A question on routine adult use: not recommended.
APRV time at P_low'Patient spends less than 1.5 sec at P low' (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 22)Time at Plow 0.2 to 0.8 s in adults; 80% to 95% of the cycle at Phigh; Tlow ends when expiratory flow reaches 50% to 75% of peak expiratory flow (Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes); T_low 0.2–0.8 s (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 26; notes 'usually <1 second'); slide 12 notes give typical open-lung T_low 0.4–0.8 s with P_high 25–30 and T_high 4–6 s, vs slide 26 T_high 3–5 sNot contradictory (Hess range lies inside the deck limit). Use the deck's '< 1.5 s' on this exam; know Hess's 0.2–0.8 s if asked about adult settings. The RCP 203 deck's 0.2–0.8 s matches Hess; for its starting settings use slide 26 (the slide text).
PAV percent assist'PAV is set to overcome 80% of the elastance and airflow resistance'; % assist e.g. 50%, 70% (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 15 notes)Clinician adjusts percentage of support from 5% to 95%, typically so WOB is 0.5 to 1.0 J/L (Hess Ch 20 — Ventilator Modes)Deck's 80% is the Chang/instructor figure; Hess gives the adjustable range. Use the deck figure if a single number is asked.
HFOV weaning steps (adult)Wean FiO₂ to 40%; reduce mPaw 2–3 cmH₂O q4–6h to 22–24; switch to PCV f 20–25, I:E 1:1, PEEP 12; titrate VT 6–8 mL/kg, Pplat < 35, mPaw < 20 (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 20 notes)Lower FiO₂ to 0.40; reduce Pmean 2–3 cmH₂O every 4–6 h to ≤ 20; then PC at pressure limit ≤ 30 with PEEP 10–12; then standard SBT (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 96)Same first steps (FiO₂ 0.40, 2–3 cmH₂O every 4–6 h). Source B is an RCP 203 deck; no RCP 202 deck gives weaning steps. Know both endpoints: 22–24 then PCV with PEEP 12 (RCP 105) vs ≤ 20 then PC ≤ 30 with PEEP 10–12 (RCP 203).
Asynchrony categoriesTrigger, cycle and mode (Hess Ch 20 — Patient–Ventilator Interaction)Trigger, flow and cycle (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 78)Source B is an RCP 203 deck. Both include trigger and cycle; list flow asynchrony (flow starvation) as its own type if asked for the deck's three.
APRV: must the patient breathe spontaneously?APRV allows but doesn't require spontaneous breathing (organ-donor cases) (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 22 notes)Should only be used on patients who can spontaneously trigger enough breaths for an adequate minute ventilation (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 26)RCP 202 deck for how the mode works (spontaneous breathing permitted, not required); RCP 203 for patient selection (best with an intact drive, which item 10 already says).
VC vs PC: which first?VC 1st choice, PC 2nd choice (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 3; RCP 105 Operating Modes)'In the NBRC hospital' either is satisfactory, VT 6–8 mL/kg PBW (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 31); no evidence the choice changes mortality in chest trauma (RCP 202 Lssn 7b Trauma slide 22)RCP 202 Advanced Modes deck: VC first. Know that the RCP 203 deck and the Trauma deck say either is acceptable if lung-protective.
Normal I:E ratio1:1.5 to 1:3 (sometimes 1:4) (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 24); about 1:1.5 to 1:3, no 1:4 (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 30 notes)1:2 to 1:3 (sometimes 1:4) (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 30 text)Use the RCP 202 deck's 1:1.5 to 1:3; 1:2 is inside both.
PEEP: level where danger startsPEEP > 10 cmH₂O (or Paw > 30; or PIP > 50) associated with alveolar rupture/barotrauma (RCP 202 Lssn 4 slide 6; RCP 105 Operating Modes slide 9); 'safe' PEEP < 10 (RCP 105 Initiation slide 32; RCP 105 Procedures slide 34); PEEP > 15 with IAP > 20 harmful (RCP 105 Principles slide 44)Aggressive PEEP of at least 13-15 cmH₂O had outcomes comparable to conservative (at least 7-9); higher PEEP may benefit moderate/severe ARDS (P/F ≤ 200); limit PEEP by Pplat > 30 or driving pressure > 15 rather than a fixed PEEP number (Hess Ch 20 - ARDS); Procedures slide 34 notes call PEEP < 10 'older practice', many ARDS patients need 10-15 or higherFor 'dangerous levels start at': PEEP > 10 cmH₂O (the RCP 202 slide, starred). Know that ARDS practice individualizes PEEP and often exceeds 10.
Plateau pressure ceilingPplat < 35 cmH₂O (RCP 105 Procedures slide 34 slide text; RCP 105 Initiation slide 32 'safe' pressures; RCP 105 Management of MV slides 20 and 64)Pplat < 30 cmH₂O (ARDSNet, on the same Procedures slide and in its notes; RCP 105 Initiation slide 13 notes; RCP 202 Burn slide 32 notes ≤ 30; Hess Ch 20 and Ch 51 'below 30 ... lower if possible'; RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 31: Pplat > 30 during VC -> probably switch to PC at 30 or less)Use < 30 cmH₂O. The Procedures notes call it today's accepted goal. Recognize < 35 as Chang's printed 'safe' value.
Driving pressure: which subtractionPIP - PEEP called 'Driving Pressure or Pressure above PEEP' (RCP 105 Principles slide 78 notes); RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 37 text: 'PIP - PEEP, referred to as the Driving Pressure or delta P (total pressure delivered!)'; Hess Ch 20 Box 20-5 (PC): 'Driving pressure: A higher difference between peak inspiratory pressure and PEEP increases VT'; RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slides 38, 44: in PC modes 'Driving Pressure, termed ∆P (PIP – PEEP)', typically 15-25 cmH₂ODriving pressure = Pplat - PEEP (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 37 notes: PIP - PEEP is 'NOT the true lung driving pressure'; RCP 105 Procedures slide 34 notes; RCP 202 ECMO slide 20 notes; Hess Ch 20 Tidal Volume and Plateau Pressure sections, Ch 51); target < 15 cmH₂OUse Pplat - PEEP (< 15). PIP - PEEP = total pressure delivered (resistive + elastic). The two are equal only when flow reaches zero at end-inspiration (usual in PC), where PIP = Pplat (Hess Ch 20 Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP; RCP 105 Waveforms slide 25 notes).
Applied PEEP to counter auto-PEEP: how muchExternal PEEP up to 80-90% of measured auto-PEEP; only for spontaneously breathing patients (RCP 203 Lssn 2b slide 42 notes)PEEP up to 75% to 85% of auto-PEEP in obstructive disease to facilitate triggering (Hess Ch 20 - Obstructive Lung Disease)Deck (RCP 203): up to 80-90%. If a question quotes Hess, 75-85%. Both say partial, not full, matching, and only after setting changes (RCP 105 Management of MV slide 35).
Breath-hold length for Pplat and auto-PEEPEnd-inspiratory pause 0.5 to 1.5 s; end-expiratory pause 0.5 to 1.5 s (Hess Ch 20 - Plateau Pressure and Auto-PEEP)End-inspiratory hold and end-expiratory pause 0.5 to 2 seconds (Hess Ch 51 - Alveolar Pressure); at least 2-3 seconds of zero flow for airway and alveolar pressures to equilibrate (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 41)Hess gives 0.5-1.5 s (Ch 20) or 0.5-2 s (Ch 51) for the routine pause; the RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 deck (slide 41) requires at least 2-3 s of zero flow, with complete muscle relaxation, for bedside static mechanics (PEEP-titration methods). Use 0.5-1.5 s for a routine plateau pause; at least 2-3 s if the stem is about static mechanics for PEEP titration.
Normal airway resistanceNormal Raw 0.6-2.4 cmH₂O/L/sec (about 1-3); > 5 think obstruction; > 10 severe (RCP 105 Principles slide 2; population not stated)Intubated adults 5-12 cmH₂O/L/sec (RCP 203 Lssn 3a slide 54); intubated, mechanically ventilated RI should be < 10 cmH₂O/L/s (Hess Ch 51)Different populations. Use 0.6-2.4 for normal airways; for an intubated patient on the ventilator, use the RCP 203 5-12 (Hess < 10).
PEEP vs lower inflection point of the P-V curveLower inflection point = best PEEP level (RCP 105 Waveforms slide 62 notes; slide 64 notes example: optimum PEEP = 10; the same deck's slide 58 notes: 'A common strategy is to set PEEP at or slightly above the Lower Inflection Point')PEEP is set slightly greater than the lower inflection point (Hess Ch 20 Box 20-6); optimal PEEP is the lowest pressure needed to exceed the LIP, typically set 2-3 cmH₂O above it (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 46 and notes)Answer 'just above the LIP' (RCP 203 deck and the course textbook agree). 'LIP = best PEEP' is the RCP 105 Waveforms wording of the same idea; recognize it if a stem quotes it.
High-pressure alarm setting10 to 15 cmH₂O above the observed PIP (RCP 105 Initiation of MV slide 27)35 cmH₂O initially if PIP not yet measured, then 10 cmH₂O above the observed PIP once stabilized (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 19 notes, 'per AARC Clinical Guidelines')Source is an RCP 203 deck. Set 10 above the observed PIP (inside both); use 35 only for an initial setting before any PIP is measured.
Low-pressure and low-volume alarm settingsLow inspiratory pressure 10 to 15 cmH₂O below observed PIP; low exhaled VT 100 mL lower than expired VT (RCP 105 Initiation of MV slide 26)Low PIP 10 cmH₂O below observed PIP or the PI setting in PC; VT alarms 150 mL above and 150 mL below the set VT (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 19 notes)Source B is an RCP 203 deck. Low PIP: 10 below fits both. Low VT: give the Initiation deck's 100 mL for a ventilator-alarm question from this course; recognize 150 mL as the RCP 203 value.
Auto-PEEP cause: expiratory time constant'Short expiratory time constant (airways take longer to empty)' (RCP 203 Lssn 7 Ch 11 slide 72 notes)Long expiratory time constant (obstructed airways, high-compliance units) (Hess Ch 20 auto-PEEP determinants)Use 'long' (Hess). The deck's own explanation (airways take longer to empty) describes a long time constant.

Is Legionella typical or atypical?Listed on the "Community-Acquired Typical Pneumonia (2 of 2)" slide (RCP 103 Infectious Pulmonary Disease, slide 10)Atypical organism (Hess Ch 36 — Etiology and Atypical Organisms; Hess Ch 56; RCP 104 Pharm Ch 14, slide 29 notes: "Mycoplasma, Chlamydia, and Legionella")Atypical: Hess Ch 36 (the source named for this ask), Hess Ch 56 and the RCP 104 deck agree. WBC in atypical pneumonia"only a moderate elevation of white cell count" (RCP 103 Infectious, slide 12)"the WBC count is usually normal" (Hess Ch 36 — Etiology)For a typical-vs-atypical contrast: typical = elevated WBC; atypical = normal or only mildly elevated. If forced to choose, use Hess (named for this ask)
VAP onset time"more than 48 to 72 hours after endotracheal intubation" (RCP 103 Infectious, slide 18); "within 48 hours after intubation" (RCP 105 Procedures, slide 43 text)"48 hours or more after endotracheal intubation" (RCP 105 Procedures, slide 43 notes "update"); "after 48 hours of endotracheal intubation" (Hess Ch 36 text); "after 2–4 days" (Hess Ch 36 Table 36-1); "for more than 48 hours" of mechanical ventilation (RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9, slide 59)≥ 48 hours after intubation (RCP 105 updated notes "48 hours or more"; RCP 203 Ch 9 deck "more than 48 hours" of ventilation; Hess text "after 48 hours"); the slide wording "within 48 hours" is contradicted by its own notes
VAP incidence10 to 25% of mechanically ventilated patients; mortality 33 to 50% (RCP 105 Procedures, slide 43)15% to 26% of intubated patients; 7 to 28 episodes/1000 ventilator-days; attributable mortality "might not be as high as was assumed" (Hess Ch 36)Deck numbers (10–25%, 33–50%) for this exam
HCAP as a categoryPresented as a current category: within 90 days hospitalization, nursing home, or IV antibiotics/chemo/wound care within 30 days (RCP 103 Infectious, slide 17)"retired in 2016"; no longer a distinct entity from HAP (Hess Ch 36 — Definition and Classification; Nosocomial Pneumonia)Know the deck definition, and know that per Hess (named for this ask) HCAP is retired. A "categories" question most safely answers CAP / HAP / VAP
Oral chlorhexidine in the VAP bundle"Providing daily oral care with chlorhexidine (aka: mouthwash)" is a key component of the CDC VAP bundle (RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9, slide 60); "use of oral chlorhexidine" listed among VAP-prevention strategies (RCP 105 Procedures, slide 46 notes); Hess Ch 36 bundle text lists "oral care with chlorhexidine"Hess Ch 36 Box 36-7: "Oral care with chlorhexidine (no longer recommended)"; text: "may prove harmful in some patients"Use the RCP 203 Ch 9 deck (instructor): daily oral chlorhexidine is in the VAP bundle. Know Hess Box 36-7 marks it "no longer recommended" and says it may harm some patients. The RCP 105 current bundle says only "oral care with antiseptic solution (follow your hospital's protocol)"
CPIS for VAP diagnosisModified CPIS > 6 may suggest VAP; a screening tool needing BAL/PSB confirmation (RCP 105 Procedures, slide 45)CPIS is "neither sensitive nor specific; thus, its use is no longer recommended" (Hess Ch 36 — Diagnosis of VAP)Know "> 6" as the deck's threshold; if asked whether CPIS is recommended, Hess says no
Resolution of aspiration pneumonitisUsually resolves in ~72 hrs if no secondary infection; peaks 12–26 h (RCP 103 Infectious, slide 21)"resolves by 48 hours" (Hess Ch 36 — Aspiration and Anaerobic Pneumonia)Deck (~72 h) for this exam
Share of pneumonia caused by S. pneumoniaeS. pneumoniae "accounts for more than 80% of cases" of bacterial pneumonia (RCP 103 Infectious, slide 9)S. pneumoniae "isolated in approximately 5% of patients hospitalized with CAP"; still the most common bacterium (Hess Ch 36)Not the same measure (share of bacterial cases vs isolation rate in all hospitalized CAP). Both say it is the most common bacterial cause; use that
Rapid influenza test sensitivity50% to 60% (Hess Ch 36 — Microbiology)40% to 50% in adults (Hess Ch 36 — Influenza Virus)Internal Hess discrepancy; quote "low (≈40–60%) sensitivity, near-100% specificity"
Viral share of pneumoniaViruses "account for about 50% of all pneumonias" (RCP 103 Infectious, slide 14)Pathogens detected in only ~38% of CAP; of those, the majority are viruses; up to one-third of ICU pneumonia may be viral (Hess Ch 36)Deck (~50%) for this exam; the Hess numbers measure different things
Greatest risk factor for VAP / nosocomial pneumonia"The single greatest risk factor for VAP is prolonged mechanical ventilation" (RCP 105 Procedures, slide 43 notes); same slide: "The ETT is the risk factor, NOT the ventilator itself""The number one risk factor for nosocomial pneumonia is an endotracheal tube" (Hess Ch 36 — Respiratory Recap "Nosocomial Pneumonia")Compatible (ETT = pathway; duration drives risk). For "single greatest risk factor for VAP" use the deck: prolonged mechanical ventilation
Peptic ulcer prophylaxis and subglottic secretion aspiration in the VAP bundleKey bundle components: PUD prophylaxis "using sucralfate rather than H2 antagonists" (slide 59) and "Using Subglottic ETT's that continuously remove secretions" (slide 60); "Aspirate subglottic secretions" is listed separately under "other strategies potentially helpful" (RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9, slides 59–60)Both marked "controversial" in Box 36-7 (Hess Ch 36 — Ventilator-Care Bundle)Deck lists PUD prophylaxis and subglottic ETTs as key bundle components (exam answer); know Hess calls PUD prophylaxis and continuous subglottic aspiration controversial

17Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Berlin P/F severity boundariesMild 200–300, Moderate 100–200, Severe ≤ 100 (RCP 103 ARDS deck, slide 12)Mild 201–300, Moderate 101–200, Severe ≤ 100 (RCP 105 Procedures deck slide 27 notes; Hess Ch 38 Table 38-1)Use 201–300 / 101–200 / ≤ 100. Both are instructor decks; the 105 notes and Hess's table agree, and the 103 deck's ranges share endpoints at 200 and 100.
Berlin severe ARDS cut-off within HessSevere: PaO₂/FIO₂ < 100 mm Hg (Hess Ch 38 text)Severe: ≤100 mm Hg (Hess Ch 38 Table 38-1; both decks)Use ≤ 100 (decks and Hess table agree).
AECC ALI cut-off wordingALI: P/F less than 300 but greater than 200 (Hess Ch 38 text); "<300 mm Hg for ALI and <200 mm Hg for ARDS" (RCP 105 Procedures slide 27 notes, Chang)ALI ≤ 300, ARDS ≤ 200 (Hess Table 38-1; RCP 105 Procedures slide 27 text and notes "For boards ... ALI ≤300 and ARDS ≤200")Use ALI ≤ 300 / ARDS ≤ 200 (the deck's board-oriented line), knowing ALI is obsolete under Berlin.
ARDS mortality rate10% to 90% (RCP 105 Procedures slide 27 notes, Chang); severe ARDS: no number, 'continues to have a high mortality', and the book's 45% 'is not a current universal number' (RCP 202 ECMO deck slide 3 and notes); 30% to 50% (ECMO deck slide 3 notes reference block)"as high as 30% to 45%", 35% mild – 46% severe, 30–40% by mid-1990s (Hess Ch 38); severe ARDS > 45% (Hess Ch 23)Not a single number in the sources. > 45% for severe ARDS is Hess's figure only; the instructor's ECMO deck notes say it is not a current universal number (mortality varies by population, severity, cause, ECMO use, center, study period). For a deck-worded question: severe ARDS mortality is high and varies; if a number is demanded, > 45% (Hess). Know Chang's 10–90% range and Hess's 30–45% recap. No source gives an ALI-only figure.
Plateau pressure limitPplat < 35 cm H₂O (RCP 105 Procedures slide 34 text; RCP 105 MgmtMV slide 64)Pplat < 30 cm H₂O (ARDSNet, cited on the same Procedures slide and its notes; Hess Ch 38 CPG 38-1 and Key Points; Burn deck ≤ 30)Use < 30 cm H₂O (the deck's own notes call it "the accepted goal").
Lung-protective tidal volume4–6 mL/kg (RCP 105 MgmtMV slide 64); 4–7 mL/kg for permissive hypercapnia (RCP 105 Procedures slide 36); 4–6 mL/kg IBW ARDSNet (RCP 105 Initiation of MV notes)4 to 8 mL/kg PBW (Hess CPG 38-1); 6 mL/kg (Hess Key Points; Procedures slide 35 notes "approximately 6 mL/kg")Use ≈ 6 mL/kg predicted body weight (every source includes it; the deck says "If you remember one number for ARDS, remember 6 mL/kg").
Driving pressure limit"15 cm H₂O or less" (Hess Ch 38)"ideally <15 cm H₂O" (RCP 105 Procedures slide 34 notes)Use ≤ 15 cm H₂O; the difference is only the boundary value.
Atelectrauma stress at open/closed junction"may exceed 100 cm H₂O" (Hess Ch 20)reached 140 cm H₂O at a peak trans-alveolar pressure of 30 cm H₂O, 1970 study (RCP 105 Procedures slide 26 notes, Chang)Not a true conflict (140 > 100); know that the junction stress is far higher than the applied pressure.
Normal lung complianceLung compliance 50–100 mL/cm H₂O, static compliance 70–100 (RCP 202 Burn deck slide 37 notes); adult static compliance 60–100, normal ≈ 80 mL/cm H₂O (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 7)Lung compliance CL = 200 mL/cm H₂O, calculated from transpulmonary pressure (Hess Ch 51)Use the deck values (50–100 lung / 60–100 static, ≈ 80). All agree compliance is decreased in ARDS.
AECC conference year1992 (RCP 105 Procedures slide 27 notes, Chang)1994 (Hess Ch 38 — Definition)Know both; the guide cites Hess 1994 and notes the deck says 1992. ALI/AECC cut-offs are the same in both.
Berlin definition yearIntroduced in 2012 (RCP 105 Procedures slide 26 notes)Task Force convened in Berlin in 2011 (Hess Ch 38)Not a true contradiction (convened 2011, introduced 2012); know both.
P/F interpretation scaleInitiation of MV slide 7 notes: 400 normal; 300–400 mild impairment; 200–300 moderate impairment (ALI); <200 severe / possible ARDSBerlin: mild 201–300, moderate 101–200, severe ≤100 (Procedures notes; Hess Table 38-1)Use Berlin categories for ARDS severity; the Initiation of MV scale is a general oxygenation scale.

18Respiratory Drugs

TopicValue A (source)Value B (source)Which to use
Succinylcholine onset and durationParalysis in 60–90 seconds, clinical duration 10–15 minutes (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 NMBA deck, slide 13 and notes)1 to 1.5 mg/kg IV push → complete relaxation in 30 to 60 seconds, lasting 4 to 5 minutes (Hess Ch 57); "fastest onset (about 1 minute)" (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 43 notes, Chang)Use the NMBA deck: onset 60–90 s, duration 10–15 min (the instructor's pharmacology deck). Know Hess's 30–60 s / 4–5 min as the book figure.
Train-of-four twitch count vs % blockade and target0 = 100%, 1 = 95%, 2 = 90%, 3 = 80%, 4 = < 75%; ICU goal 1–2 twitches (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 NMBA deck, slide 36 and notes)1–2 twitches = 80% to 90% blockade; 3 twitches ≈ 80%, "suitable for maintaining patient-ventilator synchrony" (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 49, Chang)Use the NMBA deck table and its 1–2 twitch goal for the RCP 202 pharmacology questions; recognize Chang's 3-twitch statement if it appears.
BUN normal range7–20 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck slide 23/26 notes; also "around 7–20" in RCP 104 Ch 13 notes)8–18 mg/dL (RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 53); 10–20 mg/dL (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 39); 8–20 mg/dL (RCP 104 Ch 13 azotemia note); 7–21 mg/dL (Hess Ch 5)Use 7–20 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck = this course).
Serum creatinine normal rangeMen 0.7–1.3, women 0.6–1.1 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck slide 26 notes)0.6–1.2 mg/dL (RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 53); 0.7–1.5 mg/dL (RCP 105 Principles of MV slide 39); women 0.5–1.1 / men 0.6–1.2 and "about 0.6–1.3" (RCP 104 Ch 13 notes); 0.7–1.4 mg/dL (Hess Ch 5)Use men 0.7–1.3 / women 0.6–1.1 mg/dL (RCP 202 Renal deck = this course).
Half-life definition wording"Time required for the plasma concentration of a drug to decrease by one half" (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 deck, slide 18)"the time it takes for half of the drug to be metabolized" (Hess Ch 57); "how long it takes for half of the dose to be metabolized and eliminated from the bloodstream" (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 21 notes)Use the Ch 2 deck definition (plasma concentration falls by one-half), which is the pharmacology deck's formal definition.
Propofol and analgesia"Propofol provides analgesia only at high dose" (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 67, Chang)"it does not provide analgesia" (Hess Ch 57); Weaning deck notes also say Propofol "does NOT provide pain relief"Treat propofol as a sedative with no useful analgesia; give an opioid for pain (both sources agree on that practice).
Opioid receptor subtypesmu (sedation), kappa (hypnosis), sigma (delirium) (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 58, Chang)mu, delta, and kappa receptors (Hess Ch 57); naloxone competes at mu, kappa and sigma sites (Hess Ch 57)For the deck-based exam use mu/kappa/sigma; naloxone blocks them, with greatest affinity for mu (both sources).
Agent with Hofmann (spontaneous) eliminationCisatracurium (Nimbex) — Hofmann degradation; optimal choice in hepatic or renal failure (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 NMBA deck, slide 24)"atracurium is unique because it undergoes spontaneous breakdown (Hofmann elimination)" (RCP 105 Weaning deck slide 43 notes)Not a true contradiction (related drugs), but answer "cisatracurium" for the organ-failure question, as the NMBA deck states it explicitly.
Succinylcholine in liver/kidney failure"If liver/kidneys are bad → use Cisatracurium / Avoid: Sux" (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 18 NMBA deck, slide 33 notes)"Declines in renal or hepatic function do not necessitate dose reductions" (Hess Ch 57)Use the deck: avoid Sux and use cisatracurium in liver/kidney failure.
Propofol and bronchospasmAdverse effects include laryngospasm and bronchospasm (RCP 105 Weaning slide 66, Chang)Propofol reduces airways resistance; useful induction agent in bronchospasm (Hess Ch 57)Know both; for a deck question, laryngospasm/bronchospasm is a listed propofol adverse effect.
Inhaled opioids for dyspnea"More effective than systemic opioids for decreasing the sensation of dyspnea in pts with advanced respiratory failure" and "No studies have shown improved effectiveness when inhaled" (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 20 slide 34 — both on the same slide)"no evidence to support the use of inhaled opioids" for breathlessness (Hess Ch 57)No clear pick; the deck allows the inhaled route when IV access is unavailable. Hess finds no evidence of benefit.
Classification of intrathecal routeParenteral (RCP 104 Pharm Ch 2 slides 4 notes, 5)Percutaneous or mucosal (Hess Ch 57)Use the deck: intrathecal = parenteral.
20

Still Not Covered

57 asks, or parts of asks, that no deck and no passage in Hess answers

Each was searched under its acronym, its expansion, spelling variants and the speaker notes before being listed. Several come from the PowerPoints named on the study guide that are not in the vault. Look these up in those PowerPoints or ask your instructor rather than guessing.

1Weaning Parameters and Extubation Assessment

AskWhat is missing
CXRNo source gives a chest-radiograph criterion/cutoff for weaning or extubation readiness (e.g., resolving infiltrates); only what CXR evaluates, the compliance-lowering conditions, and ETT-position confirmation
CBC (Hb/Hct, WBC)No Hb, Hct or WBC threshold stated as a weaning/extubation criterion; decks give CBC normals (RCP 103 Clinical Data), "anemic/septic → more likely to fail" (weaning deck notes) and Hb < 10 g/100 mL as an anemia-correction threshold in ventilated pts (RCP 105 Mgmt of MV slide 14). Hess Ch 23 thresholds (7–8 g/dL ECMO; 7–7.5 g/dL critical-illness studies) are not tied to weaning
SensoriumNo numeric GCS/LOC threshold for extubation; GCS ≤ 8 appears only as an intubation/airway-protection trigger (RCP 203 Lssn 2a slide 8)
Cuff-leak testNo deck covers the cuff-leak test; Hess describes a quantitative leak (volume or % of VT) but gives no numeric pass/fail cutoff
V̇E (Hess value)Hess lists 12 L/min as a predictor without stating < or >; direction not printed

2Therapeutic Hypothermia (TH)

AskWhat is missing
Cold DiuresisNo definition, mechanism or consequences anywhere in the vault
Hypokalemia (as related to TH)No source links hypokalemia (or potassium shifts during cooling/rewarming) to TH; only general hypokalemia facts given
Hypoglycemia (as related to TH)No TH-specific glucose target or TH-related hypoglycemia; only brain-dependence on glucose (RCP 105) and general glucose numbers
Apoptosis (as related to TH/HIE)Only a definition (programmed cell death) and an ARDS example; nothing on neuronal apoptosis after ischemia or TH's effect on it
Excitatory Neurotransmitters / Cell Injury (TH effect)No source states how TH affects glutamate release, calcium influx or the injury cascade
Advantages and Disadvantages of TH (PowerPoint list)The TH PowerPoint's own list (e.g. rewarming, shivering control during TH, electrolyte shifts) is not in the vault; answer assembled from RCP 105 decks and Hess Ch 18
Neonatal HIE cooling (brief hint)Hess Ch 22 and Ch 48 contain no therapeutic cooling for neonatal HIE (only prevention of hypothermia in neonates)
GCS (deck chart)RCP 105 Critical Issues slide 21 GCS chart is an image with no text; component table taken from Hess Ch 1 Table 1-4

4Burn and Inhalation Injury

AskWhat is missing
Dynamic lung compliance rangeNo source gives a normal/acceptable range for dynamic compliance; formula Cdyn = VT/(PIP − PEEP) is not printed verbatim (only 'use PIP for dynamic compliance')
CO levels: symptoms at each COHb %No symptom-by-percentage table (e.g., headache/nausea/confusion bands) in any source; only level bands (203 decks), severe-poisoning symptoms (105 Nontraditional) and Hess's 50% statement

6Renal System

AskWhat is missing
DKAFormal DKA diagnostic criteria (glucose cutoff, serum ketone/beta-hydroxybutyrate level, DKA-specific pH/HCO3-/anion-gap cutoffs); only case values are given (glucose 650 mg/dL, urine ketones 4.0 mmol/L, pH 6.98, HCO3- 6)
DKAInsulin/fluid protocol detail (doses, infusion rates, potassium replacement thresholds, transition criteria); sources say only "normal saline and IV insulin", "insulin therapy, fluids, electrolytes", and SC insulin within 48 h
UOPWeight-based oliguria definition (e.g., < 0.5 mL/kg/hr as abnormal); only a burn-resuscitation goal of ~0.5 mL/kg/hr (Burn deck) and a normal of 1 mL/kg/hr (Hess Normal Values) exist
UOPNo deck or Hess passage gives a normal daily urine output (1200–1500 mL/day), an oliguria cutoff (< 25 mL/hr or < 400 mL/day), a burn urine-output goal (1.0 mL/kg/hr), a uremia definition, or AKI/CKD definitions; these are not in this guide

7GI System: Routes of Nutrition Delivery

AskWhat is missing
Routes of nutrition delivery (enteral routes)"Jejunostomy" (J-tube) and "nasoenteric/nasojejunal/nasoduodenal" are never named as such; Hess Ch 11 describes only post-pyloric small-bowel ("enteric") tubes and percutaneous tubes "into the stomach or small intestine"; a gastrostomy–jejunostomy (G/J) tube appears in a home-supply list (Hess Ch 25 Table 25-5) and as a gastrojejunal tube for nutrition/medications after lung transplant (Hess Ch 46)
Routes of nutrition delivery (parenteral)Specific differences between peripheral parenteral nutrition (PPN) and central TPN (dextrose/osmolarity limits, allowable duration of PPN); sources say only "central or peripheral; central usually preferred" (Hess Ch 11) and that peripheral lines are for short-term, non-irritating infusions (RCP 203 deck)
Routes of nutrition delivery (parenteral risks)Named TPN complications (catheter-related bloodstream infection, hyperglycemia, hepatic dysfunction, line complications) are not listed; sources give only gut atrophy/bacterial contamination, "fewer risks of infection" with enteral, overfeeding/respiratory acidosis, and hypophosphatemia with carbohydrate overload

8Pulmonary Rehabilitation

AskWhat is missing
Pulmonary Rehab: Adequate Arterial Oxygenation limits; Hyperventilation & HypoventilationThe Pulm Rehab deck (RCP 202 Lssn 6) gives no PaO₂/SpO₂ number and does not mention hyperventilation or hypoventilation; items 1 and 5 are answered from Hess Ch 24/50 and the RCP 203/103 decks

9Trauma

AskWhat is missing
GCSThe deck's GCS point table (RCP 105 Critical Issues slide 21) is an image with no extractable text; only the notes' E4/V5/M6 maxima and one worked example survive. Full eye/verbal/motor table taken from Hess Ch 1 Table 1-4
Difference between MAP and ICPNo deck gives a numeric normal MAP range (RCP 105 Hemodynamic slide 8 MAP formula/range is an image; only MAP 60/70 floors survive; the Trauma deck gives CPP = MAP − ICP and CPP > 60 but no MAP range). Normal range 65–105 taken from Hess Table 3-1
Difference between MRI and CTNo deck contrasts CT and MRI directly; the Trauma deck (RCP 202 Lssn 7b) covers CT only (slides 18, 25) and never mentions MRI. The comparison is built mainly from Hess Ch 7 and the RCP 103 Clinical Data slide 46 list. No source states CT radiation dose figures
Pneumothorax (treatment)No source explains how the "> 25%" pneumothorax size in the RCP 105 chest-tube indication is measured, or details the "Oxygen to Treat Pneumothorax Protocol" (named only in RCP 103 TDP slide). The Trauma deck says only "Small, stable pneumothorax: Observation and oxygen" (slide 8 notes), with no flow or FiO₂

10Interventional Pulmonary Procedures

AskWhat is missing
Know difference between COPD, GERD, Emphysema, and Lung CancerNo source states why these four are grouped or contrasts them directly. The Lssn 7a deck mentions GERD (chronic cough, slide 19), emphysema (scabbard trachea, endobronchial valves, slides 13, 31) and lung cancer (biopsy, EBUS staging, slides 15–16) but never defines COPD; definitions come from 103 COPD, 103 Infectious slide 23, 103 Lung Cancer, Hess Ch 33/47/9

11Perioperative Management of Lung Transplants

AskWhat is missing
Perioperative Management of Lung Transplants (whole topic)The instructor's 'PeriOp Mngmnt of Lung Transplant PwrPnt' is not in the vault; no deck covers lung transplant beyond ECMO 'bridge to transplant' bullets
Dates for 1st Single and Double-Lung transplantsHess gives 1963 (first lung transplant, Hardy), 1983 ('first successful lung transplant', Toronto, pulmonary fibrosis) and 1986 ('first successful double-lung transplant for emphysema'); the book never literally labels the 1983 transplant 'single-lung'. The PowerPoint's (different) answer cannot be seen. Hess Ch 58 Table 58-3 also lists Hardy (year ambiguous in extracted text) and the first heart-lung transplant (Reitz, 1981), which is not a double-lung transplant
Lung Allocation Score (LAS) detailsNo source gives the LAS formula, variable weights, or any score threshold; Hess gives only the variables (Table 46-4), the 0–100 range, age ≥12, data currency (6 months), OPO-region priority and petition process
Lung protective strategies used during mechanical ventilation for lung transplant patientsHess gives postoperative targets (VT ≤6 mL/kg, PEEP max 5, FiO₂ ≤0.5) and donor targets (Pplat/PIP <30) but no intraoperative ventilator settings (only single-lung ventilation sequencing) and no stated rationale for limiting PEEP

12Respiratory Anatomy (Hess Ch 49 — "EVERYTHING!")

AskWhat is missing
Respiratory Anatomy: EVERYTHING (Table 49-4)Table 49-4 "Structural Characteristics of Tracheobronchial Tree" (diameter, generation, area served, total cross-sectional area per structure) is an image; no values in the text
Respiratory Anatomy: pulmonary circulationCh 49 does not describe the gross anatomy of the pulmonary arteries/veins or the bronchial circulation; it covers only pulmonary vascular smooth muscle, tone and hypoxic vasoconstriction
Respiratory Anatomy: Stop and Think questionsCh 49's Stop and Think prompts (C3 vs C6 cord injury muscle groups; diseases affecting the mucociliary apparatus) have no printed answers; the section answers only what the text states (C3–C5 phrenic; thoracic injury → intercostal/accessory loss per RCP 103 Neuro deck)

13Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (CPR) ("EVERYTHING!")

AskWhat is missing
CPR: EVERYTHING (Heuer Ch 15 — Providing RT Techniques in High Risk Situations)Heuer textbook is not in the vault; nothing from Heuer Ch 15 could be checked
CPR: compression:ventilation ratios, 1 and 2 rescuers, by ageNo source states a separate ADULT two-rescuer ratio (text gives 30:2 as the adult ratio and 15:2 two-rescuer only for children/infants). Table 18-5 "Summary of High-Quality CPR Components for BLS Providers" is an image with no text
CPR: AED/defibrillation (energy)No pediatric defibrillation energy (J/kg) in any source; Hess gives only "child shock dose"/child pads for < 8 yr. Adult energies come only from the Algorithm 18-5 description (biphasic 120–200 J or max; monophasic 360 J)
CPR: capnography during CPRNo source gives a numeric PETCO₂ target DURING compressions beyond "< 10 mm Hg → improve CPR" and "> 10 mm Hg after 20 min"; ROSC "typically 40 mm Hg" and post-ROSC 35–40 mm Hg are the only other numbers
CPR: ventilation during CPR with an advanced airway (children and infants)No source gives an advanced-airway breath rate for children or infants; "1 breath every 6 s (10/min)" is stated only in adult ACLS text and Algorithm 18-5 (Adult ACLS)

14Mechanical Ventilation (29 questions on the exam)

AskWhat is missing
HFOV (when to use / settings)Initial adult HFOV settings (starting amplitude, Hz, mPaw): the Management of MV slide is titled 'Initiate High Frequency Oscillatory Ventilation (HFOV) for adults' but lists only the weaning steps; no deck or Hess chapter gives initiation values
PEEP (everything about it)'Physiologic PEEP' (glottic end-expiratory pressure) and any value for it
Pressures (high and low)A normal adult PIP or plateau pressure range; sources give only ceilings (PIP < 50, Pplat < 30/35) and illustrative values (VT 500 at PIP 20; Pplat 20 -> 40)
Variables (pressure, volume, cycle, time)A named 'limit variable' for volume-control breaths; decks name the limit only for pressure breaths (PSV, PCV = pressure); Hess Ch 19 does not use 'limit' as a phase variable

15Noninvasive Support (NIPPV and HFNC)

AskWhat is missing
Describe VapothermNo source describes the Vapotherm device itself or spells out HiVNI. The RCP 203 Ch 11 slide 36 notes only name it ('HiVNI (aka: VapoTherm)') in a patient case at '45L and 45%'; the instructor's word-play list equates HFNC = VapoTherm or Optiflow, so HFNC content was used
Difference between PEEP, PAP, and Pharyngeal PressureNo source sets all three terms side by side in one place (the guide assembles the contrast from the decks' PEEP→↑PAP [pulmonary artery pressure] link and Hess Ch 45 / RCP 103 Sleep Apnea's PAP-as-pneumatic-splint description); no source gives a measured pharyngeal pressure during mask NIV. The only esophageal opening pressure number is the RCP 203 Ch 11 slide 52 limit (IPAP < 20–25 cm H₂O)
Hemodynamics – MAP rangeNo deck states a normal MAP range (decks give only the 60 mm Hg tissue-perfusion and 70 mm Hg cerebral-perfusion minimums and the formulas); the 65–105 mm Hg range is from Hess Table 3-1 only
pt asynchrony on NIPPVNo deck uses the words asynchrony/synchrony in an NIV context; deck content used is rise/I-time/leak rules and the RCP 105 Waveforms leak auto-trigger slide (an invasive-ventilator slide)

16Pneumonia (PNA)

AskWhat is missing
ChlorhexidineNo source gives the chlorhexidine concentration, oral-care technique, or its mechanism; frequency is now given only as "daily" (RCP 203 Lssn 6a — Ch 9, slide 60); otherwise sources give its use as a skin antiseptic (A-line insertion, thoracentesis prep) and its status in the VAP bundle
Legionella infectionsNo source gives the Legionella incubation period or a characteristic CXR pattern; no deck has more than a one-line mention (103 slide 10, 104 slide 29 notes, 103_ARDS notes)
Categories of PNAHess Table 36-3 (PSI risk-class mortality) is an image with no extractable text, so PSI class mortality percentages are not available

17Acute Respiratory Distress Syndrome (ARDS)

AskWhat is missing
Rates of Mortality for Acute Lung Injury (ALI)No source gives a mortality rate for ALI as a separate category. Only ARDS / "ALI/ARDS" figures exist (10–90% Chang; 10–15% of ALI/ARDS die of refractory hypoxemia; Hess 30–45%, 35% mild–46% severe).
Difference between ... Pneumotrauma ...The word "pneumotrauma" appears in no deck, no Hess chapter and not in the midterm guide; the sources use "barotrauma" (answered as barotrauma).
Difference between Atelectrauma, Biotrauma ..."Biotrauma" appears in no deck; it is defined only in Hess Ch 20 (answered from Hess).

18Respiratory Drugs

AskWhat is missing
Half-life of medsNo source gives numeric half-lives for the ICU sedatives, opioids or NMBAs; Hess Table 57-7 "Pharmacokinetics of Neuromuscular Blockers" and Weaning deck Table 13-9 are images not present in the text files. Half-life statements that do exist (now in the section): adenosine ~12 s, sleep-aid benzodiazepines > 12–24 h, lorazepam much longer than midazolam, theophylline highly variable, omalizumab 17–22 days (Hess Ch 57), milrinone shorter than inamrinone, COHb ~4–5 h RA / 60–90 min 100% O₂ / 20–30 min HBOT (Burn deck); also flecainide "long" and amiloride "moderately long" (non-respiratory).
AcetylcysteineNo nebulized NAC dose/volume is given in text (RCP 104 Ch 9 slide 8 points to Table 9.1, which is an image). Hess gives only the oral BRONCUS dose (600 mg twice daily).
Difference between Creatinine and BUNHess Ch 5's creatinine-clearance equations are missing from the text file (blank where the formulas should be); only the normal CrCl values survive.

Exam Strategy & Word Play

Word play and special precaution words, exactly as the final study guide lists them

§How to use the word-play list

Know what you know; and know you know it!!

NBRC WORLD ONLY – forget about everything else!!

TRUST YOUR “FIRST” INSTINCT WHEN YOU CHOOSE AN ANSWER!! Toooooooo many students, after reviewing the exam, wish they had stayed with their First Choice (answer)!!

Word play to watch for on ALL exams (classroom and NBRC).

Phrases That Mean the SAME THING

PhraseMeans the same thing as
Bronchoprovocation testMethacholine Challenge
Cardio-Respiratory arrestFull Arrest
Chronic HypercarbiaCO₂ Retainer
Controlled O₂ concentrationSpecific FiO₂
Heart FailureCardiac Arrest = Cardiac Failure (O₂ does NOT help!)
HFNCVapoTherm or Optiflow (Fisher & Paykel) (NOT HFNC up to 15L)
Induction AgentsAnesthetic Meds
ImbalanceMismatch
Inclusion CriteriaIndication
Liberation ParametersWeaning
Long-standingChronic
MainstayBest suited for / Therapy of choice (1st choice)
NIPPVNPPV = NIV = BiPAP
No purposeful movement to commandUnconscious
Oxygen-induced HypoventilationKnocked out the Hypoxic Drive
PBW (predicted body weight)IBW (ideal body weight)
Rapid Response Team (RRT)Medical Emergency Team (MET)
SIMVIMV

Special Precaution Words — They’re Trying to “Trick” You

  • Adequate
  • Aggressive pain control does NOT mean too much meds
  • At least
  • Blunted
  • Changes to treatment plan and “actual orders” are NOT the same
  • Eliminate does NOT mean Filter (Lungs vs. Kidneys)
  • Except
  • First
  • Laryngotracheal (Injury)
  • Mechanical Ventilation = Vent or BiPAP; it is NOT specific to Vents only
  • Most commonly used
  • Most likely
  • Next
  • Nonpulmonary
  • Not
  • Observation
  • Obliterated
  • Precedence (Importance)
  • Recommend at this time
  • Routinely
  • Severe
  • Significance
Sources. Your Study Brain lecture decks with speaker notes, and Hess, MacIntyre & Galvin, Respiratory Care: Principles and Practice, 4th ed. Every card carries the deck and slide, or the chapter, it came from.

What this version is. The RCP 202 final-exam study guide answered in full, laid out in the Tier 3 study-guide-only format, updated 25 Oct 2026 for the new and revised PowerPoints. 194 cards across 18 topics, 189 source conflicts, 57 honest gaps.

25 Oct 2026 — the Lesson 8 decks. Lssn 8a — PeriOp Management of Lung Transplants (Hess Ch 46), 34 slides is the PowerPoint the study guide names and the vault did not have; section 11 no longer says no deck covers it, and gains three cards (donor procurement, the two operations, allocation today). Lssn 8b — PostOp Respiratory Care (Hess Ch 39), 21 slides is not one of the eighteen listed topics, so it gets no section: its overlapping material was folded into Pneumonia (postoperative PNA and atelectasis), Mechanical Ventilation (intraoperative settings, postoperative failure) and Trauma (postoperative VTE and PE). Four diagrams are carried over from the midterm Tier 3 deck where the final asks the same thing: proxemic zones, VV vs VA ECMO, VC vs PC pressure waveforms, and the five processes of respiration.

Scope: final study guide only — 140 questions, 100 points, 150 minutes.
